## I / 第一章
===Original===

“Hullo! No!﻿—Yes!﻿—upon my soul, it *is* Jacob! Why, Delafield, my dear fellow, how are you?”
So saying﻿—on a February evening a good many years ago﻿—an elderly gentleman in evening dress flung himself out of his cab, which had just stopped before a house in Bruton Street, and hastily went to meet a young man who was at the same moment stepping out of another hansom a little farther down the pavement.
The pleasure in the older man’s voice rang clear, and the younger met him with an equal cordiality, expressed perhaps through a manner more leisurely and restrained.
“So you *are* home, Sir Wilfrid? You were announced, I saw. But I thought Paris would have detained you a bit.”
“Paris? Not I! Half the people I ever knew there are dead, and the rest are uncivil. Well, and how are you getting on? Making your fortune, eh?”
And, slipping his arm inside the young man’s, the speaker walked back with him, along a line of carriages, towards a house which showed a group of footmen at its open door. Jacob Delafield smiled.
“The business of a land agent seems to be to spend someone else’s﻿—as far as I’ve yet gone.”
“Land agent! I thought you were at the bar?”
“I was, but the briefs didn’t come in. My cousin offered me the care of his Essex estates. I like the country﻿—always have. So I thought I’d better accept.”
“What﻿—the Duke? Lucky fellow! A regular income, and no anxieties. I expect you’re pretty well paid?”
“Oh, I’m not badly paid,” replied the young man, tranquilly. “Of course you’re going to Lady Henry’s?”
“Of course. Here we are.”
The older man paused outside the line of servants waiting at the door, and spoke in a lower tone. “How is she? Failing at all?”
Jacob Delafield hesitated. “She’s grown very blind﻿—and perhaps rather more infirm, generally. But she is at home, as usual﻿—every evening for a few people, and for a good many on Wednesdays.”
“Is she still alone﻿—or is there any relation who looks after her?”
“Relation? No. She detests them all.”
“Except you?”
Delafield raised his shoulders, without an answering smile. “Yes, she is good enough to except me. You’re one of her trustees, aren’t you?”
“At present, the only one. But while I have been in Persia the lawyers have done all that was necessary. Lady Henry herself never writes a letter she can help. I really have heard next to nothing about her for more than a year.

===Chinese===

“喂！不对！——是！——天哪，真的是雅各布（Jacob）！哎呀，德拉菲尔德（Delafield），我亲爱的伙计，你好吗？”

说着这话——那是许多年前二月的一个傍晚——一位身着晚礼服的老先生从刚停在布鲁顿街（Bruton Street）一座宅邸门前的马车上跳了下来，匆匆迎向一位青年；那青年此刻也正从人行道稍远处另一辆双轮马车上跨下来。

老人声音里的喜悦清晰可闻，青年迎上前去，报以同样的热忱，只是那热忱或许以更从容克制的举止表现出来。

“这么说你真的回国了，威尔弗里德·伯里爵士（Sir Wilfrid Bury）？我看到报上登了。我还以为巴黎会把你多留些日子呢。”

“巴黎？我才不去！我在那边认识的人一半已经死了，剩下的一半都不懂礼数。哎，你近况如何？在发财吧，嗯？”

说话人把胳膊伸进青年的臂弯，同他一道沿着一排马车走向那座宅邸——敞开的门前侍立着一列仆役。雅各布·德拉菲尔德微微一笑。

“照我目前的经验，地产代理这行营生，似乎就是替别人花钱。”

“地产代理！我还以为你在当律师呢？”

“当过，可案子总也不来。我表兄让我替他照管埃塞克斯（Essex）的产业。我喜欢乡下——一向喜欢。所以我想不如应了下来。”

“什么——那位公爵？幸运的家伙！一份稳当的收入，还不用操心。我猜他们给你的薪水不薄吧？”

“哦，不算坏，”青年从容地答道，“你当然是去亨利夫人（Lady Henry）府上的了？”

“当然。这就到了。”

老人在门前侍立的仆役队列外停住脚，压低声音。“她怎么样？更见衰弱了么？”

雅各布·德拉菲尔德迟疑了一下。“她的眼睛更瞎了——大概整个人也更虚弱了些。但她照旧在家待客——每晚接待几个人，逢星期三则来得多些。”

“还是独个儿过？——就没有哪位亲戚照应她么？”

“亲戚？没有。她把他们个个都厌恶透了。”

“除了你？”

德拉菲尔德耸了耸肩，没有报以微笑。“是啊，承她抬举，把我也除外。你是她的受托人之一，对吧？”

“眼下还是唯一的一个。不过我在波斯（Persia）的这些年里，该办的事律师们都办妥了。亨利夫人本人，能不写的信从来一封也不写。一年多来，我实在没听到她的什么消息。”

===Original===

This morning I arrived from Paris﻿—sent round to ask if she would be at home﻿—and here I am.”
“Ah!” said Delafield, looking down. “Well, there is a lady who has been with her, now, for more than two years﻿—”
“Ah, yes, yes, I remember. Old Lady Seathwaite told me﻿—last year. Mademoiselle Le Breton﻿—isn’t that her name? What﻿—she reads to her, and writes letters for her﻿—that kind of thing?”
“Yes﻿—that kind of thing,” said the other, after a moment’s hesitation. “Wasn’t that a spot of rain? Shall I charge these gentry?”
And he led the way through the line of footmen, which, however, was not of the usual Mayfair density. For the party within was not a “crush.” The hostess who had collected it was of opinion that the chief object of your house is not to entice the mob, but to keep it out. The two men mounted the stairs together.
“What a charming house!” said the elder, looking round him. “I remember when your uncle rebuilt it. And before that, I remember his mother, the old Duchess here, with her swarm of parsons. Upon my word, London tastes good﻿—after Teheran!”
And the speaker threw back his fair, grizzled head, regarding the lights, the house, the guests, with the air of a sensitive dog on a familiar scent.
“Ah, you’re fresh home,” said Delafield, laughing. “But let’s just try to keep you here﻿—”
“My dear fellow, who is that at the top of the stairs?”
The old diplomat paused. In front of the pair some half a dozen guests were ascending, and as many coming down. At the top stood a tall lady in black, receiving and dismissing.
Delafield looked up.
“That is Mademoiselle Le Breton,” he said, quietly.
“She receives?”
“She distributes the guests. Lady Henry generally establishes herself in the back drawing-room. It doesn’t do for her to see too many people at once. Mademoiselle arranges it.”
“Lady Henry must indeed be a good deal more helpless that I remember her,” murmured Sir Wilfrid, in some astonishment.
“She is, physically. Oh, no doubt of it! Otherwise you won’t find much change. Shall I introduce you?”
They were approaching a woman whose tall slenderness, combined with a remarkable physiognomy, arrested the old man’s attention. She was not handsome﻿—that, surely, was his first impression? The cheekbones were too evident, the chin and mouth too strong.

===Chinese===

今天早上我从巴黎一到——便打发人去问她今晚在不在家——于是我这就来了。”

“啊！”德拉菲尔德说着，垂下了目光。“是这样，有位女士陪着她，如今已经两年多了——”

“啊，对，对，我想起来了。西思韦特老夫人（Lady Seathwaite）去年同我提过。勒布雷东小姐（Mademoiselle Le Breton）——是不是叫这个名字？她给她读书，替她写信——那一类的事吧？”

“是的——那一类的事，”对方迟疑了片刻，答道。“是不是飘雨点了？要不要我来向这班人物冲锋开路？”

于是他领头穿过仆役的队列——不过那队列并不像梅费尔（Mayfair）寻常人家那样密。因为里面这场聚会并非一场“大杂烩”。召集它的女主人认为，宅邸的首要职分不在招引人群，而在将人群拒之门外。两位男士一同登上楼梯。

“多雅致的宅子！”年长的那位环顾四周，说道。“我还记得你伯父重建它的光景。再早些年，我还记得他母亲——老公爵夫人——在这里，身边围着她那一大群牧师。说真的，在德黑兰（Teheran）待过之后，伦敦的味道真叫好！”

说话人向后扬起他那颗漂亮的、花白的头，打量着灯火、宅邸与宾客，那神气像一条嗅着了熟悉气味的灵敏的狗。

“啊，你才回来，”德拉菲尔德笑道。“不过我们总得设法把你留住才是——”

“亲爱的伙计，楼梯顶上那位是谁？”

老外交官停住了脚。两人前面有五六个宾客正在上楼，还有差不多数目的人正往下走。楼梯顶上立着一位穿黑衣的高挑女士，迎接着来客，又一一打发开去。

德拉菲尔德抬眼望去。

“那是勒布雷东小姐，”他平静地说。

“由她来迎客？”

“她替客人分派去处。亨利夫人一般稳坐在后面那间客厅里。一次见的人太多，她受不住。这些都是勒布雷东小姐安排的。”

“亨利夫人必定比我记忆里虚弱得多了，”威尔弗里德爵士喃喃道，颇为吃惊。

“身体上的确如此。哦，这毫无疑问！除此之外你不会发现她有什么变化。要不要我替二位引见？”

他们此刻正走近一位女士。她那高挑纤细的身段，配上一副格外出众的眉目神情，一下子攫住了老人的注意力。她算不得漂亮——这想必是他的第一印象？颧骨太显，下颏与嘴太刚硬。

===Original===

And yet the fine pallor of the skin, the subtle black-and-white, in which, so to speak, the head and face were drawn, the life, the animation of the whole﻿—were these not beauty, or more than beauty? As for the eyes, the carriage of the head, the rich magnificence of hair, arranged with an artful eighteenth-century freedom, as Madame Vigée Le Brun might have worn it﻿—with the second glance the effect of them was such that Sir Wilfrid could not cease from looking at the lady they adorned. It was an effect as of something over-living, over-brilliant﻿—an animation, an intensity, so strong that, at first beholding, a bystander could scarcely tell whether it pleased him or no.
“Mademoiselle Le Breton﻿—Sir Wilfrid Bury,” said Jacob Delafield, introducing them.
“*Is* she French?” thought the old diplomat, puzzled. “And﻿—have I ever seen her before?”
“Lady Henry will be so glad!” said a low, agreeable voice. “You are one of the old friends, aren’t you? I have often heard her talk of you.”
“You are very good. Certainly, I am an old friend﻿—a connection also.” There was the slightest touch of stiffness in Sir Wilfrid’s tone, of which the next moment he was ashamed. “I am very sorry to hear that Lady Henry has grown so much more helpless since I left England.”
“She has to be careful of fatigue. Two or three people go in to see her at a time. She enjoys them more so.”
“In my opinion,” said Delafield, “one more device of milady’s for getting precisely what she wants.”
The young man’s gay undertone, together with the look which passed between him and Mademoiselle Le Breton, added to Sir Wilfrid’s stifled feeling of surprise.
“You’ll tell her, Jacob, that I’m here?” He turned abruptly to the young man.
“Certainly﻿—when mademoiselle allows me. Ah, here comes the Duchess!” said Delafield, in another voice.
Mademoiselle Le Breton, who had moved a few steps away from the stairhead with Sir Wilfrid Bury, turned hastily. A slight, small woman, delicately fair and sparkling with diamonds, was coming up the stairs alone.
“My dear,” said the newcomer, holding out her hands eagerly to Mademoiselle Le Breton, “I felt I must just run in and have a look at you. But Freddie says that I’ve got to meet him at that tiresome Foreign Office! So I can only stay ten minutes. How are you?”

===Chinese===

然而那肌肤细腻的苍白，那——不妨这样说——以其勾勒出头部与面容的精微的黑白笔意，那周身的生气与神采——这些难道不是美，或者更胜于美么？至于那双眼睛、那昂起的头姿、那一头丰厚华美的秀发——发式带着十八世纪那种匠心独运的疏放，一如维瑞·勒布伦夫人（Madame Vigée Le Brun）当年佩戴的模样——只消再看一眼，其效果便叫威尔弗里德爵士再也无法把目光从被这一切装点的女士身上挪开。那效果仿佛某种生命力过剩、光彩过炽的东西——那般的生气，那般的烈度，叫人乍见之下，几乎说不清它究竟令自己愉悦与否。

“勒布雷东小姐——这位是威尔弗里德·伯里爵士，”雅各布·德拉菲尔德引见道。

“她真是法国人么？”老外交官纳闷地想。“还有——我从前见过她不成？”

“亨利夫人一定非常高兴！”一个低沉悦耳的声音说道。“您是她的老朋友之一，对吧？我常听她谈起您。”

“您太客气了。不错，我是老朋友——还是亲戚。”威尔弗里德爵士的语气里带着一丝极细微的生硬，下一刻他便为此羞愧了。“听说我离开英国以后，亨利夫人虚弱了许多，我很难过。”

“她得当心，不要劳累。每次只进去两三个人探望。这样她反倒更尽兴。”

“依我看，”德拉菲尔德说，“这不过是夫人的又一桩手腕，好叫样样事情都恰恰称她的心。”

青年人打趣的低语，加上他与勒布雷东小姐之间交换的那一眼，更添了威尔弗里德爵士心头那股压着的惊异。

“雅各布，你去告诉她我到了，好么？”他蓦地转向青年。

“当然——只要小姐放我进去。啊，公爵夫人来了！”德拉菲尔德换了一种声调说。

勒布雷东小姐方才同威尔弗里德·伯里爵士离了楼梯口几步，闻声急忙转身。一位纤小轻盈的女士正独自拾级而上，肤色白皙细腻，浑身钻石闪闪耀眼。

“亲爱的，”来客一面急切地朝勒布雷东小姐伸出双手，一面说道，“我觉得非得进来瞧你一眼不可。可是弗雷迪（Freddie）说我必须到那个讨厌的外交部（Foreign Office）去同他会合！所以我只能待十分钟。你好么？”

===Original===

﻿—then, in a lower voice, almost a whisper, which, however, reached Sir Wilfrid Bury’s ears﻿—“worried to death?”
Mademoiselle Le Breton raised eyes and shoulders for a moment, then, smiling, put her finger to her lip.
“You’re coming to me tomorrow afternoon?” said the Duchess, in the same half-whisper.
“I don’t think I can get away.”
“Nonsense! My dear, you must have some air and exercise! Jacob, will you see she comes?”
“Oh, I’m no good,” said that young man, turning away. “Duchess, you remember Sir Wilfrid Bury?”
“She would be an unnatural goddaughter if she didn’t,” said that gentleman, smiling. “She may be your cousin, but I knew her before you did.”
The young Duchess turned with a start.
“Sir Wilfrid! A sight for sair een. When did you get back?”
She put her slim hands into both of his, and showered upon him all proper surprise and the greetings due to her father’s oldest friend. Voice, gesture, words﻿—all were equally amiable, well trained, and perfunctory﻿—Sir Wilfrid was well aware of it. He was possessed of a fine, straw-colored mustache, and long eyelashes of the same color. Both eyelashes and mustache made a screen behind which, as was well known, their owner observed the world to remarkably good purpose. He perceived the difference at once when the Duchess, having done her social and family duty, left him to return to Mademoiselle Le Breton.
“It *was* such a bore you couldn’t come this afternoon! I wanted you to see the babe dance﻿—she’s *too* great a duck! And that Canadian girl came to sing. The voice is magnificent﻿—but she has some tiresome tricks!﻿—and *I* didn’t know what to say to her. As to the other music on the 16th﻿—I say, can’t we find a corner somewhere?” And the Duchess looked round the beautiful drawing-room, which she and her companions had just entered, with a dissatisfied air.
“Lady Henry, you’ll remember, doesn’t like corners,” said Mademoiselle Le Breton, smiling. Her tone, delicately free and allusive, once more drew Sir Wilfrid’s curious eyes to her, and he caught also the impatient gesture with which the Duchess received the remark.
“Ah, that’s all right!” said Mademoiselle Le Breton, suddenly, turning round to himself. “Here is Mr. Montresor﻿—going on, too, I suppose, to the Foreign Office. Now there’ll be some chance of getting at Lady Henry.”

===Chinese===

——随后，她用低得近乎耳语、却仍旧送到威尔弗里德·伯里爵士耳中的声音说——“是不是愁得要死？”

勒布雷东小姐抬了抬眼睛和肩膀，随即微笑着把一根手指按在唇上。

“明天下午你到我这儿来，好不好？”公爵夫人依旧半用耳语问。

“我看我走不开。”

“胡说！亲爱的，你必须透透空气、活动活动！雅各布，你负责让她来，好不好？”

“哦，我可派不上用场，”青年说着转过身去。“公爵夫人，您还记得威尔弗里德·伯里爵士吧？”

“她要是不记得，那可就枉为教女了，”爵士微笑道。“她尽管是你表妹，我认识她却比你早。”

年轻的公爵夫人吃了一惊，转过身来。

“威尔弗里德爵士！看见您真是叫人眼前一亮。您什么时候回来的？”

她把自己纤细的双手放进他的双手里，把对父亲最老的朋友应有的一整套惊异与问候，尽数倾洒在他身上。声音、姿态、言辞——全都同样和蔼可亲、训练有素，而又敷衍了事——威尔弗里德爵士对此心里透亮。他生有一撇好看的草黄色髭须，睫毛同样颜色，也同样长。这睫毛与髭须构成一道帘幕，众所周知，它们的主人正是躲在幕后，把世界观察得格外透彻。待公爵夫人尽完社交与家族的两重义务、撇下他回到勒布雷东小姐身边去时，他立刻察觉出了前后神情的差别。

“今天下午你没能来，真是太可惜了！我本想让你瞧瞧小家伙跳舞——她实在是太可爱了！还有那位加拿大姑娘来唱歌。嗓子好极了——可她有些讨人嫌的小毛病！——我都不知道该对她说什么才好。至于十六号那场音乐——哎，我们能不能找个角落说话？”公爵夫人一面四下张望这间她与同伴们刚走进的漂亮客厅，一面带着不满意的神气说。

“您记得的，亨利夫人不喜欢人躲角落，”勒布雷东小姐微笑着说。她那口吻的洒脱与弦外之音，又一次把威尔弗里德爵士好奇的目光引向她；他也捕捉到公爵夫人听了这话时那不耐烦的手势。

“啊，正好！”勒布雷东小姐忽然转身朝着他。“蒙特雷索先生（Mr. Montresor）来了——想必也要到外交部去。这下您就有机会见着亨利夫人了。”

===Original===

Sir Wilfrid looked down the drawing-room, to see the famous War Minister coming slowly through the well-filled but not crowded room, stopping now and then to exchange a greeting or a farewell, and much hampered, as it seemed, in so doing, by a pronounced and disfiguring short-sight. He was a strongly built man of more than middle height. His iron-gray hair, deeply carved features, and cavernous black eyes gave him the air of power that his reputation demanded. On the other hand, his difficulty of eyesight, combined with the marked stoop of overwork, produced a qualifying impression﻿—as of power teased and fettered, a Samson among the Philistines.
“My dear lady, good night. I must go and fight with wild beasts in Whitehall﻿—worse luck! Ah, Duchess! All very well﻿—but you can’t shirk either!”
So saying, Mr. Montresor shook hands with Mademoiselle Le Breton and smiled upon the Duchess﻿—both actions betraying precisely the same degree of playful intimacy.
“How did you find Lady Henry?” said Mademoiselle Le Breton, in a lowered voice.
“Very well, but very cross. She scolds me perpetually﻿—I haven’t got a skin left. Ah, Sir Wilfrid!﻿—*very* glad to see you! When did you arrive? I thought I might perhaps find you at the Foreign Office.”
“I’m going on there presently,” said Sir Wilfrid.
“Ah, but that’s no good. Dine with me tomorrow night?﻿—if you are free? Excellent!﻿—that’s arranged. Meanwhile﻿—send him in, mademoiselle﻿—send him in! He’s fresh﻿—let him take his turn.” And the Minister, grinning, pointed backward over his shoulder towards an inner drawing-room, where the form of an old lady, seated in a wheeled invalid-chair between two other persons, could be just dimly seen.
“When the Bishop goes,” said Mademoiselle Le Breton, with a laughing shake of the head. “But I told him not to stay long.”
“He won’t want to. Lady Henry pays no more attention to his cloth than to my gray hairs. The rating she has just given me for my speech of last night! Well, good night, dear lady﻿—good night. You *are* better, I think?”
Mr. Montresor threw a look of scrutiny no less friendly than earnest at the lady to whom he was speaking; and immediately afterwards Sir Wilfrid, who was wedged in by an entering group of people, caught the murmured words:
“Consult me when you want me﻿—at any time.”
Mademoiselle Le Breton raised her beautiful eyes to the speaker in a mute gratitude.

===Chinese===

威尔弗里德爵士顺着客厅望去，只见那位鼎鼎大名的陆军大臣正缓缓穿过这间满座而不拥挤的客厅，不时停下来与人寒暄道别，而这样做时似乎又大受掣肘——原来他深度近视，颜面大受其累。他体格结实，身量高出中人。铁灰色的头发、刀刻般的深纹、幽深凹陷的黑眼睛，赋予他一种其名声所要求的威势气象。可另一方面，他的目力不济，加上操劳过度造成的明显伛偻，又造成一种打折扣的印象——仿佛威力受了揶揄、戴了枷锁，一个陷在非利士人当中的参孙。

“亲爱的夫人，晚安。我得上白厅（Whitehall）去同野兽搏斗了——真倒霉！啊，公爵夫人！说得倒轻巧——可你也溜不掉！”

说着，蒙特雷索先生同勒布雷东小姐握了握手，又朝公爵夫人嫣然一笑——这两个动作表露出的亲昵程度分毫不差。

“您看亨利夫人精神如何？”勒布雷东小姐压低了声音问。

“好得很，就是火气大。她骂起我来没个完——我的脸皮都给她剥光了。啊，威尔弗里德爵士！——见到您真高兴！您什么时候到的？我还想着兴许能在外交部碰上您呢。”

“我过一会儿就过去，”威尔弗里德爵士说。

“啊，那可赶不上喽。明儿晚上来我这儿吃饭怎么样？——您要是有空的话？好极了！——就这么说定了。这就——小姐，把他送进去吧——送进去！他精神头正足——让他去排他的班。”大臣咧嘴笑着，回头朝里间客厅指了指。在那儿，隐约可以望见一位老太太的身影，坐在轮椅上，身边陪着两个人。

“等主教走了就送去，”勒布雷东小姐笑着摇了摇头。“不过我嘱咐过他，叫他别待久。”

“他也待不住。亨利夫人对他的教士身份，跟对我的白头发一样全不放在眼里。她方才为着我昨晚的演说，把我好一顿数落！好了，晚安，亲爱的夫人——晚安。您好些了，我想是？”

蒙特雷索先生向跟他说话的女士投去一瞥，那目光既是审视，又满含友善；紧接着，被一伙新到的宾客挤在当中的威尔弗里德爵士，听到了一句低低的话：

“什么时候用得着我——随时来找我。”

勒布雷东小姐把那双美丽的眼睛抬向说话的人，无言地流露着感激。

===Original===

“And five minutes ago I thought her plain!” said Sir Wilfrid to himself as he moved away. “Upon my word, for a *dame de compagnie* that young woman is at her ease! But where the deuce have I seen her, or her double, before?”
He paused to look round the room a moment, before yielding himself to one of the many possible conversations which, as he saw, it contained for him. It was a stately panelled room of the last century, furnished with that sure instinct both for comfort and beauty which a small minority of English rich people have always possessed. Two glorious Gainsboroughs, clad in the subtlest brilliance of pearly white and shimmering blue, hung on either side of the square opening leading to the inner room. The fair, clouded head of a girl, by Romney, looked down from the panelling above the hearth. A gowned abbé, by Vandyck, made the centre of another wall, facing the Gainsboroughs. The pictures were all famous, and had been associated for generations with the Delafield name. Beneath them the carpets were covered by fine eighteenth-century furniture, much of it of a florid Italian type subdued to a delicate and faded beauty by time and use. The room was cleverly broken into various circles and centres for conversation; the chairs were many and comfortable; flowers sheltered tête-à-têtes or made a setting for beautiful faces; the lamps were soft, the air warm and light. A cheerful hum of voices rose, as of talk enjoyed for talking’s sake; and a general effect of intimacy, or gayety, of an unfeigned social pleasure, seemed to issue from the charming scene and communicate itself to the onlooker.
And for a few moments, before he was discovered and tumultuously annexed by a neighboring group, Sir Wilfrid watched the progress of Mademoiselle Le Breton through the room, with the young Duchess in her wake. Wherever she moved she was met with smiles, deference, and eager attention. Here and there she made an introduction, she redistributed a group, she moved a chair. It was evident that her eye was everywhere, that she knew everyone; her rule appeared to be at once absolute and welcome. Presently, when she herself accepted a seat, she became, as Sir Wilfrid perceived in the intervals of his own conversation, the leader of the most animated circle in the room.

===Chinese===

“五分钟前，我还只当她相貌平常呢！”威尔弗里德爵士走开时自忖道。“说真的，就一个陪侍的女伴（dame de compagnie）而言，这位年轻女士也实在太安之若素了！可我到底在哪儿见过她——或是见过跟她一模一样的人？”

他驻足把客厅略略环顾了片刻，才让自己投身进去——他看得出，这屋里为他备着许多场可能的谈话。这是上一世纪的一间镶板客厅，陈设处处见出那种对舒适与美观兼备的可靠直觉，英国富人当中那一小部分人向来有此天赋。两幅光彩夺目的庚斯博罗（Gainsborough），浸在珍珠白与波光蓝交织的最精微的光泽里，分挂在通往里间客厅的方形门洞两侧。壁炉上方的镶板上，一幅罗姆尼（Romney）画的云鬓蓬松的少女头像俯视着下方。另一面墙的正中，是一幅凡·戴克（Vandyck）笔下的穿法衣的修道院院长，与那两幅庚斯博罗遥遥相对。这些画幅幅是名作，世世代代与德拉菲尔德这个姓氏相连。画下方的地毯上摆着精美的十八世纪家具，其中许多是浮华的意大利款式，被时光与使用磨成了幽雅黯淡的美。客厅被巧妙地隔成一圈圈、一处处的谈话中心；座椅又多又舒适；鲜花或为对对私语遮护，或为美丽面容增色；灯色柔和，空气温暖轻快。一片欢快的语声嗡然而起，仿佛人们为谈话而谈话，乐在其中；一种亲密、欢愉、毫不作伪的社交快乐的总体气氛，似乎从这迷人场景里散发出来，感染着旁观的人。

片刻之后，他还未被邻近的一伙人发觉、闹哄哄地拉将过去，威尔弗里德爵士便一直注视着勒布雷东小姐在客厅中的行进，年轻的公爵夫人尾随其后。她走到哪里，哪里便有笑脸、礼敬与殷勤。她时而替人引见，时而重新调配一伙客人，时而挪动一把座椅。显而易见，她的目光无处不在，她对人人熟识；她的号令仿佛既是绝对的，又人人乐于遵从。不多时，待她自己落了座，她便成了——威尔弗里德爵士在自己谈话的间隙里察觉到——全客厅最活跃的那一圈人的领袖。

===Original===

The Duchess, with one delicate arm stretched along the back of Mademoiselle Le Breton’s chair, laughed and chattered; two young girls in virginal white placed themselves on big gilt footstools at her feet; man after man joined the group that stood or sat around her; and in the centre of it, the brilliance of her black head, sharply seen against a background of rose brocade, the grace of her tall form, which was thin almost to emaciation, the expressiveness of her strange features, the animation of her gestures, the sweetness of her voice, drew the eyes and ears of half the room to Lady Henry’s “companion.”
Presently there was a movement in the distance. A man in knee-breeches and silver-buckled shoes emerged from the back drawing-room. Mademoiselle Le Breton rose at once and went to meet him.
“The Bishop has had a long innings,” said an old general to Sir Wilfrid Bury. “And here is Mademoiselle Julie coming for you.”
Sir Wilfrid rose, in obedience to a smiling sign from the lady thus described, and followed her floating black draperies towards the farther room.
“Who are those two persons with Lady Henry?” he asked of his guide, as they approached the penetralia where reigned the mistress of the house. “Ah, I see!﻿—one is Dr. Meredith﻿—but the other?”
“The other is Captain Warkworth,” said Mademoiselle Le Breton. “Do you know him?”
“Warkworth﻿—Warkworth? Ah﻿—of course﻿—the man who distinguished himself in the Mahsud expedition. But why is he home again so soon?”
Mademoiselle Le Breton smiled uncertainly.
“I think he was invalided home,” she said, with that manner, at once restrained and gracious, that Sir Wilfrid had already observed in her. It was the manner of someone who *counted*; and﻿—through all outward modesty﻿—knew it.
“He wants something out of the ministry. I remember the man,” was Sir Wilfrid’s unspoken comment.
But they had entered the inner room. Lady Henry looked round. Over her wrinkled face, now parchment-white, there shone a ray of pleasure﻿—sudden, vehement, and unfeigned.
“Sir Wilfrid!”
She made a movement as though to rise from her chair, which was checked by his gesture and her helplessness.
“Well, this is good fortune,” she said, as she put both her hands into both of his. “This morning, as I was dressing, I had a feeling that something agreeable was going to happen at last﻿—and then your note came. Sit down there. You know Dr.

===Chinese===

公爵夫人把一条纤细的手臂搭在勒布雷东小姐椅背上，又说又笑；两个一身洁白的女孩子挨着她坐上两只镀金大脚凳；男客们一个接一个加入围着她或立或坐的一圈；而在这圈子的中央，她那衬着玫瑰锦缎背景、轮廓分明的乌发上的神采，她那高挑得几乎清瘦的身姿的优雅，她那奇特面容的丰富表情，她那手势的生动活泼，她那嗓音的甜润，把半个客厅的耳目都引向了亨利夫人的这位“女伴”。

忽然，远处一阵动静。一个穿及膝马裤、缀银扣鞋子的男仆从后面的客厅里走了出来。勒布雷东小姐立刻起身迎上去。

“主教这一局打得可够久了，”一位老将军对威尔弗里德·伯里爵士说。“喏，朱莉（Julie）小姐来接您了。”

威尔弗里德爵士应着那位女士含笑的示意站起身，跟随她那飘拂的黑绸衣裙，朝更里面的房间走去。

“陪着亨利夫人的那两位是谁？”走近这位主宰内室的女主人的圣所时，他向引路人问道。“啊，我看见了——一位是梅雷迪思博士（Dr. Meredith）——另一位呢？”

“另一位是沃克沃思上尉（Captain Warkworth），”勒布雷东小姐说。“您认识他么？”

“沃克沃思——沃克沃思？啊——对了——就是在马赫苏德远征（Mahsud expedition）里立下战功的那个人。可他怎么这么快就回国了？”

勒布雷东小姐含糊地微微一笑。

“我想他是伤病退伍回国的吧，”她说，用的仍是威尔弗里德爵士已领教过的那种既收敛又亲切的态度。那是一种自身“有分量”的人的态度——而且，尽管外表处处谦抑——她自己心中有数。

“他想从内阁手里讨点什么。这个人我记得。”威尔弗里德爵士心里这样暗自评断。

但他们已经走进内室。亨利夫人回过头来。她那张如今已如羊皮纸般蜡黄的布满皱纹的脸上，闪过一道喜悦的光——突然的、热烈的、毫不作假的喜悦。

“威尔弗里德爵士！”

她做了个要从椅子里挣起的动作，被他一摆手、又因自身的无力，给止住了。

“这可真是好运气，”她把双手放进他的双手里，说道。“今天早上梳妆的时候，我就觉得，说什么也该有桩称心的事临头了——随后你的短简就到了。坐到那儿去。你认识梅雷迪思——”

===Original===

Meredith. He’s as quarrelsome as ever. Captain Warkworth﻿—Sir Wilfrid Bury.”
The square-headed, spectacled journalist addressed as Dr. Meredith greeted the newcomer with the quiet cordiality of one for whom the day holds normally so many events that it is impossible to make much of anyone of them. And the man on the farther side of Lady Henry rose and bowed. He was handsome, and slenderly built. The touch of impetuosity in his movement, and the careless ease with which he carried his curly head, somehow surprised Sir Wilfrid. He had expected another sort of person.
“I will give you my chair,” said the Captain, pleasantly. “I have had more than my turn.”
“Shall I bring in the Duchess?” said Mademoiselle Le Breton, in a low tone, as she stooped over the back of Lady Henry’s chair.
That lady turned abruptly to the speaker.
“Let her do precisely as she pleases,” said a voice, sharp, lowered also, but imperious, like the drawing of a sword. “If she wants me, she knows where I am.”
“She would be so sorry﻿—”
“*Ne jouez pas la comédie, ma chère!* Where is Jacob?”
“In the other room. Shall I tell him you want him?”
“I will send for him when it suits me. Meanwhile, as I particularly desired you to let me know when he arrived﻿—”
“He has only been here twenty minutes,” murmured Mademoiselle Le Breton. “I thought while the Bishop was here you would not like to be disturbed﻿—”
“You thought!” The speaker raised her shoulders fiercely. “*Comme toujours, vous vous êtes trop bien amusée pour vous souvenir de mes instructions﻿—voilà la vérité!* Dr. Meredith,” the whole imperious form swung round again towards the journalist, “unless you forbid me, I shall tell Sir Wilfrid who it was reviewed his book for you.”
“Oh, good Heavens! I forbid you with all the energy of which I am capable,” said the startled journalist, raising appealing hands, while Lady Henry, delighted with the effect produced by her sudden shaft, sank back in her chair and grimly smiled.
Meanwhile Sir Wilfrid Bury’s attention was still held by Mademoiselle Le Breton. In the conversation between her and Lady Henry he had noticed an extraordinary change of manner on the part of the younger lady. Her ease, her grace had disappeared. Her tone was humble, her manner quivering with nervous anxiety.

===Chinese===

——博士。他好斗的脾气一点儿没改。沃克沃思上尉——这位是威尔弗里德·伯里爵士。”

那位被唤作梅雷迪思博士的方额角、戴眼镜的新闻记者，以平静的亲切向新来者致意——在这样一种人的一天里，事情向来多得不可胜数，不可能对其中任何一件特别看重。坐在亨利夫人另一侧的男人起身鞠了一躬。他相貌英俊，身形修长。他举手投足间那一点急躁的冲劲，以及他顶着那颗鬈发脑袋时那副毫不在意的洒脱，不知怎的叫威尔弗里德爵士吃了一惊。他原以为会是另一种人。

“我把座位让给您，”上尉和气地说。“我待的工夫够久的了。”

“要不要把公爵夫人请进来？”勒布雷东小姐俯身在亨利夫人椅后，低声问道。

老太太蓦地转向说话的人。

“让她爱怎么样就怎么样，”一个声音说道，尖利，也同样压得低，却专横得像出鞘的剑。“她若要找我，自知道我在哪儿。”

“她会很过意不去的——”

“*Ne jouez pas la comédie, ma chère!*（别演戏了，我亲爱的！）雅各布在哪儿？”

“在隔壁房间。要我去告诉他您找他么？”

“我需要他的时候，自会打发人去叫。眼下——我明明特意嘱咐过你，他一到就来回我——”

“他来了还不到二十分钟，”勒布雷东小姐低声说。“我想着主教在这儿的时候，您总不愿让人打扰——”

“你想！”说话的人勃然耸起肩膀。*Comme toujours, vous vous êtes trop bien amusée pour vous souvenir de mes instructions—voilà la vérité!*（跟往常一样，你自己玩得太痛快，把我的嘱咐忘了个干净——就是这么回事！）“梅雷迪思博士，”那整个专横的身子又转回新闻记者那边，“你要是不拦住我，我可要告诉威尔弗里德爵士，替你那刊物评论他大作的是谁了。”

“哦，老天爷！我使出全身的力气求您别说，”吓了一跳的新闻记者举起双手告饶；而亨利夫人呢，对自己这支冷箭收到的效果大为得意，往椅背上一靠，冷冷地笑了。

与此同时，威尔弗里德·伯里爵士的注意力仍旧被勒布雷东小姐牵住。在她与亨利夫人的这一番对话里，他注意到这位年轻女士的态度起了异乎寻常的变化。她的从容、她的优雅都无影无踪。她的语气谦卑，举止因神经质的焦灼而发颤。

===Original===

And now, as she stood a moment behind Lady Henry’s chair, one trembling hand steadying the other, Sir Wilfrid was suddenly aware of yet another impression. Lady Henry had treated her companion with a contemptuous and haughty ill-humor. Face to face with her mistress, Mademoiselle Le Breton had borne it with submission, almost with servility. But now, as she stood silent behind the blind old lady who had flouted her, her wonderfully expressive face, her delicate frame, spoke for her with an energy not to be mistaken. Her dark eyes blazed. She stood for anger; she breathed humiliation.
“A dangerous woman, and an extraordinary situation,” so ran his thought, while aloud he was talking Central Asian politics and the latest Simla gossip to his two companions.
Meanwhile, Captain Warkworth and Mademoiselle Le Breton returned together to the larger drawing-room, and before long Dr. Meredith took his leave. Lady Henry and her old friend were left alone.
“I am sorry to hear that your sight troubles you more than of old,” said Sir Wilfrid, drawing his chair a little nearer to her.
Lady Henry gave an impatient sigh. “Everything troubles me more than of old. There is one disease from which no one recovers, my dear Wilfrid, and it has long since fastened upon me.”
“You mean old age? Oh, you are not so much to be pitied for that,” said Sir Wilfrid, smiling. “Many people would exchange their youth for your old age.”
“Then the world contains more fools than even I give it credit for!” said Lady Henry, with energy. “Why should anyone exchange with me﻿—a poor, blind, gouty old creature, with no chick or child to care whether she lives or dies?”
“Ah, well, that’s a misfortune﻿—I won’t deny that,” said Sir Wilfrid, kindly. “But I come home after three years. I find your house as thronged as ever, in the old way. I see half the most distinguished people in London in your drawing-room. It is sad that you can no longer receive them as you used to do: but here you sit like a queen, and people fight for their turn with you.”
Lady Henry did not smile. She laid one of her wrinkled hands upon his arm.
“Is there anyone else within hearing?” she said, in a quick undertone. Sir Wilfrid was touched by the vague helplessness of her gesture, as she looked round her.
“No one﻿—we are quite alone.”

===Chinese===

而此刻，当她立在亨利夫人座椅后面那片刻间，一只颤抖的手扶着另一只手，威尔弗里德爵士忽然又生出另一重感受。亨利夫人对待她的女伴，是轻蔑而傲慢的坏脾气。直面女主人的时候，勒布雷东小姐忍了下来，几乎是卑躬屈节。可现在，她默默立在这位方才羞辱过她的失明老太太身后，她那张表情丰富的脸、她那纤弱的身躯，正替她诉说着一种错认不了的激愤。她那双黑眼睛冒着火。她立在那儿，便是愤怒；她呼吸着的，便是屈辱。

“一个危险的女人，一种异乎寻常的处境，”他心里这样想，嘴上却在同两位同伴大谈中亚政局与西姆拉（Simla）最新的闲话。

这当口，沃克沃思上尉与勒布雷东小姐一同回到了大客厅；不多时，梅雷迪思博士也起身告辞。亨利夫人与老朋友单独留了下来。

“听说您的视力比从前更折磨您了，我很难过，”威尔弗里德爵士说着，把椅子朝她挪近了些。

亨利夫人不耐烦地叹了口气。“样样都比我从前更折磨我。有一种病，谁也好不了，我亲爱的威尔弗里德，它缠上我已经很久了。”

“您是说老境？哦，这可算不得怎么可怜，”威尔弗里德爵士含笑说。“多少人情愿拿他们的青春来换您的老年呢。”

“那这世上的傻瓜，可就比我估摸的还多了！”亨利夫人愤然道。“凭什么要人来同我换——一个又瞎又痛风的老废物，无儿无女，没一个人管她是死是活？”

“唉，这确是不幸——我不否认，”威尔弗里德爵士和善地说。“可是我离家三年回来，见您府上依旧门庭如市，一概如旧。半个伦敦最负盛名的人物都在您客厅里。您再不能像从前那样亲自迎客，固然可惜；可您在此端坐如女王，人们还得争相排队来见您呢。”

亨利夫人没有笑。她把一只皱纹累累的手搭在他的手臂上。

“近处还有旁人听得见么？”她急促地低声问。她四下张望时那副茫然无助的神气，叫威尔弗里德爵士心里一动。

“没有人——就我们两个。”

===Original===

“They are not here for *me*﻿—those people,” she said, quivering, with a motion of her hand towards the large drawing-room.
“My dear friend, what do you mean?”
“They are here﻿—come closer, I don’t want to be overheard﻿—for a *woman*﻿—whom I took in, in a moment of lunacy﻿—who is now robbing me of my best friends and supplanting me in my own house.”
The pallor of the old face had lost all its waxen dignity. The lowered voice hissed in his ear. Sir Wilfrid, startled and repelled, hesitated for his reply. Meanwhile, Lady Henry, who could not see it, seemed at once to divine the change in his expression.
“Oh, I suppose you think I’m mad,” she said, impatiently, “or ridiculous. Well, see for yourself, judge for yourself. In fact, I have been looking, hungering, for your return. You have helped me through emergencies before now. And I am in that state at present that I trust no one, talk to no one, except of *banalités*. But I should be greatly obliged if *you* would come and listen to me, and, what is more, advise me some day.”
“Most gladly,” said Sir Wilfrid, embarrassed; then, after a pause, “Who is this lady I find installed here?”
Lady Henry hesitated, then shut her strong mouth on the temptation to speak.
“It is not a story for tonight,” she said; “and it would upset me. But, when you first saw her, how did she strike you?”
“I saw at once,” said her companion after a pause, “that you had caught a personality.”
“A personality!” Lady Henry gave an angry laugh. “That’s one way of putting it. But physically﻿—did she remind you of no one?”
Sir Wilfrid pondered a moment.
“Yes. Her face haunted me, when I first saw it. But﻿—no; no, I can’t put any names.”
Lady Henry gave a little snort of disappointment.
“Well, think. You knew her mother quite well. You have known her grandfather all your life. If you’re going on to the Foreign Office, as I suppose you are, you’ll probably see him tonight. She is uncannily like him. As to her father, I don’t know﻿—but he was a rolling-stone of a creature; you very likely came across him.”
“I knew her mother and her father?” said Sir Wilfrid, astonished and pondering.
“They had no right to be her mother and her father,” said Lady Henry, with grimness.
“Ah! So if one does guess﻿—”
“You’ll please hold your tongue.”
“But at present I’m completely mystified,” said Sir Wilfrid.
“Perhaps it’ll come to you later.

===Chinese===

“那些人不是冲着我来的——”她朝大客厅那边摆了摆手，身子发颤。

“亲爱的朋友，这话怎么说？”

“他们是冲着——凑近些，我不愿叫人听见——冲着一个女人来的——一个女人，我一时的鬼迷心窍把她收留进门——如今她正夺走我最老的朋友，正在我自己的家里取我而代之。”

老脸的苍白失尽了它蜡一样的尊严。压低的嗓音在他耳畔嘶嘶作响。威尔弗里德爵士又惊又反感，一时答不上话来。这当口，亨利夫人虽看不见，却仿佛立刻就察觉了他神色的变化。

“哦，我看你觉得我疯了，”她不耐烦地说，“再不就觉得我荒唐。好啊，你自己看吧，自己去判断吧。老实说，我一直在盼着你回来，望眼欲穿。从前多少回急难，都是你帮我渡过来的。如今我到了这般田地，谁也不信，谁也不深谈，谈的净是些淡而无味的场面话。可要是你肯来听我说说——并且改日替我出出主意，那我就感激不尽了。”

“万分乐意，”威尔弗里德爵士有些局促地说；顿了顿，又问：“我在府上发现的这位女士，究竟是什么人？”

亨利夫人迟疑了一下，随即把那张有力的嘴紧紧闭住，忍住了开口的诱惑。

“这不是今晚讲的故事，”她说，“讲了我也要受不住的。可你初见她时，她给你什么印象？”

“我一眼就看出，”她的老友沉吟片刻后说，“您网罗到的是一个人物。”

“人物！”亨利夫人发出一声恼怒的笑。“这也算一种说法。可论相貌——她没叫你想起谁来么？”

威尔弗里德爵士思忖了一会儿。

“想起了。初见她时，她那张脸就缠着我不放。可是——不；不，我说不出什么名字来。”

亨利夫人失望地轻轻哼了一声。

“那你想一想。你同她母亲相当熟。她外祖父你更是认了一辈子。你要是照我想的那样这就上外交部去，今晚多半就能见着他。她像他，像得吓人。至于她父亲，我不清楚——那家伙是个滚来滚去不安生的主儿，你说不定也撞见过。”

“我认识她父亲，又认识她母亲？”威尔弗里德爵士又惊讶又思索地说。

“他们根本不配做她的母亲和父亲，”亨利夫人冷冷地说。

“啊！这么说，要是有人猜着了——”

“那你可得给我把嘴闭紧。”

“可眼下我是完全坠五里雾中了，”威尔弗里德爵士说。

“兴许你过些时候自己就想起来了。”

===Original===

You’ve a good memory generally for such things. Anyway, I can’t tell you anything now. But when’ll you come again? Tomorrow﻿—luncheon? I really want you.”
“Would you be alone?”
“Certainly. *That*, at least, I can still do﻿—lunch as I please, and with whom I please. Who is this coming in? Ah, you needn’t tell me.”
The old lady turned herself towards the entrance, with a stiffening of the whole frame, an instinctive and passionate dignity in her whole aspect, which struck a thrill through her companion.
The little Duchess approached, amid a flutter of satin and lace, heralded by the scent of the Parma violets she wore in profusion at her breast and waist. Her eye glanced uncertainly, and she approached with daintiness, like one stepping on mined ground.
“Aunt Flora, I must have just a minute.”
“I know no reason against your having ten, if you want them,” said Lady Henry, as she held-out three fingers to the newcomer. “You promised yesterday to come and give me a full account of the Devonshire House ball. But it doesn’t matter﻿—and you have forgotten.”
“No, indeed, I haven’t,” said the Duchess, embarrassed. “But you seemed so well employed tonight, with other people. And now﻿—”
“Now you are going on,” said Lady Henry, with a most unfriendly suavity.
“Freddie says I must,” said the other, in the attitude of a protesting child.
“*Alors!*” said Lady Henry, lifting her hand. “We all know how obedient you are. Good night!”
The Duchess flushed. She just touched her aunt’s hand, and then, turning an indignant face on Sir Wilfrid, she bade him farewell with an air which seemed to him intended to avenge upon his neutral person the treatment which, from Lady Henry, even so spoiled a child of fortune as herself could not resent.
Twenty minutes later, Sir Wilfrid entered the first big room of the Foreign Office party. He looked round him with a revival of the exhilaration he had felt on Lady Henry’s staircase, enjoying, after his five years in Teheran, after his long homeward journey by desert and sea, even the common trivialities of the scene﻿—the lights, the gilding, the sparkle of jewels, the scarlet of the uniforms, the noise and movement of the well-dressed crowd.

===Chinese===

你对这种事，向来记性不坏。反正这会儿我什么也不能告诉你。可你几时再来？明天——来吃午饭好不好？我真有话要跟你说。”

“到时就您一个人？”

“当然。这一点，我总还办得到——想几时吃午饭就几时吃，想同谁吃就同谁吃。这是谁进来了？啊，不用你告诉我。”

老太太朝门口转过身去，整个身子僵硬挺直，周身透出一种本能而炽烈的尊严气概，叫她的老友不由得心头一凛。

小小的公爵夫人进来了，绸缎与花边一路窸窣作响，她胸前腰间密密缀着的帕尔马紫罗兰，芬芳先行通报了她的到来。她的目光游移不定，脚步又轻又小心，像走在埋了雷的地面上。

“弗洛拉姑妈，我只讨一分钟。”

“你要是想要十分钟，我也看不出有什么不成，”亨利夫人一面说，一面朝来客伸出三根手指。“你昨天答应过，要来给我细讲德文郡府的舞会。不过不打紧——你已经忘了。”

“不，真的没忘，”公爵夫人窘迫地说。“只是今晚您瞧着叫别人侍候得那样好。再说如今——”

“如今你要走了，”亨利夫人用一种极不友好的和气说。

“弗雷迪说我非走不可，”对方摆出一副抗辩的孩子气。

“*Alors!*”（行了！）亨利夫人抬手道。“我们全都知道你多么听话。晚安！”

公爵夫人涨红了脸。她只轻轻碰了碰姑妈的手指，随即把一张愤慨的脸转向威尔弗里德爵士，向他道别时那副神气，在他看来，分明是要把她从亨利夫人那儿领受的待遇——即便是她这样天之骄女也不好发作——一齐出在他这个不相干的人身上。

二十分钟后，威尔弗里德爵士走进了外交部晚会的头一间大厅。他环顾四周，先前在亨利夫人家楼梯上体味到的那阵兴奋又活了过来。在德黑兰度过五年，又经沙漠与大海长途跋涉归来之后，他连眼前这一幕最寻常的琐屑也津津有味——灯火、鎏金、珠宝的闪烁、军装的猩红，以及衣冠楚楚的人群的喧哗与流动。

===Original===

Then, after this first physical thrill, began the second stage of pleasure﻿—the recognitions and the greetings, after long absence, which show a man where he stands in the great world, which sum up his past and forecast his future. Sir Wilfrid had no reason to complain. Cabinet ministers and great ladies, members of Parliament and the permanent officials who govern but do not rule, soldiers, journalists, barristers﻿—were all glad, it seemed, to grasp him by the hand. He had returned with a record of difficult service brilliantly done, and the English world rewarded him in its accustomed ways.
It was towards one o’clock that he found himself in a crowd pressing towards the staircase in the wake of some departing royalties. A tall man in front turned round to look for some ladies behind him from whom he had been separated in the crush. Sir Wilfrid recognized old Lord Lackington, the veteran of marvellous youth, painter, poet, and sailor, who as a gay naval lieutenant had entertained Byron in the Aegean; whose fame as one of the raciest of naval reformers was in all the newspapers; whose personality was still, at seventy-five, charming to most women and challenging to most men.
As the old man turned, he was still smiling, as though in unison with something which had just been said to him; and his black eyes under his singularly white hair searched the crowd with the animation of a lad of twenty. Through the energy of his aspect the flame of life still burned, as the evening sun through a fine sky. The face had a faulty yet most arresting brilliance. The mouth was disagreeable, the chin common. But the general effect was still magnificent.
Sir Wilfrid started. He recalled the drawing-room in Bruton Street; the form and face of Mademoiselle Le Breton; the sentences by which Lady Henry had tried to put him on the track. His mind ran over past years, and pieced together the recollections of a long-past scandal. “Of course! *Of course!*” he said to himself, not without excitement. “She is not like her mother, but she has all the typical points of her mother’s race.”

===Chinese===

这最初的感官震颤过后，第二重愉悦开始了——久别之后的相认与寒暄。它们向一个人昭示他在大千世界里的地位，总结他的过去，预示他的将来。威尔弗里德爵士没有可抱怨的。内阁大臣与贵妇名媛、议员与那些只司治理而不居其位的常任文官、军人、记者、律师——似乎人人都乐于同他一握。他带着一份艰难使命漂亮完成的资历归来，英国世界便以其惯常的方式犒赏他。

将近一点钟，他发觉自己身处一群人当中，跟在几位离席的王室成员后面，朝楼梯涌去。前面一个高个子男人回过身来，寻找在拥挤中同他失散的几位女伴。威尔弗里德爵士认出了拉金顿勋爵（Lord Lackington）——那位青春堪称奇迹的老前辈，画家、诗人兼水手，当年他以风流倜傥的海军上尉身份，曾在爱琴海（Aegean）款待过拜伦（Byron）；作为海军改革者里最泼辣大胆的一位，他的声名登遍各家报纸；而他的风度，纵然已届七十五岁，对大多数女人依旧魅力不减，对大多数男人依旧咄咄逼人。

老人转过身来时依旧含着笑意，仿佛方才有人说了什么有趣的话；他那罕见雪白的头发底下，一双黑眼睛带着二十岁小伙子的朝气，在人群中逡巡。透过他那副精神的仪表，生命之焰仍在燃烧，恰似落日穿过明净的天空。那张脸虽有瑕疵，却光彩得叫人无法移开眼睛。嘴不讨喜，下颏俗气。可整体气象依旧恢弘不凡。

威尔弗里德爵士蓦地一怔。他回想起布鲁顿街那间客厅；勒布雷东小姐的身形与面庞；亨利夫人那几句想把他引上路径的话。他的心思驰过逝去的年华，把一桩久远丑闻的零星记忆拼凑起来。“是了！*是了！*”他兴奋地自语道。“她不像她母亲，可她母亲那一族系的种种典型特征，全都在她身上。”

## II / II / 第二章
===Original===

It was a cold, clear morning in February, with a little pale sunshine playing on the bare trees of the Park. Sir Wilfrid, walking southward from the Marble Arch to his luncheon with Lady Henry, was gladly conscious of the warmth of his fur-collared coat, though none the less ready to envy careless youth as it crossed his path now and then, great-coatless and ruddy, courting the keen air.
Just as he was about to make his exit towards Mount Street he became aware of two persons walking southward like himself, but on the other side of the roadway. He soon identified Captain Warkworth in the slim, soldierly figure of the man. And the lady? There also, with the help of his glasses, he was soon informed. Her trim, black hat and her black cloth costume seemed to him to have a becoming and fashionable simplicity; and she moved in morning dress, with the same ease and freedom that had distinguished her in Lady Henry’s drawing-room the night before.
He asked himself whether he should interrupt Mademoiselle Le Breton with a view to escorting her to Bruton Street. He understood, indeed, that he and Lady Henry were to be alone at luncheon; Mademoiselle Julie had, no doubt, her own quarters and attendants. But she seemed to be on her way home. An opportunity for some perhaps exploratory conversation with her before he found himself face to face with Lady Henry seemed to him not undesirable.
But he quickly decided to walk on. Mademoiselle Le Breton and Captain Warkworth paused in their walk, about no doubt to say goodbye, but, very clearly, loath to say it. They were, indeed, in earnest conversation. The Captain spoke with eagerness; Mademoiselle Julie, with downcast eyes, smiled and listened.
“Is the fellow making love to her?” thought the old man, in some astonishment, as he turned away. “Hardly the place for it either, one would suppose.”
He vaguely thought that he would both sound and warn Lady Henry. Warn her of what? He happened on the way home to have been thrown with a couple of Indian officers whose personal opinion of Harry Warkworth was not a very high one, in spite of the brilliant distinction which the young man had earned for himself in the Afridi campaign just closed. But how was he to hand that sort of thing on to Lady Henry?﻿—and because he happened to have seen her lady companion and Harry Warkworth together?

===Chinese===

那是二月里一个清冷明澈的早晨，淡淡的阳光在公园光秃的枝桠间嬉戏。威尔弗里德爵士从大理石拱门（Marble Arch）向南步行，去亨利夫人府上共进午餐，毛皮领大衣的暖意令他心生快慰，然而每逢不穿大衣、面色红润的轻狂少年与他擦肩而过、贪享那凛冽空气时，他依旧免不了几分艳羡。
他正要从芒特街（Mount Street）方向的出口离开公园，忽然察觉马路对面有两个人也同他一样正往南走。那男子清瘦挺拔的军人身形，他很快认出是沃克沃思上尉（Captain Warkworth）。那么那位女士呢？借助眼镜，这一点他不久也弄清了。她那顶利落的黑色小帽和黑色呢料衣裙，在他看来有一种得体而入时的简洁；她一身晨间装束走动着，步态间那种自在从容，与昨夜她在亨利夫人客厅里的风采如出一辙。
他暗自盘算，要不要上前同勒布雷东小姐搭话，好陪她走回布鲁顿街去。他确实得知，午餐桌上只有他和亨利夫人两人；朱莉小姐想必自有住处和仆从。可她此刻看来正在回家的路上。趁尚未与亨利夫人当面相对，先同她做上一番或许可称试探的交谈，这样的机会在他看来并非无益。
但他很快打定主意继续赶路。勒布雷东小姐和沃克沃思上尉停下了脚步，多半是要道别了，却又分明难舍难分。他们确乎正谈得起劲。上尉说得热烈，朱莉小姐垂着眼帘，含笑倾听。
“这家伙是在向她献殷勤吗？”老人转过身去，颇感惊异地想道。“照理说，这也不是干这个的地方呀。”
他模模糊糊地打算，既要向亨利夫人探探口风，也要提醒她提防。提防什么呢？回国途中他恰与几名印度军官同行共处；虽说这年轻人在刚刚落幕的阿弗里迪战役（Afridi campaign）中为自己挣得了赫赫功名，那几位军官对哈里·沃克沃思（Harry Warkworth）其人的评价却不算高。可这种话，他怎么好原样递给亨利夫人？——就因为他碰巧撞见她的女伴同哈里·沃克沃思走在一起？

===Original===

No doubt Mademoiselle Julie was on her employer’s business.
Yet the little encounter added somehow to his already lively curiosity on the subject of Lady Henry’s companion. Thanks to a remarkable physical resemblance, he was practically certain that he had guessed the secret of Mademoiselle Le Breton’s parentage. At any rate, on the supposition that he had, his thoughts began to occupy themselves with the story to which his guess pointed.
Some thirty years before, he had known, both in London and in Italy, a certain Colonel Delaney and his wife, once Lady Rose Chantrey, the favorite daughter of Lord Lackington. They were not a happy couple. She was a woman of great intelligence, but endowed with one of those natures﻿—sensitive, plastic, eager to search out and to challenge life﻿—which bring their possessors some great joys, hardly to be balanced against a final sum of pain. Her husband, absorbed in his military life, silent, narrowly able, and governed by a strict Anglicanism that seemed to carry with it innumerable “shalts” and “shalt nots,” disagreeable to the natural man or woman, soon found her a tiring and trying companion. She asked him for what he could not give; she coquetted with questions he thought it impious to raise; the persons she made friends with were distasteful to him; and, without complaining, he soon grew to think it intolerable that a woman married to a soldier should care so little for his professional interests and ambitions. Though when she pretended to care for them she annoyed him, if possible, still more.
As for Lady Rose, she went through all the familiar emotions of the *femme incomprise*. And with the familiar result. There presently appeared in the house a man of good family, thirty-five or so, traveller, painter, and dreamer, with fine, long-drawn features bronzed by the sun of the East, and bringing with him the reputation of having plotted and fought for most of the “lost causes” of our generation, including several which had led him into conflict with British authorities and British officials. To Colonel Delaney he was an “agitator,” if not a rebel; and the careless pungency of his talk soon classed him as an atheist besides.

===Chinese===

毫无疑问，朱莉小姐是替主人办事去了。
然而这场小小的邂逅，多少加深了他对亨利夫人女伴这一话题本已颇为活跃的好奇。多亏那惊人的容貌相似，他几乎可以肯定，自己已猜中了勒布雷东小姐身世的秘密。无论如何，姑且假定他猜中了，他的思绪便开始盘桓于这一猜测所指向的那段往事。
大约三十年前，他曾在伦敦和意大利认识一位德拉尼上校（Colonel Delaney）及其夫人——后者便是当年的罗斯·钱特里小姐（Lady Rose Chantrey），拉金顿勋爵最宠爱的女儿。这对夫妇过得并不幸福。她是个极聪明的女人，却天生带着那样一种性情——敏感、可塑、渴望探寻并挑战生活——这种性情会给人带来几许巨大的欢愉，却抵不过最终结算下来的痛苦。她丈夫沉浸于军旅生活，沉默寡言，才能有限，又受着一种严苛的英国国教信仰支配——那信仰似乎随身携带着数不清的“当行”与“不可行”，叫天生的男男女女都觉得难受——他很快便觉得她是个既让人疲惫又让人头疼的伴侣。她向他索要他给不出的东西；她卖弄风情似的调笑那些他认为大不敬的问题；她结交的朋友他都看不顺眼；于是他虽不发怨言，却很快认定：一个嫁给军人的女子，竟对他职业上的志趣抱负如此漠不关心，实在叫人无法容忍。尽管当她假装关心那些事的时候，若说有什么分别，反倒更惹他恼火。
至于罗斯小姐，她把一个 femme incomprise*（自感不为人理解的女子）所惯有的种种情绪都经历了一遍。结果也是惯常的结果。不久，那宅子里出现了一个出身良好的男子，三十五岁上下，旅行家、画家兼梦想家，清瘦的长脸上带着东方烈日晒出的古铜色，随身携来的名声是为我们这一代大多数“失败的事业”密谋过、战斗过，其中几桩还使他同英国当局和英国官员发生过冲突。在德拉尼上校眼里，他即便算不上叛党，也是个“煽动家”；而他谈话中那种随随便便的尖刻，很快又让他多得一顶无神论者的帽子。

===Original===

In the case of Lady Rose, this man’s free and generous nature, his independence of money and convention, his passion for the things of the mind, his contempt for the mode, whether in dress or politics, his light evasions of the red tape of life as of something that no one could reasonably expect of a vagabond like himself﻿—these things presently transformed a woman in despair to a woman in revolt. She fell in love with an intensity befitting her true temperament, and with a stubbornness that bore witness to the dreary failure of her marriage. Marriott Dalrymple returned her love, and nothing in his view of life predisposed him to put what probably appeared to him a mere legality before the happiness of two people meant for each other. There were no children of the Delaney marriage; and in his belief the husband had enjoyed too long a companionship he had never truly deserved.
So Lady Rose faced her husband, told him the truth, and left him. She and Dalrymple went to live in Belgium, in a small country-house some twenty or thirty miles from Brussels. They severed themselves from England; they asked nothing more of English life. Lady Rose suffered from the breach with her father, for Lord Lackington never saw her again. And there was a young sister whom she had brought up, whose image could often rouse in her a sense of loss that showed itself in occasional spells of silence and tears. But substantially she never repented what she had done, although Colonel Delaney made the penalties of it as heavy as he could. Like Karennine in Tolstoy’s great novel, he refused to sue for a divorce, and for something of the same reasons. Divorce was in itself impious, and sin should not be made easy. He was at any time ready to take back his wife, so far as the protection of his name and roof were concerned, should she penitently return to him.
So the child that was presently born to Lady Rose could not be legitimized.
Sir Wilfrid stopped short at the Park end of Bruton Street, with a start of memory.
“I saw it once! I remember now﻿—perfectly.”

===Chinese===

就罗斯小姐而言，这个男人自由豁达的天性，他对金钱与世俗礼法的不以为意，他对精神事物的热忱，他对时髦套路——无论衣着还是政治——的轻蔑，以及他对待生活中繁文缛节那种轻巧的规避，仿佛那本是一个像他这样的流浪者谁也无权苛求的东西——这一切，很快便把一个绝望的女人变成了一个反叛的女人。她以契合她真实天性的炽烈、以见证其婚姻何其可悲的固执，堕入了爱河。马里奥特·达尔林普尔（Marriott Dalrymple）回应了她的爱，而他的人生观里没有任何东西会让他把一件在他看来不过是法律形式的事，置于两个天生相配之人的幸福之上。德拉尼夫妇没有子女；而且在他看来，这位丈夫享有一种他从不配享的陪伴，实在是享得太久了。
于是罗斯小姐直面丈夫，向他吐露实情，然后离开了他。她和达尔林普尔去比利时定居，住在离布鲁塞尔（Brussels）二三十英里的一座乡间小宅里。他们与英国一刀两断；不再向英国生活索取什么。同父亲决裂令罗斯小姐痛苦，因为拉金顿勋爵再没有见过她。她还有个亲手带大的妹妹，那妹妹的身影常会唤起她一种失落之感，间或化作一阵阵沉默与流泪。但大体而言，她从未后悔自己的所作所为，尽管德拉尼上校已尽力让这件事的代价变得无比沉重。正如托尔斯泰那部伟大小说中的卡列宁（Karennine）一样，他拒绝提出离婚，理由也大致相同。离婚本身即属不敬，而罪孽不该被轻纵。只要她肯悔悟归来，无论何时，他都愿意重新接纳她——就庇护其名分与屋檐而言。
于是，罗斯小姐不久后生下的那个孩子，便无法取得合法名分。
走到布鲁顿街临公园的一头，威尔弗里德爵士猛地站住，记忆倏然惊醒。
“我见过那场景一次！我现在想起来了——清清楚楚。”

===Original===

And he went on to recall a bygone moment in the Brussels Gallery, when, as he was standing before the great Quintin Matsys, he was accosted with sudden careless familiarity by a thin, shabbily dressed man, in whose dark distinction, made still more fantastic and conspicuous by the fever and the emaciation of consumption, he recognized at once Marriott Dalrymple.
He remembered certain fragments of their talk about the pictures﻿—the easy mastery, now brusque, now poetic, with which Dalrymple had shown him the treasures of the gallery, in the manner of one whose learning was merely the food of fancy, the stuff on which imagination and reverie grew rich.
Then, suddenly, his own question﻿—“And Lady Rose?”
And Dalrymple’s quiet, “Very well. She’d see you, I think, if you want to come. She has scarcely seen an English person in the last three years.”
And as when a gleam searches out some blurred corner of a landscape, there returned upon him his visit to the pair in their country home. He recalled the small eighteenth-century house, the “château” of the village, built on the French model, with its high *mansarde* roof; the shabby stateliness of its architecture matching plaintively with the field of beetroot that grew up to its very walls; around it the flat, rich fields, with their thin lines of poplars; the slow, canalized streams; the unlovely farms and cottages; the mire of the lanes; and, shrouding all, a hot autumn mist sweeping slowly through the damp meadows and blotting all cheerfulness from the sun. And in the midst of this pale landscape, so full of ragged edges to an English eye, the English couple, with their books, their child, and a pair of Flemish servants.
It had been evident to him at once that their circumstances were those of poverty. Lady Rose’s small fortune, indeed, had been already mostly spent on “causes” of many kinds, in many countries. She and Dalrymple were almost vegetarians, and wine never entered the house save for the servants, who seemed to regard their employers with a real but half-contemptuous affection. He remembered the scanty, ill-cooked luncheon; the difficulty in providing a few extra knives and forks; the wrangling with the old bonne-housekeeper, which was necessary before serviettes could be produced.

===Chinese===

他继续回想布鲁塞尔美术馆里久远的一幕：当时他正站在那幅昆廷·马赛斯（Quintin Matsys）的名画前，一个瘦削、衣衫寒酸的男人带着突兀而漫不经心的熟稔向他打招呼；那张脸黝黑出众，又因痨病的热症与消瘦而更添奇崛醒目，他一眼便认出是马里奥特·达尔林普尔。
他记得他们谈论绘画的只言片语——达尔林普尔领他看馆中珍藏时那种挥洒自如的气度，时而冷峻，时而诗意，仿佛一个人的学问不过是想象的食粮，是滋养遐思与梦想、使之丰饶起来的材料。
然后，突然，是他自己的问话——“罗斯小姐呢？”
以及达尔林普尔平静的回答：“很好。你要是想来，我想她会见你的。这三年里她几乎没见过一个英国人。”
恰似一道光线照亮风景中某个模糊的角落，对那对夫妇乡居的造访又回到他心头。他记起那座十八世纪的小宅，村中的“庄堡”（château），法式格局，高高的复折屋顶；建筑那破败的堂皇，与一直长到墙根的甜菜地哀怨地相衬；四周是平坦肥沃的田野，点缀着一行行稀疏的杨树；缓缓的、经运河疏导的溪流；面貌寒碜的农庄与村舍；泥泞的小巷；还有笼罩一切的秋天湿热雾霭，缓缓扫过潮湿的草场，把太阳仅有的一点明朗也抹去了。而在这片在英国人看来处处参差破落的苍白风景中央，是一对英国夫妇，带着他们的书、他们的孩子，和两个佛兰德仆人。
他当即看出，他们的境况属于贫穷。确实，罗斯小姐那点小小的财产，早已大多花在许多国家的各种“事业”上了。她和达尔林普尔几乎食素，家里除了仆人的份例从不进酒——那两个仆人对主人似乎怀着一种真切却带着几分轻蔑的感情。他记得那顿菲薄、烹煮失当的午餐；添几副刀叉都要费一番周折；还得同那位兼任女管家的老妈妈（bonne）吵嚷一场，餐巾才肯拿出来。

===Original===

And afterwards the library, with its deal shelves from floor to ceiling put up by Dalrymple himself, its bare, polished floor, Dalrymple’s table and chair on one side of the open hearth, Lady Rose’s on the other; on his table the sheets of verse translation from Aeschylus and Euripides, which represented his favorite hobby; on hers the socialist and economical books they both studied and the English or French poets they both loved. The walls, hung with the faded damask of a past generation, were decorated with a strange crop of pictures pinned carelessly into the silk﻿—photographs or newspaper portraits of modern men and women representing all possible revolt against authority, political, religious, even scientific, the Everlasting No of an untiring and ubiquitous dissent.
Finally, in the centre of the polished floor, the strange child, whom Lady Rose had gone to fetch after lunch, with its high crest of black hair, its large, jealous eyes, its elfin hands, and the sudden smile with which, after half an hour of silence and apparent scorn, it had rewarded Sir Wilfrid’s advances. He saw himself sitting bewitched beside it.
Poor Lady Rose! He remembered her as he and she parted at the gate of the neglected garden, the anguish in her eyes as they turned to look after the bent and shrunken figure of Dalrymple carrying the child back to the house.
“If you meet any of his old friends, don’t﻿—don’t say anything! We’ve just saved enough money to go to Sicily for the winter﻿—that’ll set him right.”
And then, barely a year later, the line in a London newspaper which had reached him at Madrid, chronicling the death of Marriott Dalrymple, as of a man once on the threshold of fame, but long since exiled from the thoughts of practical men. Lady Rose, too, was dead﻿—many years since; so much he knew. But how, and where? And the child?
She was now “Mademoiselle Le Breton”?﻿—the centre and apparently the chief attraction of Lady Henry’s once famous salon?
“And, by Jove! several of her kinsfolk there, relations of the mother or the father, if what I suppose is true!” thought Sir Wilfrid, remembering one or two of the guests. “Were they﻿—was she﻿—aware of it?”
---
The old man strode on, full of a growing eagerness, and was soon on Lady Henry’s doorstep.
“Her ladyship is in the dining-room,” said the butler, and Sir Wilfrid was ushered there straight.

===Chinese===

然后是那间书房：松木书架从地板直抵天花板，是达尔林普尔亲手钉的；裸露的、擦得光亮的地板；敞开的壁炉一侧摆着达尔林普尔的书桌和椅子，另一侧是罗斯小姐的；他桌上摊着一叠叠从埃斯库罗斯（Aeschylus）和欧里庇得斯（Euripides）译出的诗稿，那是他最心爱的消遣；她桌上则是两人共同研读的社会主义与经济学书籍，以及两人共同挚爱的英法诗人。四壁挂着上一代褪色的锦缎，缎面上潦草地别着一茬奇特的“作物”——照片和报纸上剪下的肖像，全是当今代表着一切可能反抗权威——政治的、宗教的、甚至科学的——的男男女女，是那不知疲倦、无处不在的异见所发出的永恒之“不”（Everlasting No）。
最后，在光可鉴人的地板中央，是那个古怪的孩子——午饭后罗斯小姐去抱来的——头顶一撮高耸的黑发，一双大大的、带着妒意的眼睛，一双精灵似的小手；在半个小时的沉默和明显的轻蔑之后，她忽然绽出一个笑容，算是犒赏威尔弗里德爵士的殷勤。他看见自己坐在她身边，着了魔似的。
可怜的罗斯小姐！他记得两人在那荒疏园子的门口分别时她的模样，她的目光追随着达尔林普尔抱着孩子回屋去的伛偻瘦削的身影，眼中盛满痛苦。
“你要是碰见他的哪些老朋友，千万别——千万别说什么！我们刚攒够了钱去西西里过冬——那会让他好起来的。”
再然后，不过一年之后，他在马德里读到伦敦报纸上的一行讣闻，记下马里奥特·达尔林普尔之死，说此人曾一度站在名声的门槛上，却早已被务实之人的心思放逐遗忘。罗斯小姐也死了——已有多年；这他是知道的。可怎么死的？死在哪里？还有那孩子呢？
她如今就是那位“勒布雷东小姐”？——亨利夫人那曾经名噪一时的沙龙的中心，而且显然是其最主要的魅力所在？
“天哪！若我所料不差，在场就有好几位她的亲族，不是母系的便是父系的！”威尔弗里德爵士想着，记起一两位宾客的面孔。“他们知道吗——她知道吗？”
---
老人大步向前，心头热望越来越浓，很快便到了亨利夫人的门前台阶。
“夫人在饭厅里，”管家说，威尔弗里德爵士便被径直引了进去。

===Original===

“Good morning, Wilfrid,” said the old lady, raising herself on her silver﻿—headed sticks as he entered. “I prefer to come downstairs by myself. The more infirm I am, the less I like it﻿—and to be helped enrages me. Sit down. Lunch is ready, and I give you leave to eat some.”
“And you?” said Sir Wilfrid, as they seated themselves almost side by side at the large, round table in the large, dingy room.
The old lady shook her head.
“All the world eats too much. I was brought up with people who lunched on a biscuit and a glass of sherry.”
“Lord Russell?﻿—Lord Palmerston?” suggested Sir Wilfrid, attacking his own lunch meanwhile with unabashed vigor.
“That sort. I wish we had their like now.”
“Their successors don’t please you?”
Lady Henry shook her head.
“The Tories have gone to the deuce, and there are no longer enough Whigs even to do that. I wouldn’t read the newspapers at all if I could help it. But I do.”
“So I understand,” said Sir Wilfrid; “you let Montresor know it last night.”
“Montresor!” said Lady Henry, with a contemptuous movement. “What a poseur! He lets the army go to ruin, I understand, while he joins Dante societies.”
Sir Wilfrid raised his eyebrows.
“I think, if I were you, I should have some lunch,” he said, gently pushing the admirable salmi which the butler had left in front of him towards his old friend.
Lady Henry laughed.
“Oh, my temper will be better presently, when those men are gone”﻿—she nodded towards the butler and footman in the distance﻿—“and I can have my say.”
Sir Wilfrid hurried his meal as much as Lady Henry﻿—who, as it turned out, was not at all minded to starve him﻿—would allow. She meanwhile talked politics and gossip to him, with her old, caustic force, nibbling a dry biscuit at intervals and sipping a cup of coffee. She was a wilful, characteristic figure as she sat there, beneath her own portrait as a bride, which hung on the wall behind her. The portrait represented a very young woman, with plentiful brown hair gathered into a knot on the top of her head, a high waist, a blue waist-ribbon, and inflated sleeves. Handsome, imperious, the corners of the mouth well down, the look straight and daring﻿—the Lady Henry of the picture, a bride of nineteen, was already formidable.

===Chinese===

“早上好，威尔弗里德，”老太太说着，在他进门时拄着银头手杖撑起身来。“我宁可自己下楼。我越是衰弱，越不愿这样——让人搀扶简直叫我冒火。坐吧。午饭好了，我允许你吃一些。”
“那您呢？”威尔弗里德爵士说，两人在那间宽大昏暗的房间里，几乎并肩坐到大圆桌旁。
老太太摇了摇头。
“世人都吃得太多。我从小跟着的那些人，午餐不过一块饼干、一杯雪利酒。”
“罗素勋爵？——帕默斯顿勋爵？”威尔弗里德爵士提示道，一边毫不客气地对自己那份午餐发起猛攻。
“就是那路人。真希望如今还有那样的同道。”
“他们的后继者不合您的意？”
亨利夫人摇了摇头。
“托利党已经完蛋了，如今连够格完蛋的辉格党都凑不齐了。要是能不读报纸，我一张也不会读。可我还是读。”
“我听说了，”威尔弗里德爵士说，“昨晚您就让蒙特勒索（Montresor）领教过了。”
“蒙特勒索！”亨利夫人说着，做了个鄙夷的动作。“好一个装腔作势的家伙！据我所知，他一边让陆军烂下去，一边去参加什么但丁学会。”
威尔弗里德爵士扬了扬眉毛。
“我看，我要是您，就吃点儿午饭，”他说着，把管家留在他面前的那盆上好的炖什禽轻轻推向老朋友。
亨利夫人笑了。
“哦，等那些人下去，我的脾气就会好些”——她朝远处的管家和男仆点了点头——“等我可以说个痛快的时候。”
威尔弗里德爵士匆匆用完这顿饭——亨利夫人倒也压根没打算饿着他——能多快便多快。她则一边不时咬一口干饼干、呷一口咖啡，一边以她昔日那种刻薄的劲头同他谈政治、讲闲话。她坐在那里，身后墙上挂着她做新娘时的肖像，整个人是一副任情任性、个性鲜明的模样。画中是一位非常年轻的女子，一头浓密的棕发绾成发髻顶在头顶，高腰的身段，蓝色的束腰带，鼓胀的衣袖。美貌，倨傲，嘴角两端沉沉下压，目光直率而大胆——画中的亨利夫人，十九岁的新娘，已经咄咄逼人。

===Original===

And the old woman sitting beneath it, with the strong, white hair, which the ample cap found some difficulty even now in taming and confining, the droop of the mouth accentuated, the nose more masterful, the double chin grown evident, the light of the eyes gone out, breathed pride and will from every feature of her still handsome face, pride of race and pride of intellect, combined with a hundred other subtler and smaller prides that only an intimate knowledge of her could detect. The brow and eyes, so beautiful in the picture, were, however, still agreeable in the living woman; if generosity lingered anywhere, it was in them.
The door was hardly closed upon the servants when she bent forward.
“Well, have you guessed?”
Sir Wilfrid looked at her thoughtfully as he stirred the sugar in his coffee.
“I think so,” he said. “She is Lady Rose Delaney’s daughter.”
Lady Henry gave a sudden laugh.
“I hardly expected you to guess! What helped you?”
“First your own hints. Then the strange feeling I had that I had seen the face, or some face just like it, before. And, lastly, at the Foreign Office I caught sight, for a moment, of Lord Lackington. That finished it.”
“Ah!” said Lady Henry, with a nod. “Yes, that likeness is extraordinary. Isn’t it amazing that that foolish old man has never perceived it?”
“He knows nothing?”
“Oh, nothing! Nobody does. However, that’ll do presently. But Lord Lackington comes here, mumbles about his music and his watercolors, and his flirtations﻿—seventy-four, if you please, last birthday!﻿—talks about himself endlessly to Julie or to me﻿—whoever comes handy﻿—and never has an inkling, an idea.”
“And she?”
“Oh, *she* knows. I should rather think she does.” And Lady Henry pushed away her coffee-cup with the ill-suppressed vehemence which any mention of her companion seemed to produce in her. “Well, now, I suppose you’d like to hear the story.”
“Wait a minute. It’ll surprise you to hear that I not only knew this lady’s mother and father, but that I’ve seen her, herself, before.”
“You?” Lady Henry looked incredulous.
“I never told you of my visit to that ménage, four-and-twenty years ago?”
“Never, that I remember. But if you had I should have forgotten. What did they matter to me then? I myself only saw Lady Rose once, so far as I remember, before she misconducted herself.

===Chinese===

而坐在画像之下的这位老妇人，一头浓硬的白发，那顶宽大的帽子至今仍难以将其驯服收拢；嘴角的下垂愈发分明，鼻梁愈发专横，双下巴已然显形，眼中的光彩熄灭了——但她那张依旧俊美的脸上，每一处线条都透着傲气与意志，门第的骄傲与才智的骄傲，还夹杂着上百种唯有深知她的人才辨得出的、更细微更琐碎的骄傲。不过，画中那样美的额头和眼睛，在活着的她脸上依然可亲；若说宽厚之气还残存何处，便是在这眉眼之间了。
仆人退下、门刚关上，她便俯身向前。
“怎么样，猜到了吗？”
威尔弗里德爵士若有所思地望着她，一面搅动着咖啡里的糖。
“我想是猜到了，”他说。“她是罗斯·德拉尼小姐的女儿。”
亨利夫人突然笑出声来。
“我压根没指望你能猜着！是什么提醒了你？”
“首先是您自己的暗示。其次是我有种奇怪的感觉，觉得见过这张脸，或是一张一模一样的脸。最后，我在外交部（Foreign Office）瞥见了拉金顿勋爵一眼。这一下就全明白了。”
“啊！”亨利夫人点头道。“是啊，那相似真叫惊人。那个老糊涂竟从未察觉，你说怪不怪？”
“他一无所知？”
“哦，一无所知！谁都不知道。不过，这事待会儿再说。拉金顿勋爵倒是常来这里，絮絮叨叨讲他的音乐、他的水彩画，还有他的风流韵事——劳驾，上回过生日可满七十四了！——对朱莉或对我——谁在跟前对谁——没完没了地大谈他自己，却从没有半分觉察、半分念头。”
“那她呢？”
“哦，她知道。我该说，她太知道了。”亨利夫人推开咖啡杯，那股压不住的火气——但凡提到她的女伴便会冒上来——又涌了上来。“好吧，现在，我想你愿意听听这段来历了。”
“且慢。我说出来你怕要吃惊：我不但认识这位夫人的父母，还见过她本人。”
“你？”亨利夫人一脸不信。
“我从没跟你讲过二十四年前我造访那户人家（ménage）的往事？”
“我记得是从来没有。就算你讲过，我也早忘了。他们那时与我何干？据我所忆，罗斯小姐失德之前，我自己统共只见过她一面。

===Original===

And afterwards﻿—well, one doesn’t trouble one’s self about the women that have gone under.”
Something lightened behind Sir Wilfrid’s straw-colored lashes. He bent over his coffee-cup and daintily knocked off the end of his cigarette with a beringed little finger.
“The women who have﻿—not been able to pull up?”
Lady Henry paused.
“If you like to put it so,” she said, at last. Sir Wilfrid did not raise his eyes. Lady Henry took up her strongest glasses from the table and put them on. But it was pitifully evident that even so equipped she saw but little, and that her strong nature fretted perpetually against the physical infirmity that teased it. Nevertheless, some unspoken communication passed between them, and Sir Wilfrid knew that he had effectually held up a protecting hand for Lady Rose.
“Well, let me tell you my tale first,” he said; and gave the little reminiscence in full. When he described the child, Lady Henry listened eagerly.
“Hm,” she said, when he came to an end; “she was jealous, you say, of her mother’s attentions to you? She watched you, and in the end she took possession of you? Much the same creature, apparently, then as now.”
“No moral, please, till the tale is done,” said Sir Wilfrid, smiling. “It’s your turn.”
Lady Henry’s face grew sombre.
“All very well,” she said. “What did your tale matter to you? As for mine﻿—”
The substance of hers was as follows, put into chronological order:
Lady Rose had lived some ten years after Dalrymple’s death. That time she passed in great poverty in some *chambres garnies* at Bruges, with her little girl and an old Madame Le Breton, the maid, housekeeper, and general factotum who had served them in the country. This woman, though of a peevish, grumbling temper, was faithful, affectionate, and not without education. She was certainly attached to little Julie, whose nurse she had been during a short period of her infancy. It was natural that Lady Rose should leave the child to her care. Indeed, she had no choice. An old Ursuline nun, and a kind priest who at the nun’s instigation occasionally came to see her, in the hopes of converting her, were her only other friends in the world. She wrote, however, to her father, shortly before her death, bidding him goodbye, and asking him to do something for the child. “She is wonderfully like you,” so ran part of the letter. “You won’t ever acknowledge her, I know.

===Chinese===

至于之后嘛——唉，对那些沦落了的女人，谁也不会多费心思。”
威尔弗里德爵士淡黄的睫毛后面，某种神色变得柔和了些。他俯身对着咖啡杯，用戴着戒指的小指优雅地弹了弹烟头。
“那些没能——及时回头的女人？”
亨利夫人顿了一顿。
“你愿意这么讲也行，”她终于说。威尔弗里德爵士没有抬眼。亨利夫人从桌上拿起度数最深的眼镜戴上。可即便如此装备，她所见依然寥寥，这一点显而易见得令人心酸；她那刚强的天性无时无刻不在与折磨它的身体残缺赌气较劲。尽管如此，两人之间完成了某种无言的交流，威尔弗里德爵士知道，自己已经切实地为罗斯小姐举起了一只保护之手。
“好吧，让我先讲我的故事，”他说；接着把那段小小的往事原原本本讲了一遍。讲到那孩子时，亨利夫人听得入神。
“唔，”他讲完时她说，“你是说，她妒忌她母亲对你的殷勤？她盯着你，最后把你整个占了去？看样子，当年和如今，倒还是同一副脾性。”
“故事没讲完，请先别下断语，”威尔弗里德爵士含笑说。“该你了。”
亨利夫人的脸色阴沉下来。
“说得倒是轻巧，”她说。“你的故事与你有何干系？至于我的——”
她的故事，按时间先后整理，大要如下：
达尔林普尔死后，罗斯小姐又活了十年上下。那段岁月，她带着小女儿，同一个老勒布雷东太太（Madame Le Breton）一起，在布鲁日（Bruges）几间带家具的出租屋（chambres garnies）里极度清贫地度过；这女人当年在乡间便是他们的女仆、管家兼万事总管。她虽然脾气急躁、爱发牢骚，却忠心、重情，也并非全无教养。她固然疼爱小朱莉——朱莉襁褓中的短短一段时日，她做过她的乳母。罗斯小姐把孩子托付给她，是顺理成章的事。况且，她也别无选择。除她之外，罗斯小姐在这世上仅有的朋友，不过是一位老乌尔苏拉会修女，和一位经修女怂恿、抱着感化她的指望偶尔前来看她的好心神父。然而在去世前不久，她给父亲写了信，向他诀别，求他为孩子尽一份力。“她像你像得出奇，”信里有一段这样写道，“我知道，你永远不肯认她。

===Original===

That is your strange code. But at least give her what will keep her from want, till she can earn her living. Her old nurse will take care of her, I have taught her, so far. She is already very clever. When I am gone she will attend one of the convent schools here. And I have found an honest lawyer who will receive and pay out money.”
To this letter Lord Lackington replied, promising to come over and see his daughter. But an attack of gout delayed him, and, before he was out of his room, Lady Rose was dead. Then he no longer talked of coming over, and his solicitors arranged matters. An allowance of a hundred pounds a year was made to Madame Le Breton, through the “honest lawyer” whom Lady Rose had found, for the benefit of “Julie Dalrymple,” the capital value to be handed over to that young lady herself on the attainment of her eighteenth birthday﻿—always provided that neither she nor anybody on her behalf made any further claim on the Lackington family, that her relationship to them was dropped, and her mother’s history buried in oblivion.
Accordingly the girl grew to maturity in Bruges. By the lawyer’s advice, after her mother’s death, she took the name of her old *gouvernante*, and was known thenceforward as Julie Le Breton. The Ursuline nuns, to whose school she was sent, took the precaution, after her mother’s death, of having her baptized straightway into the Catholic faith, and she made her *première communion* in their church. In the course of a few years she became a remarkable girl, the source of many anxieties to the nuns. For she was not only too clever for their teaching, and an inborn sceptic, but wherever she appeared she produced parties and the passions of parties. And though, as she grew older, she showed much adroitness in managing those who were hostile to her, she was never without enemies, and intrigues followed her.
“I might have been warned in time,” said Lady Henry, in whose wrinkled cheeks a sharp and feverish color had sprung up as her story approached the moment of her own personal acquaintance with Mademoiselle Le Breton. “For one or two of the nuns when I saw them in Bruges, before the bargain was finally struck, were candid enough. However, now I come to the moment when I first set eyes on her. You know my little place in Surrey?

===Chinese===

那是你那套古怪的规矩。可至少给她一份能免于匮乏的钱，直到她能自食其力。她的老乳母会照看她，至今都是我教的。她已经非常聪明。我死之后，她会进这里的一所修道院学校。我还找了一位诚实的律师，替我收款付款。”
对这封信，拉金顿勋爵回了信，答应渡海来看女儿。可一场痛风耽搁了他，还没等他出得了房门，罗斯小姐已经死了。于是他不再提过海的事，一应由他的律师们打点。经罗斯小姐找到的那位“诚实律师”之手，每年拨给勒布雷东太太一百英镑津贴，用于抚养“朱莉·达尔林普尔”；本金待那位小姐年满十八岁时交付她本人——前提是：她本人或任何代她出面的人都不得再向拉金顿家提出任何进一步的要求，她与该家的亲属关系一笔勾销，她母亲的那段往事就此埋葬，湮没无闻。
于是这姑娘在布鲁日长大成人。依律师之见，母亲死后，她改用了老家庭教师（gouvernante）的姓氏，从此便叫朱莉·勒布雷东。她被送进乌尔苏拉会的学校；修女们出于谨慎，在她母亲死后立刻让她受洗入了天主教，并在她们的教堂里行了初领圣体（première communion）。几年之间，她出落成一个出众的姑娘，也成了修女们诸多忧心的根源。因为她不仅聪明得超出她们所能教诲，骨子里又是个怀疑一切的人，而且无论走到哪里，都会掀起派别和派别之争。虽说随着年龄渐长，她应付敌视她的人颇为圆熟老到，却从来不缺仇家，明争暗斗总是缠着她。
“我本该及时得到警告的，”亨利夫人说；故事临近她本人结识勒布雷东小姐的那一刻，她两颊的皱纹间涌起一阵燥热的红晕。“因为在布鲁日见到她时——那笔交易尚未最后敲定——有一两个修女倒是坦率得很。不过，现在讲到我头一回见到她的时刻了。你知道我在萨里（Surrey）的那处小地方吧？”

===Original===

About a mile from me is a manor-house belonging to an old Catholic family, terribly devout and as poor as church-mice. They sent their daughters to school in Bruges. One summer holiday these girls brought home with them Julie Dalrymple as their quasi-holiday governess. It was three years ago. I had just seen Liebreich. He told me that I should soon be blind, and, naturally, it was a blow to me.”
Sir Wilfrid made a murmur of sympathy.
“Oh, don’t pity me! I don’t pity other people. This odious body of ours has got to wear out sometime﻿—it’s in the bargain. Still, just then I was low. There are two things I care about﻿—one is talk, with the people that amuse me, and the other is the reading of French books. I didn’t see how I was going to keep my circle here together, and my own mind in decent repair, unless I could find somebody to be eyes for me, and to read to me. And as I’m a bundle of nerves, and I never was agreeable to illiterate people, nor they to me, I was rather put to it. Well, one day these girls and their mother came over to tea, and, as you guess, of course, they brought Mademoiselle Le Breton with them. I had asked them to come, but when they arrived I was bored and cross, and like a sick dog in a hole. And then, as you have seen her, I suppose you can guess what happened.”
“You discovered an exceptional person?”
Lady Henry laughed.
“I was limed, there and then, old bird as I am. I was first struck with the girl’s appearance﻿—*une belle laide*﻿—with every movement just as it ought to be; infinitely more attractive to me than any pink-and-white beauty. It turned out that she had just been for a month in Paris with another schoolfellow. Something she said about a new play﻿—suddenly﻿—made me look at her. ‘*Venez vous asseoir ici, mademoiselle, s’il vous plaît﻿—près de moi*,’ I said to her﻿—I can hear my own voice now, poor fool, and see her flush up. Ah!” Lady Henry’s interjection dropped to a note of rage that almost upset Sir Wilfrid’s gravity; but he restrained himself, and she resumed: “We talked for two hours; it seemed to me ten minutes. I sent the others out to the gardens. She stayed with me. The new French books, the theatre, poems, plays, novels, memoirs, even politics, she could talk of them all; or, rather﻿—for, mark you, that’s her gift﻿—she made *me* talk. It seemed to me I had not been so brilliant for months.

===Chinese===

离我一英里左右有座庄园宅邸，属于一个古老的天主教人家，虔敬得吓人，穷得像教堂里的老鼠。他们把女儿们送到布鲁日上学。有一年暑假，这几个姑娘把朱莉·达尔林普尔带回家来，做她们名义上的假期家庭教师。那是三年前。我刚刚看过利布雷希（Liebreich）。他告诉我，我不久就会失明；这自然是当头一棒。
威尔弗里德爵士低声致以同情。
“哦，别可怜我！我从不可怜别人。我们这副可憎的皮囊总有磨坏的一天——这是买卖里讲定了的。不过，那时候我正情绪低落。我在乎的东西有两样——一样是同让我愉快的人聊天，另一样是读法文书。我想不出，若找不到一个人替我做眼睛、念书给我听，我如何维系得了这里的圈子，如何让自己这颗脑袋不致失修荒废。而我又是满身神经质，同没教养的人从来两不相容，所以着实为难了一阵。喏，有一天，那几个姑娘同她们的母亲来喝茶，你猜也猜得到，她们自然带了勒布雷东小姐同来。请客是我自己开口的，可等人到了，我又烦又躁，活像缩在洞里的一条病狗。然后呢——你既然见过她——想必猜得到发生了什么。”
“您发现了一个非凡之才？”
亨利夫人笑了。
“我当场就给粘住了，一只老鸟，照样中招。首先打动我的是那姑娘的模样——une belle laide*（一种丑中见美的美人）——一颦一动无不合度；在我看来，比任何粉白黛绿的美人都迷人得多。后来才知道，她刚同另一位同学在巴黎住了一个月。她说了句什么关于一部新戏的话——冷不丁地——让我抬眼瞧她。‘请到这儿来坐，小姐，劳驾——坐我旁边’——‘Venez vous asseoir ici, mademoiselle, s’il vous plaît—près de moi’——我当时这样对她说；如今我还听得见自己那声音，可怜的蠢货，还看得见她涨红了脸。啊！”亨利夫人这一声感叹低沉下去，化作一腔怒意，险些搅了威尔弗里德爵士的正经；他忍住了，她接着说：“我们谈了两个钟头；在我看来不过十分钟。我打发其他人到花园里去。她留在我身边。新出的法文书、戏剧、诗、剧本、小说、回忆录，连政治，她样样谈得来；或者说——你记住，这才是她的天赋——是她让我谈。我觉得自己好几个月没那样才思焕发过了。

===Original===

I was as good, in fact, as I had ever been. The difficulty in England is to find anyone to keep up the ball. She does it to perfection. She never throws to win﻿—never!﻿—but so as to leave you all the chances. You make a brilliant stroke; she applauds, and in a moment she has arranged you another. Oh, it is the most extraordinary gift of conversation﻿—and she never says a thing that you want to remember.”
There was a silence. Lady Henry’s old fingers drummed restlessly on the table. Her memory seemed to be wandering angrily among her first experiences of the lady they were discussing.
“Well,” said Sir Wilfrid, at last, “so you engaged her as *lectrice*, and thought yourself very lucky?”
“Oh, don’t suppose that I was quite an idiot. I made some inquiries﻿—I bored myself to death with civilities to the stupid family she was staying with, and presently I made her stay with me. And of course I soon saw there was a history. She possessed jewels, laces, little personal belongings of various kinds, that wanted explaining. So I laid traps for her; I let her also perceive whither my own plans were drifting. She did not wait to let me force her hand. She made up her mind. One day I found, left carelessly on the drawing-room table, a volume of Saint-Simon, beautifully bound in old French morocco, with something thrust between the leaves. I opened it. On the flyleaf was written the name Marriott Dalrymple, and the leaves opened, a little farther, on a miniature of Lady Rose Delaney. So﻿—”
“Apparently it was *her* traps that worked,” said Sir Wilfrid, smiling. Lady Henry returned the smile unwillingly, as one loath to acknowledge her own folly.
“I don’t know that I was trapped. We both desired to come to close quarters. Anyway, she soon showed me books, letters﻿—from Lady Rose, from Dalrymple, Lord Lackington﻿—the evidence was complete.﻿ ﻿…
“ ‘Very well,’ I said; ‘it isn’t your fault. All the better if you are well born﻿—I am not a person of prejudices. But understand, if you come to me, there must be no question of worrying your relations. There are scores of them in London. I know them all, or nearly all, and of course you’ll come across them. But unless you can hold your tongue, don’t come to me. Julie Dalrymple has disappeared, and I’ll be no party to her resurrection.

===Chinese===

事实上，我发挥得不输当年。在英国，难就难在找不到人接得住话球。她接得天衣无缝。她从不为赢而出球——从不！——她出球，是为了把所有机会都留给你。你打出一记妙招；她喝彩，转瞬又替你布好下一记。哦，这真是天下无双的谈话天赋——而且她从不说一句你想记住的话。”
一阵沉默。亨利夫人苍老的手指在桌上不安地敲着。她的记忆似乎正满怀愠怒，在她同眼下谈论的这位夫人最初交往的往事间逡巡。
“那么，”威尔弗里德爵士终于开口，“您便聘她做了朗读者（lectrice），还觉得自己运气好得很？”
“哦，别把我当十足的傻瓜。我做过一些打探——我对她借住的那户乏味人家客套得要死要活，随后便请她住到了我家。当然，我很快就看出这里面有段来历。她有些珠宝、花边、各式各样的小件随身之物，都亟须解释。于是我给她设下圈套；我也让她察觉我自己的打算正往哪里漂。她没等我逼她摊牌。她自己拿定了主意。有一天，我在客厅桌上发现一册圣西门（Saint-Simon），旧法兰西摩洛哥皮装帧，精美极了，书页间夹着什么东西。我翻开。扉页上写着马里奥特·达尔林普尔的名字，再往前翻几页，摊开在罗斯·德拉尼小姐的一帧微型画像上。于是——”
“看来奏效的倒是她的圈套，”威尔弗里德爵士微笑道。亨利夫人不情愿地回以一笑，像是不肯认领自己的失算。
“我不觉得我是中了圈套。我们俩都想摊开来谈。总之，她很快给我看了书、信件——罗斯小姐的、达尔林普尔的、拉金顿勋爵的——证据齐全了。 …
“‘很好，’我说，‘这不是你的过错。你若出身高贵，那更好——我不是个抱偏见的人。可你要明白，你若进我家门，就绝无纠缠你那些亲族一说。他们在伦敦的有的是。他们我全认得，或者说几乎全认得，你自然免不了撞见。但你若守不住口风，就别来找我。朱莉·达尔林普尔已经消失了，我绝不做她复活的同谋。

===Original===

If Julie Le Breton becomes an inmate of my house, there shall be no raking up of scandals much better left in their graves. If you haven’t got a proper parentage, consistently thought out, we must invent one﻿—’ ”
“I hope I may some day be favored with it,” said Sir Wilfrid.
Lady Henry laughed uncomfortably.
“Oh, I’ve had to tell lies,” she said, “plenty of them.”
“What! It was *you* that told the lies?”
Lady Henry’s look flashed.
“The open and honest ones,” she said, defiantly.
“Well,” said Sir Wilfrid, regretfully, “*some* sort were indispensable. So she came. How long ago?”
“Three years. For the first half of that time I did nothing but plume myself on my good fortune. I said to myself that if I had searched Europe through I could not have fared better. My household, my friends, my daily ways, she fitted into them all to perfection. I told people that I had discovered her through a Belgian acquaintance. Everyone was amazed at her manners, her intelligence. She was perfectly modest, perfectly well behaved. The old Duke﻿—he died six months after she came to me﻿—was charmed with her. Montresor, Meredith, Lord Robert, all my habitués congratulated me. ‘Such cultivation, such charm, such savoir-faire! Where on earth did you pick up such a treasure? What are her antecedents?’ etc., etc. So then, of course﻿—”
“I hope no more than were absolutely necessary!” said Sir Wilfrid, hastily.
“I had to do it well,” said Lady Henry, with decision; “I can’t say I didn’t. That state of things lasted, more or less, about a year and a half. And by now, where do you think it has all worked out?”
“You gave me a few hints last night,” said Sir Wilfrid, hesitating.
Lady Henry pushed her chair back from the table. Her hands trembled on her stick.
“Hints!” she said, scornfully. “I’m long past hints. I told you last night﻿—and I repeat﻿—that woman has stripped me of all my friends! She has intrigued with them all in turn against me. She has done the same even with my servants. I can trust none of them where she is concerned. I am alone in my own house. My blindness makes me her tool, her plaything. As for my salon, as you call it, it has become hers. I am a mere courtesy-figurehead﻿—her chaperon, in fact. I provide the house, the footmen, the champagne; the guests are hers. And she has done this by constant intrigue and deception﻿—by flattery﻿—by lying!”

===Chinese===

朱莉·勒布雷东若进我家的门，就不许再翻那些最好烂在坟里的旧账。你若没有一套想得周全妥帖的体面身世，我们就编造一套——’”
“但愿有朝一日能蒙您相告，”威尔弗里德爵士说。
亨利夫人笑得有些不自在。
“哦，我可是撒过谎的，”她说，“撒过不少。”
“什么！谎是您撒的？”
亨利夫人目光一闪。
“光明正大、堂堂正正的那种，”她针锋相对地说。
“唉，”威尔弗里德爵士不无遗憾地说，“总归是非撒不可的。于是她来了。多久以前的事？”
“三年。头一年半里，我净顾着为自己的好运扬扬自得。我对自己说，就是把欧洲翻个遍，也找不着更好的了。我的家事、我的朋友、我的日常起居，她样样融入得天衣无缝。我告诉人家，我是经一位比利时相识引荐发现她的。人人都惊叹她的仪态、她的聪慧。她极其谦逊，极其得体。老公爵——她来我家六个月后就去世了——被她迷住了。蒙特勒索、梅瑞狄斯、罗伯特勋爵，我那些常客个个向我道贺。‘这般教养，这般风韵，这般savoir-faire（处世手腕）！您到底从哪儿捡来这么件宝贝？她是什么来历？’如此等等。于是，自然——”
“但愿没超出万不得已的限度！”威尔弗里德爵士急忙说。
“要做就得做好，”亨利夫人斩钉截铁地说，“我不否认。那样的光景，或多或少持续了一年半上下。那么到如今，你猜这一切落了个什么下场？”
“昨晚您给了我一些暗示，”威尔弗里德爵士迟疑着说。
亨利夫人把椅子从桌边推开。她的双手拄在手杖上，颤个不停。
“暗示！”她讥诮地说。“我早过了暗示的阶段了。我昨晚说过——现在再说一遍——那女人把我的朋友剥夺得一干二净！她挨个儿勾结他们来对付我。对我的仆人，她使的也是同一套。但凡牵涉到她，我一个都信不过。我在自己家里形影孤立。我的失明把我变成她的工具、她的玩物。至于我的沙龙——随你怎么称呼——已成了她的。我不过是个礼节性的摆设——说穿了，是她的陪护。宅子、男仆、香槟，是我出的；宾客，是她的。而这一切，她靠的是不停的阴谋与欺骗——靠谄媚——靠撒谎！”

===Original===

The old face had become purple. Lady Henry breathed hard.
“My dear friend,” said Sir Wilfrid, quickly, laying a calming hand on her arm, “don’t let this trouble you so. Dismiss her.”
“And accept solitary confinement for the rest of my days? I haven’t the courage﻿—yet,” said Lady Henry, bitterly. “You don’t know how I have been isolated and betrayed! And I haven’t told you the worst of all. Listen! Do you know whom she has got into her toils?”
She paused, drawing herself rigidly erect. Sir Wilfrid, looking up sharply, remembered the little scene in the Park, and waited.
“Did you have any opportunity last night,” said Lady Henry, slowly, “of observing her and Jacob Delafield?”
She spoke with passionate intensity, her frowning brows meeting above a pair of eyes that struggled to see and could not. But the effect she listened for was not produced. Sir Wilfrid drew back uncertainly.
“Jacob Delafield?” he said. “Jacob Delafield? Are you sure?”
“Sure?” cried Lady Henry, angrily. Then, disdaining to support her statement, she went on: “He hesitates. But she’ll soon make an end of that. And do you realize what that means﻿—what Jacob’s possibilities are? Kindly recollect that Chudleigh has one boy﻿—one sickly, tuberculous boy﻿—who might die any day. And Chudleigh himself is a poor life. Jacob has more than a good chance﻿—ninety chances out of a hundred”﻿—she ground the words out with emphasis﻿—“of inheriting the dukedom.”
“Good gracious!” said Sir Wilfrid, throwing away his cigarette.
“There!” said Lady Henry, in sombre triumph. “Now you can understand what I have brought on poor Henry’s family.”
A low knock was heard at the door.
“Come in,” said Lady Henry, impatiently.
The door opened, and Mademoiselle Le Breton appeared on the threshold, carrying a small gray terrier under each arm.
“I thought I had better tell you,” she said, humbly, “that I am taking the dogs out. Shall I get some fresh wool for your knitting?”

===Chinese===

那张老脸已涨成紫色。亨利夫人呼吸粗重。
“亲爱的朋友，”威尔弗里德爵士急忙说，把一只抚慰的手搭上她的手臂，“别为这事这样糟心。辞了她。”
“然后接受余生禁闭？我还没有那份勇气——暂时没有，”亨利夫人辛酸地说。“你不知道我被孤立、被出卖到什么地步！最糟的我还没说呢。听着！你可知道她把谁网进了她那张罗网？”
她顿住了，身子绷得笔直。威尔弗里德爵士蓦地抬眼，想起公园里那小小的一幕，等着下文。
“昨晚你有没有机会，”亨利夫人缓缓地说，“观察她和雅各布·德拉菲尔德？”
她说得激切万分，紧锁的眉头底下，一双眼睛竭力想看而不能。可她侧耳等待的反应并没有出现。威尔弗里德爵士迟疑地缩了缩身。
“雅各布·德拉菲尔德？”他说。“雅各布·德拉菲尔德？您确定？”
“确定？”亨利夫人怒声道。随即，她不屑于再为自己的断言举证，径直说了下去：“他还在犹豫。可她很快就会叫那犹豫收场。你可明白这意味着什么——雅各布的前程是什么？劳驾想想，查德利（Chudleigh）膝下只有一个儿子——一个病歪歪的痨病儿子——随时可能死。查德利自己也是条不济的命。雅各布岂止有望——十成里占着九成”——她一字一顿咬着牙说——“能承袭公爵之位。”
“天哪！”威尔弗里德爵士说着，把烟一扔。
“这就对了！”亨利夫人说，阴郁中带着得胜的快意。“现在你能明白，我给可怜的亨利一家招来了什么。”
门上传来轻轻的叩门声。
“进来，”亨利夫人不耐烦地说。
门开了，勒布雷东小姐出现在门口，两臂各夹着一只灰色小梗犬。
“我想还是禀告您一声的好，”她谦卑地说，“我要带狗出去。要不要替您的毛线活儿捎些新毛线回来？”

## III / 第三章
===Original===

It was nearly four o’clock. Sir Wilfrid had just closed Lady Henry’s door behind him, and was again walking along Bruton Street.
He was thinking of the little scene of Mademoiselle Le Breton’s appearance on the threshold of Lady Henry’s dining-room; of the insolent sharpness with which Lady Henry had given her order upon order﻿—as to the dogs, the books for the circulating library, a message for her dressmaker, certain directions for the tradesmen, etc., etc.﻿—as though for the mere purpose of putting the woman who had dared to be her rival in her right place before Sir Wilfrid Bury. And at the end, as she was departing, Mademoiselle Le Breton, trusting no doubt to Lady Henry’s blindness, had turned towards himself, raising her downcast eyes upon him suddenly, with a proud, passionate look. Her lips had moved; Sir Wilfrid had half risen from his chair. Then, quickly, the door had closed upon her.
Sir Wilfrid could not think of it without a touch of excitement.
“Was she reminding me of Gherardtsloo?” he said to himself. “Upon my word, I must find some means of conversation with her, in spite of Lady Henry.”
He walked towards Bond Street, pondering the situation of the two women﻿—the impotent jealousy and rancor with which Lady Henry was devoured, the domestic slavery contrasted with the social power of Mademoiselle Le Breton. Through the obscurity and difficulty of circumstance, how marked was the conscience of race in her, and, as he also thought, of high intelligence! The old man was deeply interested. He felt a certain indulgent pity for his lifelong friend Lady Henry; but he could not get Mademoiselle Julie out of his head.
“Why on earth does she stay where she is?”
He had asked the same question of Lady Henry, who had contemptuously replied:
