“Because she likes the fleshpots, and won’t give them up. No doubt she doesn’t find my manners agreeable; but she knows very well that she wouldn’t get the chances she gets in my house anywhere else. I give her a foothold. She’ll not risk it for a few sour speeches on my part. I may say what I like to her﻿—and I intend to say what I like! Besides, you watch her, and see whether she’s made for poverty. She takes to luxury as a fish to water. What would she be if she left me? A little visiting teacher, perhaps, in a Bloomsbury lodging. That’s not her line at all.”
“But somebody else might employ her as you do?”

===Chinese===

将近四点钟。威尔弗里德·伯里爵士刚在身后带上亨利夫人的家门，又沿着布鲁顿街走去。

他心里想着方才那一幕：勒布雷东（Le Breton）小姐出现在亨利夫人餐厅门口；亨利夫人一道接一道地发号施令，语气倨傲尖刻——什么狗啦，流通图书馆订的书啦，给裁缝捎的口信啦，对各路商家的种种吩咐啦，等等等等——仿佛单单为了在威尔弗里德·伯里爵士面前，把那个胆敢与她争锋的女人打压回她本分的位子上去。而临了，就在她退出去的当口，勒布雷东小姐——无疑是仗着亨利夫人眼盲——忽而转向他，低垂的眼睛倏地抬起来望住他，目光骄傲而炽烈。她的嘴唇动了动；威尔弗里德爵士不由得从椅子上半直起身来。随即，门便在她身后关上了。

每当想起这一幕，威尔弗里德爵士总免不了心头一阵悸动。

"她这是在提醒我赫拉德斯卢（Gherardtsloo）的事么？"他自忖道，"说实话，我一定得找个法子同她说上话，哪怕亨利夫人从中作梗。"

他朝邦德街走去，一路思量着这两个女人的处境——亨利夫人被无从排遣的嫉妒与怨毒啃噬着；勒布雷东小姐呢，一面是家内的奴仆境遇，一面又是社交场上呼风唤雨的力量。身处这般晦暗艰难的境地，她身上那种与生俱来的种族自觉何其鲜明，而且——他进一步想道——还有那超群的智慧！老人深感兴趣。他对毕生的老友亨利夫人怀着几分宽容的怜悯，可脑子里怎么也撇不开朱莉（Julie）小姐。

"她究竟为什么要待在如今这个位置上？"

他曾把同样的问题问过亨利夫人，她轻蔑地答道：

"因为她贪恋富贵窝，舍不得撒手。不用说，她觉得我的做派不合她的口味；可她心里明白得很，离开我这个家，她上哪儿也得不到如今这样的机会。是我给了她一个立足之地。凭我几句刻薄话，她可不会拿它去冒险。我想对她说什么就说什么——而且我打算想说什么就说什么！再说了，你自己瞧瞧她，看她是不是受穷的料。她过奢华日子，简直如鱼得水。她若离开我，能落个什么下场？顶多在布卢姆斯伯里（Bloomsbury）赁间屋子，做个上门授课的小教师罢了。那压根儿不是她的路数。"

"可说不定别家也能像您这样雇她呀？"

===Original===

Sir Wilfrid had suggested.
“You forget I should be asked for a character,” said Lady Henry. “Oh, I admit there are possibilities﻿—on her side. That silly goose, Evelyn Crowborough, would have taken her in, but I had a few words with Crowborough, and he put his foot down. He told his wife he didn’t want an intriguing foreigner to live with them. No; for the present we are chained to each other. I can’t get rid of her, and she doesn’t want to get rid of me. Of course, things might become intolerable for either of us. But at present self-interest on both sides keeps us going. Oh, don’t tell me the thing is odious! I know it. Every day she stays in the house I become a more abominable old woman.”
A more exacting one, certainly. Sir Wilfrid thought with pity and amusement of the commissions with which Mademoiselle Julie had been loaded. “She earns her money, anyway,” he thought. “Those things will take her a hard afternoon’s work. But, bless my soul!”﻿—he paused in his walk﻿—“what about that engagement to Duchess Evelyn that I heard her make? Not a word, by-the-way, to Lady Henry about it! Oh, this is amusing!”
He went meditatively on his way, and presently turned into his club to write some letters. But at five o’clock he emerged, and told a hansom to drive him to Grosvenor Square. He alighted at the great redbrick mansion of the Crowboroughs, and asked for the Duchess. The magnificent person presiding over the hall, an old family retainer, remembered him, and made no difficulty about admitting him.
“Anybody with her grace?” he inquired, as the man handed him over to the footman who was to usher him upstairs.
“Only Miss Le Breton and Mr. Delafield, Sir Wilfrid. Her grace told me to say ‘not at home’ this afternoon, but I am sure, sir, she will see you.”
Sir Wilfrid smiled.
As he entered the outer drawing-room, the Duchess and the group surrounding her did not immediately perceive the footman nor himself, and he had a few moments in which to take in a charming scene.
A baby girl in a white satin gown down to her heels, and a white satin cap, lace-edged and tied under her chin, was holding out her tiny skirt with one hand and dancing before the Duchess and Miss Le Breton, who was at the piano.

===Chinese===

威尔弗里德爵士曾这样问道。

"你忘了，人家雇她之前是要来向我讨品行鉴定的。"亨利夫人说，"哦，我承认她那边不是没有可能。伊芙琳·克罗伯勒（Evelyn Crowborough）那只呆鹅本来要收留她的，可我跟克罗伯勒（Crowborough）说了几句，他一跺脚，断然不肯了。他告诉他妻子，他不想让一个惯会耍手段的外国女人住进他们家里。不成；眼下我们俩是拴在一根绳上了。我甩不掉她，她也不想甩掉我。当然，日子久了，对哪一边都可能变得忍无可忍。可目前是双方各自的利益凑合着往下过。哦，不用对我说这有多可憎！我清楚得很。她在家里多待一天，我就多变成一分可恶的老太婆。"

反正是一天比一天更苛刻了。威尔弗里德爵士又怜又笑地想着压在朱莉小姐身上的那一堆差事。"不管怎么说，她这份钱挣得不冤，"他想，"那些事够她苦苦奔忙一下午的。可是，天哪！"——他停下脚步——"方才我听见她应下伊芙琳公爵夫人的那个约，又该怎么算？这件事呀，对亨利夫人可一个字也不能提！嗬，这真有趣！"

他一路若有所思地走着，不多时拐进俱乐部写几封信。可五点钟一到，他又出来了，唤了辆双轮马车，叫车夫送他去格罗夫纳广场。他在克劳伯勒家那座红砖大宅前下了车，求见公爵夫人。主持门厅的那位气派堂皇的人物——一名老家人——认得他，毫不为难地放他进去了。

"公爵夫人殿下有客吗？"当那人把他交给引他上楼的男仆时，他问道。

"只有勒布雷东小姐和德拉菲尔德先生，威尔弗里德爵士。公爵夫人吩咐过，今天下午'一律不见客'，不过我敢说，爵士，她会见您的。"

威尔弗里德爵士莞尔一笑。

他走进外客厅时，公爵夫人和围着她的一群人一时没有察觉那男仆，也没察觉他，于是他有片刻工夫，把眼前一幅动人的情景尽收眼底。

一个身穿白缎长裙的小女娃，裙裾垂到脚跟，头戴白缎小帽，帽缘镶着花边，带子系在下巴底下；她一手提着小小的裙摆，正在公爵夫人和坐在钢琴前的勒布雷东小姐面前跳舞。

===Original===

The child’s other hand held up a morsel of biscuit wherewith she directed the movements of her partner, a small black spitz, of a slim and silky elegance, who, straining on his hind legs, his eager attention fixed upon the biscuit, followed every movement of his small mistress; while she, her large blue eyes now solemn, now triumphant, her fair hair escaping from her cap in fluttering curls, her dainty feet pointed, her dimpled arm upraised, repeated in living grace the picture of her great-great-grandmother which hung on the wall in front of her, a masterpiece from Reynolds’s happiest hours.
Behind Mademoiselle Le Breton stood Jacob Delafield; while the Duchess, in a low chair beside them, beat time gayly to the gavotte that Mademoiselle Julie was playing and laughed encouragement and applause to the child in front of her. She herself, with her cloud of fair hair, the delicate pink and white of her skin, the laughing lips and small white hands that rose and fell with the baby steps, seemed little more than a child. Her pale blue dress, for which she had just exchanged her winter walking-costume, fell round her in sweeping folds of lace and silk﻿—a French fairy dressed by Wörth, she was possessed by a wild gayety, and her silvery laugh held the room.
Beside her, Julie Le Breton, very thin, very tall, very dark, was laughing too. The eyes which Sir Wilfrid had lately seen so full of pride were now alive with pleasure. Jacob Delafield, also, from behind, grinned applause or shouted to the babe, “Brava, Tottie; well done!” Three people, a baby, and a dog more intimately pleased with one another’s society it would have been difficult to discover.
“Sir Wilfrid!”
The Duchess sprang up astonished, and in a moment, to Sir Wilfrid’s chagrin, the little scene fell to pieces. The child dropped on the floor, defending herself and the biscuit as best she could against the wild snatches of the dog. Delafield composed his face in a moment to its usual taciturnity. Mademoiselle Le Breton rose from the piano.
“No, no!” said Sir Wilfrid, stopping short and holding up a deprecating hand. “Too bad! Go on.”
“Oh, we were only fooling with baby!” said the Duchess. “It is high time she went to her nurse. Sit here, Sir Wilfrid. Julie, will you take the babe, or shall I ring for Mrs. Robson?”
“I’ll take her,” said Mademoiselle Le Breton.

===Chinese===

孩子另一只手举着一小角饼干，用它指挥着舞伴的动作——那是一条黑色小狐狸犬，身姿纤细、毛色丝润；它后腿撑地直立，急切的眼光盯死那块饼干，随着小主人的一举一动团团转。而她呢，蓝色的大眼睛忽而一本正经，忽而得意洋洋，浅金的头发从小帽里挣脱出来，鬈成飘拂的一团，一双秀气的小脚绷直了脚尖，带小涡的胳膊高高扬起——把挂在面前墙上的曾曾祖母的画像，以活生生的风姿重演了一遍；那幅画像出自雷诺兹（Reynolds）最得意的时光，是一幅杰作。

勒布雷东小姐身后站着雅各布·德拉菲尔德；公爵夫人坐在他们旁边一张矮椅上，为朱莉小姐弹奏的加伏特舞曲欢快地打着拍子，笑着给面前的孩子鼓劲、喝彩。她自己呢，一头金发如云，肌肤娇嫩的粉白，含笑的嘴唇和一双小白手随着娃娃的舞步起起落落，看上去比孩子大不了多少。她刚换下冬日出门的装束，那条浅蓝的裙子——花边与绸缎的裙裾层层漾开——围着她垂落：一位由沃斯（Wörth）装扮出的法兰西仙女，正被一阵忘乎所以的欢快攫住，银铃似的笑声盈满了整个房间。

她身旁的朱莉·勒布雷东，瘦削、高挑、黝黑，也在笑。威尔弗里德爵士不久前见到的盛满骄傲的那双眼睛，此刻熠熠闪着欢愉。雅各布·德拉菲尔德也在背后咧嘴喝彩，或是朝娃娃喊："好哇，托蒂（Tottie）；跳得好！"再要找出比这三个人、一个娃娃加一条狗彼此更亲昵相得的一群，怕是难了。

"威尔弗里德爵士！"

公爵夫人惊跳起来，转眼之间——让威尔弗里德爵士好不懊恼——这小小一幕便散了场。孩子扑通跌坐在地板上，拼命护住自己和那块饼干，抵挡狗儿的疯狂扑夺。德拉菲尔德的脸霎时恢复了一贯的沉默。勒布雷东小姐从钢琴前站起身。

"别，别！"威尔弗里德爵士赶紧止步，举起一只手央告道，"太可惜了！接着跳吧。"

"哦，我们不过是跟娃娃闹着玩儿！"公爵夫人说，"她也早该回奶妈那儿去了。坐这儿，威尔弗里德爵士。朱莉，你来抱娃娃呢，还是我摇铃唤罗布森（Robson）太太？"

"我来抱她。"勒布雷东小姐说。

===Original===

She knelt down by the child, who rose with alacrity. Catching her skirts round her, with one eye half laughing, half timorous, turned over her shoulder towards the dog, the baby made a wild spring into Mademoiselle Julie’s arms, tucking up her feet instantly, with a shriek of delight, out of the dog’s way. Then she nestled her fair head down upon her bearer’s shoulder, and, throbbing with joy and mischief, was carried away.
Sir Wilfrid, hat in hand, stood for a moment watching the pair. A bygone marriage uniting the Lackington family with that of the Duchess had just occurred to him in some bewilderment. He sat down beside his hostess, while she made him some tea. But no sooner had the door of the farther drawing-room closed behind Mademoiselle Le Breton, than with a dart of all her lively person she pounced upon him.
“Well, so Aunt Flora has been complaining to you?”
Sir Wilfrid’s cup remained suspended in his hand. He glanced first at the speaker and then at Jacob Delafield.
“Oh, Jacob knows all about it!” said the Duchess, eagerly. “This is Julie’s headquarters; *we* are on her staff. *You* come from the enemy!”
Sir Wilfrid took out his white silk handkerchief and waved it.
“Here is my flag of truce,” he said. “Treat me well.”
“We are only too anxious to parley with you,” said the Duchess, laughing. “Aren’t we, Jacob?”
Then she drew closer.
“What has Aunt Flora been saying to you?”
Sir Wilfrid paused. As he sat there, apparently studying his boots, his blond hair, now nearly gray, carefully parted in the middle above his benevolent brow, he might have been reckoned a tame and manageable person. Jacob Delafield, however, knew him of old.
“I don’t think that’s fair,” said Sir Wilfrid, at last, looking up. “I’m the newcomer; I ought to be allowed the questions.”
“Go on,” said the Duchess, her chin on her hand. “Jacob and I will answer all we know.”
Delafield nodded. Sir Wilfrid, looking from one to the other, quickly reminded himself that they had been playmates from the cradle﻿—or might have been.
“Well, in the first place,” he said, slowly, “I am lost in admiration at the rapidity with which Mademoiselle Le Breton does business. An hour and a half ago”﻿—he looked at his watch﻿—“I stood by while Lady Henry enumerated commissions it would have taken any ordinary man-mortal half a day to execute.”
The Duchess clapped her hands.

===Chinese===

她在孩子身旁跪下，孩子一骨碌爬起来。她把小裙子拢在身周，一只眼睛越过肩膀回头瞄着狗，半是发笑、半是害怕，随即猛地一蹿，扑进朱莉小姐怀里，双脚立刻缩起，欢叫一声，躲开了狗的扑抓。然后她把金色的小脑袋偎进抱她的人的肩窝里，欢喜和淘气在小小身子里乱颤，被抱了出去。

威尔弗里德爵士手里拿着帽子，伫立片刻，望着这一对。一件旧事此刻才模模糊糊浮上他心头：拉金顿（Lackington）家族与公爵夫人的娘家之间，曾有过一桩昔日的联姻。他在女主人身旁落座，女主人替他斟茶。可勒布雷东小姐的身影刚在内客厅的门后消失，公爵夫人便全身的活泼劲儿一齐发动，向他猛扑过来。

"哈，这么说，弗洛拉姑妈向您诉苦来着？"

威尔弗里德爵士的茶杯举在半空停住了。他先看看说话的人，又看看雅各布·德拉菲尔德。

"哦，雅各布全知道！"公爵夫人急切地说，"这里是朱莉的司令部；我们嘛，都是她手下的人。您呢，是从敌营来的！"

威尔弗里德爵士掏出白绸手帕，扬了扬。

"这是我的休战旗，"他说，"请以礼相待。"

"我们正求之不得要跟您谈判呢。"公爵夫人笑着说，"是不是，雅各布？"

说着她凑近了些。

"弗洛拉姑妈到底跟您说了些什么？"

威尔弗里德爵士顿了顿。他坐在那儿，表面上端详着自己的靴子，淡色的头发——如今已近乎花白——在慈眉善目的额头上精心地从中间分开，这样一个人，或许会被当作温顺好摆布的角色。然而雅各布·德拉菲尔德对他的底细早就了然。

"我看这不公平，"威尔弗里德爵士终于抬眼说道，"我是新来的；提问的权利该归我。"

"问吧，"公爵夫人手托着下巴说，"雅各布和我知无不言。"

德拉菲尔德点了点头。威尔弗里德爵士的目光在两人之间来回一转，蓦地想起他们俩是自摇篮起就在一起的玩伴——或者说，本可以这样。

"那么，首先，"他缓缓说道，"勒布雷东小姐办事的利索劲儿，实在令我叹服。一个半钟头之前"——他看了看表——"我就在旁边站着，听亨利夫人一样一样数着差事，那些差事，寻常的凡人得花上半天工夫才办得完。"

公爵夫人拍起手来。

===Original===

“My maid is now executing them,” she said, with glee. “In an hour she will be back. Julie will go home with everything done, and I shall have had nearly two hours of her delightful society. What harm is there in that?”
“Where are the dogs?” said Sir Wilfrid, looking round.
“Aunt Flora’s dogs? In the housekeeper’s room, eating sweet biscuit. They adore the groom of the chambers.”
“Is Lady Henry aware of this﻿—this division of labor?” said Sir Wilfrid, smiling.
“Of course not,” said the Duchess, flushing. “She makes Julie’s life such a burden to her that something has to be done. Now what *has* Aunt Flora been telling you? We were certain she would take you into council﻿—she has dropped various hints of it. I suppose she has been telling you that Julie has been intriguing against her﻿—taking liberties, separating her from her friends, and so on?”
Sir Wilfrid smilingly presented his cup for some more tea.
“I beg to point out,” he said, “that I have only been allowed *two* questions so far. But if things are to be at all fair and equal, I am owed at least six.”
The Duchess drew back, checked, and rather annoyed. Jacob Delafield, on the other hand, bent forward.
“We are *anxious*, Sir Wilfrid, to tell you all we know,” he replied, with quiet emphasis.
Sir Wilfrid looked at him. The flame in the young man’s eyes burned clear and steady﻿—but flame it was. Sir Wilfrid remembered him as a lazy, rather somnolent youth; the man’s advance in expression, in significant power, of itself, told much.
“In the first place, can you give me the history of this lady’s antecedents?”
He glanced from one to the other.
The Duchess and Jacob Delafield exchanged glances. Then the Duchess spoke﻿—uncertainly.
“Yes, we know. She has confided in us. There is nothing whatever to her discredit.”
Sir Wilfrid’s expression changed.
“Ah!” cried the Duchess, bending forward. “You know, too?”
“I knew her father and mother,” said Sir Wilfrid, simply.
The Duchess gave a little cry of relief. Jacob Delafield rose, took a turn across the room, and came back to Sir Wilfrid.
“Now we can really speak frankly,” he said. “The situation has grown very difficult, and we did not know﻿—Evelyn and I﻿—whether we had a right to explain it. But now that Lady Henry﻿—”
“Oh yes,” said Sir Wilfrid, “that’s all right. The fact of Mademoiselle Le Breton’s parentage﻿—”

公爵夫人愤然叫道，"——恰恰就是让亨利夫人妒火中烧的根由！哦，弗洛拉姑妈就是个暴君！正因为朱莉原本就属于她那个世界——论血统，属于我们这个世界，不管法律怎么说——她便忍不住要把她变成自己的对头，从早到晚地折磨她。我告诉您，威尔弗里德爵士，那可怜的姑娘忍受过什么，除了我们这些亲眼看着的人，谁也想象不到。这三年，亨利夫人欠了她一切。没有朱莉，她如今会在什么田地？她反倒说朱莉把她和朋友们隔开啦、抢她的风头啦、欺负她啦——全是那一类混话！我倒要请问：一位瞎眼的老太太，自己写不了短简、认不出面孔，单枪匹马怎么撑得起那样一个沙龙？起先她把什么都推给朱莉，为她的能干沾沾自喜，处处把她往前推，还编排些毫无必要的关于她的谎话——朱莉为这个心里非常难受——可等到朱莉大获成功，等到人们既为亨利夫人、也为她的缘故开始涌向布鲁顿街，亨利夫人便翻脸成仇，逢人就数落她，什么背信啦、弃义啦、天知道还有什么，并且开始拿她当脚底下的尘土！朱莉聪明可人，难道这是她的错——她就是聪明可人！昨天蒙特雷索（Montresor）先生还对我说：'那位小姐一进屋，我的精神就为之一振！'为什么？因为她从不想到自己，总能让别人显出最好的一面。可亨利夫人竟这样待她！"公爵夫人双手一摊，满脸不屑的谴责，"于是问题自然就是：这样下去还行不行？"

When the butler had a child very ill, it was Mademoiselle Julie who went to see it in the mews, who took it flowers and grapes﻿—”
“Lady Henry’s grapes?” threw in Sir Wilfrid.
“What does it matter!” said Delafield, impatiently. “Lady Henry has more of everything than she knows what to do with. But it wasn’t grapes only! It was time and thought and consideration. Then when the younger footman wanted to emigrate to the States, it was Mademoiselle Julie who found a situation for him, who got Mr. Montresor to write to some American friends, and finally sent the lad off, devoted to her, of course, for life. I should like to know when Lady Henry would have done that kind of thing! Naturally the servants like her﻿—she deserves it.”
“I see﻿—I see,” said Sir Wilfrid, nodding gently, his eyes on the carpet. “A very competent young lady.”
Delafield looked at the older man, half in annoyance, half in perplexity.
“Is there anything to complain of in that?” he said, rather shortly.
“Oh, nothing, nothing!” said Sir Wilfrid, hastily. “And this word intrigue that Lady Henry uses? Has mademoiselle always steered a straightforward course with her employer?”
“Oh, well,” said the Duchess, shrugging her shoulders, “how can you always be perfectly straightforward with such a tyrannical old person! She *has* to be managed. Lately, in order to be sure of every minute of Julie’s time, she has taken to heaping work upon her to such a ridiculous extent that unless I come to the rescue the poor thing gets no rest and no amusement. And last summer there was an explosion, because Julie, who was supposed to be in Paris for her holiday with a schoolfriend, really spent a week of it with the Buncombes, Lady Henry’s married niece, who has a place in Kent. The Buncombes knew her at Lady Henry’s parties, of course. Then they met her in the Louvre, took her about a little, were delighted with her, and begged her to come and stay with them﻿—they have a place near Canterbury﻿—on the way home. They and Julie agreed that it would be best to say nothing to Lady Henry about it﻿—she is too absurdly jealous﻿—but then it leaked out, unluckily, and Lady Henry was furious.”
“I must say,” said Delafield, hurriedly, “I always thought frankness would have been best there.”
“Well, perhaps,” said the Duchess, unwillingly, with another shrug. “But now what is to be done?

Lady Henry really must behave better, or Julie can’t and shan’t stay with her. Julie has a great following﻿—hasn’t she, Jacob? They won’t see her harassed to death.”

Had the Duchess pressed him into her service, and was he merely taking sides for the weaker out of a natural bent towards that way of looking at things? Or﻿—
“Well, certainly we must do our best to patch it up,” said Sir Wilfrid, after a pause. “Perhaps Mademoiselle Le Breton will allow me a word with her by-and-by. “But I must!” cried the Duchess. “Just think, there is my big bazaar on the 16th. You don’t know how clever Julie is at such things. I want to make her recite﻿—her French is too beautiful! “All the same, Evelyn,” said Delafield, uncomfortable apparently for the second time, “I really think it would be best to let Lady Henry know.”
“Well, then, we may as well give it up,” said the Duchess, pettishly, turning aside.
Delafield, who was still pacing the carpet, suddenly raised his hand in a gesture of warning. Mademoiselle Le Breton was crossing the outer drawing-room.
“Julie, come here!”

"了不得的事？"威尔弗里德爵士叫道，"那是全部，我亲爱的雅各布！"

"我不知道。"德拉菲尔德缓缓说道，"这代价也许太昂贵。"

威尔弗里德爵士好奇地打量着说话的人。这个雅各布·德拉菲尔德，从孩提时、从做学童、做大学生时起，只消一桩不公不义的行为或一段这样的传闻，总能把他从习以为常的散漫怠惰中激醒。如今是公爵夫人把他拉进了自己的阵营、他也不过是出于天性偏向弱者才站在那一边吗？还是——

"嗯，我们当然要尽力把这裂缝补上。"威尔弗里德爵士顿了顿说，"回头或许勒布雷东小姐肯容我同她说句话。我想我在亨利夫人面前还有几分影响力。不过，亲爱的教女"——他俯身把手搭在公爵夫人的手上——"别再让那位女伴替她跑差事。"

"可我非这样不可！"公爵夫人叫道，"您想想，16号我那场盛大的义卖会呢。您不知道朱莉操办这类事有多能干。我还要让她登台朗诵——她那口法语美极了！再说她那么有巧思、那么有主意！什么事情到她手里就成了。可我若跟弗洛拉姑妈透半个字，她就会给我们处处下绊子。凡是能让朱莉出头露角的事，她一想起来就恨。准会的！"

"话虽如此，伊芙琳，"德拉菲尔德说道，这已显然是他第二次显出局促，"我确实觉得最好还是让亨利夫人知道。"

"那好啊，那这事干脆拉倒。"公爵夫人赌气说着，把脸扭向一边。

仍在地毯上踱步的德拉菲尔德忽然抬手做了个警告的姿势。勒布雷东小姐正穿过外客厅走来。

"朱莉，过来！"

===Original===

cried the Duchess, springing up and running towards her. “Jacob is making himself so disagreeable. He thinks we ought to tell Lady Henry about the 16th.”
The speaker put her arm through Julie Le Breton’s, looking up at her with a frowning brow. The contrast between her restless prettiness, the profusion of her dress and hair, and Julie’s dark, lissome strength, gowned and gloved in neat, close black, was marked enough.
As the Duchess spoke, Julie looked smiling at Jacob Delafield.
“I am in your hands,” she said, gently. “Of course I don’t want to keep anything from Lady Henry. Please decide for me.”
Sir Wilfrid’s mouth showed a satirical line. He turned aside and began to play with a copy of the *Spectator*.
“Julie,” said the Duchess, hesitating, “I hope you won’t mind, but we have been discussing things a little with Sir Wilfrid. I felt sure Aunt Flora had been talking to him.”
“Of course,” said Julie, “I knew she would.” She looked towards Sir Wilfrid, slightly drawing herself up. Her manner was quiet, but all her movements were somehow charged with a peculiar and interesting significance. The force of the character made itself felt through all disguises.
In spite of himself, Sir Wilfrid began to murmur apologetic things.
“It was natural, mademoiselle, that Lady Henry should confide in me. She has perhaps told you that for many years I have been one of the trustees of her property. That has led to her consulting me on a good many matters. And evidently, from what she says and what the Duchess says, nothing could be of more importance to her happiness, now, in her helpless state, than her relations to you.”
He spoke with a serious kindness in which the tinge of mocking habitual to his sleek and well-groomed visage was wholly lost. Julie Le Breton met him with dignity.
“Yes, they are important. But, I fear they cannot go on as they are.”
There was a pause. Then Sir Wilfrid approached her:
“I hear you are returning to Bruton Street immediately. Might I be your escort?”
“Certainly.”
The Duchess, a little sobered by the turn events had taken and the darkened prospects of her bazaar, protested in vain against this sudden departure. Julie resumed her furs, which, as Sir Wilfrid, who was curious in such things; happened to notice, were of great beauty, and made her farewells. Did her hand linger in Jacob Delafield’s?

===Chinese===

公爵夫人一跃而起，向她跑去。"雅各布讨嫌得很。他觉得16号的事应该告诉亨利夫人。"

说话人挎住朱莉·勒布雷东的胳膊，蹙着眉仰头望她。她那副躁动不安的娇美，那衣饰与满头金发的繁盛，同朱莉那黝黑柔韧的气力——一身剪裁服帖的黑色衣裙，一双黑色手套——对比得再鲜明不过。

公爵夫人说话间，朱莉含笑望着雅各布·德拉菲尔德。

"我听凭你们安排。"她温和地说，"我当然不愿瞒着亨利夫人什么。请你们替我拿主意吧。"

威尔弗里德爵士的嘴角浮出一丝讥诮。他转过身去，拿起一份《旁观者》（Spectator）周刊摆弄起来。

"朱莉，"公爵夫人迟疑着说，"希望你别介意——我们刚才同威尔弗里德爵士稍稍谈了些你的事。我料定弗洛拉姑妈找他说过了。"

"当然。"朱莉说，"我早知道她会的。"她微微挺直身子，朝威尔弗里德爵士望去。她的举止沉静，可她的一举一动不知怎的都蕴着一种独特而耐人寻味的意味。那性格的力量穿透一切遮掩，教人无从忽视。

威尔弗里德爵士不由自主地嗫嚅起致歉的话来。

"小姐，亨利夫人向我吐露心曲，原是自然的。她也许对您说过，多年来我一直是她财产的受托人之一。因此许多事情她都找我商量。而显然，从她的话和公爵夫人的话来看，她如今处在这样无依无靠的境地，再没有什么比她与您的关系更关乎她的幸福了。"

他说得恳切而慈和，那张保养得光洁儒雅的脸上惯常带的一丝嘲弄之色，此刻消失得无影无踪。朱莉·勒布雷东落落大方地迎着他。

"是的，这关系确实要紧。可我怕，它照眼下这样是维持不下去的。"

一时静默。随后威尔弗里德爵士走近她：

"听说您这就回布鲁顿街去。可否容我送您一程？"

"当然可以。"

事态的这般转折，加上义卖会的前景眼看着黯淡下来，公爵夫人多少敛了兴头，对这突如其来的告辞百般挽留，终归徒劳。朱莉重新披上裘皮——威尔弗里德爵士素来留意这类物件，恰好看在眼里：那袭裘皮美极了——然后与众人作别。她的手，在雅各布·德拉菲尔德的手中，可曾多停留了一瞬？

===Original===

Did the look with which that young man received it express more than the steadfast support which justice offers to the oppressed? Sir Wilfrid could not be sure.
As they stepped out into the frosty, lamp-lit dark of Grosvenor Square, Julie Le Breton turned to her companion.
“You knew my mother and father,” she said, abruptly. “I remember your coming.”
What was in her voice, her rich, beautiful voice? Sir Wilfrid only knew that while perfectly steady, it seemed to bring emotion near, to make all the aspects of things dramatic.
“Yes, yes,” he replied, in some confusion. “I knew her well, from the time when she was a girl in the schoolroom. Poor Lady Rose!”
The figure beside him stood still.
“Then if you were my mother’s friend,” she said, huskily, “you will hear patiently what I have to say, even though you are Lady Henry’s trustee.”
“Indeed I will!” cried Sir Wilfrid, and they walked on.

===Chinese===

那年轻人接住这只手时的神情，可曾表达出比正义给予受压迫者的坚定支持更多的东西？威尔弗里德爵士说不准。

两人跨出大门，走进格罗夫纳广场严寒的、灯火点点的夜色里，朱莉·勒布雷东转向同伴。

"您认识我的母亲和父亲。"她突兀地说，"您来的那回，我记得。"

她那嗓音里——那丰润、美妙的嗓音里——含着什么？威尔弗里德爵士只知道，它虽平稳之极，却仿佛把激情带近了身边，使万事万物的种种面目都带上了戏剧的意味。

"是的，是的。"他有些慌乱地答道，"我很早就认识她，从她还是学房里小姑娘的时候起。可怜的罗斯（Rose）小姐！"

身旁的身影站住了。

"那么，您既是我母亲的朋友，"她嗓音发哑地说，"哪怕您是亨利夫人的受托人，也会耐心听一听我要说的话。"

"我一定听！"威尔弗里德爵士大声说。两人继续朝前走去。

## IV / IV / 第四章
===Original===

“But, first of all,” said Mademoiselle Le Breton, looking in some annoyance at the brace of terriers circling and barking round them, “we must take the dogs home, otherwise no talk will be possible.”
“You have no more business to do?”
His companion smiled.
“Everything Lady Henry wants is here,” she said, pointing to the bag upon her arm which had been handed to her, as Sir Wilfrid remembered, after some whispered conversation, in the hall of Crowborough House by an elegantly dressed woman, who was no doubt the Duchess’s maid.
“Allow me to carry it for you.”
“Many thanks,” said Mademoiselle Le Breton, firmly retaining it, “but those are not the things I mind.”
They walked on quickly to Bruton Street. The dogs made conversation impossible. If they were on the chain it was one long battle between them and their leader. If they were let loose, it seemed to Sir Wilfrid that they ranged every area on the march, and attacked all elderly gentlemen and most errand-boys.
“Do you always take them out?” he asked, when both he and his companion were crimson and out of breath.
“Always.”
“Do you like dogs?”
“I used to. Perhaps some day I shall again.”
“As for me, I wish they had but one neck!” said Sir Wilfrid, who had but just succeeded in dragging Max, the bigger of the two, out of the interior of a pastrycook’s handcart which had been rashly left with doors open for a few minutes in the street, while its responsible guardian was gossiping in an adjacent kitchen. Mademoiselle Julie meanwhile was wrestling with Nero, the younger, who had dived to the very heart of a peculiarly unsavory dust-box, standing near the entrance of a mews.
“So you commonly go through the streets of London in this whirlwind?” asked Sir Wilfrid, again, incredulous, when at last they had landed their charges safe at the Bruton Street door.
“Morning and evening,” said Mademoiselle Julie, smiling. Then she addressed the butler: “Tell Lady Henry, please, that I shall be at home in half an hour.”
As they turned westward, the winter streets were gay with lights and full of people. Sir Wilfrid was presently conscious that among all the handsome and well-dressed women who brushed past them, Mademoiselle Le Breton more than held her own. She reminded him now not so much of her mother as of Marriott Dalrymple.

===Chinese===

“不过，首先，”勒布雷东小姐说，有些懊恼地望着围着他俩打转狂吠的那对梗犬，“我们得先把狗送回家，不然什么话也没法谈。”
“您没有别的事要办了？”
女伴微微一笑。
“亨利夫人要的东西都在这儿了，”她说着，指了指臂上挎着的提包；威尔弗里德爵士记得，那是在克罗伯勒府（Crowborough House）的门厅里，经一番耳语之后，由一位衣饰雅致的妇人交到她手中的——那无疑是大公爵夫人的贴身女仆。
“让我替您拿着吧。”
“多谢，”勒布雷东小姐说着，牢牢把包留住，“不过我在意的可不是这些。”
两人快步朝布鲁顿街走去。那两条狗叫人没法说话。拴上链子，它们同牵狗人便是一场旷日持久的鏖战；放了链子，在威尔弗里德爵士看来，它们一路扫荡沿途每一处宅前的空场，袭击一切上了年纪的先生和大多数跑腿的伙计。
“您总亲自遛它们？”他问。此时他和女伴都涨红了脸，气喘吁吁。
“总是。”
“您喜欢狗吗？”
“从前喜欢。也许将来有一天还会再喜欢。”
“要我说，真希望它们俩只长一个脑袋！”威尔弗里德爵士说。他刚刚好不容易才把两条中较大的那条——马克斯（Max）——从一辆点心贩子的手推车里拖出来；那车子被冒失地敞着门在街上撂了几分钟，看管的伙计正在隔壁厨房里闲磕牙。这会儿朱莉小姐正同较小那条尼禄（Nero）角力，那条狗一头扎进了马厩巷口边一只气味格外难闻的垃圾箱最深处。
“这么说，您平日就是顶着这么阵旋风穿过伦敦的街市？”等他们终于把这两个家伙平安送到布鲁顿街门口，威尔弗里德爵士又问，还是不敢相信。
“早晚各一趟，”朱莉小姐含笑说。随即她吩咐管家：“请禀告亨利夫人，我半小时后回来。”
两人折向西行，冬日的街道灯火热闹，行人如织。威尔弗里德爵士很快便察觉，在一切与他们擦肩而过的美貌华服的女子当中，勒布雷东小姐非但不落丝毫下风，反而更胜一筹。此刻她令他想起的，与其说是她母亲，不如说是马里奥特·达尔林普尔。

===Original===

Sir Wilfrid had first seen this woman’s father at Damascus, when Dalrymple, at twenty-six, was beginning the series of Eastern journeys which had made him famous. He remembered the brillance of the youth; the power, physical and mental, which radiated from him, making all things easy; the scorn of mediocrity, the incapacity for subordination.
“I should like you to understand,” said the lady beside him, “that I came to Lady Henry prepared to do my very best.”
“I am sure of that,” said Sir Wilfrid, hastily recalling his thoughts from Damascus. “And you must have had a very difficult task.”
Mademoiselle Le Breton shrugged her shoulders.
“I knew, of course, it must be difficult. And as to the drudgery of it﻿—the dogs, and that kind of thing﻿—nothing of that sort matters to me in the least. But I cannot be humiliated before those who have become my friends, entirely because Lady Henry wished it to be so.”
“Lady Henry at first showed you every confidence?”
“After the first month or two she put everything into my hands﻿—her household, her receptions, her letters, you may almost say her whole social existence. She trusted me with all her secrets.” (“No, no, my dear lady,” thought Sir Wilfrid.) “She let me help her with all her affairs. And, honestly, I did all I could to make her life easy.”
“That I understand from herself.”
“Then why,” cried Mademoiselle Le Breton, turning round to him with sudden passion﻿—“why couldn’t Lady Henry leave things alone? Are devotion, and﻿—and the kind of qualities she wanted, so common? I said to myself that, blind and helpless as she was, she should lose nothing. Not only should her household be well kept, her affairs well managed, but her salon should be as attractive, her Wednesday evenings as brilliant, as ever. The world was deserting her; I helped her to bring it back. She cannot live without social success; yet now she hates me for what I have done. Is it sane﻿—is it reasonable?”
“She feels, I suppose,” said Sir Wilfrid, gravely, “that the success is no longer hers.”
“So she says. But will you please examine that remark? When her guests assemble, can I go to bed and leave her to grapple with them? I have proposed it often, but of course it is impossible. And if I am to be there I must behave, I suppose, like a lady, not like the housemaid. Really, Lady Henry asks too much.

===Chinese===

威尔弗里德爵士初次见到这女人的父亲是在大马士革（Damascus），那时达尔林普尔二十六岁，正开始那一系列后来使他名扬天下的东方之旅。他记得那青年光彩照人的模样；那种身心两方面的力量从他身上辐散出来，使万事都变得轻易；对平庸之辈的蔑视，以及不甘居人下的秉性。
“我想让您明白，”身边这位夫人说，“我来到亨利夫人这里时，是打定主意要竭尽全力的。”
“这我相信，”威尔弗里德爵士说，慌忙把思绪从大马士革收回来。“而您的差事一定非常难做。”
勒布雷东小姐耸了耸肩。
“我当然知道必定不容易。至于那些苦差——遛狗之类——这类事我全不放在心上。可我不能在那些人面前受辱，他们成了我的朋友，完全是亨利夫人自己愿意如此。”
“亨利夫人起初对您推心置腹？”
“头一两个月过后，她便把一切都交到我手里——家事、待客、书信，几乎可以说她整个社交生涯。她把所有秘密都放心地托付给了我。”（“未必，未必，亲爱的夫人，”威尔弗里德爵士心想。）“她让我经手她的一切事务。而且，说实话，我已竭尽所能让她的日子过得省心。”
“这个我从她本人那里知道。”
“那么为什么，”勒布雷东小姐蓦地转过身来，情绪激愤——“为什么亨利夫人就不能撒手不管呢？忠心，还有——还有她所要的那类品性，难道就那么常见吗？我对自己说，她纵然失明无助，也不该失去任何东西。不仅要家事井井，诸事妥帖，她的沙龙也要一如既往地吸引人，她的星期三晚会也要一如既往地焕发光彩。上流社会正在抛离她；是我帮她把它拉了回来。离开了社交上的成功她便活不下去；如今她却因我做成的事恨我。这算清醒——算通情理吗？”
“我想，她觉得，”威尔弗里德爵士神色庄重地说，“那成功已不再属于她了。”
“她是这么说。可劳驾您把这话掂量掂量。宾客临门时，我能自去安寝，撇下她一个人去应付吗？我提过多少回，当然，那是不可能的。而既然我必须在场，我想，我就得像个贵妇人那样行事，而不是像个女仆。说真的，亨利夫人要求得太多了。

===Original===

In my mother’s little flat in Bruges, with the two or three friends who frequented it, I was brought up in as good society and as good talk as Lady Henry has ever known.”
They were passing an electric lamp, and Sir Wilfrid, looking up, was half thrilled, half repelled by the flashing energy of the face beside him. Was ever such language on the lips of a paid companion before? His sympathy for Lady Henry revived.
“Can you really give me no clue to the﻿—to the sources of Lady Henry’s dissatisfaction?” he said, at last, rather coldly.
Mademoiselle Le Breton hesitated.
“I don’t want to make myself out a saint,” she said, at last, in another voice and with a humility which was, in truth, hardly less proud than her self-assertion. “I﻿—I was brought up in poverty, and my mother died when I was fifteen. I had to defend myself as the poor defend themselves﻿—by silence. I learned not to talk about my own affairs. I couldn’t afford to be frank, like a rich English girl. I dare say, sometimes I have concealed things which had been better made plain. They were never of any real importance, and if Lady Henry had shown any consideration﻿—”
Her voice failed her a little, evidently to her annoyance. They walked on without speaking for a few paces. “Never of any real importance?” Sir Wilfrid wondered.
Their minds apparently continued the conversation though their lips were silent, for presently Julie Le Breton said, abruptly:
“Of course I am speaking of matters where Lady Henry might have some claim to information. With regard to many of my thoughts and feelings, Lady Henry has no right whatever to my confidence.”
“She gives us fair warning,” thought Sir Wilfrid.
Aloud he said:
“It is not a question of thoughts and feelings, I understand, but of actions.”
“Like the visit to the Duncombes’?” said Mademoiselle Le Breton, impatiently. “Oh, I quite admit it﻿—that’s only one of several instances Lady Henry might have brought forward. You see, she led me to make these friendships; and now, because they annoy her, I am to break them. But she forgets. Friends are too﻿—too new in my life, too precious﻿—”
Again the voice wavered. How it thrilled and penetrated! Sir Wilfrid found himself listening for every word.
“No,” she resumed. “If it is a question of renouncing the friends I have made in her house, or going﻿—it will be going. That may as well be quite clear.”

===Chinese===

在我母亲布鲁日那间小公寓里，在常来往的两三位朋友中间，我自幼所受的交际熏陶、所听的谈吐，绝不逊于亨利夫人平生所知的任何圈子。”
两人正走过一盏电灯，威尔弗里德爵士抬眼望去，身边那张脸上迸发的神采令他半是心折，半是反感。一个受雇的女伴，唇边何曾有过这样的言谈？他对亨利夫人的同情又复苏了。
“您当真不能给我一点线索，说说——说说亨利夫人不满的缘由？”他终于开口，语气相当冷淡。
勒布雷东小姐迟疑了。
“我不想把自己扮成圣徒，”她终于说，换了一种声音，带着一种谦卑——那谦卑，说实在的，同她的自负一样傲气。“我——我在贫穷中长大，母亲去世时我十五岁。我不得不像穷人自卫那样自卫——靠沉默。我学会了不谈自己的事。我担不起坦诚，担不起像富家英国小姐那样坦诚。我承认，有时我把一些本该挑明的事瞒了下来。那些事从来没有一件真正要紧，而亨利夫人但凡有一点体谅——”
她的声音有点发颤，显然这让她自己恼火。两人默默走了几步。“从来没有一件真正要紧？”威尔弗里德爵士心里犯疑。
嘴唇虽缄默，两人的心思显然还在继续交谈，因为不久朱莉·勒布雷东便突兀地说：
“我说的当然是那些亨利夫人或许有权知情的事务。至于我的许多想法和感受，亨利夫人无权要求我推心置腹。”
“她这是先打招呼了，”威尔弗里德爵士想。
口中却说：
“据我理解，问题不在想法和感受，而在行为。”
“比如去邓库姆家（Duncombes）做客那回？”勒布雷东小姐不耐烦地说。“哦，我完全承认——那不过是亨利夫人可能举出的好几件事里的一件。您瞧，当初是她引我去结下这些交情的；如今只因这些交情惹恼了她，我就得一一斩断。可她忘了。朋友，在我生命里还太——太新鲜，太珍贵——”
声音又颤了起来。这嗓音何其动人，何其透人心扉！威尔弗里德爵士发觉自己屏息去捕捉每一个字。
“不，”她接着说，“如果非要在舍弃我于她府上结下的朋友与离开之间做一抉择——那将是离开。这一点不妨现在就说个明白。”

===Original===

Sir Wilfrid looked up.
“Let me ask you one question, mademoiselle.”
“Certainly. Whatever you like.”
“Have you ever had, have you now, any affection for Lady Henry?”
“Affection? I could have had plenty. Lady Henry is most interesting to watch. It is magnificent, the struggles she makes with her infirmities.”
Nothing could have been more agreeable than the modulation of these words, the passage of the tone from a first note of surprise to its grave and womanly close. Again, the same suggestions of veiled and vibrating feeling. Sir Wilfrid’s nascent dislike softened a little.
“After all,” he said, with gentleness, “one must make allowance for old age and weakness, mustn’t one?”
“Oh, as to that, you can’t say anything to me that I am not perpetually saying to myself,” was her somewhat impetuous reply. “Only there is a point when ill-temper becomes not only tormenting to me but degrading to herself.﻿ ﻿… Oh, if you only knew!”﻿—the speaker drew an indignant breath. “I can hardly bring myself to speak of such *misères*. But everything excites her, everything makes her jealous. It is a grievance that I should have a new dress, that Mr. Montresor should send me an order for the House of Commons, that Evelyn Crowborough should give me a Christmas present. Last Christmas, Evelyn gave me these furs﻿—she is the only creature in London from whom I would accept a farthing or the value of a farthing.”
She paused, then rapidly threw him a question:
“Why, do you suppose, did I take it from her?”
“She is your kinswoman,” said Wilfrid, quietly.
“Ah, you knew that! Well, then, mayn’t Evelyn be kind to me, though I am what I am? I reminded Lady Henry, but she only thought me a mean parasite, sponging on a duchess for presents above my station. She said things hardly to be forgiven. I was silent. But I have never ceased to wear the furs.”
With what imperious will did the thin shoulders straighten themselves under the folds of chinchilla! The cloak became symbolic, a flag not to be struck.
“I never answer back, please understand﻿—never,” she went on, hurriedly. “You saw today how Lady Henry gave me her orders. There is not a servant in the house with whom she would dare such a manner. Did I resent it?”
“You behaved with great forbearance. I watched you with admiration.”
“Ah, *forbearance*! I fear you don’t understand one of the strangest elements in the whole case.

===Chinese===

威尔弗里德爵士抬起眼来。
“请允许我问您一个问题，小姐。”
“当然。随您问什么。”
“您可曾有过——如今可还有——对亨利夫人的一点点感情？”
“感情？我本可以有很多。看亨利夫人是件极有意思的事。她同自身残缺搏斗的样子，可敬可叹。”
这几句话的抑扬顿挫再悦耳不过，语调从起初的一点讶异缓缓转入端庄温婉的收束。又是那种掩映颤动的情愫。威尔弗里德爵士初生的反感软化了几分。
“说到底，”他温和地说，“人总得体谅年迈与衰弱，不是吗？”
“哦，这个嘛，您能对我说的一切，没有一句不是我在时时刻刻对自己说的，”她的回答有几分急切。“只是火气大到某个地步，就不仅折磨我，也糟践她自己了。……唉，您要是知道就好了！”——说话的人愤然吸了口气。“这种种misères（难堪事），我几乎都难以启齿。可什么事都刺激她，什么都让她妒忌。我做件新衣是罪过，蒙特勒索先生送我一张下议院的旁听凭证是罪过，伊芙琳·克罗伯勒（Evelyn Crowborough）送我件圣诞礼物也是罪过。去年圣诞，伊芙琳送了我这身皮草——全伦敦，我只肯收她一个人的东西，哪怕一个铜板，或值一个铜板的东西。”
她顿了顿，随即抛给他一个问题：
“您猜，我为什么肯收她的？”
“她是您的亲族，”威尔弗里德平静地说。
“啊，这个您知道！那么，纵然我是我这样的身份，伊芙琳就不能对我好一点吗？我向亨利夫人提过，她却只当我是下贱的食客，攀附着公爵夫人讨要超出我身份的礼物。她说过些几乎不可原谅的话。我沉默了。可这身皮草我从未停止穿。”
那瘦削的双肩在青紫蓝（chinchilla）皮氅的褶裥下挺得笔直，透着何等倨傲的意志！那斗篷成了象征，一面绝不肯降下的旗帜。
“我从不还嘴，请您明白——从不，”她急急地接着说。“您今天亲眼见到亨利夫人是怎样对我发号施令的。这宅子里的仆人，她一个也不敢用这种态度。我可曾露过一点愠色？”
“您表现得极有涵养。我看着，心里佩服。”
“啊，涵养！恐怕您不理解这桩事里最奇怪的一点。

===Original===

I am *afraid* of Lady Henry, mortally afraid! When she speaks to me I feel like a child who puts up its hands to ward off a blow. My instinct is not merely to submit, but to grovel. When you have had the youth that I had, when you have existed, learned, amused yourself on sufferance, when you have had somehow to maintain yourself among girls who had family, friends, money, name, while you﻿—”
Her voice stopped, resolutely silenced before it broke. Sir Wilfrid uncomfortably felt that he had no sympathy to produce worthy of the claim that her whole personality seemed to make upon it. But she recovered herself immediately.
“Now I think I had better give you an outline of the last six months,” she said, turning to him. “Of course it is my side of the matter. But you have heard Lady Henry’s.”
And with great composure she laid before him an outline of the chief quarrels and grievances which had embittered the life of the Bruton Street house during the period she had named. It was a wretched story, and she clearly told it with repugnance and disgust. There was in her tone a note of offended personal delicacy, as of one bemired against her will.
Evidently, Lady Henry was hardly to be defended. The thing had been “odious,” indeed. Two women of great ability and different ages, shut up together and jarring at every point, the elder furiously jealous and exasperated by what seemed to her the affront offered to her high rank and her past ascendency by the social success of her dependant, the other defending herself, first by the arts of flattery and submission, and then, when these proved hopeless, by a social skill that at least wore many of the aspects of intrigue﻿—these were the essential elements of the situation; and, as her narrative proceeded, Sir Wilfrid admitted to himself that it was hard to see any way out of it. As to his own sympathies, he did not know what to make of them.
“No. I have been only too yielding,” said Mademoiselle Le Breton, sorely, when her tale was done. “I am ashamed when I look back on what I have borne. But now it has gone too far, and something must be done. If I go, frankly, Lady Henry will suffer.”
Sir Wilfrid looked at his companion.
“Lady Henry is well aware of it.”
“Yes,” was the calm reply, “she knows it, but she does not realize it. You see, if it comes to a rupture she will allow no half-measures.

===Chinese===

我怕亨利夫人，怕到了骨头里！她一开口，我便觉得自己像个抬起小手去挡巴掌的孩子。我的本能不单是顺从，而是伏地求饶。您若度过我那样的少年时光，若一直仰人鼻息地活着、求学、寻乐，若不得不在一群有家世、有朋友、有钱财、有名分的姑娘中间勉强撑住自己，而您却——”
话音戛然而止，在破碎之前被断然掐灭。威尔弗里德爵士不安地感到，她整个人的气性仿佛在向他索取同情，而他拿不出配得上这份索取的东西。但她立刻恢复了常态。
“现在我想，我最好把最近六个月的情形给您理个大概，”她转过身来说。“当然，这只是我的一面之词。可亨利夫人那面您已经听过了。”
她以极大的镇静，把她所说的那段日子里毒化了布鲁顿街这座宅子生活的主要口角与怨隙，向他理了个头绪。那是个不堪的故事，她讲来显然满怀厌恶与反感。她的语气里有一种洁身自好者受了玷辱的意味，仿佛一个违背己愿被溅了一身泥的人。
显然，亨利夫人几乎无从辩护。事情确实“糟透了”。两个才干出众而年龄悬殊的女人，同处一宅，处处龃龉：年长的那个狂热地妒忌，为她依附者社交上的成功视作对自己显贵门第与昔日威望的冒犯而怒不可遏；年轻的那个先是靠谄媚与顺从自卫，待这些证明无用之后，便靠一种至少在许多方面近于权术的社交手腕——这便是局面的基本要素；而且，随着她的叙述展开，威尔弗里德爵士自己也承认，很难看出有什么出路。至于他自己的同情偏向何方，他也摸不清楚。
“不，我一向退让得太过了，”故事讲完，勒布雷东小姐痛心地说。“回首我忍下的一切，我自觉羞愧。可如今已闹到这一步，总得有个了断。倘使我走，坦白说，亨利夫人要吃苦头。”
威尔弗里德爵士看看身边的女伴。
“亨利夫人对此心中有数。”
“是的，”回答平静无波，“她知道，但她并不真明白。您瞧，一旦闹到决裂，她是绝不会容许半途手段的。

===Original===

Those who stick to me will have to quarrel with her. And there will be a great many who will stick to me.”
Sir Wilfrid’s little smile was not friendly.
“It is indeed evident,” he said, “that you have thought it all out.”
Mademoiselle Le Breton did not reply. They walked on a few minutes in silence, till she said, with a suddenness and in a low tone that startled her companion:
“If Lady Henry could ever have felt that she *humbled* me, that I acknowledged myself at her mercy! But she never could. She knows that I feel myself as well born as she, that I am *not* ashamed of my parents, that my principles give me a free mind about such things.”
“Your principles?” murmured Sir Wilfrid.
“You were right,” she turned upon him with a perfectly quiet but most concentrated passion. “I have *had* to think things out. I know, of course, that the world goes with Lady Henry. Therefore I must be nameless and kinless and hold my tongue. If the world knew, it would expect me to hang my head. I *don’t*! I am as proud of my mother as of my father. I adore both their memories. Conventionalities of that kind mean nothing to me.”
“My dear lady﻿—”
“Oh, I don’t expect you or anyone else to feel with me,” said the voice which for all its low pitch was beginning to make him feel as though he were in the centre of a hailstorm. “You are a man of the world, you knew my parents, and yet I understand perfectly that for you, too, I am disgraced. So be it! So be it! I don’t quarrel with what anyone may choose to think, but﻿—”
She recaptured herself with difficulty, and there was silence. They were walking through the purple February dusk towards the Marble Arch. It was too dark to see her face under its delicate veil, and Sir Wilfrid did not wish to see it. But before he had collected his thoughts sufficiently his companion was speaking again, in a wholly different manner.
“I don’t know what made me talk in this way. It was the contact with someone, I suppose, who had seen us at Gherardtsloo.” She raised her veil, and he thought that she dashed away some tears. “That never happened to me before in London. Well, now, to return. If there is a breach﻿—”
“Why should there be a breach?” said Sir Wilfrid. “My dear Miss Le Breton, listen to me for a few minutes. I see perfectly that you have a great deal to complain of, but I also see that Lady Henry has something of a case.”

===Chinese===

凡站在我这边的，都得同她闹翻。而站在我这边的，将大有人在。”
威尔弗里德爵士脸上泛起一丝笑意，却并不友善。
“看得出，”他说，“这一切您确已盘算周全了。”
勒布雷东小姐没有答话。两人默默走了几分钟，直到她忽然开口，声音低得让身边人一惊：
“倘使亨利夫人能有一回觉得她压倒了我、觉得我自认任凭她发落，那也好！可她永远不能。她知道，我自认门第不输于她；她知道，我并不以父母为耻；她知道，我的信念让我在这类事情上心思自由。”
“您的信念？”威尔弗里德爵士喃喃道。
“您说得对，”她转过脸来对着他，语气极静，情意却浓烈到了极处。“我不得不把一切想个透亮。我当然知道，世人站在亨利夫人一边。所以我必须无名无姓、无亲无族，闭口不言。世人若知道了，便会指望我垂头丧气。我偏不！我以母亲为荣，一如以父亲为荣。我景仰他们两位的亡灵。那类世俗成规，于我一钱不值。”
“亲爱的夫人——”
“哦，我不指望您，也不指望任何人与我感同身受，”那嗓音虽说低沉，却已开始叫他觉得自己仿佛置身雹暴的中心。“您是深谙世故之人，您认识我的父母，可我完全明白，在您眼里，我也是蒙羞之人。那就这样吧！就这样吧！任何人爱怎么想，我无异议，只是——”
她好不容易才收摄住自己，随之而来的是沉默。两人正走过二月紫色的暮霭，朝大理石拱门而去。天太黑了，看不清她轻纱下的脸，威尔弗里德爵士也不想看。可他还没来得及理清思绪，身边人又开了口，神情口吻已判若两人。
“我不知道自己怎么说了这些话。大概是因为碰上了见过我们在赫拉德斯卢（Gherardtsloo）的人吧。”她掀起面纱，他觉得她拭去了几点泪。“在伦敦，这种事从前从未落到过我头上。好了，言归正传。倘若真有决裂——”
“为什么要有决裂呢？”威尔弗里德爵士说。“亲爱的勒布雷东小姐，请听我说几分钟。我完全明白，您有满腹委屈可诉；可我也看得出，亨利夫人这头并非全无道理。”

===Original===

And with a courteous authority and tact worthy of his trade, the old diplomat began to discuss the situation.
Presently he found himself talking with an animation, a friendliness, an intimacy that surprised himself. What was there in the personality beside him that seemed to win a way inside a man’s defences in spite of him? Much of what she had said had seemed to him arrogant or morbid. And yet as she listened to him, with an evident dying down of passion, an evident forlornness, he felt in her that woman’s weakness and timidity of which she had accused herself in relation to Lady Henry, and was somehow, manlike, softened and disarmed. She had been talking wildly, because no doubt she felt herself in great difficulties. But when it was his turn to talk she neither resented nor resisted what he had to say. The kinder he was, the more she yielded, almost eagerly at times, as though the thorniness of her own speech had hurt herself most, and there were behind it all a sad life, and a sad heart that only asked in truth for a little sympathy and understanding.
“I shall soon be calling her ‘my dear’ and patting her hand,” thought the old man, at last, astonished at himself. For the dejection in her attitude and gait began to weigh upon him; he felt a warm desire to sustain and comfort her. More and more thought, more and more contrivance did he throw into the straightening out of this tangle between two excitable women, not, it seemed, for Lady Henry’s sake, not, surely, for Miss Le Breton’s sake. But﻿—ah! those two poor, dead folk, who had touched his heart long ago, did he feel the hovering of their ghosts beside him in the wintry wind?
At any rate, he abounded in shrewd and fatherly advice, and Mademoiselle Le Breton listened with a most flattering meekness.
“Well, now I think we have come to an understanding,” he urged, hopefully, as they turned down Bruton Street again.
Mademoiselle Le Breton sighed.
“It is very kind of you. Oh, I will do my best. But﻿—”
She shook her head uncertainly.
“No﻿—no ‘buts,’ ” cried Sir Wilfrid, cheerfully. “Suppose, as a first step,” he smiled at his companion, “you tell Lady Henry about the bazaar?”
“By all means. She won’t let me go. But Evelyn will find someone else.”
“Oh, we’ll see about that,” said the old man, almost crossly. “If you’ll allow me I’ll try my hand.”

===Chinese===

于是，带着不愧于本行的谦和威仪与圆熟手腕，老外交家开始剖析起这桩局面来。
不多时，他发觉自己谈兴渐浓，言辞间透出一种连他自己都诧异的亲切与熨帖。身边这个人的品性里到底有什么，仿佛能不由分说地攻进一个人的心防？她方才说的许多话，在他听来本觉傲慢或乖张。可此刻她听着他讲，激愤显然渐渐平息，透出一副显然的凄惶，他在她身上感到了她自陈面对亨利夫人时那种女人的软弱与怯懦，于是，像男人常有的那样，心肠一软，戒备尽卸。她方才言语激烈，无疑是因为自觉深陷困境。而轮到他说话时，他对她说的她既不愠恼，也不抗拒。他越是和颜悦色，她越是退让顺服，有时几乎是急切地退让，仿佛她自己那些带刺的话刺得最深的是她自己，而这一切背后，是一段辛酸的生活，一颗其实只求一点点同情与理解的心。
“再这样下去，我就要唤她‘亲爱的’、拍她的手了，”老人终于想，惊异于自己。因为她神情步态里的沮丧开始压上他的心头；他感到一股热望，想扶持她、宽慰她。他为解开这两个易怒女人之间的乱麻投入了越来越多的心思、越来越多的筹谋，似乎不是为了亨利夫人，当然也不是为了勒布雷东小姐。而是——啊！那两个早已作古的可怜人，多年前曾打动过他的心，此刻，在凛冽寒风中，他可觉得他们的魂灵正萦绕在自己身旁？
总之，他的忠告源源不断，既精明又慈父般恳切，勒布雷东小姐听着，温顺得叫人受宠若惊。
“好了，我想我们总算达成谅解了，”两人再折进布鲁顿街时，他满怀希望地敦促道。
勒布雷东小姐叹了口气。
“您太好了。哦，我会尽力的。只是——”
她茫然地摇了摇头。
“不行——不许说‘只是’，”威尔弗里德爵士朗声道，满脸笑意。“比方说，作为第一步，”他朝女伴微微一笑，“您把义卖会的事亲口告诉亨利夫人，如何？”
“完全可以。她不会放我去的。不过伊芙琳会另找别人的。”
“哦，这个我们走着瞧，”老人说，几乎有些恼了。“您若容许，我来试上一手。”

===Original===

Julie Le Breton did not reply, but her face glimmered upon him with a wistful friendliness that did not escape him, even in the darkness. In this yielding mood her voice and movements had so much subdued sweetness, so much distinction, that he felt himself more than melting towards her.
Then, of a sudden, a thought﻿—a couple of thoughts﻿—sped across him. He drew himself rather sharply together.
“Mr. Delafield, I gather, has been a good deal concerned in the whole matter?”
Mademoiselle Le Breton laughed and hesitated.
“He has been very kind. He heard Lady Henry’s language once when she was excited. It seemed to shock him. He has tried once or twice to smooth her down. Oh, he has been most kind!”
“Has he any influence with her?”
“Not much.”
“Do you think well of him?”
He turned to her with a calculated abruptness. She showed a little surprise.
“I? But everybody thinks well of him. They say the Duke trusts everything to him.”
“When I left England he was still a rather lazy and unsatisfactory undergraduate. I was curious to know how he had developed. Do you know what his chief interests are now?”
Mademoiselle Le Breton hesitated.
“I’m really afraid I don’t know,” she said, at last, smiling, and, as it were, regretful. “But Evelyn Crowborough, of course, could tell you all about him. She and he are very old friends.”
“No birds out of that cover,” was Sir Wilfrid’s inward comment.
The lamp over Lady Henry’s door was already in sight when Sir Wilfrid, after some talk of the Montresors, with whom he was going to dine that night, carelessly said:
“That’s a very good-looking fellow, that Captain Warkworth, whom I saw with Lady Henry last night.”
“Ah, yes. Lady Henry has made great friends with him,” said Mademoiselle Julie, readily. “She consults him about her memoir of her husband.”
“Memoir of her husband!” Sir Wilfrid stopped short. “Heavens above! Memoir of Lord Henry?”
“She is halfway through it. I thought you knew.”
“Well, upon my word! Whom shall we have a memoir of next? Henry Delafield! Henry Delafield! Good gracious!”
And Sir Wilfrid walked along, slashing at the railings with his stick, as though the action relieved him. Julie Le Breton quietly resumed:
“I understand that Lord Henry and Captain Warkworth’s father went through the Indian Mutiny together, and Captain Warkworth has some letters﻿—”
“Oh, I dare say﻿—I dare say,” muttered Sir Wilfrid.

===Chinese===

朱莉·勒布雷东没有作声，可她的脸在黑暗中向他微微发亮，带着一种惆怅的友善，未能逃过他的眼睛。在这份顺服的情绪里，她的声音与举止蕴着那许多含蓄的温柔、那许多出众的风致，他觉得自己对她岂止是心软。
接着，倏忽间，一个念头——一对念头——掠过他的心头。他霍然收摄心神。
“我猜，德拉菲尔德先生在这整件事里卷得颇深？”
勒布雷东小姐笑了，又有些迟疑。
“他一直很好心。有一回亨利夫人激动起来，他听见了她的那些话。似乎让他很震惊。他试过一两回给她消气。哦，他好得很！”
“他对她说得上话吗？”
“说不上多少。”
“您对他印象好吗？”
他拿捏着分寸，突兀地转过脸去问她。她露出一丝诧异。
“我？人人都对他印象好呀。听说公爵把什么事都交托给他。”
“我离开英国时，他还是个相当懒散、难让人满意的本科生。我很好奇他如今长成了什么样。您知道他现在主要的志趣是什么吗？”
勒布雷东小姐迟疑了。
“真抱歉，我确实不知道，”她终于说，含着笑，又似乎带着歉意。“不过伊芙琳·克罗伯勒当然能把他的一切讲给您听。他们俩是极老的朋友。”
“这片树丛里惊不出鸟来，”威尔弗里德爵士心中暗道。
亨利夫人门前的灯已经遥遥在望，威尔弗里德爵士谈了几句蒙特勒索夫妇——当晚他要到他们家赴宴——随口说道：
“那位沃克沃思上尉真是一表人才，昨晚我在亨利夫人府上见到的那位。”
“啊，是的。亨利夫人同他交情笃厚，”朱莉小姐应声答道。“她正为写亡夫的回忆录向他讨教呢。”
“亡夫的回忆录！”威尔弗里德爵士猛地站住。“老天爷！亨利勋爵的回忆录？”
“她已写了一半。我还以为您知道呢。”
“哎呀，真没想到！下一个又该给谁作回忆录了？亨利·德拉菲尔德！亨利·德拉菲尔德！天哪！”
威尔弗里德爵士一路走去，用手杖抽打着栏杆，仿佛这动作能让他痛快些。朱莉·勒布雷东不紧不慢地接着说：
“我听说亨利勋爵同沃克沃思上尉的父亲一起经历了印度兵变（Indian Mutiny），沃克沃思上尉手里存着一些信件——”
“哦，兴许是——兴许是，”威尔弗里德爵士咕哝道。

===Original===

“What’s this man home for just now?”
“Well, I *think* Lady Henry knows,” said Mademoiselle Julie, turning to him an open look, like one who, once more, would gladly satisfy a questioner if they could. “He talks to her a great deal. But why shouldn’t he come home?”
“Because he ought to be doing disagreeable duty with his regiment instead of always racing about the world in search of something to get his name up,” said Sir Wilfrid, rather sharply. “At least, that’s the view his brother officers mostly take of him.”
“Oh,” said Mademoiselle Julie, with amiable vagueness, “is there anything particular that you suppose he wants?”
“I am not at all in the secret of his ambitions,” said Sir Wilfrid, lifting his shoulders. “But you and Lady Henry seemed well acquainted with him.”
The straw-colored lashes veered her way.
“I had some talk with him in the Park this morning,” said Julie Le Breton, reflectively. “He wants me to copy his father’s letters for Lady Henry, and to get her to return the originals as soon as possible. He feels nervous when they are out of his hands.”
“Hm!” said Sir Wilfrid.
At that moment Lady Henry’s doorbell presented itself. The vigor with which Sir Wilfrid rang it may, perhaps, have expressed the liveliness of his unspoken scepticism. He did not for one moment believe that General Warkworth’s letters had been the subject of the conversation he had witnessed that morning in the Park, nor that filial veneration had had anything whatever to say to it.
Julie Le Breton gave him her hand.
“Thank you very much,” she said, gravely and softly.
Sir Wilfrid at the moment before had not meant to press it at all. But he did press it, aware the while of the most mingled feelings.
“On the contrary, you were very good to allow me this conversation. Command me at any time if I can be useful to you and Lady Henry.”
Julie Le Breton smiled upon him and was gone.
Sir Wilfrid ran down the steps, chafing at himself.
“She somehow gets round one,” he thought, with a touch of annoyance. “I wonder whether I made any real impression upon her. Hm! Let’s see whether Montresor can throw any more light upon her. He seemed to be pretty intimate. Her ‘principles,’ eh? A dangerous view to take, for a woman of that *provenance*.”
---
An hour or two later Sir Wilfrid Bury presented himself in the Montresors’ drawing-room in Eaton Place.

===Chinese===

“这个人眼下回国干什么来了？”
“嗯，我想亨利夫人是知道的，”朱莉小姐说，转向他，一脸坦然，像那种——又是那样——只要办得到便乐于让提问者满意的人。“他同她谈得很多。可他为什么就不能回国呢？”
“因为他该留在团里干那些不痛快的差事，而不是总满世界乱跑，找机会给自己扬名，”威尔弗里德爵士话说得相当尖刻。“至少，他团里的同僚多半是这么看他的。”
“哦，”朱莉小姐说，一副客客气气的含糊，“那您以为他别有什么特别的图谋么？”
“他的雄心抱负我全不知情，”威尔弗里德爵士耸耸肩。“不过您和亨利夫人跟他倒像是熟得很。”
那淡黄的睫毛转了向她那边。
“今天上午我在公园里同他谈了一会儿，”朱莉·勒布雷东若有所思地说。“他想让我替亨利夫人抄录他父亲的信件，并设法让她尽早归还原件。信件一离手，他就心里发慌。”
“哼！”威尔弗里德爵士应了一声。
正说着，亨利夫人家的门铃到了眼前。威尔弗里德爵士按铃那股狠劲，或许正表达了他心中无言的怀疑之强烈。他一刻也不曾相信，沃克沃思将军的信件会是他清晨在公园里亲眼所见那场谈话的主题，更不相信那里面掺着半分孝心。
朱莉·勒布雷东向他伸出手来。
“多谢您，”她说，庄重而轻柔。
前一瞬间，威尔弗里德爵士原不打算握它的。可他还是握了，同时还意识到自己百感交集。
“哪里，承蒙您容我作了这番交谈，是您厚道。但凡我对您和亨利夫人能有所帮助，请随时吩咐。”
朱莉·勒布雷东向他嫣然一笑，转身进去了。
威尔弗里德爵士跑下台阶，直怪自己不争气。
“她总有办法把人笼络过去，”他想，有几分恼意。“不知我那些话她可真听进去了。哼！倒要看看蒙特勒索能不能再多透露些她的底细。他跟她似乎过从甚密。她的那套‘信念’，嗯？对那样provenance（出身来历）的女子来说，这可是个危险的观念。”

一两个小时后，威尔弗里德·伯里爵士出现在伊顿广场（Eaton Place）蒙特勒索家的客厅里。

===Original===

He had come home feeling it essential to impress upon the cabinet a certain line of action with regard to the policy of Russia on the Persian Gulf. But the first person he perceived on the hearthrug, basking before the Minister’s ample fire, was Lord Lackington. The sight of that vivacious countenance, that shock of white hair, that tall form still boasting the spareness and almost the straightness of youth, that unsuspecting complacency, confused his ideas and made him somehow feel the whole world a little topsy-turvy.
Nevertheless, after dinner he got his fifteen minutes of private talk with his host, and conscientiously made use of them. Then, after an appointment had been settled for a longer conversation on another day, both men felt that they had done their duty, and, as it appeared, the same subject stirred in both their minds.
“Well, and what did you think of Lady Henry?” said Montresor, with a smile, as he lighted another cigarette.
“She’s very blind,” said Sir Wilfrid, “and more rheumatic. But else there’s not much change. On the whole she wears wonderfully well.”
“Except as to her temper, poor lady!” laughed the Minister. “She has really tried all our nerves of late. And the worst of it is that most of it falls upon that poor woman who lives with her”﻿—the Minister lowered his voice﻿—“one of the most interesting and agreeable creatures in the world.”
Sir Wilfrid glanced across the table. Lord Lackington was telling scandalous tales of his youth to a couple of Foreign Office clerks, who sat on either side of him, laughing and spurring him on. The old man’s careless fluency and fun were evidently contagious; animation reigned around him; he was the spoiled child of the dinner, and knew it.
“I gather that you have taken a friendly interest in Miss Le Breton,” said Bury, turning to his host.
“Oh, the Duchess and Delafield and I have done our best to protect her, and to keep the peace. I am quite sure Lady Henry has poured out her grievances to you, hasn’t she?”
“Alack, she has!”
“I knew she couldn’t hold her tongue to you, even for a day. She has really been losing her head over it. And it is a thousand pities.”
“So you think all the fault’s on Lady Henry’s side?”
The Minister gave a shrug.
“At any rate, I have never myself seen anything to justify Lady Henry’s state of feeling.

===Chinese===

他回国时本觉得，务必要让内阁记住一条关于俄国在波斯湾政策的行动路线。可在炉边地毯上、就着部长先生那炉旺火取暖的头一个人，偏偏是拉金顿勋爵。一看到那张生气勃勃的脸、那头蓬乱的白发、那副依然以清瘦甚至近乎挺直见夸的高挑身量、那份毫无戒心的自得，他的思绪便乱了，只觉得整个世界多少有些颠倒。
不过，晚饭后他还是争取到与主人单独密谈的一刻钟，并且本本分分地用足了它。待约定了他日再作长谈，两人都觉得已尽了职责；而看样子，同一桩心事也在两人心里同时翻涌起来。
“对了，你觉得亨利夫人如何？”蒙特勒索又点上一支烟，含笑问道。
“眼睛更坏了，”威尔弗里德爵士说，“风湿也更重了。除此之外倒没怎么变。总的说来，她保养老到，实在难得。”
“只是那脾气除外，可怜的夫人！”部长笑道。“近来她实在把我们每个人的神经都考验遍了。最糟的是，这些多半都落在了同她住在一起的可怜女人身上”——部长压低了声音——“那可是世上最有趣、最讨人喜欢的女子之一。”
威尔弗里德爵士朝桌子那头瞥了一眼。拉金顿勋爵正给坐在他两侧的两名外交部（Foreign Office）职员讲自己年轻时的荒唐故事，两人边听边笑，还怂恿他讲下去。老人那漫不经心的口才与谐趣显然富有感染力；他周围笑语喧阗；他是这顿晚宴的宠儿，而且他自己也知道。
“我猜，您对勒布雷东小姐颇有好意的关照，”伯里转向主人说。
“哦，公爵夫人、德拉菲尔德，还有我，都尽力护着她，维持和局。亨利夫人肯定把满腹苦水都倒给您了，是不是？”
“唉，可不嘛！”
“我就知道，她对着您连一天也憋不住。这件事她真要失态了。可惜，太可惜了。”
“这么说，您认为错全在亨利夫人一方？”
部长耸了耸肩。
“无论如何，我自己从未见过任何能证明亨利夫人这般心绪有理的事。

===Original===

On the famous Wednesdays, Mademoiselle Julie always appears to make Lady Henry her first thought. And in other ways she has really worn herself to death for the old lady. It makes one rather savage sometimes to see it.”
“So in your eyes she is a perfect companion?”
Montresor laughed.
“Oh, as to perfection﻿—”
“Lady Henry accuses her of intrigue. You have seen no traces of it?”
The Minister smiled a little oddly.
“Not as regards Lady Henry. Oh, Mademoiselle Julie is a very astute lady.”
A ripple from some source of secret amusement spread over the dark-lined face.
“What do you mean by that?”
“She knows how to help her friends better than most people. I have known three men, at least, *made* by Mademoiselle Le Breton within the last two or three years. She has just got a fresh one in tow.”
Sir Wilfrid moved a little closer to his host. They turned slightly from the table and seemed to talk into their cigars.
“Young Warkworth?” said Bury.
The Minister smiled again and hesitated.
“Oh, she doesn’t bother me, she is much too clever. But she gets at me in the most amusing, indirect ways. I know perfectly well when she has been at work. There are two or three men﻿—high up, you understand﻿—who frequent Lady Henry’s evenings, and who are her very good friends.﻿ ﻿… Oh, I dare say she’ll get what she wants,” he added, with nonchalance.
“Between you and me, do you suspect any direct interest in the young man?”
Montresor shrugged his shoulders.
“I don’t know. Not necessarily. She loves to feel herself a power﻿—all the more, I think, because of her anomalous position. It is very curious﻿—at bottom very feminine and amusing﻿—and quite harmless.”
“You and others don’t resent it?”
“No, not from her,” said the Minister, after a pause. “But she is rather going it, just now. Three or four batteries have opened upon me at once. She must be thinking of little else.”
Sir Wilfrid grew a trifle red. He remembered the comedy of the doorstep. “Is there anything that he particularly wants?” His tone assumed a certain asperity.
“Well, as for me, I cannot help feeling that Lady Henry has something to say for herself. It is very strange﻿—mysterious even﻿—the kind of ascendency this lady has obtained for herself in so short a time.”
“Oh, I dare say it’s hard for Lady Henry to put up with,” mused Montresor.

逢到那些有名的星期三，朱莉小姐总是一心先想着亨利夫人。在别的方面，她也真真是为老太太熬干了心血。有时看在眼里，叫人冒火。”
“那么在您眼里，她是个无可挑剔的伴侣了？”
蒙特勒索笑了。
“哦，说到无可挑剔——”
“亨利夫人指摘她弄权使诈。您没见过蛛丝马迹？”
部长的笑意有些古怪。
“就亨利夫人而论，没有。哦，朱莉小姐是位非常精明的主儿。”
一丝暗自得趣的涟漪掠过那张线条深沉的脸。
“这话什么意思？”
“她比大多数人更懂得怎样帮朋友的忙。这两三年里，据我所知，至少有三个男人是勒布雷东小姐一手成全的。眼下她又刚钩上一个新主。”
威尔弗里德爵士朝主人这边挪近了些。两人微微侧身避开桌子，像是各自对着雪茄说话。
“年轻的沃克沃思？”伯里说。
部长又是微微一笑，有些迟疑。
“哦，她不来烦我，她聪明得很。可她会用最有趣、最迂回的路子打动我。她什么时候在暗中使力，我知道得一清二楚。有两三位——身居高位的，您明白——常来亨利夫人晚会的先生，都是她的至交。……哦，我说得准，她想要什么就会到手，”他补充道，满不在乎。
“咱俩私下说说，您疑心她对那年轻人有什么直接的兴味吗？”
蒙特勒索耸了耸肩。
“说不准。未必。她喜欢感到自己是个有势力的人——我想，正因她身份暧昧，这份喜好反而更甚。很有意思——骨子里十足是女人脾气，挺逗人——而且全然无害。”
“您和其他各位不反感？”
“不，出之于她，倒不，”部长顿了顿说。“只是眼下她闹得有些凶。三四门大炮同时冲着我开了火。她心里想必装不下别的了。”
威尔弗里德爵士脸微微一红。他记起了门前台阶上的那出戏。“他有什么特别想要的东西吗？”他的语气带上了几分尖利。
“这个嘛，说我自己，我不由得觉得亨利夫人也有她可自陈的道理。这位女士能在这样短的时日里为自己挣下这样一种威望，实在蹊跷——简直神秘。”
“哦，我说得准，亨利夫人很难咽下这口气，”蒙特勒索若有所思地说。

===Original===

“Without family, without connections﻿—”
He raised his head quietly and put on his eyeglasses. Then his look swept the face of his companion.
Sir Wilfrid, with a scarcely perceptible yet significant gesture, motioned towards Lord Lackington. Mr. Montresor started. The eyes of both men travelled across the table, then met again.
“You know?” said Montresor, under his breath.
Sir Wilfrid nodded. Then some instinct told him that he had now exhausted the number of the initiated.
---
When the men reached the drawing-room, which was rather emptily waiting for the “reception” Mrs. Montresor was about to hold in it, Sir Wilfrid fell into conversation with Lord Lackington. The old man talked well, though flightily, with a constant reference of all topics to his own standards, recollections, and friendships, which was characteristic, but in him not unattractive. Sir Wilfrid noticed certain new and pitiful signs of age. The old man was still a rattle. But every now and then the rattle ceased abruptly and a breath of melancholy made itself felt﻿—like a chill and sudden gust from some unknown sea.
They were joined presently, as the room filled up, by a young journalist﻿—an art critic, who seemed to know Lord Lackington and his ways. The two fell eagerly into talk about pictures, especially of an exhibition at Antwerp, from which the young man had just returned.
“I looked in at Bruges on the way back for a few hours,” said the newcomer, presently. “The pictures there are much better seen than they used to be. When were you there last?” He turned to Lord Lackington.
“Bruges?” said Lord Lackington, with a start. “Oh, I haven’t been there for twenty years.”
And he suddenly sat down, dangling a paper-knife between his hands, and staring at the carpet. His jaw dropped a little. A cloud seemed to interpose between him and his companions.
Sir Wilfrid, with Lady Henry’s story fresh in his memory, was somehow poignantly conscious of the old man. Did their two minds hold the same image﻿—of Lady Rose drawing her last breath in some dingy room beside one of the canals that wind through Bruges, laying down there the last relics of that life, beauty, and intelligence that had once made her the darling of the father, who, for some reason still hard to understand, had let her suffer and die alone?

“既没有家世，又没有裙带——”
他静静抬起头，戴上单片眼镜。目光随即扫过对面人的脸。
威尔弗里德爵士做一个几乎难以察觉却意味深长的手势，朝拉金顿勋爵那边示意。蒙特勒索先生一怔。两人的目光越过桌面投向那边，随后又彼此相接。
“您知道？”蒙特勒索低声道。
威尔弗里德爵士点了点头。随即某种直觉告诉他，知情人到此为止了。

男宾们回到客厅时，那屋子正空落落地等着蒙特勒索夫人即将在此举行的“招待会”。威尔弗里德爵士同拉金顿勋爵攀谈起来。老人谈吐不俗，只是天马行空，什么话题都要拉回他自己的标准、回忆与交游上去，这是他的本色，但在他身上倒不惹人厌。威尔弗里德爵士注意到某些新添的、令人心酸的老态。老人仍旧健谈得像只响板。可那响板时时戛然而止，一阵忧郁之气便悄然而至——像某片陌生海上吹来的一股又冷又骤的风。
随着客厅渐满，一位年轻记者也加入了他们——一位艺术评论家，似乎认得拉金顿勋爵，也熟他的脾性。两人热切地谈起绘画，尤其谈到安特卫普（Antwerp）的一个画展，年轻人刚从那里回来。
“回程时我顺路去布鲁日看了几个钟头，”新来的人过了一会儿说。“那边的画现在比从前可看得清楚多了。您上回去是什么时候？”他转向拉金顿勋爵。
“布鲁日？”拉金顿勋爵一怔。“哦，我有二十年没去过了。”
他忽然坐了下去，两手间摆弄着一把裁纸刀，眼睛盯着地毯。下巴微微松垂。一片阴云仿佛隔在了他与众人之间。
亨利夫人的故事犹自在心头，威尔弗里德爵士对老人的感知竟隐隐作痛。他们两人的脑海里，可悬着同一幅图景——罗斯小姐在布鲁日蜿蜒河道旁某间阴暗的屋子里咽下最后一口气，在那里放下一生最后的遗存：那曾经使她成为父亲掌上明珠的生命、美貌与才智；而那位父亲，出于某种至今仍难索解的缘由，竟任她独自受苦、独自死去？

## V / 第五章
===Original===

On leaving the Montresors, Sir Wilfrid, seeing that it was a fine night with mild breezes abroad, refused a hansom, and set out to walk home to his rooms in Duke Street, St. James’s. He was so much in love with the mere streets, the mere clatter of the omnibuses and shimmer of the lamps, after his long absence, that every step was pleasure. At the top of Grosvenor Place he stood still awhile only to snuff up the soft, rainy air, or to delight his eye now with the shining pools which some showers of the afternoon had left behind them on the pavement, and now with the light veil of fog which closed in the distance of Piccadilly.
“And there are silly persons who grumble about the fogs!” he thought, contemptuously, while he was thus yielding himself heart and sense to his beloved London.
As for him, dried and wilted by long years of cloudless heat, he drank up the moisture and the mist with a kind of physical passion﻿—the noises and the lights no less. And when he had resumed his walk along the crowded street, the question buzzed within him, whether he must indeed go back to his exile, either at Teheran, or nearer home, in some more exalted post? “I’ve got plenty of money; why the deuce don’t I give it up, and come home and enjoy myself? Only a few more years, after all; why not spend them here, in one’s own world, among one’s own kind?”
It was the weariness of the governing Englishman, and it was answered immediately by that other instinct, partly physical, partly moral, which keeps the elderly man of affairs to his task. Idleness? No! That way lies the end. To slacken the rush of life, for men of his sort, is to call on death﻿—death, the secret pursuer, who is not far from each one of us. No, no! Fight on! It was only the long drudgery behind, under alien suns, together with the iron certainty of fresh drudgery ahead, that gave value, after all, to this rainy, this enchanting Piccadilly﻿—that kept the string of feeling taut and all its notes clear.
“Going to bed, Sir Wilfrid?” said a voice behind him, as he turned down St. James’s Street.
“Delafield!” The old man faced round with alacrity. “Where have you sprung from?”
Delafield explained that he had been dining with the Crowboroughs, and was now going to his club to look for news of a friend’s success or failure in a north-country election.
“Oh, that’ll keep!” said Sir Wilfrid.

===Chinese===

离开蒙特雷索家时，威尔弗里德爵士见夜色晴好，微风和煦，便谢了马车，步行回圣詹姆斯杜克街（Duke Street, St. James's）的寓所去。阔别多年之后，他实在爱煞了这寻常的街市——寻常的公共马车辘辘声、灯光的微芒——每走一步都是享受。到了格罗夫纳坊（Grosvenor Place）顶头，他伫立片刻，只为吸一口温润的带雨的空气，一忽儿欣赏午后几阵骤雨留在人行道上的亮晶晶的水洼，一忽儿又望着远处皮卡迪利（Piccadilly）合拢上来的薄薄雾霭。

"居然还有蠢人对这雾发牢骚！"他一边把身心整个交付给他钟爱的伦敦，一边鄙夷地想。

至于他自己，被长年无云的烈日烤得干透、蔫透了，此刻带着一种近乎肉体上的渴慕，把湿气与雾霭一饮而尽——种种声响与灯火，也无异于此。待他重新汇入熙攘的街道，一个问题在心里嗡嗡作响：他当真还得回到流放地去么——不是在德黑兰（Teheran），就是离家近些，再任一个更显要的职位？"我的钱够花一辈子了；见鬼，我为什么不撒手不干，回老家来享享清福？横竖只剩没几年了，为什么不待在这儿，待在自己的世界里，待在自己人中间？"

这是治国理政的英国人特有的倦怠；而它立刻被另一种本能顶了回去——那本能一半是体力的，一半是道义的，正是它把上了年纪的干员拴在职守上。闲居？不！那条路的尽头便是终结。对他这种人来说，放慢生活的奔涌，无异于把死亡招来——死亡，那个秘密的追猎者，离我们每个人都不远。不，不！接着战斗！说到底，正是因为身后异邦烈日下漫长的苦役，加上前方新苦役铁一般的必然，才给这落雨的、这迷人的皮卡迪利赋予了价值——才让感受之弦绷得紧、音色清亮。

"这就去睡了，威尔弗里德爵士？"他拐进圣詹姆斯街时，身后有个声音说。

"德拉菲尔德！"老人欣然回过身来。"你从哪儿冒出来的？"

德拉菲尔德解释说，他在克劳伯勒家吃了晚饭，现在正要去俱乐部打听一位朋友在北方一场选举中成败的消息。

"哦，那个不急！"威尔弗里德爵士说。

===Original===

“Turn in with me for half an hour. I’m at my old rooms, you know, in Duke Street.”
“All right,” said the young man, after what seemed to Sir Wilfrid a moment of hesitation.
“Are you often up in town this way?” asked Bury, as they walked on. “Land agency seems to be a profession with mitigations.”
“There is some London business thrown in. We have some large milk depots in town that I look after.”
There was just a trace of hurry in the young man’s voice, and Bury surveyed him with a smile.
“No other attractions, eh?”
“Not that I know of. By-the-way, Sir Wilfrid, I never asked you how Dick Mason was getting on?”
“Dick Mason? Is he a friend of yours?”
“Well, we were at Eton and Oxford together.”
“Were you? I never heard him mention your name.”
The young man laughed.
“I don’t mean to suggest he couldn’t live without me. You’ve left him in charge, haven’t you, at Teheran?”
“Yes, I have﻿—worse luck. So you’re deeply interested in Dick Mason?”
“Oh, come﻿—I liked him pretty well.”
“Hm﻿—I don’t much care about him. And I don’t somehow believe you do.”
And Bury, with a smile, slipped a friendly hand within the arm of his companion.
Delafield reddened.
“It’s decent, I suppose, to inquire after an old schoolfellow?”
“Exemplary. But﻿—there are things more amusing to talk about.”
Delafield was silent. Sir Wilfrid’s fair mustaches approached his ear.
“I had my interview with Mademoiselle Julie.”
“So I suppose. I hope you did some good.”
“I doubt it. Jacob, between ourselves, the little Duchess hasn’t been a miracle of wisdom.”
“No﻿—perhaps not,” said the other, unwillingly.
“She realizes, I suppose, that they are connected?”
“Of course. It isn’t very close. Lady Rose’s brother married Evelyn’s aunt, her mother’s sister.”
“Yes, that’s it. She and Mademoiselle Julie *ought* to have called the same person uncle; but, for lack of certain ceremonies, they don’t. By-the-way, what became of Lady Rose’s younger sister?”
“Lady Blanche? Oh, she married Sir John Moffatt, and has been a widow for years. He left her a place in Westmoreland, and she lives there generally with her girl.”
“Has Mademoiselle Julie ever come across them?”
“No.”
“She speaks of them?”
“Yes. We can’t tell her much about them, except that the girl was presented last year, and went to a few balls in town. But neither she nor her mother cares for London.”
“Lady Blanche Moffatt﻿—Lady Blanche Moffatt?”

"陪我进去坐半个钟头。我还住老地方，你知道的，杜克街。"

"捎带着有些伦敦的事务。城里几家大牛奶场归我照管。"

年轻人的话音里恰好有一丝匆遽，伯里含笑打量着他。

"就没有别的吸引你的去处了，嗯？"

"据我所知没有。对了，威尔弗里德爵士，我一直没问您，迪克·梅森（Dick Mason）近况如何？"

"迪克·梅森？他是你的朋友？"

"嗯，我们一同上的伊顿和牛津。"

"是吗？我可从没听他提起过你的名字。"

年轻人笑了。

"我不是说他离了我就活不成。您把他留在德黑兰主持事务了，是吧？"

"是的——真倒霉。这么说，你对迪克·梅森深为关切？"

"哦，得了——我挺喜欢他的。"

"哼——我不大喜欢他。而且我也不大相信你喜欢他。"

伯里边说边含笑把一只亲热的手臂挽进同伴的臂弯。

德拉菲尔德涨红了脸。

"打听一下老同学的近况，总还算体面事吧？"

"堪称楷模。不过——还有更有意思的事可谈。"

德拉菲尔德不言语了。威尔弗里德爵士那两撇浅色的小胡子凑近他的耳朵。

"我跟朱莉小姐谈过了。"

"我猜也是。但愿您谈出了点好处。"

"我拿不准。雅各布，咱们私下说，那位小小的公爵夫人可没显出什么智慧的神迹。"

"是——也许没有。"对方不情愿地说。

"她总该知道她们俩是亲戚吧？"

"当然。不算太近。罗斯小姐的哥哥娶了伊芙琳的姨妈，她母亲的姐姐。"

"对，就是这么回事。她跟朱莉小姐本该管同一个人叫舅舅；只是因为缺了几道仪式，才没能叫成。对了，罗斯小姐的妹妹后来怎么样了？"

"布兰奇小姐？哦，她嫁了约翰·莫法特爵士（Sir John Moffatt），守寡好些年了。他给她留了西摩兰（Westmoreland）的一处宅子，她一般带着女儿住在那儿。"

"朱莉小姐跟她们有过往来吗？"

"没有。"

"她提起过她们？"

"提过。关于她们我们也说不上什么，只知道那姑娘去年进了宫，在城里赴了几场舞会。不过她跟她母亲都不喜欢伦敦。"

"布兰奇·莫法特夫人——布兰奇·莫法特夫人？"

===Original===

said Sir Wilfrid, pausing. “Wasn’t she in India this winter?”
“Yes. I believe they went out in November and are to be home by April.”
“Somebody told me they had met her and the girl at Peshawar and then at Simla,” said Sir Wilfrid, ruminating. “Now I remember! She’s a great heiress, isn’t she, and pretty to boot? I know! Somebody told me that fellow Warkworth had been making up to her.”
“Warkworth?” Jacob Delafield stood still a moment, and Sir Wilfrid caught a sudden contraction of the brow. “That, of course, was just a bit of Indian gossip.”
“I don’t think so,” said Sir Wilfrid, dryly. “My informants were two frontier officers﻿—I came from Egypt with them﻿—who had recently been at Peshawar; good fellows both of them, not at all given to take young ladies’ names in vain.”
Jacob made no reply. They had let themselves into the Duke Street house and were groping their way up the dim staircase to Sir Wilfrid’s rooms.
There all was light and comfort. Sir Wilfrid’s valet, much the same age as himself, hovered round his master, brought him his smoking-coat, offered Delafield cigars, and provided Sir Wilfrid, strange to say, with a large cup of tea.
“I follow Mr. Gladstone,” said Sir Wilfrid, with a sigh of luxury, as he sank into an easy-chair and extended a very neatly made pair of legs and feet to the blaze. “He seems to have slept the sleep of the just﻿—on a cup of tea at midnight﻿—through the rise and fall of cabinets. So I’m trying the receipt.”
“Does that mean that you are hankering after politics?”
“Heavens! When you come to doddering, Jacob, it’s better to dodder in the paths you know. I salute Mr. G.’s physique, that’s all. Well, now, Jacob, do you know anything about this Warkworth?”
“Warkworth?” Delafield withdrew his cigar, and seemed to choose his words a little. “Well, I know what all the world knows.”
“Hm﻿—you seemed very sure just now that he wasn’t going to marry Miss Moffatt.”
“Sure? I’m not sure of anything,” said the young man, slowly.
“Well, what I should like to know,” said Sir Wilfrid, cradling his teacup in both hands, “is, what particular interest has Mademoiselle Julie in that young soldier?”
Delafield looked into the fire.
“Has she any?”
“She seems to be moving heaven and earth to get him what he wants. By-the-way, what does he want?”
“He wants the special mission to Mokembe, as I understand,” said Delafield, after a moment.

===Chinese===

威尔弗里德爵士顿住脚说。"今年冬天她不是在印度吗？"

"是啊。我记得他们是十一月去的，四月里回。"

"有人跟我说，在白沙瓦（Peshawar）碰见过她母女俩，后来又在西姆拉（Simla），"威尔弗里德爵士沉吟道，"我想起来了！她是一大笔遗产的继承人，是不是？人还长得俏！对了！有人告诉我，沃克沃思（Warkworth）那个家伙一直在向她献殷勤。"

"沃克沃思？"雅各布·德拉菲尔德站住了一瞬，威尔弗里德爵士察觉他的眉头倏地一皱。"那当然不过是印度传来的一段闲话。"

"我看未必，"威尔弗里德爵士淡淡地说，"告诉我的人是两位驻防边疆的军官——我是同他们一道从埃及回来的——不久前刚在白沙瓦；都是好人，绝不是随口糟蹋小姐闺名的那种人。"

雅各布没有作声。两人已进了杜克街那所宅子，正摸黑走上昏暗的楼梯，去威尔弗里德爵士的寓所。

屋里灯烛明亮，一应舒适。威尔弗里德爵士的贴身男仆——年纪同他相仿——在主人身前身后张罗，给他取来吸烟服，给德拉菲尔德奉上雪茄，还端给威尔弗里德爵士——说来奇怪——一大杯茶。

"我效法格莱斯顿（Gladstone）先生，"威尔弗里德爵士说着，舒舒服服叹了口气，陷进安乐椅里，把两条收拾得干干净净的腿伸向炉火。"此公靠午夜一杯茶，在内阁的起起落落之间睡得香甜——睡得像个正人君子。所以我也来试试这方子。"

"这是说你心里还惦记着政治？"

"天哪！人到了老糊涂的年纪，雅各布，还是在熟路上糊涂的好。我不过是向格莱斯顿先生的体格致敬，仅此而已。好了，雅各布，你了解这个沃克沃思的底细吗？"

"沃克沃思？"德拉菲尔德把雪茄从嘴边拿开，字句似有斟酌。"嗯，我知道的，也就是世人皆知的那点儿。"

"哼——方才你似乎很笃定，说他不会娶莫法特小姐。"

"笃定？我对什么都不笃定。"年轻人缓缓道。

"那么，我很想知道的是，"威尔弗里德爵士双手捧着茶杯说，"朱莉小姐对那个年轻军官有什么特别的兴头？"

德拉菲尔德望着炉火。

"她有吗？"

"她好像正在翻天覆地替他张罗他要的东西。对了，他要什么？"

"据我了解，他要的是去莫肯贝（Mokembe）的那个特使差事。"德拉菲尔德过了一会儿说。

===Original===

“But several other people want it too.”
“Indeed!” Sir Wilfrid nodded reflectively. “So there is to be one! Well, it’s about time. The travellers of the other European firms have been going it lately in that quarter. Jacob, your mademoiselle also is a bit of an intriguer!”
Delafield made a restless movement. “Why do you say that?”
“Well, to say the least of it, frankness is not one of her characteristics. I tried to question her about this man. I had seen them together in the Park, talking as intimates. So, when our conversation had reached a friendly stage, I threw out a feeler or two, just to satisfy myself about her. But﻿—”
He pulled his fair mustaches and smiled.
“Well?” said the young man, with a kind of reluctant interrogation.
“She played with me, Jacob. But really she overdid it. For such a clever woman, I assure you, she overdid it!”
“I don’t see why she shouldn’t keep her friendships to herself,” said Delafield, with sudden heat.
“Oh, so you admit it is a friendship?”
Delafield did not reply. He had laid down his cigar, and with his hands on his knees was looking steadily into the fire. His attitude, however, was not one of reverie, but rather of a strained listening.
“What is the meaning, Jacob, of a young woman taking so keen an interest in the fortunes of a dashing soldier﻿—for, between you and me, I hear she is moving heaven and earth to get him this post﻿—and then concealing it?”
“Why should she want her kindnesses talked of?” said the young man, impetuously. “She was perfectly right, I think, to fence with your questions, Sir Wilfrid. It’s one of the secrets of her influence that she can render a service﻿—and keep it dark.”
Sir Wilfrid shook his head.
“She overdid it,” he repeated. “However, what do you think of the man yourself, Jacob?”
“Well, I don’t take to him,” said the other, unwillingly. “He isn’t my sort of man.”
“And Mademoiselle Julie﻿—you think nothing but well of her? I don’t like discussing a lady; but, you see, with Lady Henry to manage, one must feel the ground as one can.”
Sir Wilfrid looked at his companion, and then stretched his legs a little farther towards the fire. The lamplight shone full on his silky eyelashes and beard, on his neatly parted hair, and the diamond on his fine left hand. The young man beside him could not emulate his easy composure.

===Chinese===

"可还有好几个人也在打它的主意。"

"是吗！"威尔弗里德爵士若有所思地点头。"这么说真要有这么一桩使命了！嗯，也到时候了。欧洲别家商行的旅行商们近来在那一带跑得很欢呢。雅各布，你那位小姐也有几分耍手段的本事！"

德拉菲尔德不安地动了一下。"您为什么这么说？"

"嗯，往轻里说，坦白可算不上她的长处。我想就这个人探探她的话。我看见过他们俩一起在公园里，谈得跟老相识似的。所以，等我们的谈话到了融洽的火候，我便旁敲侧击了一两句，想探个明白。可是——"

他捋着浅色的小胡子笑了。

"怎样？"年轻人问，语气里带着几分勉强的诘问。

"她把我耍了，雅各布。不过说实话，她也耍过头了。她那么聪明的女人，我向你担保，是耍过头了！"

"我不明白，她自己的交情为什么不能自己藏着。"德拉菲尔德突然激动起来。

"哦，这么说你承认那是一段交情了？"

德拉菲尔德没有回答。他放下雪茄，双手按膝，目不转睛地盯着炉火。不过那姿态并非出神，倒像是在紧张地谛听。

"一个年轻女子，对一位英武军官的前程如此热切——告诉你吧，我听说她正翻天覆地替他谋这个职位——却又把它遮掩起来，雅各布，这作何解释？"

"她凭什么要让人把她的好处处处挂在嘴上？"年轻人急切地说，"我觉得，她跟您的问话打太极，完全是对的。她能帮人一把——又不声张——这正是她有影响力的一大诀窍。"

威尔弗里德爵士摇摇头。

"她耍过头了，"他重复道，"不过，你自己觉得这个人怎么样，雅各布？"

"呃，我不喜欢他，"对方不情愿地说，"他不是我喜欢的那类人。"

"那么朱莉小姐——你认为她白璧无瑕喽？我不愿意议论一位女士；可是你知道，还要对付亨利夫人，总得尽量摸清底细呀。"

威尔弗里德爵士望了望同伴，又把腿朝炉火伸得更开了些。灯光正照在他丝缎般的睫毛和胡须上，照在他精心分缝的头发上，照在他那只好看的左手的钻石戒指上。旁边的年轻人却学不来他这份从容。

===Original===

He fidgeted nervously as he replied, with warmth:
“I think she has had an uncommonly hard time, that she wants nothing but what is reasonable, and that if she threw you off the scent, Sir Wilfrid, with regard to Warkworth, she was quite within her rights. You probably deserved it.”
He threw up his head with a quick gesture of challenge. Sir Wilfrid shrugged his shoulders.
“I vow I didn’t,” he murmured. “However, that’s all right. What do you do with yourself down in Essex, Jacob?”
The lines of the young man’s attitude showed a sudden unconscious relief from tension. He threw himself back in his chair.
“Well, it’s a big estate. There’s plenty to do.”
“You live by yourself?”
“Yes. There’s an agent’s house﻿—a small one﻿—in one of the villages.”
“How do you amuse yourself? Plenty of shooting, I suppose?”
“Too much. I can’t do with more than a certain amount.”
“Golfing?”
“Oh yes,” said the young man, indifferently. “There’s a fair links.”
“Do you do any philanthropy, Jacob?”
“I like ‘bossing’ the village,” said Delafield, with a laugh. “It pleases one’s vanity. That’s about all there is to it.”
“What, clubs and temperance, that kind of thing? Can you take any real interest in the people?”
Delafield hesitated.
“Well, yes,” he said, at last, as though he grudged the admission. “There’s nothing else to take an interest in, is there? By-the-way”﻿—he jumped up﻿—“I think I’ll bid you good night, for I’ve got to go down tomorrow in a hurry. I must be off by the first train in the morning.”
“What’s the matter?”
“Oh, it’s only a wretched old man﻿—that two beasts of women have put into the workhouse infirmary against his will. I only heard it tonight. I must go and get him out.”
He looked round for his gloves and stick.
“Why shouldn’t he be there?”
“Because it’s an infernal shame!” said the other, shortly. “He’s an old laborer who’d saved quite a lot of money. He kept it in his cottage, and the other day it was all stolen by a tramp. He has lived with these two women﻿—his sister-in-law and her daughter﻿—for years and years. As long as he had money to leave, nothing was too good for him. The shock half killed him, and now that he’s a pauper these two harpies will have nothing to say to nursing him and looking after him. He told me the other day he thought they’d force him into the infirmary. I didn’t believe it. But while I’ve been away they’ve gone and done it.”

===Chinese===

他一边不安地挪动身子，一边热切地答道：

"我觉得她受的苦格外多，她要的没有一样出格；她要是把您从沃克沃思这条线上岔开了，威尔弗里德爵士，那也完全是她的权利。多半是您自找的。"

他猛一扬头，是一副挑战的姿态。威尔弗里德爵士耸了耸肩。

"我发誓不是这样，"他低声说，"不过算了，没什么。你在埃塞克斯乡下都做些什么，雅各布？"

年轻人身姿的线条忽然显出一种不自觉的松弛。他往后一靠，仰进椅子里。

"嗯，那是份大家业。要做的事多着呢。"

"一个人过？"

"是。各村里有一处经纪人的住宅——不大的一所。"

"怎么消遣？打猎的场次想必管够？"

"太多了。超过一定分量我就受不了。"

"打高尔夫？"

"哦，是。"年轻人淡然说，"有一块还不错的球场。"

"做些慈善事业吗，雅各布？"

"我喜欢'统管'村子，"德拉菲尔德笑道，"满足虚荣心。差不多就为这个。"

"什么，俱乐部啦、戒酒运动啦，那一套？你对那些人真能上心？"

德拉菲尔德迟疑了。

"嗯，能。"他终于说，仿佛这承认是勉强给出去的。"也没有别的可上心的了，是不是？对了"——他跳起来——"我想我该道晚安了，明天得赶回乡下去。明早头班车就得走。"

"出什么事了？"

"哦，不过是一个可怜的老人——让两个畜生似的女人违背他的意愿送进了济贫院病房。我今晚才听说。我得去把他弄出来。"

他环顾四周找手套和手杖。

"他凭什么不能待在那儿？"

"因为那是伤天害理！"对方没好气地说。"他是个老雇工，攒下了相当一笔钱，藏在自家小屋里，前几天叫一个流浪汉偷了个精光。他多年来一直跟这两个女人过活——他兄弟的媳妇和她的女儿。只要他还有钱可留，她们待他好得不能再好。失窃的打击差点要了他的命；如今他成了穷光蛋，这两只母夜叉就再不肯照看他、伺候他了。前几天他还对我说，他怕她们会硬把他送进济贫院。我当时不信。可我出门这几天，她们居然真干了。"

“Well, what’ll you do now?”
“Get him out.”
“And then?”

Sir Wilfrid rose.
“I think I’ll run down and see you some day. Will there be paupers in all the bedrooms?”
Delafield grinned.
“You’ll find a rattling good cook and a jolly snug little place, I can tell you. Do come. But I shall see you again soon. I must be up next week, and very likely I shall be at Lady Henry’s on Wednesday.”
“All right. I shall see her on Sunday, so I can report.”
“Not before Sunday?” Delafield paused. His clear blue eyes looked down, dissatisfied, upon Sir Wilfrid.
“Impossible before. I have all sorts of official people to see tomorrow and Saturday. And, Jacob, keep the Duchess quiet. She may have to give up Mademoiselle Julie for her bazaar.”
“I’ll tell her.”
“By-the-way, is that little person happy?” said Sir Wilfrid, as he opened the door to his departing guest. “When I left England she was only just married.”
“Oh yes, she’s happy enough, though Crowborough’s rather an ass.”

Delafield smiled.
“Well, he’s rather a sticky sort of person. He thinks there’s something particularly interesting in dukes, which makes him a bore.”
“Take care, Jacob! Who knows that you won’t be a duke yourself some day?”
“What *do* you mean?” The young man glowered almost fiercely upon his old friend.

“No, no. Sunday, certainly﻿—honor bright. Oh, I think we shall straighten it out.”
