我刚要——刚要收回几分主权，公爵夫人就登场了。"

"哦，这个小傻瓜！"威尔弗里德爵士低声叹道。

"她来，当然，是又求又闹的。她主动提出，种种我根本不需要的多余事情，她都愿意效劳——只要我把朱莉让给她那场荒唐的义卖会。这么一来我的火又蹿上来了，我便就她搬弄是非、把朱莉逼进一个彻头彻尾的假局面的种种，给她讲了几句不中听的实话。她一听便大发雷霆，说了朱莉一大堆糊涂浑话，倒叫我从中听出，勒布雷东小姐违背了她同我的庄严约定，把她的家史原原本本告诉了伊芙琳，还告诉了雅各布·德拉菲尔德。单凭这一条，就足够构成我辞退她的理由。不是吗？"

"哦，是啊。"威尔弗里德爵士喃喃道，"只要你想辞退她。"

"这个我们回头再说。"亨利夫人没好气地说，"请你想想，这些隐情若是再传远一步——谁知传没传？——会给我惹来什么样的难处。老拉金顿勋爵（Lord Lackington）——他对亲生女儿活着的时候那副兽行你是知道的，可他又从头到脚是个装腔作势的人——说不定哪天晚上就闯到这里来，认领他的外孙女——或者满世界造谣，说我虐待了她。一旦走漏她是罗斯小姐的孩子，天晓得会闹出什么荒唐事。常来这儿的，我一口气能数出半打人来，他们若是知道了——你我知道的这件事——准会觉得受了侮辱。"

"受侮辱？因为她母亲——"

"因为她母亲犯了第七诫？哦，才不呢！照我看，如今这档子事人们是不大理会的。他们是气自己被蒙在鼓里——仅此而已。是虚荣，不是道德。"

"据我查证，"威尔弗里德爵士沉吟道，"知道内情的，只有公爵夫人、德拉菲尔德、蒙特雷索和我。"

"蒙特雷索！"亨利夫人失声叫道，气得变了脸色，"蒙特雷索！这我可是头一回听说。哦，她得马上走——马上！"她重重地喘着气。

"稍等。你同雅各布谈过没有？"

"我才不呢！伊芙琳自然张口闭口都是他——雅各布长雅各布短的。他好像成天住在她口袋里，他们仨没日没夜地合伙算计我。

===Original===

Where Julie has found the time I can’t imagine; I thought I had kept her pretty well occupied.”
Sir Wilfrid surveyed his angry companion and held his peace.
“So you don’t know what Jacob thinks?”
“Why should I want to know?” said Lady Henry, disdainfully. “A lad whom I sent to Eton and Oxford, when his father couldn’t pay his bills﻿—what does it matter to me what he thinks?”
“Women are strange folk,” thought Sir Wilfrid. “A man wouldn’t have said that.”
Then, aloud:
“I thought you were afraid lest he should want to marry her?”
“Oh, let him cut his throat if he likes!” said Lady Henry, with the inconsistency of fury. “What does it matter to me?”
“By-the-way, as to that”﻿—he spoke as though feeling his way﻿—“have you never had suspicions in quite another direction?”
“What do you mean?”
“Well, I hear a good deal in various quarters of the trouble Mademoiselle Le Breton is taking﻿—on behalf of that young soldier who was here just now﻿—Harry Warkworth.”
Lady Henry laughed impatiently.
“I dare say. She is always wanting to patronize or influence somebody. It’s in her nature. She’s a born *intrigante*. If you knew her as well as I do, you wouldn’t think much of that. Oh no﻿—make your mind easy. It’s Jacob she wants﻿—it’s Jacob she’ll get, very likely. What can an old, blind creature like me do to stop it?”
“And as Jacob’s wife﻿—the wife perhaps of the head of the family﻿—you still mean to quarrel with her?”
“Yes, I *do* mean to quarrel with her!” and Lady Henry lifted herself in her chair, a pale and quivering image of war﻿—“Duchess or no Duchess! Did you see the audacious way in which she behaved this afternoon?﻿—*how* she absorbs my guests?﻿—how she allows and encourages a man like Montresor to forget himself?﻿—eggs him on to put slights on me in my own drawing-room!”
“No, no! You are really unjust,” said Sir Wilfrid, laying a kind hand upon her arm. “That was not her fault.”
“It *is* her fault that she is what she is!﻿—that her character is such that she *forces* comparisons between us﻿—between *her* and *me*!﻿—that she pushes herself into a prominence that is intolerable, considering who and what she is﻿—that she makes me appear in an odious light to my old friends. No, no, Wilfrid, your first instinct was the true one. I shall have to bring myself to it, whatever it costs. She must take her departure, or I shall go to pieces, morally and physically.

===Chinese===

朱莉哪儿挤出的这些工夫，我想不出来；我原以为我把她拴得够紧的了。"

威尔弗里德爵士端详着怒气冲冲的老友，默不作声。

"这么说，你不知道雅各布怎么想？"

"我干吗要知道？"亨利夫人鄙夷地说，"他父亲付不起账单的时候，是我送他去上的伊顿和牛津——他怎么想，与我何干？"

"女人真是奇怪的族类，"威尔弗里德爵士心想，"男人是说不出这种话的。"

随后他出声说：

"我还以为，你担心的是他要娶她？"

"哦，他要割喉咙就让他割去！"亨利夫人带着盛怒之下不顾前后的口气说，"与我何干！"

"对了，说到这个"——他说话的口气像在探路——"你可曾在——相当不同的另一个方向上起过疑心？"

"什么意思？"

"嗯，各处我都听到不少，说勒布雷东小姐正费大力气——为了方才来过的那个年轻军人——哈里·沃克沃思（Harry Warkworth）。"

亨利夫人不耐烦地笑了。

"多半是的。她总想着庇护谁、影响谁，天性如此。她天生就是个耍阴谋的坯子。你要像我一样了解她，就不会拿这个当回事。哦不——你放一百个心。她要的是雅各布——她到手的八成也是雅各布。像我这么个又老又瞎的东西，拦得住什么？"

"那么做了雅各布的妻子——或许还是这个家族主母的妻子——你仍打算同她作对？"

"是，我就是打算同她作对！"亨利夫人在椅子里挺起身来，一幅苍白战栗的战神像——"管她公爵夫人不公爵夫人！你没看见今天下午她那副放肆样儿？她吸走我的客人吸得多狠？她容任、怂恿蒙特雷索这样的人物忘乎所以——撺掇他当着我的面、在我自己的客厅里轻慢我！"

"不，不！你实在冤枉她了。"威尔弗里德爵士把一只手慈和地搭上她的手臂，"那不是她的过错。"

"她长成这么个人，就是她的过错！——她的品性逼着人拿我们俩相比——拿她和我相比！——她把自己推到一种叫人忍无可忍的显眼地步，想想她的出身来历！——她叫我在老朋友眼里成了个可憎的形象。不，不，威尔弗里德，你的第一直觉是对的。不管付出什么代价，我也得横下这条心。她必须走，不然我就要垮了，精神上垮，身体上也垮。

===Original===

To be in a temper like this, at my age, shortens one’s life﻿—you know that.”
“And you can’t subdue the temper?” he asked, with a queer smile.
“No, I can’t! That’s flat. She gets on my nerves, and I’m not responsible. *C’est fini.*”
“Well,” he said, slowly, “I hope you understand what it means?”
“Oh, I know she has plenty of friends!” she said, defiantly. But her old hands trembled on her knee.
“Unfortunately they were and are yours. At least,” he entreated, “don’t quarrel with everybody who may sympathize with her. Let them take what view they please. Ignore it﻿—be as magnanimous as you can.”
“On the contrary!” She was now white to the lips. “Whoever goes with her gives me up. They must choose﻿—once for all.”
“My dear friend, listen to reason.”
And, drawing his chair close to her, he argued with her for half an hour. At the end of that time her gust of passion had more or less passed away; she was, to some extent, ashamed of herself, and, as he believed, not far from tears.
“When I am gone she will think of what I have been saying,” he assured himself, and he rose to take his leave. Her look of exhaustion distressed him, and, for all her unreason, he felt himself astonishingly in sympathy with her. The age in him held out secret hands to the age in her﻿—as against encroaching and rebellious youth.
Perhaps it was the consciousness of this mood in him which at last partly appeased her.
“Well, I’ll try again. I’ll *try* to hold my tongue,” she granted him, sullenly. “But, understand, she, shan’t go to that bazaar!”
“That’s a great pity,” was his naive reply. “Nothing would put you in a better position than to give her leave.”
“I shall do nothing of the kind,” she vowed. “And now good night, Wilfrid﻿—good night. You’re a very good fellow, and if I *can* take your advice, I will.”
---
Lady Henry sat alone in her brightly lighted drawing-room for some time. She could neither read nor write nor sew, owing to her blindness, and in the reaction from her passion of the afternoon she felt herself very old and weary.
But at last the door opened and Julie Le Breton’s light step approached.
“May I read to you?” she said, gently.
Lady Henry coldly commanded the *Observer* and her knitting.
She had no sooner, however, begun to knit than her very acute sense of touch noticed something wrong with the wool she was using.

===Chinese===

这把年纪发这样的火，是折寿的——这你知道。"

"这火你就压不住？"他问，带着一丝古怪的笑。

"压不住，就是压不住！明说了吧。她刺激我的神经，我管不住自己。完了。"

"那么，"他缓缓说道，"我希望你明白这意味着什么？"

"哦，我知道她朋友多得很！"她倔强地说。可是她那双老手在膝上直抖。

"可惜那些朋友从前也是、如今也是你的。至少，"他恳求道，"别同每一个可能同情她的人闹翻。他们爱怎么看就怎么看。不理会——能宽容就尽量宽容。"

"恰恰相反！"她此刻嘴唇都白了，"谁向着她，谁就是抛下我。他们必须选择——一刀两断地选择。"

"亲爱的朋友，讲点道理。"

他把椅子拉到她身边，同她讲了半个钟头的道理。到末了，她那阵激情的风暴多少过去了；她对自己多少有些羞愧，而且——他相信——离落泪不远了。

"等我走了，她会想起我说的话。"他这样宽慰自己，随即起身告辞。她那副筋疲力尽的样子令他难受；尽管她这样不讲理，他却发现自己出奇地同她一条心。他身上的暮年向她身上的暮年暗暗伸出手去——共同对付那进逼的、犯上的青春。

或许正是察觉了他这番心绪，她终于平静了一半。

"好吧，我再试试。我试着——管住我的舌头。"她悻悻地让了步，"可是你要明白，她，休想去那场义卖会！"

"那太可惜了。"他答得天真，"再没有比准她去更能让你占上风的事了。"

"我绝不做这种事。"她斩钉截铁，"好了，晚安，威尔弗里德——晚安。你是个大好人，只要我——能听得进你的劝，我一定听。"

---

亨利夫人独自在她灯烛通明的客厅里坐了好一阵。因为眼盲，她既不能读书，也不能写字、做针线；下午那一场大怒过后，余波之中，她觉得自己又老又乏。

可门终于开了，朱莉·勒布雷东轻盈的脚步声近了。

"我给您读点什么好吗？"她轻轻说。

亨利夫人冷冷地吩咐要《观察家》（Observer）报和她的毛线活。

可是她刚织了几针，那极灵敏的手指便察觉，手上这团毛线有点不对。

===Original===

“This is not the wool I ordered,” she said, fingering it carefully. “You remember, I gave you a message about it on Thursday? What did they say about it at Winton’s?”
Julie laid down the newspaper and looked in perplexity at the ball of wool.
“I remember you gave me a message,” she faltered.
“Well, what did they say?”
“I suppose that was all they had.”
Something in the tone struck Lady Henry’s quick ears. She raised a suspicious face.
“Did you ever go to Winton’s at all?” she said, quickly.
“I am so sorry. The Duchess’s maid was going there,” said Julie, hurriedly, “and she went for me. I thought I had given her your message most carefully.”
“Hm,” said Lady Henry, slowly. “So you didn’t go to Winton’s. May I ask whether you went to Shaw’s, or to Beatson’s, or the Stores, or any of the other places for which I gave you commissions?” Her voice cut like a knife.
Julie hesitated. She had grown very white. Suddenly her face settled and steadied.
“No,” she said, calmly. “I meant to have done all your commissions. But I was persuaded by Evelyn to spend a couple of hours with her, and her maid undertook them.”
Lady Henry flushed deeply.
“So, mademoiselle, unknown to me, you spent two hours of my time amusing yourself at Crowborough House. May I ask what you were doing there?”
“I was trying to help the Duchess in her plans for the bazaar.”
“Indeed? Was anyone else there? Answer me, mademoiselle.”
Julie hesitated again, and again spoke with a kind of passionate composure.
“Yes. Mr. Delafield was there.”
“So I supposed. Allow me to assure you, mademoiselle”﻿—Lady Henry rose from her seat, leaning on her stick; surely no old face was ever more formidable, more withering﻿—“that whatever ambitions you may cherish, Jacob Delafield is not altogether the simpleton you imagine. I know him better than you. He will take some time before he really makes up his mind to marry a woman of your disposition﻿—and your history.”
Julie Le Breton also rose.
“I am afraid, Lady Henry, that here, too, you are in the dark,” she said, quietly, though her thin arm shook against her dress. “I shall not marry Mr. Delafield. But it is because﻿—I have refused him twice.”
Lady Henry gasped. She fell back into her chair, staring at her companion.
“You have﻿—refused him?”
“A month ago, and last year. It is horrid of me to say a word. But you forced me.”

===Chinese===

"这不是我订的那种毛线。"她细细捻着说，"你记得吧，星期四我给过你口信的？温顿（Winton）店里的人怎么说？"

朱莉放下报纸，困惑地望着那团毛线。

"我记得您给过我口信。"她支吾道。

"那么，他们怎么说？"

"我想那就是他们仅有的了。"

话音里有什么东西触着了亨利夫人敏锐的耳朵。她抬起一张狐疑的脸。

"你到底去没去温顿店？"她很快地问。

"真对不起。公爵夫人的贴身女仆正要去那儿。"朱莉急忙说，"是她替我去的。我原以为我把您的口信交代得清清楚楚了。"

"哼。"亨利夫人慢悠悠地说，"这么说，你没去温顿店。可否请问，肖（Shaw）家、比森（Beatson）家、商店联盟，还有我托你办的其他各家，你去了没有？"她的声音像刀子一样。

朱莉迟疑着。她的脸白得厉害。忽然，她的脸沉静下来，稳住了。

"没去。"她平静地说，"我原打算把您的事全办了的。可我让伊芙琳劝得在她那儿耽了两个钟头，她的女仆把差事包了下来。"

亨利夫人满脸涨红。

"这么说，小姐，背着我，你把我两个钟头的光阴拿去克劳伯勒府上取乐了。可否请问，你在那儿做什么来着？"

"我在帮公爵夫人筹划她的义卖会。"

"是吗？那儿还有旁人吗？回答我，小姐。"

朱莉又迟疑了一下，随即又带着那种含怒的镇定开了口。

"有。德拉菲尔德先生在。"

"我料到了。请容我告诉你，小姐"——亨利夫人拄着手杖从座位上站起来；天下老者的脸，怕再没有比这更狰狞、更教人枯萎的了——"不论你怀着什么样的痴心妄想，雅各布·德拉菲尔德可完全不是你心目中的那种傻瓜。我比你了解他。要他真下决心娶一个你这种脾性、你这种来历的女人，还得有些日子呢。"

朱莉·勒布雷东也站了起来。

"恐怕，亨利夫人，这一回您又是蒙在鼓里的。"她静静地说，尽管贴着裙子的瘦胳膊在发抖。"我不会嫁给德拉菲尔德先生。只因为——我已经回绝过他两次了。"

亨利夫人倒抽一口气，跌回椅子里，直瞪着她的女伴。

"你——回绝过他？"

"一个月以前一回，去年一回。我本不该吐露一个字。是您逼出来的。"

===Original===

Julie was now leaning, to support herself, on the back of an old French chair. Feeling and excitement had blanched her no less than Lady Henry, but her fine head and delicate form breathed a will so proud, a dignity so passionate, that Lady Henry shrank before her.
“Why did you refuse him?”
Julie shrugged her shoulders.
“That, I think, is my affair. But if﻿—I had loved him﻿—I should not have consulted your scruples, Lady Henry.”
“That’s frank,” said Lady Henry. “I like that better than anything you’ve said yet. You are aware that he *may* inherit the dukedom of Chudleigh?”
“I have several times heard you say so,” said the other, coldly.
Lady Henry looked at her long and keenly. Various things that Wilfrid Bury had said recurred to her. She thought of Captain Warkworth. She wondered.
Suddenly she held out her hand.
“I dare say you won’t take it, mademoiselle. I suppose I’ve been insulting you. But﻿—you have been playing tricks with me. In a good many ways, we’re quits. Still, I confess, I admire you a good deal. Anyway, I offer you my hand. I apologize for my recent remarks. Shall we bury the hatchet, and try and go on as before?”
Julie Le Breton turned slowly and took the hand﻿—without unction.
“I make you angry,” she said, and her voice trembled, “without knowing how or why.”
Lady Henry gulped.
“Oh, it mayn’t answer,” she said, as their hands dropped. “But we may as well have one more trial. And, mademoiselle, I shall be delighted that you should assist the Duchess with her *bazaar*.”
Julie shook her head.
“I don’t think I have any heart for it,” she said, sadly; and then, as Lady Henry sat silent, she approached.
“You look very tired. Shall I send your maid?”
That melancholy and beautiful voice laid a strange spell on Lady Henry. Her companion appeared to her, for a moment, in a new light﻿—as a personage of drama or romance. But she shook off the spell.
“At once, please. Another day like this would put an end to me.”

===Chinese===

朱莉此刻撑着一把古旧的法国式座椅的椅背，好站稳身子。激动和震荡使她的脸同亨利夫人一样惨白；可她那颗秀美的头颅、那纤秾合度的身姿，透出一种何等骄傲的意志、何等炽烈的尊严，以致亨利夫人在她面前退缩了。

"你为什么回绝他？"

朱莉耸了耸肩。

"我想，那是我的私事。不过，倘若——我爱他——我是不会来请示您的顾忌的，亨利夫人。"

"倒爽快。"亨利夫人说，"这比你说过的任何话都叫我受用。你可知道，他有可能继承查德利公爵位？"

"您这么说过好几次了。"对方冷冷地答。

亨利夫人久久地、锐利地端详她。威尔弗里德·伯里说过的种种话重新浮上心头。她想起沃克沃思上尉。她疑惑了。

忽然她伸出手去。

"料想你不会握它，小姐。想是我方才侮辱了你。可是——你也一直在跟我耍花招。好些方面，咱们两讫了。不过我承认，我相当佩服你。不管怎样，我把手伸给你。我为我方才的话道歉。咱们化干戈为玉帛，照旧往下过，好不好？"

朱莉·勒布雷东缓缓转过身，握了那只手——不带一点热乎气。

"我惹您动气，"她说，声音颤抖着，"自己却不知是怎么惹的、为什么惹的。"

亨利夫人咽了口唾沫。

"哦，兴许行不通。"两人的手垂下时，她说，"不过不妨再试一回。还有，小姐，你能去帮公爵夫人操办那场义卖会，我是——很高兴的。"

朱莉摇了摇头。

"我想我提不起这份兴致了。"她凄然说；随后，见亨利夫人默然坐着，她走上前来。

"您看上去倦得很。要我叫您的女仆来吗？"

那忧郁而美丽的声音，给亨利夫人施了一道奇异的符咒。她的女伴在她眼前显现了一瞬新的光景——俨然一个戏剧或传奇中的人物。可她挣脱了这道符咒。

"马上就去，劳驾。再有这么一天，我就完了。"

## VII / VII / 第七章
===Original===

Julie Le Breton was sitting alone in her own small sitting-room. It was the morning of the Tuesday following her Sunday scene with Lady Henry, and she was busy with various household affairs. A small hamper of flowers, newly arrived from Lady Henry’s Surrey garden, and not yet unpacked, was standing open on the table, with various empty flower-glasses beside it. Julie was, at the moment, occupied with the “Stores order” for the month, and Lady Henry’s cook-housekeeper had but just left the room after delivering an urgent statement on the need for “relining” a large number of Lady Henry’s copper saucepans.
The room was plain and threadbare. It had been the schoolroom of various generations of Delafields in the past. But for an observant eye it contained a good many objects which threw light upon its present occupant’s character and history. In a small bookcase beside the fire were a number of volumes in French bindings. They represented either the French classics﻿—Racine, Bossuet, Châteaubriand, Lamartine﻿—which had formed the study of Julie’s convent days, or those other books﻿—George Sand, Victor Hugo, Alfred de Musset, Mazzini, Leopardi, together with the poets and novelists of revolutionary Russia or Polish nationalism or Irish rebellion﻿—which had been the favorite reading of both Lady Rose and her lover. They were but a hundred in all; but for Julie Le Breton they stood for the bridge by which, at will, memory and dreamful pity might carry her back into that vanished life she had once shared with her parents﻿—those strange beings, so calm and yet so passionate in their beliefs, so wilful and yet so patient in their deeds, by whose acts her own experience was still wholly conditioned. In her little room there were no portraits of them visible. But on a side-table stood a small carved triptych. The oblong wings, which were open, contained photographs of figures from one of the great Bruges Memlings. The centre was covered by two wooden leaves delicately carved, and the leaves were locked. The inquisitive housemaid who dusted the room had once tried to open them.﻿—in vain.
On a stand near the fire lay two or three yellow volumes﻿—some recent French essays, a volume of memoirs, a tale of Bourget’s, and so forth.

===Chinese===

朱莉·勒布雷东独自坐在自家的小客厅里。这是她同亨利夫人那个星期天风波之后的星期二上午，她正忙着各项家务。一小筐花刚从亨利夫人萨里的花园送到，还没拆开，敞着口立在桌上，旁边摆着几只空花瓶。朱莉此刻正埋头填写本月的“百货订购单”，而亨利夫人的厨娘兼管家刚发表完一通紧急声明——亨利夫人的大批铜炖锅亟须“换衬”——退了出去。
房间朴素而寒旧。过去，这里曾是德拉菲尔德家一代代孩子们的书房。但在有心人眼里，屋中不少物件正映照着如今这位主人的性情与来历。炉边一只小书架上摆着若干法式装帧的书。其中一部分是法国经典——拉辛（Racine）、博叙埃（Bossuet）、夏多布里昂（Châteaubriand）、拉马丁（Lamartine）——它们构成了朱莉修道院岁月的课业；另一部分则是那些书——乔治·桑（George Sand）、维克多·雨果（Victor Hugo）、阿尔弗雷德·德·缪塞（Alfred de Musset）、马志尼（Mazzini）、莱奥帕尔迪（Leopardi），还有革命俄国、波兰民族主义或爱尔兰起义的诗人与小说家——它们曾是罗斯小姐和她的情人共同钟爱的读物。这些书统共不过一百册；但对朱莉·勒布雷东而言，它们是一座桥，凭这座桥，记忆与如梦的哀悯可以随时把她带回那段与父母共度的、已然消逝的生活——那两个奇特的人，信起人来那样平静，又那样热烈；行事那样任性，又那样坚韧；她自己的经历至今仍全然受着他们行止的支配。在她这间小屋里，看不见他们的画像。但一张边桌上立着一座小小的雕花三联屏。两侧长方形的翼板敞开着，里面嵌着布鲁日梅姆林（Memling）名画中人物的相片。中央覆着两扇雕工精致的木门，门上了锁。那个来掸尘的好奇女仆曾试图打开它们。——徒劳。
炉边的架上摊着两三册黄皮书——几种新近的法国随笔、一卷回忆录、一篇布尔热（Bourget）的小说，诸如此类。

===Original===

These were flanked by Sir Henry Maine’s *Popular Government*, and a recent brilliant study of English policy in Egypt﻿—both of them with the name “Richard J. Montresor” on the title page. The last number of Dr. Meredith’s paper, *The New Rambler*, was there also; and, with the paper-knife still in its leaves, the journal of the latest French traveller in Mokembe, a small “H. W.” inscribed in the top right-hand corner of its gray cover.
Julie finished her Stores order with a sigh of relief. Then she wrote half a dozen business notes, and prepared a few checks for Lady Henry’s signature. When this was done the two dachshunds, who had been lying on the rug spying out her every movement, began to jump upon her.
But Julie laughed in their faces. “It’s raining,” she said, pointing to the window﻿—“*raining!* So there! Either you won’t go out at all, or you’ll go with John.”
John was the second footman, whom the dogs hated. They returned crestfallen to the rug and to a hungry waiting on Providence. Julie took up a letter on foreign paper which had reached her that morning, glanced at the door, and began to reread its closely written sheets. It was from an English diplomat on a visit to Egypt, a man on whom the eyes of Europe were at that moment fixed. That he should write to a woman at all, on the subjects of the letter, involved a compliment *hors ligne*; that he should write with this ease, this abandonment, was indeed remarkable. Julie flushed a little as she read. But when she came to the end she put it aside with a look of worry. “I *wish* he’d write to Lady Henry,” was her thought. “She hasn’t had a line from him for weeks. I shouldn’t wonder if she suspects already. When anyone talks of Egypt, I daren’t open my lips.”
For fear of betraying the very minute and firsthand information that was possessed by Lady Henry’s companion? With a smile and a shrug she locked the letter away in one of the drawers of her writing-table, and took up an envelope which had lain beneath it. From this﻿—again with a look round her﻿—she half drew out a photograph. The grizzled head and spectacled eyes of Dr. Meredith emerged. Julie’s expression softened; her eyebrows went up a little; then she slightly shook her head, like one who protests that if something has gone wrong, it isn’t﻿—isn’t﻿—their fault. Unwillingly she looked at the last words of the letter:

===Chinese===

它们旁边是亨利·梅因爵士（Sir Henry Maine）的《平民政府》（Popular Government），和一部研究英国埃及政策的近著，笔力透纸——两册的扉页上都有“理查德·J·蒙特勒索”（Richard J. Montresor）的署名。梅雷迪思博士（Dr. Meredith）主编的《新漫游者》（The New Rambler）最新一期也在那里；还有最新一位赴莫肯贝（Mokembe）旅行的法国人写的游记，裁纸刀还夹在书页间，灰色封面的右上角题着小小的“H. W.”两个缩写字母。
朱莉如释重负地叹了口气，写完了订购单。接着她写了六张事务便条，又备好几张支票待亨利夫人签字。这些刚忙完，那两条一直趴在毯上侦察她一举一动的腊肠犬便扑了上来。
朱莉却冲着它们的脸笑了。“下雨呢，”她指着窗户说，“下大雨呢！就这么定了！要么你们干脆别出门，要么就跟约翰去。”
约翰是二等男仆，狗都恨他。它们垂头丧气回到毯子上，饥肠辘辘地静候上苍安排。朱莉拿起一封那天早晨收到的、写在外国信纸上的信，朝门口瞥了一眼，开始重读那写得很密的几页信笺。写信的是一位正在埃及访问的英国外交官，此时全欧洲的目光都系在他身上。这样一个人居然肯给一个女人写信，写的还是信中这样的内容，本身就是一种 hors ligne*（出类拔萃的）抬举；而他写得这般随意、这般推心置腹，更属难得。朱莉读着读着脸上微微发热。可读到末尾，她把信搁到一边，面带忧色。“我要是他，就写信给亨利夫人了，”她想。“她已有好几个星期没收到他一行字了。难保她不起疑心。这会儿谁一谈起埃及，我连嘴都不敢张。”
是怕暴露亨利夫人的女伴掌握的那第一手内幕消息么？她微笑着耸了耸肩，把信锁进写字台的一只抽屉，又拿起压在下面的一只信封。从中——她又环顾了一下四周——她半抽出一张照片。梅雷迪思博士那灰白的头颅和戴眼镜的眼睛显露出来。朱莉的神情柔和了；眉毛微微扬起；随后她轻轻摇了摇头，像一个人分辩说，倘若有什么出了岔子，那不算——不算——他们的错。她不情愿地看了看那封信的末尾几行：

===Original===

> “So, remember, I can give you work if you want it, and paying work. I would rather give you my life and my all. But these, it seems, are commodities for which you have no use. So be it. But if you refuse to let me serve you, when the time comes, in such ways as I have suggested in this letter, then, indeed, you would be unkind﻿—I would almost dare to say ungrateful!
> Yours always

**“F. M.”**

===Chinese===

> “那么，记住，只要你愿意，我可以给你工作，有报酬的工作。我本宁愿把我的生命和我的一切都给你。可这些东西，看来你是用不着的。那也随你。但若到了时机来临时，你仍拒绝让我以这封信里提议的方式为你效劳，那你可就狠心了——我几乎敢说一句：忘恩负义了！
> 永远属于你的

**“F. M.”**

===Original===

This letter also she locked away. But her hand lingered on the last of all. She had read it three times already, and knew it practically by heart. So she left the sheets undisturbed in their envelope. But she raised the whole to her lips, and pressed it there, while her eyes, as they slowly filled with tears, travelled﻿—unseeing﻿—to the wintry street beyond the window. Eyes and face wore the same expression as Wilfrid Bury had surprised there﻿—the dumb utterance of a woman hard pressed, not so much by the world without as by some wild force within.
In that still moment the postman’s knock was heard in the street outside. Julie Le Breton started, for no one whose life is dependent on a daily letter can hear that common sound without a thrill. Then she smiled sadly at herself. “*My* joy is over for today!” And she turned away with the letter in her hand.
But she did not place it in the same drawer with the others. She moved across to the little carved triptych, and, after listening a moment to the sounds in the house, she opened its closed doors with a gold key that hung on her watch-chain and had been hidden in the bosom of her dress.
The doors fell open. Inside, on a background of dark velvet, hung two miniatures, lightly framed in gold and linked together by a graceful scroll-work in gold. They were of fine French work, and they represented a man and woman, both handsome, young, and of a remarkable distinction of aspect. The faces, nevertheless, hardly gave pleasure. There was in each of them a look at once absent and eager﻿—the look of those who have cared much and ardently for “man,” and very little, comparatively, for men.
The miniatures had not been meant for the triptych, nor the triptych for them. It had been adapted to them by loving hands; but there was room for other things in the velvet-lined hollow, and a packet of letters was already reposing there. Julie slipped the letter of the morning inside the elastic band which held the packet; then she closed and locked the doors, returning the key to its place in her dress. Both the lock and hinges of this little hiding-place were well and strongly made, and when the wings also were shut and locked one saw nothing but a massively framed photograph of the Bruges belfry resting on a wooden support.
She had hardly completed her little task when there was a sudden noise of footsteps in the passage outside.

===Chinese===

这封信她也锁了起来。可她的手在最后那一封上流连不去。那封她已读过三遍，几乎能背出来。于是她让信笺原封不动地留在信封里，只把整封信举到唇边，紧紧贴着，而她的眼睛慢慢盈满泪水，茫然望向窗外冬日的街道。眼里的、脸上的神情，同威尔弗里德·伯里那天撞见的一模一样——一个被逼得太紧的女人无言的倾诉，逼她的与其说是外部的世界，不如说是体内某种狂野的力量。
就在那静默的一刻，街上传来了邮差的叩门声。朱莉·勒布雷东一惊——凡是指望每日一信活着的人，听见这寻常声响没有不心头一颤的。随即她对自己苦笑了一下。“我的欢喜，今天到头了！”她拿着那封信转过身去。
可她没有把它同旁的信放在一个抽屉里。她走向那座小小的雕花三联屏，侧耳听了听宅子里的动静，然后用挂在表链上、一直藏在衣襟里的一把金钥匙，打开了那两扇闭着的木门。
两扇门豁然开启。里面，深色丝绒衬底上悬着两帧微型画像，金框纤巧，由一弯典雅的金卷叶连缀在一起。那是精致的法国工艺，画着一男一女，都是俊美的年轻人，气象超凡脱俗。然而那两张脸却很难令人愉悦。两人脸上都有一种既恍惚又热切的神情——那是对“人类”挚爱至深、燃烧至切的人的神情；相形之下，对一个个具体的人，他们所怀的心肠便少得多了。
两帧画像本非为这座三联屏而作，三联屏也本非为它们而设。是一双深情的手把它改造成它们的居所；但丝绒衬里的凹匣里还放得下别的东西，一叠信已经安卧其中。朱莉把早晨那封信塞进扎信的松紧带里；随后关上木门落了锁，把钥匙送回衣襟内。这个小暗龛的锁与铰链都做得精良坚固，等两翼也合拢上锁之后，看上去便只是一幅装在厚重框子里的照片，搁在木架上——布鲁日的钟楼。
她这桩小小的差事刚一做完，外面的走廊里突然响起一阵脚步声。

===Original===

“Julie!” said a light voice, subdued to a laughing whisper. “May I come in?”
The Duchess stood on the threshold, her small, shell-pink face emerging from a masterly study in gray, presented by a most engaging costume.
Julie, in surprise, advanced to meet her visitor, and the old butler, who was Miss Le Breton’s very good friend, quickly and discreetly shut the door upon the two ladies.
“Oh, my dear!” said the Duchess, throwing herself into Julie’s arms. “I came up so quietly! I told Hutton not to disturb Lady Henry, and I just crept upstairs, holding my skirts. Wasn’t it heroic of me to put my poor little head into the lion’s den like this? But when I got your letter this morning saying you couldn’t come to me, I vowed I would just see for myself how you were, and whether there was anything left of you. Oh, you poor, pale thing!”
And drawing Julie to a chair, the little Duchess sat down beside her, holding her friend’s hands and studying her face.
“Tell me what’s been happening﻿—I believe you’ve been crying! Oh, the old wretch!”
“You’re quite mistaken,” said Julie, smiling. “Lady Henry says I may help you with the bazaar.”
“No!” The Duchess threw up her hands in amazement. “How have you managed that?”
“By giving in. But, Evelyn, I’m not coming.”
“Oh, Julie!” The Duchess threw herself back in her chair and fixed a pair of very blue and very reproachful eyes on Miss Le Breton.
“No, I’m not coming. If I’m to stay here, even for a time, I mustn’t provoke her any more. She says I may come, but she doesn’t mean it.”
“She couldn’t mean anything civil or agreeable. How has she been behaving﻿—since Sunday?”
Julie looked uncertain.
“Oh, there is an armed truce. I was made to have a fire in my bedroom last night. And Hutton took the dogs out yesterday.”
The Duchess laughed.
“And there was quite a scene on Sunday? You don’t tell me much about it in your letter. But, Julie”﻿—her voice dropped to a whisper﻿—“was anything said about Jacob?”
Julie looked down. A bitterness crept into her face.
“Yes. I can’t forgive myself. I was provoked into telling the truth.”
“You did! Well? I suppose Aunt Flora thought it was all your fault that he proposed, and an impertinence that you refused?”
“She was complimentary at the time,” said Julie, half smiling. “But since﻿—No, I don’t feel that she is appeased.”
“Of course not. Affronted, more likely.”
There was a silence.

===Chinese===

“朱莉！”一个轻快的声音说，压成了带笑的耳语。“我能进来吗？”
公爵夫人立在门口，那张小小的、贝壳般粉嫩的脸，从一身灰色的大师手笔中探出来——这身装束实在迷人。
朱莉惊讶地迎上前去，而那位同勒布雷东小姐交情极好的老管家，机敏而得体地带上了门，把两位夫人留在屋里。
“哦，亲爱的！”公爵夫人叫着，扑进朱莉怀里。“我上楼时轻手轻脚的！我嘱咐赫顿（Hutton）别惊动亨利夫人，然后提着裙裾，就这么蹑手蹑脚爬上楼来。我把我这颗可怜的小脑袋这么伸进狮子窝，够英雄吧？可今天早晨接到你的信，说你不能来我这里，我便暗暗发誓，定要亲眼看看你怎么样了，看看你还剩下多少。哦，你这可怜的、脸色苍白的小东西！”
小个子公爵夫人把朱莉拉到椅边，挨着她坐下，握住朋友的双手，端详她的脸。
“告诉我出了什么事——我看你哭过了！哦，那个老刁婆！”
“你全猜错了，”朱莉含笑说。“亨利夫人说，我可以帮你办义卖会了。”
“不会吧！”公爵夫人惊得双手一举。“你是怎么做到的？”
“靠让步。不过，伊芙琳，我不去。”
“哦，朱莉！”公爵夫人往椅背上一仰，一双极蓝、极含责备的眼睛盯住了勒布雷东小姐。
“不，我不去。我既然还要在这里待下去，哪怕只待一段时日，也不该再招惹她了。她说我可以去，可那不是真心话。”
“她也说不出什么真心好话、好态度。她这几天怎么样——自星期天以来？”
朱莉神色不定。
“哦，算是武装休战。昨晚他们非要在我的卧室里生火。昨天还是赫顿带的狗出去。”
公爵夫人笑了。
“星期天可是大闹了一场？你信里没怎么告诉我。可是朱莉”——她的声音低成了耳语——“提到雅各布了吗？”
朱莉垂下眼睛。一丝苦涩悄悄爬上她的脸。
“提了。我不能原谅自己。我被激得说出了实话。”
“你说了！后来呢？我猜弗洛拉姑妈准以为他求婚全是你的不是，而你拒绝他又是不识抬举？”
“当时她倒还说了几句好话，”朱莉半含笑地说。“可是打那以后——不，我觉得她的气并没消。”
“当然没消。多半还觉得受了冒犯。”
一阵沉默。

===Original===

The Duchess was looking at Julie, but her thoughts were far away. And presently she broke out, with the *étourderie* that became her:
“I wish I understood it myself, Julie. I know you like him.”
“Immensely. But﻿—we should fight!”
Miss Le Breton looked up with animation.
“Oh, that’s not a reason,” said the Duchess, rather annoyed.
“It’s *the* reason. I don’t know﻿—there is something of *iron* in Mr. Delafield;” and Julie emphasized the words with a shrug which was almost a shiver. “And as I’m not in love with him, I’m afraid of him.”
“That’s the best way of being in love,” cried the Duchess. “And then, Julie”﻿—she paused, and at last added, naively, as she laid her little hands on her friend’s knee﻿—“haven’t you got *any* ambitions?”
“Plenty. Oh, I should like very well to play the duchess, with you to instruct me,” said Julie, caressing the hands. “But I must choose my duke. And till the right one appears, I prefer my own wild ways.”
“Afraid of Jacob Delafield? How odd!” said the Duchess, with her chin on her hands.
“It may be odd to you,” said Julie, with vivacity. “In reality, it’s not in the least odd. There’s the same quality in him that there is in Lady Henry﻿—something that beats you down,” she added, under her breath. “There, that’s enough about Mr. Delafield﻿—quite enough.”
And, rising, Julie threw up her arms and clasped her hands above her head. The gesture was all strength and will, like the stretching of a seabird’s wings.
The Duchess looked at her with eyes that had begun to waver.
“Julie, I heard such an odd piece of news last night.”
Julie turned.
“You remember the questions you asked me about Aileen Moffatt?”
“Perfectly.”
“Well, I saw a man last night who had just come home from Simla. He saw a great deal of her, and he says that she and her mother were adored in India. They were thought so quaint and sweet﻿—unlike other people﻿—and the girl so lovely, in a sort of gossamer way. And who do you think was always about with them﻿—at Peshawar first, and then at Simla﻿—so that everybody talked? Captain Warkworth! My man believed there was an understanding between them.”
Julie had begun to fill the flower-glasses with water and unpack the flower-basket. Her back was towards the Duchess. After a moment she replied, her hands full of forced narcissuses:
“Well, that would be a coup for him.”
“I should think so.

===Chinese===

公爵夫人望着朱莉，心思却飘在远处。过了一会儿，她带着她特有的那种轻率冒失（étourderie），脱口说道：
“这事我自己也想不明白呢，朱莉。我知道你喜欢他。”
“非常喜欢。可是——我们准会掐起来！”
勒布雷东小姐神采飞扬地抬起头来。
“哦，这算什么理由，”公爵夫人有些着恼。
“这就是理由。我说不好——德拉菲尔德先生身上有种铁一样的东西；”朱莉说这话时耸了耸肩，那耸肩几乎是一阵战栗。“而既然我不爱他，我就怕他。”
“这可是恋爱的最佳状态呢，”公爵夫人叫道。“再说，朱莉”——她顿了顿，最后把两只小手放在朋友膝上，天真地补了一句——“你就没有什么抱负吗？”
“多着呢。哦，有您这样的名师指点，做公爵夫人这出戏我倒是很乐意演一演，”朱莉说着，抚了抚那双手。“可我的公爵得我自己挑。正主儿没露面之前，我还是过我自己的野路子。”
“怕雅各布·德拉菲尔德？多怪呀！”公爵夫人双手托着下巴说。
“你觉得怪，”朱莉说得活泼，“其实一点儿也不怪。他身上有股同亨利夫人一样的气质——一种能把你压垮的东西，”她压低声音补充道。“行了，德拉菲尔德先生的事到此为止——很够了。”
说着朱莉站起身，双臂上扬，十指在头顶相扣。这个姿态全是力量与意志，像海鸟展开的翅膀。
公爵夫人望着她，目光已开始动摇。
“朱莉，我昨晚听到一件好奇怪的事。”
朱莉转过身来。
“你还记得你问过我那些关于艾琳·莫法特（Aileen Moffatt）的话吗？”
“记得清清楚楚。”
“是这样，我昨晚见到一位刚从西姆拉（Simla）回来的人。他同她来往很多，说她们母女在印度极受宠爱。人人都觉得她们又古雅又甜美——同旁人不一样——那姑娘美得很，像薄纱一样轻灵。你再猜猜，是谁总在她们身边转——先在白沙瓦（Peshawar），后来在西姆拉——闹得人人议论？沃克沃思上尉！我那位先生相信，他们俩早有了默契。”
朱莉已经开始往花瓶里注水、拆花筐了。她背朝着公爵夫人。过了一会儿她答话了，手里满捧着温室催开的水仙：
“嗯，那对他可是一步妙棋。”
“可不是嘛。”

===Original===

She is supposed to have half a million in coal-mines alone, besides land. Has Captain Warkworth ever said anything to you about them?”
“No. He has never mentioned them.”
The Duchess reflected, her eyes still on Julie’s back.
“Everybody wants money nowadays. And the soldiers are just as bad as anybody else. They don’t *look* money, as the City men do﻿—that’s why we women fall in love with them﻿—but they *think* it, all the same.”
Julie made no reply. The Duchess could see nothing of her. But the little lady’s face showed the flutter of one determined to venture yet a little farther on thin ice.
“Julie, I’ve done everything you’ve asked me. I sent a card for the 20th to that *rather* dreadful woman, Lady Froswick. I was very clever with Freddie about that living; and I’ve talked to Mr. Montresor. But, Julie, if you don’t mind, I really should like to know why you’re so keen about it?”
The Duchess’s cheeks were by now one flush. She had a romantic affection for Julie, and would not have offended her for the world.
Julie turned round. She was always pale, and the Duchess saw nothing unusual.
“Am I so keen?”
“Julie, you have done everything in the world for this man since he came home.”
“Well, he interested me,” said Julie, stepping back to look at the effect of one of the vases. “The first evening he was here, he saved me from Lady Henry﻿—twice. He’s alone in the world, too, which attracts me. You see, I happen to know what it’s like. An only son, and an orphan, and no family interest to push him﻿—”
“So you thought you’d push him? Oh, Julie, you’re a darling﻿—but you’re rather a wire-puller, aren’t you?”
Julie smiled faintly.
“Well, perhaps I like to feel, sometimes, that I have a little power. I haven’t much else.”
The Duchess seized one of her hands and pressed it to her cheek.
“You have power, because everyone loves and admires you. As for me, I would cut myself in little bits to please you.﻿ ﻿… Well, I only hope, when he’s married his heiress, if he does marry her, they’ll remember what they owe to you.”
Did she feel the hand lying in her own shake? At any rate, it was brusquely withdrawn, and Julie walked to the end of the table to fetch some more flowers.
“I don’t want any gratitude,” she said, abruptly, “from anyone. Well, now, Evelyn, you understand about the bazaar? I wish I could, but I can’t.”
“Yes, I understand. Julie!”

===Chinese===

光煤矿里的股份，据说就有五十万镑，还不算地产。沃克沃思上尉同你提起过她们吗？”
“没有。他从没提过。”
公爵夫人沉吟着，目光仍停在朱莉背上。
“如今人人都要钱。当兵的也不比谁强。他们不像城里人那样把钱挂在脸上——这正是我们女人爱他们的缘故——可他们心里照样盘算着钱。”
朱莉没有作答。公爵夫人一点也看不见她的脸。可这位小夫人的脸上透出那种决意在薄冰上再往前闯一闯的忐忑。
“朱莉，你要我办的事我都办了。我给那个相当可怕的弗罗斯威克夫人（Lady Froswick）寄了二十号的请柬。关于那份圣职俸禄，我在弗雷迪面前耍了手腕；我还同蒙特勒索先生谈过了。可是朱莉，你要是不介意，我实在想知道，你为什么对这事这样上心？”
公爵夫人的两颊这时已红成一片。她对朱莉怀着一种近乎浪漫的感情，万万不愿开罪她。
朱莉转过身来。她素来脸色苍白，公爵夫人看不出有什么异样。
“我有那么上心吗？”
“朱莉，他回国以来，你为他把天底下的事都做遍了。”
“这个嘛，他引起了我的兴趣，”朱莉说，退后一步端详一只花瓶的效果。“他来的头一个晚上，就两次救我脱离亨利夫人。他也是孑然一身，这一点吸引我。你知道，这种滋味我恰好尝过。独生子，父母双亡，没有一门亲眷肯为他出力——”
“于是你就打算自己来推他一把？哦，朱莉，你真是个宝贝——可你也真有点儿幕后牵线的本事，是不是？”
朱莉淡淡一笑。
“也许吧，有时候我喜欢感到自己有几分势力。旁的，我实在没有多少。”
公爵夫人一把抓住她的一只手，贴在自己脸颊上。
“你有势力，因为人人都爱慕你。至于我，为讨你欢心，把我自己切成碎块都愿意。……我只盼着，他若真娶了那位女继承人，他们得记得欠了你什么。”
她可感到握在自己手里的那只手颤了一下？无论如何，它被猛地抽了回去，朱莉走到桌子那头去再取些花来。
“我不要任何人的感激，”她突兀地说，“谁的我都不稀罕。好了，伊芙琳，义卖会的事你都明白了？我倒想去，可我去不成。”
“明白了。朱莉！”

===Original===

The Duchess rose impulsively, and threw herself into a chair beside the table where she could watch the face and movements of Mademoiselle Le Breton. “Julie, I want so much to talk to you﻿—about *business*. You’re not to be offended. Julie, *if* you leave Lady Henry, how will you manage?”
“How shall I live, you mean?” said Julie, smiling at the euphemism in which this little person, for whom existence had rained gold and flowers since her cradle, had enwrapped the hard facts of bread-and-butter﻿—facts with which she was so little acquainted that she approached them with a certain delicate mystery.
“You must have some money, you know, Julie,” said the Duchess, timidly, her upraised face and Paris hat well matched by the gay poinsettias, the delicate eucharis and arums with which the table was now covered.
“I shall earn some,” said Julie, quietly.
“Oh, but, Julie, you can’t be bothered with any other tiresome old lady!”
“No. I should keep my freedom. But Dr. Meredith has offered me work, and got me a promise of more.”
The Duchess opened her eyes.
“Writing! Well, of course, we all know you can do anything you want to do. And you won’t let anybody help you at all?”
“I won’t let anybody give me money, if that’s what you mean,” said Julie, smiling. But it was a smile without accent, without gayety.
The Duchess, watching her, said to herself, “Since I came in she is changed﻿—quite changed.”
“Julie, you’re horribly proud!”
Julie’s face contracted a little.
“How much ‘power’ should I have left, do you think﻿—how much self-respect﻿—if I took money from my friends?”
“Well, not money, perhaps. But, Julie, you know all about Freddie’s London property. It’s abominable how much he has. There are always a few houses he keeps in his own hands. If Lady Henry *does* quarrel with you, and we could lend you a little house﻿—for a time﻿—*wouldn’t* you take it, Julie?”
Her voice had the coaxing inflections of a child. Julie hesitated.
“Only if the Duke himself offered it,” she said, finally, with a brusque stiffening of her whole attitude.
The Duchess flushed and stood up.
“Oh, well, that’s all right,” she said, but no longer in the same voice. “Remember, I have your promise. Goodbye, Julie, you darling!﻿ ﻿… Oh, by-the-way, what an idiot I am! Here am I forgetting the chief thing I came about. Will you come with me to Lady Hubert tonight? Do!

===Chinese===

公爵夫人一时兴起，站起身来，一屁股坐进桌边一把椅子里，从那里正好看得见勒布雷东小姐的脸和举手投足。“朱莉，我有好多话要同你讲——是正事。你可别生气。朱莉，你若离开亨利夫人，往后怎么打算？”
“你是说，我怎么谋生？”朱莉说，不由得笑了——这位小妇人自摇篮起，生活对她下的全是金雨和鲜花，如今她把面包黄油的硬道理裹进这样一件委婉语的外衣里；这些道理她生疏得很，走近时还带着几分精致的神秘。
“朱莉，你总得有点钱呀，你知道吧，”公爵夫人怯怯地说，她仰起的脸和那顶巴黎帽子，正同满桌鲜艳的一品红、清雅的油加律和海芋相映成趣。
“我会挣的，”朱莉平静地说。
“哦，可是朱莉，你总不能再去伺候别的什么讨厌的老太太呀！”
“不去了。我要保住我的自由。不过梅雷迪思博士已经给了我工作，还替我谋到了更多工作的允诺。”
公爵夫人睁大了眼睛。
“写作！哦，当然，我们都知道你想做什么就能做成什么。那你就不肯让任何人帮一点忙了？”
“要是你指的是这个——我不肯让任何人给我钱，”朱莉含笑说。可那笑意平平，没有一点光彩。
公爵夫人端详着她，心里自语：“自打我进门，她变了——全变了。”
“朱莉，你骄傲得吓人！”
朱莉的脸微微一缩。
“你想，我若拿朋友的钱，剩下的还有多少‘势力’——还有多少自尊？”
“那么，钱也许算了。可是朱莉，弗雷迪在伦敦的房产你全清楚。他名下的房子多得可恨。他手里总留着几处自己管的宅子。万一亨利夫人真同你闹翻，我们可以借你一处小宅子——暂时住住——朱莉，你还不肯要么？”
她的声调带着孩子哄人的央告。朱莉迟疑了。
“除非公爵亲口提出，”她终于说，整个人的姿态蓦地一凛。
公爵夫人脸一红，站起身来。
“哦，好吧，那就这么着，”她说，声音却不再是方才那个调子了。“记住，你答应过我。再见，朱莉，我的心肝！……哦，对了，瞧我多糊涂！差点把正事给忘了。今晚你陪我一起去休伯特夫人（Lady Hubert）府上，好不好？一定来！

===Original===

Freddie’s away, and I hate going by myself.”
“To Lady Hubert’s?” said Julie, starting a little. “I wonder what Lady Henry would say?”
“Tell her Jacob won’t be there,” said the Duchess, laughing. “Then she won’t make any difficulties.”
“Shall I go and ask her?”
“Gracious! let me get out of the house first. Give her a message from me that I will come and see her tomorrow morning. We’ve got to make it up, Freddie says; so the sooner it’s over, the better. Say all the civil things you can to her about tonight, and wire me this afternoon. If all’s well, I come for you at eleven.”
The Duchess rustled away. Julie was left standing by the table, alone. Her face was very still, but her eyes shone, her teeth pressed her lip. Unconsciously her hand closed upon a delicate blossom of eucharis and crushed it.
“I’ll go,” she said, to herself. “Yes, I’ll go.”
Her letter of the morning, as it happened, had included the following sentences:
“I think tonight I must put in an appearance at the Hubert Delafields’, though I own that neither the house nor the son of the house is very much to my liking. But I hear that he has gone back to the country. And there are a few people who frequent Lady Hubert, who might just now be of use.”
Lady Henry gave her consent that Mademoiselle Le Breton should accompany the Duchess to Lady Hubert’s party almost with effusion. “It will be very dull,” she said. “My sister-in-law makes a desert and calls it society. But if you want to go, go. As to Evelyn Crowborough, I am engaged to my dentist tomorrow morning.”
When at night this message was reported to the Duchess, as she and Julie were on their way to Rutland Gate, she laughed.
“How much leek shall I have to swallow? What’s tomorrow? Wednesday. Hm﻿—cards in the afternoon; in the evening I appear, sit on a stool at Lady Henry’s feet, and look at you through my glasses as though I had never seen you before. On Thursday I leave a French book; on Friday I send the baby to see her. Goodness, what a time it takes!” said the Duchess, raising her very white and very small shoulders. “Well, for my life, I mustn’t fail tomorrow night.”
At Lady Hubert’s they found a very tolerable, not to say lively, gathering, which quite belied Lady Henry’s slanders. There was not the same conscious brilliance, the same thrill in the air, as pertained to the gatherings in Bruton Street.

===Chinese===

弗雷迪不在家，我又最怕一个人出门。”
“去休伯特夫人家？”朱莉说，微微一惊。“不知亨利夫人会怎么说？”
“告诉她雅各布不会去，”公爵夫人笑着说。“那她就一个磕绊也不会打了。”
“我现在去问她？”
“天哪！先让我出了这座宅子再说。替我带句话，就说我明天上午来看她。弗雷迪说，我们总得把这场别扭解开；越早了结越好。今晚的事，拣好听的尽量说给她听，下午给我拍个电报。若是一切顺遂，我十一点来接你。”
公爵夫人窸窸窣窣地走了。朱莉独自立在桌旁。她的脸十分平静，眼睛却灼灼发亮，牙齿咬着下唇。不知不觉间，她的手合拢在一朵娇嫩的油加律花上，把它捏碎了。
“我要去，”她自言自语。“是的，我要去。”
说来也巧，她早晨收到的那封信里，正有这样几句：
“我想今晚我必须到休伯特·德拉菲尔德家露一露面，虽说那座宅子也罢，宅子的少爷也罢，都很难让我喜欢。不过我听说他已经回乡下去了。而常去休伯特夫人那里的有几个人，眼下或许用得着。”
勒布雷东小姐陪公爵夫人赴休伯特夫人的晚会，亨利夫人应允得几乎算得上热烈。“会闷得很，”她说。“我那位妯娌造出一片沙漠，偏管它叫交际场。可你要想去，就去吧。至于伊芙琳·克罗伯勒，我明天上午约了我的牙医。”
当晚，这个口信转达到公爵夫人那里时——她和朱莉正驱车前往拉特兰门（Rutland Gate）——她笑了。
“我这顿韭菜得吃多少才够？明天是星期几？星期三。唔——下午打牌；晚上我到场，坐在亨利夫人脚边一只小凳上，隔着眼镜瞧你，装作从前没见过你似的。星期四我留下一本法文书；星期五我把娃娃送去给她看。天哪，多费工夫呀！”公爵夫人耸着她那雪白瘦小的肩膀说。“唉，性命交关，明晚可万万误不得。”
在休伯特夫人家，她们见到一场蛮像样、甚至称得上热闹的聚会，全然拆穿了亨利夫人的诽谤。这里没有布鲁顿街聚会里那种自觉的煊赫、空气里那种战栗的兴奋。

===Original===

But there was a more solid social comfort, such as befits people untroubled by the certainty that the world is looking on. The guests of Bruton Street laughed, as well-bred people should, at the estimation in which Lady Henry’s salon was held, by those especially who did not belong to it. Still, the mere knowledge of this outside estimate kept up a certain tension. At Lady Hubert’s there was no tension, and the agreeable nobodies who found their way in were not made to blush for the agreeable nothings of their conversation.
Lady Hubert herself made for ease﻿—partly, no doubt, for stupidity. She was fair, sleepy, and substantial. Her husband had spent her fortune, and ruffled all the temper she had. The Hubert Delafields were now, however, better off than they had been﻿—investments had recovered﻿—and Lady Hubert’s temper was once more placid, as Providence had meant it to be. During the coming season it was her firm intention to marry her daughter, who now stood beside her as she received her guests﻿—a blonde, sweet-featured girl, given, however, so it was said, to good works, and not at all inclined to trouble herself overmuch about a husband.
The rooms were fairly full; and the entry of the Duchess and Mademoiselle Le Breton was one of the incidents of the evening, and visibly quickened the pulses of the assembly. The little Dresden-china Duchess, with her clothes, her jewels, and her smiles, had been, since her marriage, one of the chief favorites of fashion. She had been brought up in the depths of the country, and married at eighteen. After six years she was not in the least tired of her popularity or its penalties. All the life in her dainty person, her glancing eyes, and small, smiling lips rose, as it were, to meet the stir that she evoked. She vaguely saw herself as Titania, and played the part with childish glee. And like Titania, as she had more than once ruefully reflected, she was liable to be chidden by her lord.
But the Duke was on this particular evening debating high subjects in the House of Lords, and the Duchess was amusing herself.

===Chinese===

这里有的是一种更扎实的社交安逸，合乎那种并不确知世人正睁眼看着的人们的身份。布鲁顿街的宾客们，自有教养地取笑着外间——尤其是圈外人——对亨利夫人沙龙的这番估量。然而单是知道外间有此一说，便足以维持某种紧张。休伯特夫人家没有这种紧张，那些混进来的可亲的无名之辈，也不必为自己言谈里那些可亲的无聊话脸红。
休伯特夫人本人一味图省事——多半，无疑也是天性愚钝。她肤色白皙，睡眼惺忪，体态敦实。她丈夫花光了她的财产，也把她仅有的一点脾气磨了个精光。不过休伯特·德拉菲尔德夫妇如今境况比从前强了——投资翻了本——休伯特夫人的心境重归平和，一如上天的本意。来年社交季里，她铁了心要把女儿嫁出去；女儿此刻正站在她身旁陪她迎客——一个金发、眉目甜美的姑娘，不过据说一心向善做功德，压根不打算为嫁人这桩事过分操心。
几个厅堂都相当满；公爵夫人和勒布雷东小姐的进场，成了当晚的一桩事件，满场人的脉搏分明都快了几拍。这位德累斯顿瓷娃娃般的小公爵夫人，连同她的衣装、她的珠宝、她的微笑，自出嫁以来一直是时髦界的头号宠儿之一。她在穷乡僻壤长大，十八岁出嫁。六年过去，她对这份风光连同它的代价，非但没有半点厌倦。她那娇小身躯里的全部生气、她顾盼的眼睛、含笑的小嘴，仿佛全都腾起来迎向她自己搅起的那阵骚动。她模模糊糊把自己看作泰坦尼娅（Titania），并且怀着孩子气的欢快扮演这个角色。而正如她不止一次懊恼地想到的，她也同泰坦尼娅一样，免不了要受丈夫的训斥。
可就在这个特别的晚上，公爵正在上议院（House of Lords）里辩那些宏大的题目，公爵夫人便自寻其乐。

===Original===

Sir Wilfrid Bury, who arrived not long after his goddaughter, found her the centre first of a bodyguard of cousins, including among them apparently a great many handsome young men, and then of a small crowd, whose vaguely smiling faces reflected the pleasure that was to be got, even at a distance, out of her young and merry beauty.
Julie Le Breton was not with her. But in the next room Sir Wilfrid soon perceived the form and face which, in their own way, exacted quite as much attention from the world as those of the Duchess. She was talking with many people, and, as usual, he could not help watching her. Never yet had he seen her wide, black eyes more vivid than they were tonight. Now, as on his first sight of her, he could not bring himself to call them beautiful. Yet beautiful they were, by every canon of form and color. No doubt it was something in their expression that offended his own well-drilled instincts.
He found himself thinking suspicious thoughts about most of the conversations in which he saw her engaged. Why was she bestowing those careful smiles on that intolerable woman, Lady Froswick? And what an acquaintance she seemed to have among these elderly soldiers, who might at all times be reckoned on at Lady Hubert’s parties! One gray-haired veteran after another recalled himself to her attention, got his few minutes with her, and passed on smiling. Certain high officials, too, were no less friendly. Her court, it seemed to him, was mainly composed of the middle-aged; tonight, at any rate, she left the young to the Duchess. And it was on the whole a court of men. The women, as he now perceived, were a trifle more reserved. There was not, indeed, a trace of exclusion. They were glad to see her; glad, he thought, to be noticed by her. But they did not yield themselves﻿—or so he fancied﻿—with the same wholeness as their husbands.
“How old is she?” he asked himself. “About nine-and-twenty?﻿ ﻿… Jacob’s age﻿—or a trifle older.”
After a time he lost sight of her, and in the amusement of his own evening forgot her. But as the rooms were beginning to thin he walked through them, looking for a famous collection of miniatures that belonged to Lady Hubert. English family history was one of his hobbies, and he was far better acquainted with the Delafield statesmen, and the Delafield beauties of the past, than were any of their modern descendants.

===Chinese===

威尔弗里德·伯里爵士比他的教女晚到不久，便发现她先是被一队堂表兄弟团团围住——其中显然有许多俊美的青年男子——继而又被一小圈人簇拥着，那些人脸上浮着隐隐的笑意，映出哪怕只是远远领受她那青春快活的美貌，也是一桩乐事。
朱莉·勒布雷东不在她身边。但在隔壁厅里，威尔弗里德爵士很快便认出了那副身影与面庞——以它们自己的方式，它们向世人索取的关注，丝毫不亚于公爵夫人那副。她正同许多人交谈，而他也一如既往，忍不住注视她。他还从没见过她那双睁大的黑眼睛像今晚这样顾盼生辉。此刻同初见时一样，他怎么也说不出“美”这个字来。然而论形论色，处处合乎美的法度。无疑，是那眼神里的某种东西，触犯了他自己那训练有素的本能。
他发觉自己对她参与的大多数交谈都起了疑心。她为什么把那些斟酌过的微笑，一一赐给弗罗斯威克夫人那个叫人无法忍受的女人？还有，在这些年长军人中间——休伯特夫人的晚会任何时候都少不了这路人——她的相识怎么这样广！一个接一个的灰发老将上前向她重叙旧谊，得她几分钟的工夫，然后含笑走开。某些高官显宦也不见外。在她周围，据他看，朝臣多半是中年人；至少今晚，她把年轻人都留给了公爵夫人。而且总的说来，这是个男人的宫廷。女人们呢，他此刻察觉，要矜持几分。倒不是有什么排挤的痕迹。她们乐意见到她；而且，他想，也乐意受她垂青。可她们不像自家丈夫那样——或者说，他是这么遐想——整个儿缴械。
“她多大年纪？”他自问。“二十九上下？……同雅各布同岁——或稍长一点。”
过了一阵，他看不见她了，自己也聊得高兴，便把她忘了。可待到厅堂里人渐渐稀落，他穿行各厅，去找休伯特夫人那套有名的微型画像收藏。英国的家族史是他的癖好之一；对于德拉菲尔德家昔日的政治家与美人，他远比这个家族如今的任何后裔都熟悉。

===Original===

Lady Hubert’s Cosways and Plimers had made a lively impression upon him in days gone by, and he meant to renew acquaintance with them.
But they had been moved from the room in which he remembered them, and he was led on through a series of drawing-rooms, now nearly empty, till on the threshold of the last he paused suddenly.
A lady and gentleman rose from a sofa on which they had been sitting. Captain Warkworth stood still. Mademoiselle Le Breton advanced to the newcomer.
“Is it very late?” she said, gathering up her fan and gloves. “We have been looking at Lady Hubert’s miniatures. That lady with the muff”﻿—she pointed to the case which occupied a conspicuous position in the room﻿—“is really wonderful. Can you tell me, Sir Wilfrid, where the Duchess is?”
“No, but I can help you find her,” said that gentleman, forgetting the miniatures and endeavoring to look at neither of his companions.
“And I must rush,” said Captain Warkworth, looking at his watch. “I told a man to come to my rooms at twelve. Heavens!”
He shook hands with Miss Le Breton and hurried away.
Sir Wilfrid and Julie moved on together. That he had disturbed a most intimate and critical conversation was somehow borne in upon Sir Wilfrid. But kind and even romantic as was the old man’s inmost nature, his feelings were not friendly.
“How does the biography get on?” he asked his companion, with a smile.
A bright flush appeared in Mademoiselle Le Breton’s cheek.
“I think Lady Henry has dropped it.”
“Ah, well, I don’t imagine she will regret it;” he said, dryly.
She made no reply. He mentally accused himself for a brute, and then shook off the charge. Surely a few pinpricks were her desert! That she should defend her own secrets was, as Delafield had said, legitimate enough. But when a man offers you his services, you should not befool him beyond a certain point.
She must be aware of what he was thinking. He glanced at her curiously; at the stately dress gleaming with jet, which no longer affected anything of the girl; at the fine but old-fashioned necklace of pearls and diamonds﻿—no doubt her mother’s﻿—which clasped her singularly slender throat. At any rate, she showed nothing. She began to talk again of the Delafield miniatures, using her fan the while with graceful deliberation; and presently they found the Duchess.
“Is she an adventuress, or is she not?”

===Chinese===

休伯特夫人收藏的科斯韦（Cosway）和普利默（Plimer）作品，当年曾给他留下鲜活的印象，他有心同它们叙叙旧。
可它们已从他所记得的那个厅里挪走了，他被一路引着穿过一间间如今近乎空荡的客厅，直到最后一间的门槛上，他猛地停住了。
沙发上坐着一男一女，此刻双双站起身来。沃克沃思上尉立着没动。勒布雷东小姐迎向新来的人。
“很晚了吗？”她一面说，一面拾起扇子和手套。“我们方才在看休伯特夫人的微型画像。那位带手筒的夫人”——她指着屋内一处显要位置上的画匣——“真是精彩。威尔弗里德爵士，您能告诉我公爵夫人在哪儿吗？”
“说不上，不过我可以帮您找她，”那位先生说，微型画像已抛在脑后，还竭力不去看两位同伴中的任何一位。
“我也得赶紧走了，”沃克沃思上尉看看表说。“我约了一个人十二点到我寓所。天哪！”
他同勒布雷东小姐握了握手，匆匆走了。
威尔弗里德爵士同朱莉一道往前走。他打断的是一场何等亲密、何等紧要的谈话——这个感觉不知怎么就钻进了威尔弗里德爵士心里。可尽管老人最深的性子里满是善良、甚至浪漫，此刻他的心绪却并不友善。
“那部传记写得怎么样了？”他含笑问身边的同伴。
勒布雷东小姐颊上泛起一片鲜艳的红晕。
“我想亨利夫人已经把它搁下了。”
“啊，是了，我想她不会惋惜的，”他干巴巴地说。
她没有答话。他在心里骂自己是个畜生，随即又甩开了这项罪名。让她挨几枚针扎，不是应得的么！她守卫自己的秘密，正如德拉菲尔德所说，本无可指摘。可一个男人肯为你效劳，你总不该把人家耍弄得没了限度。
她必定猜到了他在想什么。他好奇地瞥她一眼；瞥她那身缀着黑玉、仪态庄重的衣裳——那上面已寻不见半点少女的做派；瞥她那串精美然而老式的珍珠钻石项链——多半是她母亲的——扣在格外纤细的颈间。可她什么也没有流露。她又谈起德拉菲尔德家的微型画像来，一边从容优雅地摇着扇子；不久，他们便找到了公爵夫人。

===Original===

thought Bury, as his hansom carried him away from Rutland Gate. “If she marries Jacob, it will be a queer business.”

===Chinese===

“她究竟是不是一个女冒险家？”伯里坐在双轮马车上驶离拉特兰门时想。“她若嫁了雅各布，那可就是一桩蹊跷事了。”

## VIII / 第八章
===Original===

Meanwhile the Duchess had dropped Julie Le Breton at Lady Henry’s door. Julie groped her way upstairs through the sleeping house. She found her room in darkness, and she turned on no light. There was still a last glimmer of fire, and she sank down by it, her long arms clasped round her knees, her head thrown back as though she listened still to words in her ears.
“Oh, such a child! Such a dear, simple-minded child! Report engaged her to at least ten different people at Simla. She had a crowd of cavaliers there﻿—I was one of them. The whole place adored her. She is a very rare little creature, but well looked after, I can tell you﻿—a long array of guardians in the background.”
How was it possible not to trust that aspect and that smile? Her mind travelled back to the autumn days when she had seen them first; reviewed the steps, so little noticed at first, so rapid lately and full of fate, by which she had come into this bondage wherein she stood. She saw the first appearance of the young soldier in Lady Henry’s drawing-room; her first conversation with him; and all the subtle development of that singular relation between them, into which so many elements had entered. The flattering sense of social power implied both in the homage of this young and successful man, and in the very services that she, on her side, was able to render him; impulsive gratitude for that homage, at a time when her very soul was smarting under Lady Henry’s contemptuous hostility; and then the sweet advances of a “friendship” that was to unite them in a bond, secret and unique, a bond that took no account of the commonplaces of love and marriage, the link of equal and kindred souls in a common struggle with hard and sordid circumstance.

===Chinese===

话说公爵夫人把朱莉·勒布雷东送到了亨利夫人门口。朱莉摸索着上楼，穿过沉睡的宅子。她的房间一片漆黑，她也没有开灯。炉里还剩最后一点微光，她颓然坐倒在炉边，长长的双臂抱住膝盖，头向后仰着，仿佛还在谛听耳畔的话语。

"这么个孩子！这么个天真可爱的小东西！据传闻，在西姆拉同她订过婚的至少有十个人。她在那儿有一大群追求者——我也是其中一个。整个地方都迷她。她是个非常难得的小人儿，不过有人好好看护着呢，我告诉你——背后有一长串监护人。"

那样的神情、那样的微笑，怎能不信？她的思绪回到了初见它们的那个秋天；一路检点着自己走到今日这般束缚里的台阶——起初多么不为人留意，近来多么迅疾、又多么充满宿命的意味。她看见这年轻军人初现在亨利夫人客厅里的样子；她同他的第一次交谈；还有他们之间那段奇异关系的全部微妙发展——其中掺进了多少因素啊。这个年轻得志的人的殷勤，连同她自己这一边能为他出的种种力，都暗含着一种令人飘飘然的社交权势感；在她的灵魂正被亨利夫人轻蔑的敌意灼痛的当口，对这份殷勤油然而生的感激；随后是那段"友谊"的甜美进袭——这友谊要把他们联结进一种秘密而独一无二的纽带之中，一条不计爱情婚姻那套俗套的纽带，两个平等而相亲的灵魂，在同艰难浊世的搏斗中结成的同盟。

===Original===

> “I have neither family nor powerful friends,” he had written to her a few weeks after their first meeting; “all that I have won, I have won for myself. Nobody ever made ‘interest’ for me but you. You, too, are alone in the world. You, too, have to struggle for yourself. Let us unite our forces﻿—cheer each other, care for each other﻿—and keep our friendship a sacred secret from the world that would misunderstand it. I will not fail you, I will give you all my confidence; and I will try and understand that noble, wounded heart of yours, with its memories, and all those singular prides and isolations that have been imposed on it by circumstance. I will not say, let me be your brother; there is something banal in that; ‘friend’ is good enough for us both; and there is between us a community of intellectual and spiritual interest which will enable us to add new meaning even to that sacred word. I will write to you every day; you shall know all that happens to me; and whatever grateful devotion can do to make your life smoother shall be done.”

===Chinese===

> "我既没有家世，也没有得力的朋友，"初见几个星期后他给她写信说；"我赢得的一切，都是靠我自己赢来的。除了你，从没有人为我打过'招呼'。你，也是孤身在这世上。你，也得为自己奋斗。让我们联合起来吧——互相打气，互相关照——并把我们的友谊当作一个神圣的秘密，瞒过那个会误解它的世界。我不会辜负你，我对你推心置腹；你那颗高贵而受伤的心，连同它的记忆，连同环境强加给它的种种奇特的骄傲与孤僻，我会试着去理解。我不会说，让我做你的兄长；这话未免俗套；'朋友'对我们两个已经足够；而且我们之间还有一种心智与精神上的共同旨趣，能让我们给这个神圣的字眼再添新的意义。我要每天给你写信；我的种种际遇，你都会知道；凡是感激的忠诚所能做的、能叫你的生活更顺遂的事，我都去做。"

===Original===

Five months ago was it, that that letter was written?
Its remembered phrases already rang bitterly in an aching heart. Since it reached her, she had put out all her powers as a woman, all her influence as an intelligence, in the service of the writer.
And now, here she sat in the dark, tortured by a passion of which she was ashamed, before which she was beginning to stand helpless in a kind of terror. The situation was developing, and she found herself wondering how much longer she would be able to control herself or it. Very miserably conscious, too, was she all the time that she was now playing for a reward that was secretly, tacitly, humiliatingly denied her. How could a poor man, with Harry Warkworth’s ambitions, think for a moment of marriage with a woman in her ambiguous and dependent position? Her common sense told her that the very notion was absurd. And yet, since the Duchess’s gossip had given point and body to a hundred vague suspicions, she was no longer able to calm, to master herself.
Suddenly a thought of another kind occurred to her. It added to her smart that Sir Wilfrid, in their meeting at Lady Hubert’s, had spoken to her and looked at her with that slight touch of laughing contempt. There had been no insincerity in that emotion with which she had first appealed to him as her mother’s friend; she did truly value the old man’s good opinion. And yet she had told him lies.
“I can’t help it,” she said to herself, with a little shiver. The story about the biography had been the invention of a moment. It had made things easy, and it had a small foundation in the fact that Lady Henry had talked vaguely of using the letters lent her by Captain Warkworth for the elucidation﻿—perhaps in a *Nineteenth Century* article﻿—of certain passages in her husband’s Indian career.
Jacob Delafield, too. There also it was no less clear to her than to Sir Wilfrid that she had “overdone it.” It was true, then, what Lady Henry said of her﻿—that she had an overmastering tendency to intrigue﻿—to a perpetual tampering with the plain fact?
“Well, it is the way in which such people as I defend themselves,” she said, obstinately, repeating to herself what she had said to Sir Wilfrid Bury.
And then she set against it, proudly, that disinterestedness of which, as she vowed to herself, no one but she knew the facts.

===Chinese===

那封信，是五个月前写的么？

信里那些记得的字句，已在一颗作痛的心里发出苦涩的回响。自收到信起，她便把身为女人的全部本领、身为才智之人的全部影响，都用来为写信的人效力。

而现在，她坐在这黑暗里，受着一种自己引以为耻的激情的煎熬，并且开始在一阵恐惧中束手无策地立在它面前。局面正在发展，她发现自己已经在琢磨：这样的自制力、这样的局面，她还能维持多久。同时她也时时刻刻痛切地意识到：她如今所谋的报偿，正被人暗暗地、心照不宣地、屈辱地回绝着。一个像哈里·沃克沃思那样野心勃勃的穷男人，怎么可能有一刻想到娶一个她这样身份暧昧、寄人篱下的女人？她的常识告诉她，这念头本身就荒唐。然而，自从公爵夫人那番闲话给一百种模糊的疑心安上了锋芒和血肉，她就再也平静不下来、驾驭不住自己了。

忽然，另一种念头又攫住了她。在休伯特夫人（Lady Hubert）家的那次会面里，威尔弗里德爵士对她说话、看她时，带着那一丝含笑的轻蔑——这也给她添了几分刺痛。她当初把他当作母亲的朋友向他求助时，那份情感并非做伪；她是真心珍视这位老人的好评。然而她却对他说了谎。

"我没法子。"她微微打了个寒噤，自语道。那套传记的说辞，原是一时情急编出来的。它把事情变得顺当，而且也有一点小小的根据：亨利夫人确实含糊地提起过，要用沃克沃思上尉借给她的那些信件，来阐明——也许写成一篇《十九世纪》（Nineteenth Century）的文章——她丈夫印度生涯中的某些段落。

还有雅各布·德拉菲尔德。在那件事上，她也同威尔弗里德爵士一样清楚：她"演过头了"。那么，亨利夫人说她的那些话竟是真的——她有一种压倒一切的爱耍手段的倾向——一种对明摆着的事实没完没了动手脚的癖性？

"唉，像我这样的人，自卫就只有这个法子。"她倔强地自语，把对威尔弗里德·伯里爵士说过的话又对自己说了一遍。

随后她又骄傲地把另一件事搬出来对冲——那桩无私，那桩只有她自己知道底细、她对自己发誓过的无私。

===Original===

It was true, what she had said to the Duchess and to Sir Wilfrid. Plenty of people would give her money, would make her life comfortable, without the need for any daily slavery. She would not take it. Jacob Delafield would marry her, if she lifted her finger; and she would not lift it. Dr. Meredith would marry her, and she had said him nay. She hugged the thought of her own unknown and unapplauded integrity. It comforted her pride. It drew a veil over that wounding laughter which had gleamed for a moment through those long lashes of Sir Wilfrid Bury.
Last of all, as she sank into her restless sleep, came the remembrance that she was still under Lady Henry’s roof. In the silence of the night the difficulties of her situation pressed upon and tormented her. What was she to do? Whom was she to trust?
---
“Dixon, how is Lady Henry?”
“Much too ill to come downstairs, miss. She’s very much put out; in fact, miss (the maid lowered her voice), you hardly dare go near her. But she says herself it would be absurd to attempt it.”
“Has Hatton had any orders?”
“Yes, miss. I’ve just told him what her ladyship wishes. He’s to tell everybody that Lady Henry’s very sorry, and hoped up to the last moment to be able to come down as usual.”
“Has Lady Henry all she wants, Dixon? Have you taken her the evening papers?”
“Oh yes, miss. But if you go in to her much her ladyship says you’re disturbing her; and if you don’t go, why, of course, everybody’s neglecting her.”
“Do you think I may go and say good night to her, Dixon?”
The maid hesitated.
“I’ll ask her, miss﻿—I’ll certainly ask her.”
The door closed, and Julie was left alone in the great drawing-room of the Bruton Street house. It had been prepared as usual for the Wednesday﻿—evening party. The flowers were fresh; the chairs had been arranged as Lady Henry liked to have them; the parquet floors shone under the electric light; the Gainsboroughs seemed to look down from the walls with a gay and friendly expectancy.
For herself, Julie had just finished her solitary dinner, still buoyed up while she was eating it by the hope that Lady Henry would be able to come down. The bitter winds of the two previous days, however, had much aggravated her chronic rheumatism.

===Chinese===

她对公爵夫人和威尔弗里德爵士说的是实话。多少人肯给她钱，肯让她过舒服日子，不必受这份日常的奴役。她不要。雅各布·德拉菲尔德，只要她抬一抬手指，就会娶她；她偏不抬。梅雷迪思医生也会娶她，她回绝了他。她把这份无人知晓、无人喝彩的清白搂在怀里。它安慰了她的骄傲。它给方才从威尔弗里德·伯里爵士那长长的睫毛后面闪出过一瞬的、伤人的笑意，蒙上了一层纱。

最后，就在她坠入不安的睡眠之际，还有一个念头浮上来：她仍在亨利夫人的屋檐底下。静夜里，她处境的种种难处一齐压上她、折磨她。她该怎么办？还能信谁？

---

"狄克逊（Dixon），亨利夫人怎么样？"

"病得太重，下不了楼了，小姐。她烦得很；说真的，小姐，"女仆压低了声音，"您简直不敢走近她。不过她自己说，想撑着下楼未免可笑。"

"哈顿得了吩咐没有？"

"得了，小姐。我刚刚把夫人的意思告诉了他。他要向每个人转达：亨利夫人很抱歉，直到最后一刻她还指望能照常下楼呢。"

"亨利夫人要的东西都齐了么，狄克逊？晚报给她送去了吗？"

"送了，小姐。可您若是进屋去得勤了，夫人说您打搅她；您若是不去呢，得，当然，又是人人把她撇在脑后。"

"你看我还能进去道个晚安么，狄克逊？"

女仆迟疑了。

"我去问她，小姐——一定去问。"

门关上了，朱莉独自留在布鲁顿街宅子的大客厅里。客厅照常为星期三晚会做了准备。鲜花是新换的；椅子按亨利夫人喜欢的样子摆好；镶木地板在电灯下闪光；墙上的庚斯博罗（Gainsborough）画作仿佛正带着欢快友好的期待俯视着下方。

至于她自己，朱莉刚独自用完晚餐，吃饭时还一直抱着亨利夫人能下楼来的指望。然而，前两日凛冽的寒风大大加重了那位夫人的老风湿。

===Original===

She was certainly ill and suffering; but Julie had known her make such heroic efforts before this to keep her Wednesdays going that not till Dixon appeared with her verdict did she give up hope.
So everybody would be turned away. Julie paced the drawing-room a solitary figure amid its lights and flowers﻿—solitary and dejected. In a couple of hours’ time all her particular friends would come to the door, and it would be shut against them. “Of course, expect me tonight,” had been the concluding words of her letter of the morning. Several people also had announced themselves for this evening whom it was extremely desirable she should see. A certain eminent colonel, professor at the Staff College, was being freely named in the papers for the Mokembe mission. Never was it more necessary for her to keep all the threads of her influence in good working order. And these Wednesday evenings offered her the occasions when she was most successful, most at her ease﻿—especially whenever Lady Henry was not well enough to leave the comparatively limited sphere of the back drawing-room.
Moreover, the gatherings themselves ministered to a veritable craving in Julie Le Breton﻿—the craving for society and conversation. She shared it with Lady Henry, but in her it was even more deeply rooted. Lady Henry had ten talents in the Scriptural sense﻿—money, rank, all sorts of inherited bonds and associations. Julie Le Breton had but this one. Society was with her both an instinct and an art. With the subtlest and most intelligent ambition she had trained and improved her natural gift for it during the last few years. And now, to the excitement of society was added the excitement of a new and tyrannous feeling, for which society was henceforth a mere weapon to be used.
She fumed and fretted for a while in silence. Every now and then she would pause in front of one of the great mirrors of the room, and look at the reflection of her tall thinness and the trailing satin of her gown.
“The girl﻿—so pretty, in a gossamer sort of way,” The words echoed in her mind, and vaguely, beside her own image in the glass, there rose a vision of girlhood﻿—pale, gold hair, pink cheeks, white frock﻿—and she turned away, miserable, from that conscious, that intellectual distinction with which, in general, she could persuade herself to be very fairly satisfied.

她病得不轻，也实在痛苦；可是朱莉见过她从前为了撑住星期三晚会下过的那些英雄般的狠劲，直到狄克逊带来她的裁夺，她才死了心。

于是人人都得吃闭门羹了。朱莉在灯光与花丛之间来回踱步，形单影只——又孤单，又沮丧。再过两个钟头，她那些至交好友就要陆续上门了，而大门将对他们闭着。"晚上千万等我来。"这是她早晨那封信的收尾话。今晚还有几位宣布要来的人，是她极其需要见的。某位声名显赫的上校、参谋学院的教授，正被报章纷纷点名，说是莫肯贝使命的人选。她的影响之线，从来没有像现在这样样样需要绷在工作状态上。而这些星期三晚会，给了她最成功、最从容的施展场合——尤其每当亨利夫人身体不济、困在后客厅那个相对有限的小天地里时。

何况这样的聚会本身，也滋养着朱莉·勒布雷东的一种真切的渴求——对社交、对谈话的渴求。这一点她与亨利夫人共有，但在她身上扎得还要深。亨利夫人有十样本钱，照《圣经》的意思——钱财、门第、种种与生俱来的纽带与人脉。朱莉·勒布雷东只有这一样。社交于她既是本能，又是艺术。近几年来，她怀着最精细、最聪明的野心，训练、磨砺着这方面的天分。而如今，社交的兴奋之上，又添了一种新的、专横的情感的兴奋——从此社交在她手里不过是一件武器。

她默默烦躁了一阵子。时不时地，她会在客厅一面大镜子前停下，望着镜中自己高瘦的身影和裙裾曳地的缎子长裙。

"那姑娘——那么俊俏，是那种轻飘飘路数的俊俏。"这句话在她脑子里回响，恍惚间，镜中她自己的身影旁边，浮起了一个少女的幻影——苍白、金发、粉颊、白裙——她痛苦地转过身去，避开了那副自觉的、智慧的风仪——平日里，她原可以哄自己对这副风仪相当满意的。

Hutton, the butler, came in to look at the fire.
“Will you be sitting here tonight, miss?”
“Oh no, Hutton. I shall go back to the library. I think the fire in my own room is out.”
“I had better put out these lights, anyway,” said the man, looking round the brilliant room.
“Oh, certainly,” said Julie, and she began to assist him to do so.
Suddenly a thought occurred to her.
“Hutton!” She went up to him and spoke in a lower tone. “If the Duchess of Crowborough comes tonight, I should very much like to see her, and I know she wants to see me. Do you think it could possibly disturb Lady Henry if you were to show her into the library for twenty minutes?”
The man considered.
“I don’t think there could be anything heard upstairs, miss. I should, of course, warn her grace that her ladyship was ill.”
“Well, then, Hutton, please ask her to come in,” said Miss Le Breton, hurriedly. “And, Hutton, Dr. Meredith and Mr. Montresor, you know how disappointed they’ll be not to find Lady Henry at home?”
“Yes, miss. They’ll want to know how her ladyship is, no doubt. I’ll tell them you’re in the library. And Captain Warkworth, miss?﻿—he’s never missed a Wednesday evening for weeks.”
“Oh, well, if he comes﻿—you must judge for yourself, Hutton,” said Miss Le Breton, occupying herself with the electric switches. “I should like to tell them all﻿—the old friends﻿—how Lady Henry is.”
The butler’s face was respectful discretion itself.
“Of course, miss. And shall I bring tea and coffee?”
“Oh no,” said Miss Le Breton, hastily; and then, after reflection, “Well, have it ready; but I don’t suppose anybody will ask for it. Is there a good fire in the library?”
“Oh yes, miss. I thought you would be coming down there again. Shall I take some of these flowers down? The room looks rather bare, if anybody’s coming in.”
Julie colored a little.
“Well, you might﻿—not many. And, Hutton, you’re sure we can’t disturb Lady Henry?”
Hutton’s expression was not wholly confident.
“Her ladyship’s very quick of hearing, miss. But I’ll shut those doors at the foot of the back stairs, and I’ll ask everyone to come in quietly.”
“Thank you, Hutton﻿—thank you. That’ll be very good of you. And, Hutton﻿—”
“Yes, miss.” The man paused with a large vase of white arums in his hand.
“You’ll say a word to Dixon, won’t you? If anybody comes in, there’ll be no need to trouble Lady Henry about it.

"哦，不，赫顿。我回书房去。我自己房里的炉火我想是熄了。"

"那这些灯我最好还是熄了。"管家环顾这满室辉煌，说。

"哦，当然。"朱莉说，便动手帮他熄灯。

忽然她想起一件事。

"赫顿！"她走近他，放低了声音。"要是克劳伯勒公爵夫人今晚来了，我很想见见她，我知道她也想见我。要是您把她领到书房去坐二十分钟，您看会不会搅了亨利夫人？"

管家思忖了一下。

"楼上我想什么也听不见的，小姐。我当然会先向公爵夫人殿下禀明夫人玉体欠安。"

"那么，赫顿，就请她进来吧。"勒布雷东小姐急忙说，"还有，赫顿，梅雷迪思医生和蒙特雷索先生，您知道他们见不着亨利夫人该多失望？"

"知道，小姐。他们准要打听夫人的病况。我会告诉他们您在书房。还有沃克沃思上尉，小姐？——好几个星期了，星期三晚上他从没缺过席。"

"哦，那个——你自己斟酌着办吧，赫顿。"勒布雷东小姐说着，只顾去拨弄电灯开关。"我想把老朋友们——亨利夫人的病况一一告诉他们。"

管家的脸，就是恭谨与缜密本身。

"当然了，小姐。茶和咖啡要端上来吗？"

"哦，不用。"勒布雷东小姐急忙说；想了想又改口，"嗯，备着吧；不过我想不会有人要的。书房里炉火旺吗？"

"旺，小姐。我料着您还要下来，就留着呢。这些花要不要搬一些下去？要是有人进来，那屋子瞧着太素净了。"

朱莉微微红了脸。

"嗯，可以——别太多。还有，赫顿，你敢肯定我们吵不着亨利夫人？"

赫顿的神情可不十分有把握。

"夫人的耳朵尖得很，小姐。不过我会把后楼梯脚那几道门都关上，再请每位客人放轻脚步进来。"

"谢谢你，赫顿——谢谢你。你真是太好。还有，赫顿——"

"是，小姐。"管家手里捧着一大瓶白色马蹄莲，停住了。

"你跟狄克逊说一声，好不好？要是有人进来，用不着事事去回亨利夫人。

===Original===

I can tell her tomorrow.”
“Very good, miss. Dixon will be down to her supper presently.”
The butler departed. Julie was left alone in the now darkened room, lighted only by one lamp and the bright glow of the fire. She caught her breath﻿—suddenly struck with the audacity of what she had been doing. Eight or ten of these people certainly would come in﻿—eight or ten of Lady Henry’s “intimates.” If Lady Henry discovered it﻿—after this precarious truce between them had just been patched up!
Julie made a step towards the door as though to recall the butler, then stopped herself. The thought that in an hour’s time Harry Warkworth might be within a few yards of her, and she not permitted to see him, worked intolerably in heart and brain, dulling the shrewd intelligence by which she was ordinarily governed. She was conscious, indeed, of some profound inner change. Life had been difficult enough before the Duchess had said those few words to her. But since!
Suppose he had deceived her at Lady Hubert’s party! Through all her mounting passion her acute sense of character did not fail her. She secretly knew that it was quite possible he had deceived her. But the knowledge merely added to the sense of danger which, in this case, was one of the elements of passion itself.
“He must have money﻿—of course he must have money,” she was saying, feverishly, to herself. “But I’ll find ways. Why should he marry yet﻿—for years? It would be only hampering him.”
Again she paused before the mirrored wall; and again imagination evoked upon the glass the same white and threatening image﻿—her own near kinswoman﻿—the child of her mother’s sister! How strange! Where was the little gossamer creature now﻿—in what safe haven of money and family affection, and all the spoiling that money brings? From the climbing paths of her own difficult and personal struggle Julie Le Breton looked down with sore contempt on such a degenerate ease of circumstance. She had heard it said that the mother and daughter were lingering abroad for a time on their way home from India. Yet was the girl all the while pining for England, thinking not of her garden, her horse, her pets, but only of this slim young soldier who in a few minutes, perhaps, would knock at Lady Henry’s door, in quest of Aileen Moffatt’s unknown, unguessed-of cousin? These thoughts sent wild combative thrills through Julie’s pulses.

朱莉朝门口迈了一步，像要把管家叫回来，随即又收住了。想到一个钟头之后，哈里·沃克沃思也许就离她几步之遥，而她竟无由相见，这念头在心头、在脑中翻搅得叫她无法忍受，把她平日赖以行事的精明心智都磨钝了。她确实觉出自己内里某种深刻的蜕变。公爵夫人说出那几句话之前，生活已经够难了。可自那以后！

假如他在休伯特夫人的舞会上骗了她！在她节节攀升的激情之下，她那敏锐的识人力并未背弃她。她心底清楚，他骗了她，大有可能。可这一层认识，只不过给危险之感又添一分——而在这种事上，危险感本身正是激情的要素之一。

"他必须有钱——他当然必须有钱。"她狂乱地自语道，"可我会想出法子来。他干吗这么早就结婚——等上多少年不行？眼下结了婚只会绊住他。"

她又在一面镜墙前站住；想象又一次在镜面上唤出那个白衣的、咄咄逼人的形象——她自己近在咫尺的亲属——她姨妈的孩子！多奇怪！那个轻飘飘的小东西如今在哪儿——在哪个金钱与骨肉疼爱的安全港里，享受着金钱带来的一切娇宠？从她自己这条艰辛的、独自攀爬的路上，朱莉·勒布雷东带着锥心的轻蔑，俯视着这样一种堕落的安逸日子。她听说那母女俩从印度回来的路上，还在国外盘桓。可那姑娘是不是时时刻刻苦恋着英国——想的不是她的花园、她的马、她的爱畜，只想着这个瘦削的年轻军人——而这个人，也许再过几分钟，就要来敲亨利夫人的门，寻访艾琳·莫法特（Aileen Moffatt）那位无人知晓、无人疑心的表姐？这些念头，把一浪一浪好斗的战栗送过朱莉的脉管。

===Original===

She turned to one of the old French clocks. How much longer now﻿—till he came?
“Her ladyship would like to see you, miss.”
The voice was Dixon’s, and Julie turned hurriedly, recalling all her self-possession. She climbed some steep stairs, still unmodernized, to Lady Henry’s floor. That lady slept at the back of the house, so as to be out of noise. Her room was an old-fashioned apartment, furnished about the year Queen Victoria came to the throne, with furniture, chintzes, and carpet of the most approved early Victorian pattern. What had been ugly then was dingy now; and its strong mistress, who had known so well how to assimilate and guard the fine decorations and noble pictures of the drawing-rooms, would not have a thing in it touched. “It suits me,” she would say, impatiently, when her stout sister-in-law pleaded placidly for white paint and bright colors. “If it’s ugly, so am I.”
Fierce, certainly, and forbidding she was on this February evening. She lay high on her pillow, tormented by her chronic bronchitis and by rheumatic pain, her brows drawn together, her vigorous hands clasped before her in an evident tension, as though she only restrained herself with difficulty from defying maid, doctor, and her own sense of prudence.
“Well, you have dressed?” she said, sharply, as Julie Le Breton entered her room.
“I did not get your message till I had finished dinner. And I dressed before dinner.”
Lady Henry looked her up and down, like a cat ready to pounce.
“You didn’t bring me those letters to sign?”
“No, I thought you were not fit for it.”
“I said they were to go tonight. Kindly bring them at once.”
Julie brought them. With groans and flinchings that she could not repress, Lady Henry read and signed them. Then she demanded to be read to. Julie sat down, trembling. How fast the hands of Lady Henry’s clock were moving on!
Mercifully, Lady Henry was already somewhat sleepy, partly from weakness, partly from a dose of bromide.
“I hear nothing,” she said, putting out an impatient hand. “You should raise your voice. I didn’t mean you to shout, of course. Thank you﻿—that’ll do. Good night. Tell Hutton to keep the house as quiet as he can. People must knock and ring, I suppose; but if all the doors are properly shut it oughtn’t to bother me. Are you going to bed?”
“I shall sit up a little to write some letters. But﻿—I shan’t be late.”
“Why should you be late?”

===Chinese===

她转向一座古老的法国座钟。还要多久——他才来？

"夫人请小姐过去一趟。"

是狄克逊的声音。朱莉慌忙转身，唤回全部的自持。她爬上一段仍旧古旧的陡楼梯，上到亨利夫人那一层。这位夫人睡在宅子后部，图个清静。她的房间是老式格局，家具、印花布、地毯，全是维多利亚女王登基前后那些年最时兴的款式，当年便已难看，如今更是晦暗；而这位强硬的女主人——她那么懂得如何守护并吸纳客厅里那些精美的陈设与名贵的画作——却容不得房里改动一样东西。"我住着正好。"每当她那位胖胖的弟媳妇好脾气地劝她刷刷白漆、添些亮色时，她就焦躁地说，"屋子丑，我也丑。"

这个二月的夜晚，她确实又凶悍又骇人。她高枕躺着，老支气管炎和风湿痛折磨着她，眉头紧锁，一双有力的手紧紧交叠在身前，显出一股绷紧的劲头，仿佛只是勉强按捺着，才没有向女仆、向医生、向她自己的清醒算计一齐开战。

"怎么，你打扮过了？"朱莉·勒布雷东进屋时，她尖声说。

"我吃完晚饭才得到您的口信。晚饭前我就换好装了。"

亨利夫人把她上上下下打量了一遍，像一只蓄势欲扑的猫。

"那些要我签字的信你没带来？"

"没有，我想您不宜劳神。"

"我说过信今晚要发。劳驾，马上拿来。"

朱莉取来了。亨利夫人一路呻吟、抽搐着，强忍不住，把信一一读过、签了。随后她又要人读书给她听。朱莉坐下，手直发颤。亨利夫人座钟的指针走得多快啊！

所幸亨利夫人已经有些困了，一半因为体弱，一半因为服了一剂溴化盐。

"我什么也听不见。"她伸出一只不耐烦的手，"你该提高点声音。当然，我不是要你喊。谢谢——行了。晚安。告诉赫顿，让他把宅子收拾得越静越好。客人们敲门、按铃，那没法子；可只要各处的门都关严了，吵不着我。你去睡么？"

"我还要坐一会儿，写几封信。不过——我不会弄得太晚。"

"你为什么会晚？"

===Original===

said Lady Henry, tartly, as she turned away.

Julie made her way downstairs with a beating heart. All the doors were carefully shut behind her. When she reached the hall it was already half-past ten o’clock. She hurried to the library, the large panelled room behind the dining-room. How bright Hutton had made it look! Up shot her spirits. With a gay and dancing step she went from chair to chair, arranging everything instinctively as she was accustomed to do in the drawing-room. She made the flowers less stiff; she put on another light; she drew one table forward and pushed its fellow back against the wall. What a charming old room, after all! What a pity Lady Henry so seldom used it! It was panelled in dark oak, while the drawing-room was white. But the pictures, of which there were two or three, looked even better here than upstairs. That beautiful Lawrence﻿—a “red boy” in gleaming satin﻿—that pair of Hoppners, fine studies in blue, why, who had ever seen them before? And another light or two would show them still better.
A loud knock and ring. Julie held her breath. Ah! A distant voice in the hall. She moved to the fire, and stood quietly reading an evening paper.
“Captain Warkworth would be glad if you would see him for a few minutes, miss. He would like to ask you himself about her ladyship.”
“Please ask him to come in, Hutton.”
Hutton effaced himself, and the young man entered, Then Julie raised her voice.
“Remember, please, Hutton, that I *particularly* want to see the Duchess.”
Hutton bowed and retired. Warkworth came forward.
“What luck to find you like this!”
He threw her one look﻿—Julie knew it to be a look of scrutiny﻿—and then, as she held out her hand, he stooped and kissed it.
“He wants to know that my suspicions are gone,” she thought. “At any rate, he should believe it.”
“The great thing,” she said, with her finger to her lip, “is that Lady Henry should hear nothing.”
She motioned her somewhat puzzled guest to a seat on one side of the fire, and, herself, fell into another opposite. A wild vivacity was in her face and manner.
“Isn’t this amusing? Isn’t the room charming? I think I should receive very well”﻿—she looked round her﻿—“in my own house.”
“You would receive well in a garret﻿—a stable,” he said. “But what is the meaning of this? Explain.”
“Lady Henry is ill and is gone to bed. That made her very cross﻿—poor Lady Henry!

朱莉心跳着下楼。身后一道道门都仔细关严了。她到门厅时，已是十点半。她匆匆走进书房——餐厅后面那间镶着壁板的大房间。赫顿把它收拾得多么亮堂！她的精神一下提了起来。她迈着轻快得近乎雀跃的步子，从一把椅子走到另一把椅子，凭着本能，照她在客厅里惯做的样子把一切安顿妥帖。她把花插得随意了些；又开了一盏灯；把一张桌子往前挪了挪，把另一张推回墙边。说到底，多迷人的一间老屋！可惜亨利夫人难得用它！它镶着深色橡木壁板，客厅却是白色的。可墙上那两三幅画，挂在这儿竟比楼上更出彩。那幅漂亮的劳伦斯（Lawrence）——一个穿亮缎的"红衣少年"——那一对霍普纳（Hoppner），两幅蓝色调的妙品，嗬，有谁从前见过它们？再添一两盏灯，还能照得更美。

一阵响亮的敲门声和门铃声。朱莉屏住了呼吸。啊！门厅里远远传来人声。她走到炉边站定，静静地读一份晚报。

"沃克沃思上尉想请问小姐，能否赏几分钟见他一面。他想亲自向小姐打听夫人的病况。"

"请他进来吧，赫顿。"

赫顿退了出去，年轻人走了进来。于是朱莉提高了声音：

"请记住，赫顿，我特别——特别要见公爵夫人。"

赫顿躬身退下。沃克沃思走上前来。

"这样撞见您，多好的运气！"

他向她投去一眼——朱莉知道那是一道审视的目光——随后，趁她伸出手来，他俯身吻了它。

"他想确认我的疑心已经消了。"她想，"无论如何，该让他这么信。"

"头一件要紧事，"她把手指抵在唇上说，"是千万别让亨利夫人听见。"

她把这位多少有点困惑的客人让到炉火一边的座位上，自己落进对面另一把。她脸上、举止里，都是一种狂野的鲜活。

"这不有趣么？这屋子讨人喜欢不？我看，要是在我自己家里——"她环顾四周——"我准会待客待得很好。"

"你就是住阁楼、住马厩，也会待客待得好。"他说，"可这是什么名堂？说给我听。"

"亨利夫人病了，睡下了。这叫她脾气坏得很——可怜的亨利夫人！

===Original===

She thinks I, too, am in bed. But you see﻿—you forced your way in﻿—didn’t you?﻿—to inquire with greater minuteness after Lady Henry’s health.”
She bent towards him, her eyes dancing.
“Of course I did. Will there presently be a swarm on my heels, all possessed with a similar eagerness, or﻿—?”
He drew his chair, smiling, a little closer to her. She, on the contrary, withdrew hers.
“There will, no doubt, be six or seven,” she said, demurely, “who will want personal news. But now, before they come”﻿—her tone changed﻿—“is there anything to tell me?”
“Plenty,” he said, drawing a letter out of his pocket. “Your writ, my dear lady, runs as easily in the City as elsewhere.” And he held up an envelope.
She flushed.
“You have got your allotment? But I knew you would. Lady Froswick promised.”
“And a large allotment, too,” he said, joyously. “I am the envy of all my friends. Some of them have got a few shares, and have already sold them﻿—grumbling. I keep mine three days more on the best advice﻿—the price may go higher yet. But, anyway, there”﻿—he shook the envelope﻿—“there it is﻿—deliverance from debt﻿—peace of mind for the first time since I was a lad at school﻿—the power of going, properly fitted out and equipped, to Africa﻿—*if* I go﻿—and not like a beggar﻿—all in that bit of paper, and all the work of﻿—someone you and I know. Fairy godmother! tell me, please, how to say a proper thank you.”
The young soldier dropped his voice. Those blue eyes which had done him excellent service in many different parts of the globe were fixed with brilliance on his companion; the lines of a full-lipped mouth quivered with what seemed a boyish pleasure. The comfort of money relief was never acknowledged more frankly or more handsomely.
Julie hurriedly repressed him. Did she feel instinctively that there are thanks which it sometimes humiliates a man to remember, lavishly as he may have poured them out at the moment﻿—thanks which may easily count in the long run, not for, but against, the donor? She rather haughtily asked what she had done but say a chance word to Lady Froswick? The shares had to be allotted to somebody. She was glad, of course, very glad, if he were relieved from anxiety.﻿ ﻿…
So did she free herself and him from a burdensome gratitude; and they passed to discussing the latest chances of the Mokembe appointment.

她只当我也睡了。可是你看——是你硬闯进来的——不是么？——为了更细致地探问亨利夫人的贵恙。"

她朝他俯过身去，眼睛闪闪的。

"我当然是。待会儿是不是还有一大群人跟着我的脚后跟涌进来，个个怀着同样的热望？还是说——？"

他微笑着把椅子朝她拉近了些。她反倒把自己的椅子挪远了。

"想必会有六七位，"她端庄地说，"都要来打听第一手消息。不过趁他们没来——"她的语气变了——"有什么可告诉我的么？"

"多着呢。"他从口袋里抽出一封信，"亲爱的夫人，您的手令，在金融城跟在别处一样好使。"他举起一只信封。

她脸红了。

"你的配股拿到了？我就知道您拿得到。弗罗斯威克夫人（Lady Froswick）答应过的。"

"还配了一大笔呢。"他喜滋滋地说，"我的朋友们个个眼红。有几个弄到几股，已经抛了——嘴里还直嘀咕。我依着最可靠的主意，还要再握三天——兴许价钱还能往上走。反正，瞧——"他抖了抖那只信封——"就在这儿——偿清债务——自打做学童以来头一回的心安——还有盘缠，能像模像样、装备齐全地去非洲——若是去得成——而不是像个叫花子——全在这小小一片纸里，而这一切，都是——你我都认得的某个人办的。仙女教母！请教教我，该怎样好好地道一声谢。"

年轻军人放低了声音。那双蓝眼睛——曾在全球许多地方为他立过大功——炯炯地盯住他的同伴；那两片丰厚嘴唇的线条，因一种近乎少年人的欢悦而颤动。金钱解困的舒坦，从来没有被这样坦率、这样体面地承认过。

朱莉急忙拦住了他。她是否本能地觉得，有些感激，男人在倾泻的那一刻尽管慷慨，事后回想起来却往往成了屈辱——而它们天长日久，算起账来，多半不是归向、反倒背离施与的人？她相当倨傲地说，她不过对弗罗斯威克夫人偶尔提了一句罢了，又算得了什么？股份总得配给什么人。她当然高兴——若是这能叫他从此宽了心，她非常高兴。……

就这样，她把自己和他从一份沉重的感激里解脱出来；两人转而讨论起莫肯贝任命的最新胜算。
