"很好。"她竭力说得轻快，"我就拿这话把您扣下。哎呀！我忘了，还有封信必须写。"

于是她假装写起信来，德拉菲尔德则埋进了报纸堆。

## XIII / XIII / 第十三章
===Original===

Julie’s curiosity﻿—passing and perfunctory as it was﻿—concerning the persons and influences that had worked upon Jacob Delafield since his college days, was felt in good earnest by not a few of Delafield’s friends. For he was a person rich in friends, reserved as he generally was, and crotchety as most of them thought him. The mixture of self-evident strength and manliness in his physiognomy with something delicate and evasive, some hindering element of reflection or doubt, was repeated in his character. On the one side he was a robust, healthy Etonian, who could ride, shoot, and golf like the rest of his kind, who used the terse, slangy ways of speech of the ordinary Englishman, who loved the land and its creatures, and had a natural hatred for a poacher; and on another he was a man haunted by dreams and spiritual voices, a man for whom, as he paced his tired horse homeward after a day’s run, there would rise on the grays and purples of the winter dusk far-shining “cities of God” and visions of a better life for man. He read much poetry, and the New Testament spoke to him imperatively, though in no orthodox or accustomed way. Ruskin, and the earlier work of Tolstoy, then just beginning to take hold of the English mind, had affected his thought and imagination, as the generation before him had been affected by Carlyle, Emerson, and George Sand.
This present phase of his life, however, was the outcome of much that was turbulent and shapeless in his first youth. He seemed to himself to have passed through Oxford under a kind of eclipse. All that he could remember of two-thirds of his time there was an immoderate amount of eating, drinking, and sleeping. A heavy animal existence, disturbed by moments of unhappiness and remorse, or, at best, lightened by intervals and gleams of friendship with two or three men who tried to prod him out of his lethargy, and cherished what appeared, to himself in particular, a strange and unreasonable liking for him. Such, to his own thinking, had been his Oxford life, up to the last year of his residence there.
Then, when he was just making certain of an ignominious failure in the final schools, he became more closely acquainted with one of the college tutors, whose influence was to be the spark which should at last fire the clay.

===Chinese===

朱莉的好奇——尽管转瞬即逝、浮光掠影——关于自大学时代以来塑造过雅各布·德拉菲尔德的那些人与影响，其实也是德拉菲尔德的不少朋友们认真怀有的疑问。因为尽管他平日缄默，尽管他们大多觉得他古怪，他却是个朋友很多的人。
他相貌中那种一目了然的刚强与丈夫气，同某种精致、闪避的东西——某种思考或怀疑的滞碍成分——相摊杂；这摊杂又复现于他的性格。一面，他是个健壮爽朗的伊顿学子，骑马、射击、打高尔夫皆不输同侪，言语是普通英国人那种简峭俚俗的调子，爱土地和土地上的生灵，对偷猎者怀着天然的憎恨；另一面，他却是个被梦想和心灵之声萦绕的人——一天围猎之后，当他驱着疲惫的马踱回家去，冬日暮色的灰紫之中，会有远放光华的“上帝之城”向他升起，有人类更美好生活的幻象。
他读很多诗，《新约》以命令的口吻对他说话，虽然那方式绝不正统、绝不循常。罗斯金（Ruskin），和托尔斯泰（Tolstoy）的早期著作——那时刚开始攫住英国人的心——影响了他的思想与想象，正如他的上一代人受卡莱尔（Carlyle）、爱默生（Emerson）和乔治·桑（George Sand）的影响一样。
然而他人生的现阶段，是青春初期种种纷乱无形之物的结果。在他自己看来，他是在一种日蚀之下走过牛津的。那三分之二的时光，他记得的无非是过量的吃、喝、睡。一种沉重的动物性生存，间或被悔恨与不快搅扰，至多被两三段友谊的间隙与微光照亮——那两三个人想把他从昏沉里戳醒，并且怀着一种在他尤其看来古怪而无缘由的喜爱。在他自己想来，直到在牛津的最后一年，他的牛津生活便是如此。
后来，就在他笃定要在毕业大考中一败涂地的时候，他同学院的一位导师渐渐熟络起来；此人的影响，将成为那粒终于点燃陶土的火星。

===Original===

This modest, heroic, and learned man was a paralyzed invalid, owing to an accident in the prime of life. He had lost the use of his lower limbs﻿—“dead from the waist down.” Yet such was the strength of his moral and intellectual life that he had become, since the catastrophe, one of the chief forces of his college. The invalid-chair on which he wheeled himself, recumbent, from room to room, and from which he gave his lectures, was, in the eyes of Oxford, a symbol not of weakness, but of touching and triumphant victory. He gave himself no airs of resignation or of martyrdom. He simply lived his life﻿—except during those crises of weakness or pain when his friends were shut out﻿—as though it were like any other life, save only for what he made appear an insignificant physical limitation. Scholarship, college business or college sports, politics and literature﻿—his mind, at least, was happy, strenuous, and at home in them all. To have pitied him would have been a mere impertinence. While in his own heart, which never grieved over himself, there were treasures of compassion for the weak, the tempted, and the unsuccessful, which spent themselves in secret, simple ways, unknown to his most intimate friends.
This man’s personality it was which, like the branch of healing on bitter waters, presently started in Jacob Delafield’s nature obscure processes of growth and regeneration. The originator of them knew little of what was going on. He was Delafield’s tutor for Greats, in the ordinary college routine; Delafield took essays to him, and occasionally lingered to talk. But they never became exactly intimate. A few conversations of “pith and moment”; a warm shake of the hand and a keen look of pleasure in the blue eyes of the recumbent giant when, after one year of superhuman but belated effort, Delafield succeeded in obtaining a second class; a little note of farewell, affectionate and regretful, when Delafield left the university; an occasional message through a common friend﻿—Delafield had little more than these to look back upon, outside the discussions of historical or philosophical subjects which had entered into their relation as pupil and teacher.
And now the paralyzed tutor was dead, leaving behind him a volume of papers on classical subjects, the reputation of an admirable scholar, and the fragrance of a dear and honored name.

===Chinese===

这位谦冲、英勇而博学的人，因盛年时的一场事故成了瘫痪的病人。他双腿失去了机能——“腰部以下全死了。”然而他道德与心智的生命力是那样强，自那场灾祸以来，他竟成了学院的一股中坚力量。他躺着摇着自己那辆轮椅，从一个房间到另一个房间，讲课也在这轮椅上；在牛津眼里，这轮椅不是软弱的象征，而是动人而辉煌的胜利的象征。他不作顺天认命之态，也不作殉道者之态。他只是照常过他的日子——只在虚弱或剧痛发作、朋友被拒之门外的时候除外——仿佛它同任何人的生活并无二致，只除了他视作无足轻重的身体限制。学问、院务或院际竞技、政治与文学——他的头脑，至少，在其中样样安家，样样奋发而快乐。怜悯他，反倒是一种冒失。而在他自己那颗从不为自身哀伤的心里，藏着对弱者、受诱者、失意者的无边慈悲；这些慈悲以隐秘而质朴的方式流布，连他最亲密的朋友也无从知晓。
正是这个人的品格，如苦水上的医治之枝，不久便在雅各布·德拉菲尔德的天性里启动了隐晦的生长与更新。启动这一切的人自己却几乎不知发生了什么。按学院常规，他是德拉菲尔德古典学的导师；德拉菲尔德把文章送来，偶尔留下谈谈。但他们始终算不得亲密。几次“有分量、有关系”的谈话；当德拉菲尔德经过一年超人却迟来的努力，终于考取二等学位时，那位卧姿巨人蓝眼睛里一阵热切的喜悦，一次热烈的握手；德拉菲尔德离校时一张惜别的短简，深情而怅惆；偶尔托共同的朋友捎句问候——除了那些渗入师生之谊的历史或哲学问题的讨论，德拉菲尔德能回首的，就只有这些了。
如今，那位瘫痪的导师已经故去，身后留下一卷古典学论文、一个可敬学者的令名，和一个亲爱的、受人尊崇的名字的余馨。

===Original===

His pupils had been many; they counted among the most distinguished of England’s youth; and all of them owed him much. Few people thought of Delafield when the list of them was recited; and yet, in truth, Jacob’s debt was greater than any; for he owed this man nothing less than his soul.
No doubt the period at Oxford had been rather a period of obscure conflict than of mere idleness and degeneracy, as it had seemed to be. But it might easily have ended in physical and moral ruin, and, as it was﻿—thanks to Courtenay﻿—Delafield went out to the business of life, a man singularly master of himself, determined to live his own life for his own ends.
In the first place, he was conscious, like many other young men of his time, of a strong repulsion towards the complexities and artificialities of modern society. As in the forties, a time of social stir was rising out of a time of stagnation. Social settlements were not yet founded, but the experiments which led to them were beginning. Jacob looked at the life of London, the clubs and the country-houses, the normal life of his class, and turned from it in aversion. He thought, sometimes, of emigrating, in search of a new heaven and a new earth, as men emigrated in the forties.
But his mother and sister were alone in the world﻿—his mother a somewhat helpless being, his sister still very young and unmarried. He could not reconcile it to his conscience to go very far from them.
He tried the bar, amid an inner revolt that only increased with time. And the bar implied London, and the dinners and dances of London, which, for a man of his family, the probable heir to the lands and moneys of the Chudleighs, were naturally innumerable. He was much courted, in spite, perhaps because, of his oddities; and it was plain to him that with only a small exercise of those will-forces he felt accumulating within him, most of the normal objects of ambition were within his grasp. The English aristocratic class, as we all know, is no longer exclusive. It mingles freely with the commoner world on apparently equal terms. But all the while its personal and family cohesion is perhaps greater than ever. The power of mere birth, it seemed to Jacob, was hardly less in the England newly possessed of household suffrage than in the England of Charles James Fox’s youth, though it worked through other channels.

===Chinese===

他教过的学生很多；其中不乏英国青年之翘楚；人人都受他深恩。人们历数这些名字时，很少有人想到德拉菲尔德；然而实际上，雅各布欠他的比谁都多；因为他欠这个人的，不是别的，正是他的灵魂。
无疑，牛津那段岁月，与其说是懒惰与沉沦，不如说是一场隐晦的挣扎，尽管表面看来是前者。但它本可轻易地以身心俱毁收场；而实际的结局——多亏了考特尼（Courtenay）——是德拉菲尔德走向人生的战场时，成了一个格外能主宰自己的人，决意为自己的目标过自己的生活。
首先，同当时许多青年一样，他对现代社会的繁复与矫饰怀着强烈的反感。正如上一个四十年代，一个社会骚动的时代正从停滞的时代中升起。社会服务团尚未创立，但导向它们的种种实验已经开始。雅各布看着伦敦的生活、俱乐部和乡间宅邸——他那个阶层的常规生活——怀着厌恶掉头而去。他有时想移居海外，去寻一片新天新地，如同四十年代的人们远走他乡那样。
可他的母亲和妹妹在世上孤苦无依——母亲是个多少不能自立的人，妹妹还十分年轻，尚未出嫁。他无法说服自己的良心远走她们身边。
他试了律师这一行，内心却日甚一日地反抗着。律师意味着伦敦，意味着伦敦的宴席和舞会——对一个出身他这种门第的人，一个查德利家地产和钱财的可能继承人来说，这些自然数不胜数。尽管——或者说正因为——他古怪，他颇受人追捧；他也看得明白，只需略略动用一下他感到正在体内积蓄的那些意志力，寻常的进取目标大多唾手可得。英国的贵族阶层，众所周知，已不再封闭。它以表面平等的条款同平民世界自由往来。但与此同时，它的个人与家族凝聚力或许更胜于以往。在雅各布看来，单凭出身的力量，在这个刚获得户主选举权的英国，比起查尔斯·詹姆斯·福克斯（Charles James Fox）青年时代的英国，并未衰减多少，只是另走别的渠道罢了。

===Original===

And for the persons in command of this power, a certain *appareil de vie* was necessary, taken for granted. So much income, so many servants, such and such habits﻿—these things imposed themselves. Life became a soft and cushioned business, with an infinity of layers between it and any hard reality﻿—a round pea in a silky pod.
And he meanwhile found himself hungry to throw aside these tamed and trite forms of existence, and to penetrate to the harsh, true, simple things behind. His imagination and his heart turned towards the primitive, indispensable labors on which society rests﻿—the life of the husbandman, the laborer, the smith, the woodman, the builder; he dreamed the old, enchanted dream of living with nature; of becoming the brother not of the few, but of the many. He was still reading in chambers, however, when his first cousin, the Duke, a melancholy semi-invalid, a widower, with an only son tuberculous almost from his birth, arrived from abroad. Jacob was brought into new contact with him. The Duke liked him, and offered him the agency of his Essex property. Jacob accepted, partly that he might be quit of the law, partly that he might be in the country and among the poor, partly for reasons, or ghosts of reasons, unavowed even to himself. The one terror that haunted his life was the terror of the dukedom. This poor, sickly lad, the heir, with whom he soon made warm friends, and the silent, morbid Duke, with the face of Charles V at St. Just﻿—he became, in a short time, profoundly and pitifully attached to them. It pleased him to serve them; above all did it please him to do all he could, and to incite others to do all they could, to keep these two frail persons cheered and alive. His own passionate dread lest he should suddenly find himself in their place, gave a particular poignancy to the service he was always ready to render them of his best.
The Duke’s confidence in him had increased rapidly. Delafield was now about to take over the charge of another of the Duke’s estates, in the Midlands, and much of the business connected with some important London property was also coming into his hands. He had made himself a good man of business where another’s interests were concerned, and his dreams did no harm to the Duke’s revenues.

===Chinese===

而执掌这种权力的人，就必须有一套 appareil de vie*（生活的排场），这被视为理所当然。这么多进项，这么多仆人，这样那样的习惯——这些东西自行强加于人。生活变成一桩柔软垫衬的事务，同任何坚硬的现实之间隔着无穷的夹层——一颗圆润的豌豆，裹在丝质的豆荚里。
而与此同时，他发现自己渴望抛开这些驯化而陈腐的生存形式，穿透到背后那些粗粝、真实、质朴的东西。他的想象与心，转向社会赖以立足的那些原始而不可或缺的劳作——农夫、雇工、铁匠、樵夫、工匠的生活；他做着那个古老的、着魔般的梦：与自然共同生活；不做少数人的兄弟，而做多数人的兄弟。然而，他还正在律师事务所里见习，他的表兄、公爵——一个忧郁的半病人，一个鳏夫，带着一个几乎生来就患痨病的独生子——从国外回来了。雅各布同他有了新的接触。公爵喜欢他，提出把埃塞克斯产业的经管事务交给他。雅各布接受了，一半为脱离法律行当，一半为住到乡下、住到穷人中间，还有一半——为着一些连他自己也不肯承认的理由，或者说理由的幽灵。萦绕他一生的那桩恐怖，就是对公爵之位的恐怖。那个可怜的、病弱的少年——继承人，他很快同他结成了热络的友情；还有那位沉默、忧郁、长着圣朱斯特画中查理五世（Charles V）那张脸的公爵——没过多久，他便对这两个人生出深切的、怜惜的依恋。为他们效劳使他快乐；尤其快乐的，是竭尽自己所能，并激励旁人竭尽所能，让这两个孱弱的人活得开心、活得下去。他自己那股炽烈的恐惧——生怕有朝一日突然发现自己站到他们的位置上——给他时刻准备着为他们奉献的最佳效劳，添了一种特别的酸楚。
公爵对他的信任与日俱增。德拉菲尔德如今即将接手掌管公爵在中部的另一处产业，同伦敦几宗重要地产相关的事务，也有许多正转入他手中。在涉及他人利益的事务上，他已把自己练成一把好手，而他的梦想也丝毫没有亏损公爵的收益。

===Original===

He gave, indeed, a liberal direction to the whole policy of the estate, and, as he had said to Julie, the Duke did not forbid experiments.
As to his own money, he gave it away as wisely as he could, which is, perhaps, not saying very much for the schemes and quixotisms of a young man of eight-and-twenty. At any rate, he gave it away﻿—to his mother and sister first, then to a variety of persons and causes. Why should he save a penny of it? He had some money of his own, besides his income from the Duke. It was disgusting that he should have so much, and that it should be, apparently, so very easy for him to have indefinitely more if he wanted it.
He lived in a small cottage, in the simplest, plainest way compatible with his work and with the maintenance of two decently furnished rooms for any friend who might chance to visit him. He read much and thought much. But he was not a man of any commanding speculative or analytic ability. It would have been hard for him to give any very clear or logical account of himself and his deepest beliefs. Nevertheless, with every year that passed he became a more remarkable *character*﻿—his will stronger, his heart gentler. In the village where he lived they wondered at him a good deal, and often laughed at him. But if he had left them, certainly the children and the old people would have felt as though the sun had gone out.
In London he showed little or nothing of his peculiar ways and pursuits; was, in fact, as far as anybody knew﻿—outside half a dozen friends﻿—just the ordinary, well-disposed young man, engaged in a business that everyone understood. With Lady Henry, his relations, apart from his sympathy with Julie Le Breton, had been for some time rather difficult. She made gratitude hard for one of the most grateful of men. When the circumstances of the Hubert Delafields had been much straitened, after Lord Hubert’s death, Lady Henry had come to their aid, and had, in particular, spent fifteen hundred pounds on Jacob’s school and college education. But there are those who can make a gift burn into the bones of those who receive it. Jacob had now saved nearly the whole sum, and was about to repay her.

===Chinese===

确实，他为产业的整个经营方针注入了开明的取向；而且正如他对朱莉说过的，公爵并不禁止实验。
至于他自己的钱，他尽可能明智地散了出去——对一个二十八岁的年轻人的种种计划和堂吉诃德式行径来说，这话说得或许不算高明。无论如何，他散了出去——先给母亲和妹妹，再给形形色色的人与事业。他何必要攒下一个铜板？除了从公爵那里得的薪酬，他自己还有些钱。他竟有这么多，而且显然，只要他愿意，还可以轻而易举地得到无限多——这实在叫人恶心。
他住在一座小村舍里，以同他的工作相称、同两间像样家具的客房相称的最简单、最朴素的方式生活——客房是为可能偶然来访的朋友备的。他读得多，想得多。但他并不是个思辨或分析能力出众的人。要他把自己和自己最深的信念讲个清楚、讲出条理来，怕是很难。然而，年复一年，他成了愈发出众的一个“人物”——意志愈坚，心肠愈软。在他住的村子里，人们对他啧啧称奇，也常笑话他。可他若离开，村里的孩子和老人准会觉得，太阳熄灭了。
在伦敦，他几乎从不显露他那些独特的做派与追求；事实上，据任何人所知——六七个朋友之外——他不过是个随和的普通青年，干着一桩人人都懂的营生。同亨利夫人的关系，撇开他对朱莉·勒布雷东的同情不谈，一度颇为难处。她能把感恩变难，叫他这个天下最知恩的人难以感恩。休伯特·德拉菲尔德家在休伯特勋爵身后境况窘迫时，亨利夫人曾出手援助，尤其为雅各布的中学和大学教育花了一千五百英镑。然而世上偏有一种人，能让礼物烧进受赠者的骨头。雅各布如今已攒下几乎全数，正要偿还她。

===Original===

Meanwhile his obligation, his relationship, and her age made it natural, or rather imperative, that he should be often in her house; but when he was with her the touch of arrogant brutality in her nature, especially towards servants and dependants, roused him almost to fury. She knew it, and would often exercise her rough tongue merely for the pleasure of tormenting him.
No sooner, therefore, had he come to know the fragile, distinguished creature whom Lady Henry had brought back with her one autumn as her companion than his sympathies were instantly excited, first by the mere fact that she was Lady Henry’s dependant, and then by the confidence, as to her sad story and strange position, which she presently reposed in him and his cousin Evelyn. On one or two occasions, very early in his acquaintance with her, he was a witness of some small tyranny of Lady Henry’s towards her. He saw the shrinking of the proud nature, and the pain thrilled through his own nerves as though the lash had touched himself. Presently it became a joy to him whenever he was in town to conspire with Evelyn Crowborough for her pleasure and relief. It was the first time he had ever conspired, and it gave him sometimes a slight shock to see how readily these two charming women lent themselves, on occasion, to devices that had the aspect of intrigue, and involved a good deal of what, in his own case, he would have roundly dubbed lying. And, in truth, if he had known, they did not find him a convenient ally, and he was by no means always in their confidence.
Once, about six months after Julie’s arrival in Bruton Street, he met her on a spring morning crossing Kensington Gardens with the dogs. She looked startlingly white and ill, and when he spoke to her with eager sympathy her mouth quivered and her dark eyes clouded with tears. The sight produced an extraordinary effect on a man large-hearted and simple, for whom women still moved in an atmosphere of romance. His heart leaped within him as she let herself be talked with and comforted. And when her delicate hand rested in his as they said goodbye, he was conscious of feelings﻿—wild, tumultuous feelings﻿—to which, in his walk homeward through the spring glades of the park, he gave impetuous course.
Romantic, indeed, the position was, for romance rests on contrast.

===Chinese===

而他的人情、亲情，加之她的年事，都使他理当——不如说必须——常去她的宅邸；可同她在一起时，她天性中那股倨傲的粗横，尤其对仆人和依附者的那副样子，几乎能把他的怒火激起。她知道这一点，还常常单为了折磨他的乐趣，放肆她的毒舌。
因此，当他认识那个某年秋天被亨利夫人带回家做女伴的纤秀出众的人儿时，他的同情立刻被激发起来——先是单单因为她也是亨利夫人的依附者，随后又因为她不久便把她的伤心史和奇特处境向他和他的表妹伊芙琳吐露了。结识她之初，他有一两回亲眼目睹亨利夫人对她的小小暴虐。他看见那颗骄傲的心如何退缩，而那痛楚竟窜过他自己的神经，仿佛鞭子抽在了他自己身上。不久，每逢进城，同伊芙琳·克罗伯勒合谋为她谋快乐、谋喘息，成了他的一桩乐事。这是他平生第一次搞合谋；这两个可爱的人儿有时那样轻而易举地投身于那些形同权术的手段，还夹带着许多——若出之于他——他要直言不讳称作撒谎的东西，这有时叫他微微心惊。而实情是——倘若他知晓的话——她们并不觉得他是个顺手的同谋，他也绝没有分享她们全部的机密。
有一次，大约朱莉来布鲁顿街六个月后，一个春天的早晨，他遇见她带着狗穿过肯辛顿花园（Kensington Gardens）。她白得吓人，一副病容；当他热切地对她表示同情，她的嘴唇颤抖起来，黑眼睛蒙上泪水。这一幕对这个心地宽厚、性情质朴——在他眼里，女人仍行走于罗曼司的氛围之中——的男人，产生了异乎寻常的效果。当她任由自己被劝解、被宽慰时，他的心在胸膛里欢跳。道别时，她那只纤手停在他手中，他意识到了种种情感——狂放而翻腾的情感——归家路上穿过园中春日的林间幽径，他任其奔涌了一路。
这处境的确罗曼蒂克，因为罗曼司正建立在反差之上。

===Original===

Jacob, who knew Julie Le Breton’s secret, was thrilled or moved by the contrasts of her existence at every turn. Her success and her subjection; the place in Lady Henry’s circle which Lady Henry had, in the first instance, herself forced her to take, contrasted with the shifts and evasions, the poor, tortuous ways by which, alas! she must often escape Lady Henry’s later jealousy; her intellectual strength and her most feminine weaknesses; these things stirred and kept up in Jacob a warm and passionate pity. The more clearly he saw the specks in her glory, the more vividly did she appear to him a princess in distress, bound by physical or moral fetters not of her own making. None of the wellborn, well-trained damsels who had been freely thrown across his path had so far beguiled him in the least. Only this woman of doubtful birth and antecedents, lonely, sad, and enslaved amid what people called her social triumphs, stole into his heart﻿—beautified by what he chose to consider her misfortunes, and made none the less attractive by the fact that as he pursued, she retreated; as he pressed, she grew cold.
When, indeed, after their friendship had lasted about a year, he proposed to her and she refused him, his passion, instead of cooling, redoubled. It never occurred to him to think that she had done a strange thing from the worldly point of view﻿—that would have involved an appreciation of himself, as a prize in the marriage market, he would have loathed to make. But he was one of the men for whom resistance enhances the value of what they desire, and secretly he said to himself, “Persevere!” When he was repelled or puzzled by certain aspects of her character, he would say to himself:
“It is because she is alone and miserable. Women are not meant to be alone. What soft, helpless creatures they are!﻿—even when intellectually they fly far ahead of us. If she would but put her hand in mine I would so serve and worship her, she would have no need for these strange things she does﻿—the doublings and ruses of the persecuted.” Thus the touches of falsity that repelled Wilfrid Bury were to Delafield’s passion merely the stains of rough travel on a fair garment.
But she refused him, and for another year he said no more. Then, as things got worse and worse for her, he spoke again﻿—ambiguously﻿—a word or two, thrown out to sound the waters.

===Chinese===

雅各布深知朱莉·勒布雷东的秘密，她处境中的种种反差，处处令他震撼、令他动情。她的成功与她的屈从；她在亨利夫人圈子里的位置——那位置当初本是亨利夫人亲手逼她占下的——同后来为躲避亨利夫人的妒火，她——唉！——不得不常使的那些闪转腾挪、那些可怜而曲折的路数两相对照；她的智性之强与她十足的女性之弱；这些在雅各布心里搅起并维系着一份炽热的怜惜。他越清楚看见她荣光中的斑驳，她在他眼中便越发活脱脱是一位落难的公主，被并非出于己手的枷锁——有形的或无形的——捆缚着。那些门第优良、教养完备、被随意抛到他路上的小姐们，没有一个曾稍稍打动过他。唯独这个出身来历可疑的女人，孤独、忧伤，在人们所谓的社交凯旋中为奴，悄悄潜入了他的心——被他认定是不幸的遭遇衬得美了，又因他追她退、他逼她冷，而愈发教他着迷。
果然，当他们的友情持续了约一年后，他向她求婚而遭拒绝，他的激情非但没有冷却，反而加倍了。他从未想过，从世俗眼光看，她做了一件怪事——那样想就得掂量他自己这个婚姻市场上的奖品，这是他深恶痛绝的。但他属于那种抗拒反而抬高所愿之物身价的男人，于是暗暗对自己说：“坚持下去！”当他被她性格的某些侧面推拒或迷惑时，他便对自己说：
“这是因为她孤独、她不幸。女人本不该孤身一人。她们是何等柔软无助的造物！——哪怕智性上远远飞在我们前头。她若肯把手放进我的手心，我必这样侍奉她、敬慕她，她便再不需要这些她所做的怪事——那些受迫害者的迂回与计谋。”于是，那令威尔弗里德·伯里生厌的几丝虚伪，在德拉菲尔德的激情眼里，不过是华美衣裳上风尘仆仆的溃痕。
可她拒绝了他，此后一年，他再没提起。后来，她的处境每况愈下，他又开口了——闪烁其词——一两句话，抛出来试探水深。

===Original===

Her manner of silencing him on this second occasion was not what it had been before. His suspicions were aroused, and a few days later he divined the Warkworth affair.
When Sir Wilfrid Bury spoke to him of the young officer’s relations to Mademoiselle Le Breton, Delafield’s stiff defence of Julie’s prerogatives in the matter masked the fact that he had just gone through a week of suffering, wrestling his heart down in country lanes; a week which had brought him to somewhat curious results.
In the first place, as with Sir Wilfrid, he stood up stoutly for her rights. If she chose to attach herself to this man, whose business was it to interfere? If he was worthy and loved her, Jacob himself would see fair play, would be her friend and supporter.
But the scraps of gossip about Captain Warkworth which the Duchess﻿—who had disliked the man at first sight﻿—gathered from different quarters and confided to Jacob were often disquieting. It was said that at Simla he had entrapped this little heiress, and her obviously foolish and incapable mother, by devices generally held to be discreditable; and it had taken two angry guardians to warn him off. What was the state of the case now no one exactly knew; though it was shrewdly suspected that the engagement was only dormant. The child was known to have been in love with him; in two years more she would be of age; her fortune was enormous, and Warkworth was a poor and ambitious man.
There was also an ugly tale of a civilian’s wife in a hill station, referring to a date some years back; but Delafield did not think it necessary to believe it.
As to his origins﻿—there again, Delafield, making cautious inquiries, came across some unfavorable details, confided to him by a man of Warkworth’s own regiment. His father had retired from the army immediately after the Mutiny, broken in health, and much straitened in means. Himself belonging to a family of the poorer middle class, he had married late, a good woman not socially his equal, and without fortune. They settled in the Isle of Wight, on his half-pay, and harassed by a good many debts. Their two children, Henry and Isabella, were then growing up, and the parents’ hopes were fixed upon their promising and good-looking son. With difficulty they sent him to Charterhouse and a “crammer.”

===Chinese===

这第二回，她封住他口的方式已不同于前。他起了疑心，几天之后，他窥破了沃克沃思其事。
当威尔弗里德·伯里爵士同他谈起那年轻军官与勒布雷东小姐的关系时，德拉菲尔德为朱莉在此事上的自主之权所作的强硬辩护，掩盖了一个事实：他刚刚熬过痛苦的一周，在乡间小路上把自己的心摁倒在地；那一周，把他带到了相当古怪的结果跟前。
首先，同威尔弗里德爵士一样，他坚决维护她的权利。她若选中这个人，旁人有什么资格干涉？此人若配得上她、爱她，雅各布自当主持公道，做她的朋友与后盾。
但是公爵夫人——她对那人一见便生厌恶——从各处搜来、又吐给雅各布听的那些关于沃克沃思上尉的闲言碎语，常令人心绪不宁。据说在西姆拉，他用公认不光彩的手段，网住了那位小小的女继承人及其显然愚昧无能的母亲；两位怒不可遏的监护人出面，才把他吓退。如今此案究竟如何，谁也说不清；不过人们精明地疑心，那婚约只是蛰伏。据说那孩子已爱上了他；再过两年她便成年；她的财产巨大，而沃克沃思是个又穷又野心勃勃的人。
还有一桩关于某山地驿站一位文官妻子的丑陋传闻，指的是几年前的事；德拉菲尔德却觉得不必信它。
至于他的出身——在这一层上，德拉菲尔德谨慎打探，又撞见几段不利详情，是沃克沃思同一团的一位军人吐露给他的。他父亲在印度兵变之后即刻退伍，健康垮了，手头也窘迫至极。他自己出身一个较穷的中产家庭，结婚很晚，娶了个善良的女人，社会地位不般配，也没有财产。两人靠半薪在怀特岛（Isle of Wight）安家，还被一身债务纠缠。他们的两个孩子，亨利和伊莎贝拉，那时渐渐长大，父母的希望全押在那个前程看好、相貌堂堂的儿子身上。他们勉力供他进了查特豪斯公学（Charterhouse），又进了一家“应试补习班”。

===Original===

The boy coveted a “crack” regiment; by dint of mustering all the money and all the interest they could, they procured him his heart’s desire. He got unpardonably into debt; the old people’s resources were lessening, not expanding; and ultimately the poor father died broken down by the terror of bankruptcy for himself and disgrace for Henry. The mother still survived, in very straitened circumstances.
“His sister,” said Delafield’s informant, “married one of the big London tailors, whom she met first on the Ryde pier. I happen to know the facts, for my father and I have been customers of his for years, and one day, hearing that I was in Warkworth’s regiment, he told me some stories of his brother-in-law in a pretty hostile tone. His sister, it appears, has often financed him of late. She must have done. How else could he have got through? Warkworth may be a fine, showy fellow when there’s fighting about. In private life he’s one of the most self-indulgent dogs alive. And yet he’s ashamed of the sister and her husband, and turns his back on them whenever he can. Oh, he’s not a person of nice feeling, is Warkworth﻿—but, mark my words, he’ll be one of the most successful men in the army.”
There was one side. On the other was to be set the man’s brilliant professional record; his fine service in this recent campaign; the bulldog defence of an isolated fort, which insured the safety of most important communications; contempt of danger, thirst, exposure; the rescue of a wounded comrade from the glacis of the fort, under a murderous fire; facts, all of them, which had fired the public imagination and brought his name to the front. No such acts as these could have been done by any mere self-indulgent pretender.
Delafield reserved his judgment. He set himself to watch. In his inmost heart there was a strange assumption of the right to watch, and, if need be, to act. Julie’s instinct had told her truly. Delafield, the individualist, the fanatic for freedom﻿—he, also, had his instinct of tyranny. She should not destroy herself, the dear, weak, beloved woman! He would prevent it.
---
Thus, during these hours of transition, Delafield thought much of Julie. Julie, on the other hand, had no sooner said good night to him after the conversation described in the last chapter than she drove him from her thoughts﻿—one might have said, with vehemence.

===Chinese===

这孩子一心想进个“王牌”团队；老两口拼凑起所能凑到的一切钱财与门路，替他弄到了他心心念念的东西。他毫无顾忌地欠下债；老人的财源日削月割，不见增益；终于，可怜的父亲死于恐惧——怕自己破产，怕亨利蒙羞。母亲还健在，境况十分拮据。
“他姐姐，”德拉菲尔德的线人说，“嫁给了伦敦一位大牌裁缝，两人是在赖德（Ryde）码头上初识的。这些事我恰好知道，因为我父亲和我做他家的主顾多年了；有一天他听说我在沃克沃思的团里，便用相当敌意的口气给我讲了他内弟的一些事。看来，他姐姐近来常接济他。想必如此。不然他怎么过得下去？有仗打的时候，沃克沃思或许是个漂亮神气的家伙。私生活里，他是天下最自我放纵的一条狗。可他还为姐姐姐夫害臊，能躲就躲。哦，沃克沃思可不是个感情细腻的人——不过，记住我的话，他会成为陆军里最春风得意的人物之一。”
这是一面。另一面则须摆上此人辉煌的职业履历；他在最近这场战役中的出色战功；那场对一座孤堡的斗牛犬式死守，保住了至关紧要的交通线；对危险、干渴、暴露的蔑视；在杀人火力之下，从堡垒斜堤上救回一名负伤的战友——桩桩件件，都曾点燃公众的想象，把他的名字推到了最前面。单是一个自我放纵的骗子，做不出这样的事。
德拉菲尔德悬置了他的判断。他开始留心监视。在他内心深处，潜藏着一种古怪的、自命有权监视、必要时还有权出手的假设。朱莉的直觉没有骗她。德拉菲尔德，这个个人主义者，这个自由的信徒——他也有他的专横本能。她不该毁掉自己，这个可爱的、软弱的、被深爱的女人！他要阻止它。
---
就这样，在这变动的时日里，德拉菲尔德频频想着朱莉。朱莉那边呢，上一章所述的谈话一结束，同他道过晚安，她便把他从心头驱走了——简直可以说，是猛力驱走的。

===Original===

---
The *Times* of the following morning duly contained the announcement of the appointment of Captain Warkworth, D.S.O., of the Queen’s Grays, to the command of the military mission to Mokembe recently determined on by her Majesty’s government. The mission would proceed to Mokembe as soon as possible, but of two officers who on the ground of especial knowledge would form part of it, under Captain Warkworth’s command, one was at present in Canada and the other at the Cape. It would, therefore, hardly be possible for the mission to start from the coast for the interior before the beginning of May. In the same paper certain promotions and distinctions on account of the recent Mahsud campaign were reprinted from the *Gazette*. Captain Henry Warkworth’s brevet majority was among them.
The *Times* leader on the announcement pointed out that the mission would be concerned with important frontier questions, still more with the revival of the prestige of England in regions where a supine government had allowed it to wither unaccountably. Other powers had been playing a filching and encroaching game at the expense of the British lion in these parts, and it was more than time that he should open his sleepy eyes upon what was going on. As to the young officer who was to command the mission, the great journal made a few civil though guarded remarks. His record in the recent campaign was indeed highly distinguished; still it could hardly be said that, take it as a whole, his history so far gave him a claim to promotion so important as that which he had now obtained.
Well, now he had his chance. English soldiers had a way of profiting by such chances. The *Times* courteously gave him the benefit of the doubt, prophesying that he would rise to the occasion and justify the choice of his superiors.
The Duchess looked over Julie’s shoulder as she read.
“Schemer,” she said, as she dropped a kiss on the back of Julie’s neck, “I hope you’re satisfied. The *Times* doesn’t know what to make of it.”
Julie put down the paper with a glowing cheek.
“They’ll soon know,” she said, quietly.
“Julie, do you believe in him so much?”
“What does it matter what I think? It is not I who have appointed him.”
“Not so sure,” laughed the Duchess. “As if he would have had a chance without you. Whom did he know last November when you took him up?”
Julie moved to and fro, her hands behind her.

次日上午的《泰晤士报》（Times）照例刊出公告：女王灰骑兵团的沃克沃思上尉、D.S.O.（杰出服务勋章）获得者，受命统率女王陛下政府新近决意派遣的赴莫肯贝军事使团。使团将尽快前往莫肯贝，但以专门学识入选使团、在沃克沃思上尉麾下任职的两名军官，一名远在加拿大，另一名在开普。因此，使团最早也难在五月初之前从海岸启程深入内陆。同一版面上，还从《宪报》（Gazette）转载了因最近马赫苏德战役而颁行的若干晋升与叙奖。亨利·沃克沃思上尉的荣誉少校衔亦在其中。
《泰晤士报》就此公告所发的社论指出，该使团将涉重大边界问题，更将致力于重振英国在那些地区的威望——先前一个懒怠的政府竟任其莫名凋萎。列强一直在这些地方趁英国狮子打盹行窃攘蚕食之能事，狮子早该睁开惺忪睡眼，看看周围了。至于统率使团的这位年轻军官，这份大报作了几句客气而存保留的评论。他在最近战役中的表现确实极为出众；不过，通盘而论，还不能说他过往的经历足以为他挣得这一次如此重要的晋升之权。
好吧，如今他得了机会。英国军人向来有抓住这类机会的本事。《泰晤士报》客气地往好里替他说了话，预言他必能不负此时机，证明上级知人之明。
朱莉读报时，公爵夫人从她肩后凑过来看。
“小阴谋家，”她说着，在朱莉颈后轻轻落下一吻，“我希望你满意了。《泰晤士报》都看不懂这是怎么回事呢。”
朱莉放下报纸，脸颊灼灼发亮。
“他们很快就会懂的，”她平静地说。
“朱莉，你这么信他？”
“我怎么想有什么要紧？任命他的又不是我。”
“那可说不准，”公爵夫人笑道。“仿佛没有你，他还能有机会似的。去年十一月你提携他的时候，他认识谁来着？”
朱莉背着手，在屋里走来走去。

===Original===

The tremor on her lip, the light in her eye showed her sense of triumph.
“What have I done,” she said, laughing, “but push a few stones out of the way of merit?”
“Some of them were heavy,” said the Duchess, making a little face. “Need I invite Lady Froswick any more?”
Julie threw her arms about her.
“Evelyn, what a darling you’ve been! Now I’ll never worry you again.”
“Oh, for some people I would do ten times as much!” cried the Duchess. “But, Julie, I wish I knew why you think so well of this man. I﻿—I don’t always hear very nice things about him.”
“I dare say not,” said Julie, flushing. “It is easy to hate success.”
“No, come, we’re not as mean as that!” cried the Duchess. “I vow that all the heroes I’ve ever known had a ripping time. Julie”﻿—she kissed her friend impulsively﻿—“Julie, don’t like him too much. I don’t think he’s good enough.”
“Good enough for what?” said Julie’s bitter voice. “Make yourself easy about Captain Warkworth, Evelyn; but please understand﻿—*anything* is good enough for me. Don’t let your dear head be troubled about my affairs. They are never serious, and nothing counts﻿—except,” she added, recklessly, “that I get a little amusement by the way.”
“Julie,” cried the Duchess, “as if Jacob﻿—”
Julie frowned and released herself; then she laughed.
“Nothing that one ever says about ordinary mortals applies to Mr. Delafield. He is, of course, hors concours.”
“Julie!”
“It is you, Evelyn, who make me *méchante*. I could be grateful﻿—and excellent friends with that young man﻿—in my own way.”
The Duchess sighed, and held her tongue with difficulty.
---
When the successful hero arrived that night for dinner he found a solitary lady in the drawing-room.
Was this, indeed, Julie Le Breton﻿—this soft, smiling vision in white?
He expected to have found a martyr, pale and wan from the shock of the catastrophe which had befallen her, and, even amid the intoxication of his own great day, he was not easy as to how she might have taken his behavior on the fatal night. But here was someone, all joy, animation, and indulgence﻿—a glorified Julie who trod on air. Why? Because good-fortune had befallen her friend? His heart smote him. He had never seen her so touching, so charming. Since the incubus of Lady Henry’s house and presence had been removed she seemed to have grown years younger.

===Chinese===

唇边的颤动、眼中的光焰，显出她的得胜之感。
“我做了什么，”她笑着说，“不过把几块石头从英才的路上搬开罢了？”
“有几块可不轻呢，”公爵夫人做了个鬼脸。“弗罗斯威克夫人，我还用再请她吗？”
朱莉张开双臂掳住她。
“伊芙琳，你可真是宝贝！往后我再不折腾你了。”
“哦，为了有些人，十倍的事我也肯干！”公爵夫人叫道。“可是朱莉，我真想知道，你为什么这样看好这个人。我——我听到关于他的话，可不全好听。”
“那自然，”朱莉涨红着脸说。“恨成功，最容易不过了。”
“不，得了，咱们还没那么刻薄！”公爵夫人叫道。“我敢起誓，我认得的英雄个个都过得快活透顶。朱莉”——她冲动地吻了吻朋友——“朱莉，别太喜欢他了。我觉得他配不上。”
“配不上什么？”朱莉的声音透着苦味。“关于沃克沃思上尉，你就放一百个心吧，伊芙琳；不过请你明白——对我，什么都配得上。别让你那颗可爱的脑袋为我的事烦心。我的事从来不当真，什么都算不得数——只除了，”她不顾一切地补上一句，“一路上我还捞到一点乐子。”
“朱莉，”公爵夫人叫道，“难道雅各布——”
朱莉皱起眉，挣脱开身子；随即又笑了。
“凡是人们谈论凡人的话，没有一句适用于德拉菲尔德先生。他自然属 hors concours*（超轶辈流，不入比较之列）。”
“朱莉！”
“是你，伊芙琳，把我弄得这么 méchante*（促狭）。我本可以心怀感激——同那位年轻人做顶好的朋友——用我自己的方式。”
公爵夫人叹了口气，好不容易才管住舌头。
---
当晚，那位得胜的英雄前来赴宴，发现客厅里只有一位孤零零的女士。
这当真是朱莉·勒布雷东么——这一袭白衣、笑靥温柔的幻影？
他原以为会见到一位殉道者，被降临于她的灾祸打击得苍白憔悴；即便在自己大喜之日的酺然之中，他对她会如何计较那个致命之夜自己的行径，也并没有把握。可眼前这位，满心欢喜，神采飞扬，一派娇纵——一个踩着云朵、遍体生辉的朱莉。为什么？因为好运落在了她朋友头上？他心里微微刺痛。他从没见过她这样动人、这样迷人。亨利夫人的宅邸与身影这具梦魇既已移去，她仿佛年轻了好几岁。

===Original===

A white muslin dress of her youth, touched here and there by the Duchess’s maid, replaced the familiar black satin. When Warkworth first saw her he paused unconsciously in surprise.
Then he advanced to meet her, broadly smiling, his blue eyes dancing.
“You got my note this morning?”
“Yes,” she said, demurely. “You were much too kind, and much﻿—much too absurd. I have done nothing.”
“Oh, nothing, of course.” Then, after a moment: “Are you going to tie me to that fiction, or am I to be allowed a little decent sincerity? You know perfectly well that you have done it all. There, there; give me your hand.”
She gave it, shrinking, and he kissed it joyously.
“Isn’t it jolly!” he said, with a schoolboy’s delight as he released her hand. “I saw Lord M﻿—﻿— this morning.” He named the Prime Minister. “Very civil, indeed. Then the Commander-in-Chief﻿—and Montresor gave me half an hour. It is all right. They are giving me a capital staff. Excellent fellows, all of them. Oh, you’ll see, I shall pull it through﻿—I shall pull it through. By George! it is a chance!”
And he stood radiant, rubbing his hands over the blaze.
The Duchess came in accompanied by an elderly cousin of the Duke’s, a white-haired, black-gowned spinster, Miss Emily Lawrence﻿—one of those single women, travelled, cultivated, and good, that England produces in such abundance.
“Well, so you’re going,” said the Duchess, to Warkworth. “And I hear that we ought to think you a lucky man.”
“Indeed you ought, and you must,” he said, gayly. “If only the climate will behave itself. The blackwater fever has a way of killing you in twenty-four hours if it gets hold of you; but short of that﻿—”
“Oh, you will be quite safe,” said the Duchess. “Let me introduce you to Miss Lawrence. Emily, this is Captain Warkworth.”
The elderly lady gave a sudden start. Then she quietly put on her spectacles and studied the young soldier with a pair of intelligent gray eyes.
---
Nothing could have been more agreeable than Warkworth at dinner. Even the Duchess admitted as much.

===Chinese===

她少女时代的一袭白色细棉布裙——经公爵夫人的女仆这里那里点拨修饰过——取代了那身熟悉的黑缎。沃克沃思初见她时，不由自主地怔了一怔。
随即他含笑大步迎上前去，蓝眼睛闪闪跳动。
“今早收到我的短简了？”
“收到了，”她娴静地说。“你太客气了，也太——太荒唐了。我什么也没做。”
“哦，当然，什么也没做。”顿了片刻他又说：“你打算把我拴死在这套虚文上呢，还是许我一点像样的真心？你心里清楚得很，这一切都是你做成的。好了，好了；把手给我。”
她缩了缩，还是把手给了他，他欢天喜地地吻了它。
“这不是妙极了么！”他松开她的手，带着学童般的欢悦说。“今早我见了M——勋爵。”他说出首相的名字。“客气得很。然后是总司令——蒙特勒索还给了我半个钟头。都妥了。他们给我配了一流的随员班子。个个都是好样的。哦，你瞧吧，我会把这差事办成——我会办成的。老天！这可是个机会！”
他光彩焕发地站着，对着炉火搓手。
公爵夫人进来了，陪着公爵家一位年长的表亲——白发黑衣的老小姐，埃米莉·劳伦斯小姐（Miss Emily Lawrence）——英国所盛产的那类独身女子之一：游历广、有教养、心地好。
“好啊，你要走了，”公爵夫人对沃克沃思说。“我还听说，我们该把你当作幸运儿呢。”
“该当如此，也必须如此，”他快活地说。“只要那边的气候守规矩。黑水热这东西，一旦沾上你，二十四小时内就能要你的命；不过只要躲开它——”
“哦，你会平安无事的，”公爵夫人说。“我来给你介绍劳伦斯小姐。埃米莉，这位是沃克沃思上尉。”
老小姐猛然一怔。随后她静静地戴上眼镜，用一双聪慧的灰眼睛，细细打量这位年轻军人。
---
晚宴上的沃克沃思，再可亲不过了。连公爵夫人也承认这一点。

===Original===

He talked easily, but not too much, of the task before him; told amusing tales of his sporting experience of years back in the same regions which were now to be the scene of his mission; discussed the preparations he would have to make at Denga, the coast town, before starting on his five weeks’ journey to the interior; drew the native porter and the native soldier, not to their advantage, and let fall, by the way, not a few wise or vivacious remarks as to the races, resources, and future of this illimitable and mysterious Africa﻿—this cavern of the unknown, into which the waves of white invasion, one upon another, were now pressing fast and ceaselessly, towards what goal, only the gods knew.
A few other men were dining; among them two officers from the staff of the Commander-in-Chief. Warkworth, much their junior, treated them with a skilful deference; but through the talk that prevailed his military competence and prestige appeared plainly enough, even to the women. His good opinion of himself was indeed sufficiently evident; but there was no crude vainglory. At any rate, it was a vainglory of youth, ability, and good looks, ratified by these budding honors thus fresh upon him, and no one took it amiss.
When the gentlemen returned to the drawing-room, Warkworth and Julie once more found themselves together, this time in the Duchess’s little sitting-room at the end of the long suite of rooms.
“When do you go?” she asked him, abruptly.
“Not for about a month.” He mentioned the causes of delay.
“That will bring you very late﻿—into the worst of the heat?” Her voice had a note of anxiety.
“Oh, we shall all be seasoned men. And after the first few days we shall get into the uplands.”
“What do your home people say?” she asked him, rather shyly. She knew, in truth, little about them.
“My mother? Oh, she will be greatly pleased. I go down to the Isle of Wight for a day or two to see her tomorrow. But now, dear lady, that is enough of my wretched self. You﻿—do you stay on here with the Duchess?”
She told him of the house in Heribert Street. He listened with attention.
“Nothing could be better. You will have a most distinguished little setting of your own, and Lady Henry will repent at leisure. You won’t be lonely?”
“Oh no!” But her smile was linked with a sigh.
He came nearer to her.
“You should never be lonely if I could help it,” he said, in a low voice.

===Chinese===

他从容地、却不过多地谈着面前的使命；讲些有趣的往事，说他多年前在同一带地方打猎的经历——那地方如今将成为他使命的舞台；商量着在海岸城镇登加（Denga）须作的准备，再从那里踏上五个星期深入内陆的旅程；他勾勒土著挑夫与土著士兵的样相，绝无恭维之意；还顺口漏下不少关于这无边而神秘的非洲——这未知的洞窟——的种族、资源与未来的隽语妙谈；白色入侵的浪潮正一波接一波，急促而不歇地涌进这洞窟，涌向什么归宿，只有诸神知道。
同桌还有几位男士，其中两名是总司令参谋部的军官。沃克沃思资历远低于他们，应对却得体而恭谨；但在满席谈笑之间，他的军事才干与声望显豁地透出来，连女人们也看得出。他的自许确实够明显；但并无粗俗的自夸。无论如何，那是一种青春、才干和俊美的自负，又得到这些新落其身的初绽荣衔的加冕，谁也不以为忤。
男宾们回到客厅后，沃克沃思和朱莉又到了一处，这回是在长长一列房间尽头、公爵夫人的那间小起居室里。
“你什么时候走？”她突兀地问。
“大约一个月内不走。”他说了几句耽搁的缘由。
“那就很晚了——正赶上最毒的暑热？”她的声音里有一丝焦灼。
“哦，我们都将是熬惯了的人。头几天过后就进高地了。”
“你家里人怎么说？”她问得有些羞怯。说实话，她对他们所知甚少。
“我母亲？哦，她会高兴坏的。明天我就去怀特岛，看她一两天。好了，亲爱的夫人，我这个可厌的自己说得够多了。你呢——你就同公爵夫人住下去？”
她把赫里伯特街的房子讲给他听。他凝神听着。
“再好不过了。你将拥有自己的一方最别致的小天地，亨利夫人会慢慢悔去的。你不会寂寞吧？”
“哦，不会！”可她的微笑里系着一丝叹息。
他向她挨近了些。
“只要我能做主，你就永远不该寂寞，”他低声说。

===Original===

“When people are nameless and kinless,” was her passionate reply, in the same undertone as his, “they must be lonely.”
He looked at her with eagerness. She lay back in the firelight, her beautiful brow and eyes softly illuminated. He felt within him a sudden snapping of restraints. Why﻿—why refuse what was so clearly within his grasp? Love has many manners﻿—many entrances﻿—and many exits.
“When will you tell me all that I want to know about you?” he said, bending towards her with tender insistence. “There is so much I have to ask.”
“Oh, some time,” she said, hurriedly, her pulses quickening. “Mine is not a story to be told on a great day like this.”
He was silent a moment, but his face spoke for him.
“Our friendship has been a beautiful thing, hasn’t it?” he said, at last, in a voice of emotion. “Look here!” He thrust his hand into his breast-pocket and half withdrew it. “Do you see where I carry your letters?”
“You shouldn’t﻿—they are not worthy.”
“How charming you are in that dress﻿—in that light! I shall always see you as you are tonight.”
A silence. Excitement mounted in their veins. Suddenly he stooped and kissed her hands. They looked into each other’s eyes, and the seconds passed like hours.
Presently, in the nearer drawing-room, there was a sound of approaching voices and they moved apart.
“Julie, Emily Lawrence is going,” said the Duchess’s voice, pitched in what seemed to Julie a strange and haughty note. “Captain Warkworth, Miss Lawrence thinks that you and she have common friends﻿—Lady Blanche Moffatt and her daughter.”
Captain Warkworth murmured some conventionality, and passed into the next drawing-room with Miss Lawrence.
Julie rose to her feet, the color dying out of her face, her passionate eyes on the Duchess, who stood facing her friend, guiltily pale, and ready to cry.

===Chinese===

“人若无名无姓、无亲无族，”她以同样低的声音、同样激切的语气回答，“就注定寂寞。”
他热切地望着她。她向后倚在炉光里，秀美的额头和眼睛被柔和地照亮。他感到体内咔的一声，束缚断了。为什么——为什么拒绝这明明白白握在掌中之物？爱有许多姿态——许多入口——也有许多出口。
“你什么时候把你的一切讲给我听？”他俯身向她，温柔而不肯放松。“我要问的太多了。”
“哦，改天吧，”她急急地说，脉息加快。“我那段事，不是在今天这样的好日子里讲的。”
他沉默了片刻，脸却替他诉说着。
“我们的友情一直是件美好的事，是不是？”他终于开口，声音动了情。“你看！”他把手插进胸袋，半抽出来。“看见我把你的信放在哪儿了吗？”
“你不该——它们不配。”
“你穿这身衣裳——在这片光里——多迷人啊！在我眼里，你将永远是今晚这个样子。”
一阵沉默。兴奋在两人的血脉里升腾。他忽然俯身，吻了她的双手。他们彼此凝视着对方的眼睛，一秒一秒，长得如同时光。
片刻后，近处客厅传来渐渐走近的人声，两人分开了。
“朱莉，埃米莉·劳伦斯要走了，”公爵夫人的声音传进来，在朱莉听来，那声调古怪而倨傲。“沃克沃思上尉，劳伦斯小姐认为你们有共同的朋友——布兰奇·莫法特夫人（Lady Blanche Moffatt）和她的女儿。”
沃克沃思上尉含糊应了一句客套，同劳伦斯小姐走进隔壁客厅去了。
朱莉站起身来，脸上的血色褪尽，一双炽烈的眼睛盯住公爵夫人——公爵夫人正对着她的朋友立着，白得心虚，眼看就要哭出来。

## XIV / 第十四章
===Original===

On the morning following these events, Warkworth went down to the Isle of Wight to see his mother. On the journey he thought much of Julie. They had parted awkwardly the night before. The evening, which had promised so well, had, after all, lacked finish and point. What on earth had that tiresome Miss Lawrence wanted with him? They had talked of Simla and the Moffatts. The conversation had gone in spurts, she looking at him every now and then with eyes that seemed to say more than her words. All that she had actually said was perfectly insignificant and trivial. Yet there was something curious in her manner, and when the time came for him to take his departure she had bade him a frosty little farewell.
She had described herself once or twice as a *great* friend of Lady Blanche Moffatt. Was it possible?
But if Lady Blanche, whose habits of sentimental indiscretion were ingrained, *had* gossiped to this lady, what then? Why should he be frowned on by Miss Lawrence, or anybody else? That malicious talk at Simla had soon exhausted itself. His present appointment was a triumphant answer to it all. His slanderers﻿—including Aileen’s ridiculous guardians﻿—could only look foolish if they pursued the matter any further. What “trap” was there﻿—what mésalliance? A successful soldier was good enough for anybody. Look at the first Lord Clyde, and scores besides.
The Duchess, too. Why had she treated him so well at first, and so cavalierly after dinner? Her manners were really too uncertain.
What was the matter, and why did she dislike him? He pondered over it a good deal, and with much soreness of spirit. Like many men capable of very selfish or very cruel conduct, he was extremely sensitive, and took keen notice of the fact that a person liked or disliked him.
If the Duchess disliked him it could not be merely on account of the Simla story, even though the old maid might conceivably have given her a jaundiced account. The Duchess knew nothing of Aileen, and was little influenced, so far as he had observed her, by considerations of abstract justice or propriety, affecting persons whom she had never seen.
No, she was Julie’s friend, the little wilful lady, and it was for Julie she ruffled her feathers, like an angry dove.
So his thoughts had come back to Julie, though, indeed, it seemed to him that they were never far from her.

===Chinese===

这些事过后的第二天早晨，沃克沃思南下怀特岛（Isle of Wight）去看母亲。旅途中他满脑子都是朱莉。前一夜他们分得别别扭扭。那个晚上，本来开头那么好，到头来却收得平淡、没了劲头。那个讨厌的劳伦斯小姐找他到底要干什么？他们谈了西姆拉，谈了莫法特家母女。谈话一阵一阵的，她时不时看他一眼，那眼神仿佛话里有话。她实际说出口的，却全是些无关痛痒的琐碎。可她的神气里有点什么蹊跷；到他该告辞的时候，她给他的是一个冷冰冰的小小道别。

她曾一两回自说是布兰奇·莫法特夫人（Lady Blanche Moffatt）的至交。可能么？

可万一布兰奇·莫法特夫人——她那感伤轻率的积习是根深蒂固的——当真同这位小姐嚼过舌头，那又怎样？他凭什么该受劳伦斯小姐、或者任何人的白眼？西姆拉那段恶毒闲话，早自己烂掉了。他眼下的任命，就是对那一切的响亮回答。他的诽谤者——包括艾琳那班可笑的监护人——若再纠缠下去，只能自讨没趣。哪里有什么"圈套"——哪里有什么门不当户不对？一个得胜的军人，配谁都不辱没。看看克莱德勋爵（Lord Clyde），再看看此外多少人。

还有公爵夫人。她起先待他那么好，晚饭后却那样怠慢。她的态度实在太没个准了。

到底是怎么回事，她为什么讨厌他？他为此想了许久，心里酸酸的。同许多干得出极端自私或极端残忍行径的男人一样，他极其敏感，谁喜欢他、谁不喜欢他，他一清二楚，件件记在心上。

公爵夫人若讨厌他，决不会仅仅因为西姆拉那段传闻，尽管那老姑娘向她添油加醋一番也并非不可能。公爵夫人对艾琳一无所知；而且就他对她的观察而言，她也绝少为着抽象的是非体统——何况牵涉的还是她从未谋面的人——动什么心思。

不，她是朱莉的朋友，那个任性的小夫人；她是为着朱莉才竖起羽毛，像一只动怒的鸽子。

于是他的思绪又回到了朱莉身上——其实，在他看来，这思绪何曾离开过她。

===Original===

As he looked absently from the train windows on the flying landscape, Julie’s image hovered between him and it﻿—a magic sun, flooding soul and senses with warmth. How unconsciously, how strangely his feelings had changed towards her! That coolness of temper and nerve he had been able to preserve towards her for so long was, indeed, breaking down. He recognized the danger, and wondered where it would lead him. What a fascinating, sympathetic creature!﻿—and, by George! what she had done for him!
Aileen! Aileen was a little sylph, a pretty child-angel, white-winged and innocent, who lived in a circle of convent thoughts, knowing nothing of the world, and had fallen in love with him as the first man who had ever made love to her. But this intelligent, full-blooded woman, who could understand at a word, or a half word, who had a knowledge of affairs which many a high-placed man might envy, with whom one never had a dull moment﻿—this courted, distinguished Julie Le Breton﻿—his mind swelled with half-guilty pride at the thought that for six months he had absorbed all her energies, that a word from him could make her smile or sigh, that he could force her to look at him with eyes so melting and so troubled as those with which she had given him her hands﻿—her slim, beautiful hands﻿—that night in Grosvenor Square.
How freedom became her! Dependency had dropped from her, like a cast-off cloak, and beside her fresh, melancholy charm, the airs and graces of a child of fashion and privilege like the little Duchess appeared almost cheap and trivial. Poor Julie! No doubt some social struggle was before her. Lady Henry was strong, after all, in this London world, and the solider and stupider people who get their way in the end were not, she thought, likely to side with Lady Henry’s companion in a quarrel where the facts of the story were unquestionably, at first sight, damaging to Miss Le Breton. Julie would have her hours of bitterness and humiliation; and she would conquer by boldness, if she conquered at all﻿—by originality, by determining to live her own life. That would preserve for her the small circle, if it lost her the large world. And the small circle was what she lived for, what she ought, at any rate, to live for.
It was not likely she would marry. Why should she desire it?

===Chinese===

他心不在焉地望着车窗外飞掠而过的风景，朱莉的形象悬浮在他与风景之间——一轮有魔力的太阳，把温暖灌满他的灵魂与感官。他对她的感情，变得多么不知不觉、多么不可思议啊！那份他长时间保持得住的冷静——脾性与神经上的冷静——真的在崩溃了。他认出了这危险，也想知道它会把他引向哪里。多么迷人、多么知心的人儿！——而且，天哪！她为他做过多少事！

艾琳！艾琳是个小小的气精，一个漂亮的小天使，白翅无辜，活在修道院式的一圈念想里，不谙世事，把他当第一个向她求爱的男人爱上了。可这一个呢——这智能饱满、血肉充盈的女人，一句话、半个字就能会意，那份通达世务的本事连多少身居高位的人都眼红，同她在一起没有一刻沉闷——这众星捧月的、出色的朱莉·勒布雷东——想到六个月来她把全部的精力都倾在了他一个人身上，想到他一句话就能叫她笑、叫她叹，想到他能逼她用那样的眼睛望他——那样荡漾、那样慌乱的眼睛，像那天晚上在格罗夫纳广场她把双手递给他时——那双纤秀美丽的手——他的心便胀满了半是负疚的得意。

自由多么衬她！依附从她身上脱落下去，像一件甩掉的斗篷；比起她那清新鲜明的哀婉风致，小公爵夫人那种时髦宠儿的做派和娇态，几乎显得廉价而琐碎。可怜的朱莉！她面前无疑还有一场社交上的苦斗。亨利夫人在这座伦敦城里毕竟根基深厚；而那些最终总能得遂所愿的更倔、更笨的人，朱莉想，多半不会站在亨利夫人女伴一边——这一案的事实，乍看之下明明对勒布雷东小姐不利。朱莉自有她的苦时辰、她的屈辱时辰；而她若赢，也只能凭胆气赢——凭她的独树一帜，凭她横下心过自己的日子。这会替她保住那个小圈子，哪怕失掉那个大世界。而小圈子正是她为之活着的——至少是她该为之活着的。

她不大会嫁人。她又何必想嫁？

===Original===

From any blundering tragedy a woman of so acute a brain would, of course, know how to protect herself. But within the limits of her life, why should she refuse herself happiness, intimacy, love?
His heart beat fast; his thoughts were in a whirl. But the train was nearing Portsmouth, and with an effort he recalled his mind to the meeting with his mother, which was then close upon him.
He spent nearly a week in the little cottage at Sea View, and Mrs. Warkworth got far more pleasure than usual, poor lady, out of his visit. She was a thin, plain woman, not devoid of either ability or character. But life had gone hardly with her, and since her husband’s death what had been reserve had become melancholy. She had always been afraid of her only son since they had sent him to Charterhouse, and he had become so much “finer” than his parents. She knew that he must consider her a very ignorant and narrow-minded person; when he was with her she was humiliated in her own eyes, though as soon as he was gone she resumed what was in truth a leading place among her own small circle.
She loved him, and was proud of him; yet at the bottom of her heart she had never absolved him from his father’s death. But for his extravagance, and the misfortunes he had brought upon them, her old general would be alive still﻿—pottering about in the spring sunshine, spudding the daisies from the turf, or smoking his pipe beneath the thickening trees. Silently her heart still yearned and hungered for the husband of her youth; his son did not replace him.
Nevertheless, when he came down to her with this halo of glory upon him, and smoked up and down her small garden through the mild spring days, gossiping to her of all the great things that had befallen him, repeating to her, word for word, his conversation with the Prime Minister, and his interview with the Commander-in-Chief, or making her read all the letters of congratulation he had received, her mother’s heart thawed within her as it had not done for long.

===Chinese===

以她那样机敏的头脑，自然懂得怎样护住自己，不叫任何笨拙的悲剧沾身。可在她生活的界限之内，她又何苦拒绝幸福、拒绝亲密、拒绝爱？

他的心怦怦跳；思绪乱成一团。可火车快到朴次茅斯（Portsmouth）了，他费了把劲，把心思唤回到即将到来的同母亲的会面上。

他在海景镇（Sea View）那所小村舍里住了将近一星期，沃克沃思太太——可怜的夫人——从他的来访里得到的快乐远比平日多。她是个瘦削、平常的女人，能力与性情都不缺。可生活亏待了她；自丈夫死后，昔日的矜持郁结成了忧伤。打独子被送进卡特公学（Charterhouse）那时候起——他变得比父母"高贵"多了——她就一直怕他。她知道，他必定当她是个无知、心眼窄的人；他一在家，她就在自己眼里矮了半截；可他一走，她在自己那个小圈子里便照旧稳坐头把交椅。

她爱他，也以他为傲；可心底里，她从未赦免他对她丈夫之死的那份干系。若不是他挥霍无度、若不是他带回家来的那些祸事，她的老将军如今还活着——还在春天的太阳底下转悠，把小雏菊从草皮里剜掉，或在渐密的树荫下抽烟斗。她心里至今还默默地恋着、饿着想着她年轻时的丈夫；儿子替代不了他。

然而，当他顶着那圈光荣的晕轮下来看她，在温煦的春日里在她的小园子中边抽烟边踱步，把她撞上的种种大事讲给她听，把同首相的谈话、同总司令的会面一字一句复述给她，或是叫她读他收到的所有贺信，她那颗母亲的心化了，多年来没有这样化过。

===Original===

Her ears told her that he was still vain and a boaster; her memory held the indelible records of his past selfishness; but as he walked beside her, his fair hair blown back from his handsome brow, and eyes that were so much younger than the rest of the face, his figure as spare and boyish now as when he had worn the colors of the Charterhouse eleven, she said to herself, in that inward and unsuspected colloquy she was always holding with her own heart about him, that if his father could have seen him now he would have forgiven him everything. According to her secret Evangelical faith, God “deals” with every soul he has created﻿—through joy or sorrow, through good or evil fortune. He had dealt with herself through anguish and loss. Henry, it seemed, was to be moulded through prosperity. His good fortune was already making a better man of him.
Certainly he was more affectionate and thoughtful than before. He would have liked to give her money, of which he seemed to have an unusual store; but she bade him keep what he had for his own needs. Her own little bit of money, saved from the wreck of their fortunes, was enough for her. Then he went into Ryde and brought her back a Shetland shawl and a new tablecloth for her little sitting-room, which she accepted with a warmer kiss than she had given him for years.
He left her on a bright, windy morning which flecked the blue Solent with foam and sent the clouds racing to westward. She walked back along the sands, thinking anxiously of the African climate and the desert hardships he was going to face. And she wondered what significance there might be in the fact that he had written twice during his stay with her to a Miss Le Breton, whose name, nevertheless, he had not mentioned in their conversations. Well, he would marry soon, she supposed, and marry well, in circles out of her ken. With the common prejudice of the English middle class, she hoped that if this Miss Le Breton were his choice, she might be only French in name and not in blood.
Meanwhile, Warkworth sped up to London in high spirits, enjoying the comforts of a good conscience.
He drove first to his club, where a pile of letters awaited him﻿—some letters of congratulation, others concerned with the business of his mission. He enjoyed the first, noticing jealously who had and who had not written to him; then he applied himself to the second.

===Chinese===

耳朵告诉她，他还是那么虚荣、那么爱吹；记忆里存着他昔日自私的铁证；可当他走在她身旁，漂亮的额头上浅色的头发向后吹开，一双比脸上其余部分年轻得多的眼睛，身形清瘦，还是当年穿卡特公学板球队服时那副少年模样——她便在她那场与自己的心永不停歇、无人知晓的对谈里对自己说：他父亲若能看见他今天这样，会饶了他一切的。照她那种秘而不宣的福音派信仰，上帝要"处置"他造的每一个灵魂——或借喜乐，或借忧患；或借顺境，或借逆境。他处置她，用的是丧痛。而亨利，看来是要用顺境来捏塑了。他的好运已经在把他捏成个更好的人。

的确，他比从前亲热了、体贴了。他想给她钱——他手头似乎格外宽裕；可她叫他留着自己用。她自己那一小笔从家产残骸里省出来的钱，够用了。于是他进城到赖德（Ryde），给她买回一条设得兰披肩，一块小客厅用的新桌布；她收下时给他的那个吻，比多年来给他的都热。

他走的那天早晨，天色晴朗，风大，蓝色的索伦特海峡缀满白沫，云彩赛着往西跑。她沿着沙滩往回走，焦心地想着他要去面对的非洲气候和荒漠劳苦。她还琢磨着一件怪事：同她住着的这几天里，他给一位勒布雷东小姐写过两回信，而这个名字，他在谈话里从没提起过。唔，他快娶亲了吧，她想，还会娶得很好，娶进她够不着的圈子。带着英国中层阶级共有的偏见，她只盼着：若这位勒布雷东小姐真是他选中的人，但愿她只是名字像法国人，血统并不是。

与此同时，沃克沃思兴冲冲赶回伦敦，享受着良心清白的舒适。

他先驱车到俱乐部，那里一措信等着他——一些是贺信，另一些是特派团的公事。他享用着前者，斤斤计较地留意着谁写了信、谁没写；然后埋头对付后者。

===Original===

His mind worked vigorously and well; he wrote his replies in a manner that satisfied him. Then throwing himself into a chair, with a cigar, he gave himself up to the close and shrewd planning of the preparations necessary for his five weeks’ march, or to the consideration of two or three alternative lines of action which would open before him as soon as he should find himself within the boundaries of Mokembe. Some five years before, the government of the day had sent a small expedition to this Debatable Land, which had failed disastrously, both from the diplomatic and the military points of view. He went backward and forward to the shelves of the fine “Service” library which surrounded him, taking down the books and reports which concerned this expedition. He buried himself in them for an hour, then threw them aside with contempt. What blunders and short-sight everywhere! The general public might well talk of the stupidity of English officers. And blunders so easily avoided, too! It was sickening. He felt within himself a fullness of energy and intelligence, a perspicacity of brain which judged mistakes of this kind unpardonable.
As he was replacing some of the books he had been using in the shelves, the club began to fill up with men coming in to lunch. A great many congratulated him; and a certain number who of old had hardly professed to know him greeted him with cordiality. He found himself caught in a series of short but flattering conversations, in which he bore himself well﻿—neither over-discreet nor too elate. “I declare that fellow’s improved,” said one man, who might certainly have counted as Warkworth’s enemy the week before, to his companion at table. “The government’s been beastly remiss so far. Hope he’ll pull it off. Ripping chance, anyway. Though what they gave it to him for, goodness knows! There were a dozen fellows, at least, did as well as he in the Mahsud business. And the Staff-College man had a thousand times more claim.”
Nevertheless, Warkworth felt the general opinion friendly, a little surprised, no doubt, but showing that readiness to believe in the man coming to the front, which belongs much more to the generous than to the calculating side of the English character. Insensibly his mental and moral stature rose.

===Chinese===

他的头脑开动得又猛又顺；回信写得自己都满意。随后他往椅子里一躺，点上一支雪茄，专心致志、精打细算地筹划那五个星期行军的种种准备，或者盘算一旦踏进莫肯贝地界便会摊在他面前的两三条行动路线。约莫五年前，当届政府曾往这块"争议之地"派过一支小小的远征队，无论外交上还是军事上都败得稀里哗啦。他在环绕四周的那间精美的"军事"藏书室里来回穿梭，把同那次远征有关的书和报告一本本抽下来。他埋在里面读了一个钟头，随后鄙夷地把它们扔到一边。处处是昏招、是短视！怪不得老百姓要讲英国军官的蠢。而且那些昏招又那么容易避掉！真叫人恶心。他觉出自己浑身心智充沛，脑子亮得能照见一切，这样的失误在他看来不可饶恕。

他正把用过的几本书归回架上，俱乐部里渐渐坐满了来用午餐的男人。许多人向他道贺；还有几位往日几乎算不上认识他的，也热络地招呼他。他陷进一场接一场短小而受用的谈话，应对得体——既不过分矜持，也不忘形。"我声明，这家伙出息了。"一个上星期绝对算得上沃克沃思仇家的人，对同桌的伙伴说。"政府至今混账怠工。但愿他办得成。反正是个绝妙的机会。不过为什么给他，天晓得！马赫苏德那档子事，至少有一打人干得不比他差。参谋学院那位，资格强他一千倍。"

尽管如此，沃克沃思感到舆论总的是友好的——无疑带点惊异，却显出英国性格里慷慨多于算计的那一面：乐于相信一个正在出头的人。不知不觉间，他心智与品格的身量都拔高了。

===Original===

He exchanged a few words on his way out with one of the most distinguished members of the club, a man of European reputation, whom he had seen the week before in the Commander-in-Chief’s room at the War Office. The great man spoke to him with marked friendliness, and Warkworth walked on air as he went his way. Potentially he felt himself the great man’s equal; the gates of life seemed to be opening before him.
And with the rise of fortune came a rush of magnanimous resolution. No more shady episodes; no more mean devices; no more gambling, and no more debt. Major Warkworth’s sheet was clean, and it should remain so. A man of his prospects must run straight.
He felt himself at peace with all the world. By-the-way, just time to jump into a cab and get to Park Crescent in time for his sister’s luncheon. His last interview with his brother-in-law had not been agreeable. But now﻿—he felt for the checkbook in his pocket﻿—he was in a position to repay at least half the last sum of money which Bella had lent him. He would go and give it her now, and report news of the mother. And if the two chicks were there﻿—why, he had a free hour and he would take them to the Zoo﻿—he vowed he would!﻿—give them something pleasant to remember their uncle by.
And a couple of hours later a handsome, soldierly man might have been seen in the lion-house at the Zoo, leading a plump little girl by either hand. Rose and Katie Mullins enjoyed a golden time, and started a wholly new adoration for the uncle who had so far taken small notice of them, and was associated in their shrewd, childish minds rather with tempests at home than buns abroad. But this time buns, biscuits, hansom-drives and elephant-rides were showered upon them by an uncle who seemed to make no account of money, while his gracious and captivating airs set their little hearts beating in a common devotion.
“Now go home﻿—go home, little beggars!” said that golden gentleman, as he packed them into a hansom and stood on the step to accept a wet kiss on his mustache from each pink mouth. “Tell your mother all about it, and don’t forget your uncle Harry. There’s a shilling for each of you. Don’t you spend it on sweets. You’re quite fat enough already. Goodbye!”
“That’s the hardest work I’ve done for many a long day,” he said to himself, with a sigh of relief, as the hansom drove away.

===Chinese===

出门时，他同俱乐部里最负盛名的一位成员——一个享有全欧声望的人物——交谈了几句；上星期他还在陆军部总司令的房间里见过他。这位大人物同他说话，友好得格外分明，沃克沃思一路飘飘然。他觉得自己已然是这大人物的匹敌；生活的大门仿佛正向他敞开。

随着时运的高涨，涌来一阵宽宏的决心。再没有暧昧的插曲；再没有卑鄙的手段；不再赌，也不再欠债。沃克沃思少校的账页是干净的，而且要保持干净。有他这样前程的人，必须走正路。

他觉得同全世界都讲和了。对了，正好来得及跳上一辆马车，赶到公园新月街（Park Crescent）赶姐姐的午饭。上回同姐夫那番见面可不痛快。可现在——他摸了摸口袋里的支票簿——他有能力至少先还上贝拉（Bella）借他的最后一笔钱的一半了。他这就去交给她，顺便报告母亲的消息。两个小丫头要是在家——嗐，他正好有一个钟头空闲，带她们去动物园——他发誓要去！——给她们留点甜头，好记得她们这位舅舅。

两小时后，动物园的狮馆里便可以见到一个英俊威武的男人，两手各牵一个胖乎乎的小姑娘。罗丝和凯蒂·马林斯（Rose and Katie Mullins）过了一段黄金岁月，从此对这位舅舅开始了一场全新的崇拜——这位舅舅此前难得正眼看她们，在她们精明的小孩子心里，他一向只同家里的风暴连在一起，同外面的甜面包毫无干系。可这一回，甜面包、饼干、马车游、骑大象，雨点般落在她们头上，落自一个仿佛全不把钱当回事的舅舅；他那副和蔼迷人的风度，叫两颗小小的心齐齐狂跳。

"回家去吧——回家去，两个小叫花子！"那位金光闪闪的绅士说着，把她们塞进一辆双轮马车，站在踏板上，任两只粉红的小嘴各在他小胡子上印一个湿吻。"回去一五一十讲给你们妈妈听，别忘了你们的哈里舅舅。一人一个先令。别拿去买糖吃。你们已经够胖的了。再见！"

"这是多少年来我干过的最累的活儿。"马车开走后，他松了口气，自语道。

===Original===

“I shan’t turn nursemaid when other trades fail. But they’re nice little kids all the same.
“Now, then, Cox’s﻿—and the City”﻿—he ran over the list of his engagements for the afternoon﻿—“and by five o’clock shall I find my fair lady﻿—at home﻿—and established? Where on earth is Heribert Street?”
---
He solved the question, for a few minutes after five he was on Miss Le Breton’s doorstep. A quaint little house﻿—and a strange parlormaid! For the door was opened to him by a large-eyed, sickly child, who looked at him with the bewilderment of one trying to follow out instructions still strange to her.
“Yes, sir, Miss Le Breton is in the drawing-room,” she said, in a sweet, deliberate voice with a foreign accent, and she led the way through the hall.
Poor little soul﻿—what a twisted back, and what a limp! She looked about fourteen, but was probably older. Where had Julie discovered her?
Warkworth looked round him at the little hall with its relics of country-house sports and amusements; his eye travelled through an open door to the little dining-room and the Russell pastels of Lady Mary’s parents, as children, hanging on the wall. The *character* of the little dwelling impressed itself at once. Smiling; he acknowledged its congruity with Julie. Here was a lady who fell on her feet!
The child, leading him, opened the door to the left.
“Please walk in, sir,” she said, shyly, and stood aside.
As the door opened, Warkworth was conscious of a noise of tongues.
So Julie was not alone? He prepared his manner accordingly.
He entered upon a merry scene. Jacob Delafield was standing on a chair, hanging a picture, while Dr. Meredith and Julie, on either side, directed or criticised the operation. Meredith carried picture-cord and scissors; Julie the hammer and nails. Meredith was expressing the profoundest disbelief in Jacob’s practical capacities; Jacob was defending himself hotly; and Julie laughed at both.
Towards the other end of the room stood the tea-table, between the fire and an open window. Lord Lackington sat beside it, smiling to himself, and stroking a Persian kitten. Through the open window the twinkling buds on the lilacs in the Cureton House garden shone in the still lingering sun. A recent shower had left behind it odors of earth and grass.

"别的行当砸了饭碗，我也不会沦落到当保姆。不过这两个小家伙倒也可爱。

"现在，考克斯店——还有金融城。"他把下午的日程在脑子里过了一遍，"五点钟，我那位美人儿——该到家了吧——该安顿下了吧？赫里伯特街到底在哪儿？"

---

这个问题他解决了，因为五点过后几分钟，他已经站在勒布雷东小姐的门阶上。一所古雅的小宅——还有个古怪的应门女仆！开门的是个大眼睛、病恹恹的孩子，一脸惶惑地望着他，像正努力照着还生疏的吩咐行事。

"是的，先生，勒布雷东小姐在客厅。"她用一口带外国口音的、甜而慢条斯理的声音说，领他穿过门厅。

可怜的小人儿——背驼成那样，走路一瘸一拐的！看去约莫十四岁，实际怕不止。朱莉从哪儿寻来这么一个？

沃克沃思环顾小小的门厅，四壁挂着乡村宅邸游猎玩乐的旧物；他的目光穿过一道敞开的门，望见小餐厅墙上挂着的拉塞尔（Russell）粉笔画——玛丽小姐的父母儿时的画像。这所小宅的品性一下印进他心里。他微笑着承认：它同朱莉正相配。这位小姐，落地的总是脚！

领路的孩子推开左手边的门。

"请进，先生。"她怯怯地说，退在一旁。

门一开，沃克沃思先听见一片人声。

这么说朱莉不是一个人？他相应地备好了神态。

他走进的是一幕欢腾景象。雅各布·德拉菲尔德站在椅子上挂画，梅雷迪思和朱莉一边一个，指挥着、挑剔着这项工程。梅雷迪思手里拿着挂画的绳子和剪刀，朱莉拿着锤子和钉子。梅雷迪思正对雅各布的实际能力表示最深刻的不信任；雅各布激愤地自辩；朱莉把两个人都取笑。

房间另一头，炉火与一扇敞开的窗之间摆着茶桌。拉金顿勋爵坐在桌旁，自顾自微笑着，抚弄一只波斯小猫。敞开的窗外，丘尔顿府花园里丁香初绽的嫩芽，在迟迟未落的夕阳里闪烁。一阵新雨过后，留下泥土和青草的气息。

===Original===

Even in this London air they spoke of the spring﻿—the spring which already in happier lands was drawing veils of peach and cherry blossom, over the red Sienese earth or the green terraces of Como. The fire crackled in the grate. The pretty, old-fashioned room was fragrant with hyacinth and narcissus; Julie’s books lay on the tables; Julie’s hand and taste were already to be felt everywhere. And Lord Lackington with the kitten, beside the fire, gave the last touch of home and domesticity.
“So I find you established?” said Warkworth, smiling, to the lady with the nails, while Delafield nodded to him from the top of the steps and Meredith ceased to chatter.
“I haven’t a hand, I fear,” said Julie. “Will you have some tea? *Ah, Léonie, tu vas en faire de nouveau, n’est-ce pas, pour ce monsieur?*”
A little woman in black, with a shawl over her shoulders, had just glided into the room. She had a small, wrinkled face, bright eyes, and a much-flattened nose.
“*Tout de suite, monsieur*,” she said, quickly, and disappeared with the teapot. Warkworth guessed, of course, that she was Madame Bornier, the foster-sister﻿—the “Propriety” of this ménage.
“Can’t I help?” he said to Julie, with a look at Delafield.
“It’s just done,” she said, coldly, handing a nail to Delafield. “*Just* a trifle more to the right. *Ecco!* Perfection!”
“Oh, you spoil him,” said Meredith. “And not one word of praise for me!”
“What have you done?” she said, laughing. “Tangled the cord﻿—that’s all!”
Warkworth turned away. His face, so radiant as he entered, had settled into sharp, sudden lines. What was the meaning of this voice, this manner? He remembered that to his three letters he had received no word of reply. But he had interpreted that to mean that she was in the throes of moving and could find no time to write.
As he neared the tea-table, Lord Lackington looked up. He greeted the newcomer with the absent stateliness he generally put on when his mind was in a state of confusion as to a person’s identity.
“Well, so they’re sending you to D﻿—﻿—? There’ll be a row there before long. Wish you joy of the missionaries!”
“No, not D﻿—﻿—,” said Warkworth, smiling. “Nothing so amusing. Mokembe’s my destination.”
“Oh, Mokembe!” said Lord Lackington, a little abashed. “That’s where Cecil Ray, Lord R.’s second son, was killed last year﻿—lion-hunting? No, it was of fever that he died.

即便在伦敦的空气里，它们也诉说着春天——那已经在更幸福的国度里，用桃红樱白的面纱，覆上锡耶纳赭红的土地或科莫翠绿阶陌的春天。炉火在炉格里噌啪作响。这间漂亮的老式房间氤氲着风信子和水仙的香气；朱莉的书摊在桌上；处处能觉出朱莉的手、朱莉的趣味。炉边抚猫的拉金顿勋爵，给这一切添上最后一笔家的暖意。

"这么说，我赶上你安顿好了？"沃克沃思含笑对那位拿钉子的小姐说；德拉菲尔德从椅子顶上朝他点头，梅雷迪思也住了口。

"我这只手腾不出来。你要用点茶吗？啊，蕾奥妮（Léonie），你再给这位先生沏一壶，好不好？"

一个披肩搭在肩头的黑衣小妇人，刚刚飘进屋来。一张小小的、起皱的脸，眼睛亮，鼻子塌塌的。

"马上就好，先生。"她急急应了一声，端着茶壶不见了。沃克沃思自然猜到，这就是博尔尼耶夫人，那位奶姐妹——这户人家的"规矩"。

"帮不上忙么？"他对朱莉说，眼睛瞟了瞟德拉菲尔德。

"都弄好了。"她冷冷地说，递给德拉菲尔德一枚钉子，"再——再往右一点点。成了！十全十美！"

"哦，你把他惯坏了。"梅雷迪思说，"对我可没有一个字的夸奖！"

"你干了什么？"她笑着说，"把绳子缠成乱麻——就这些！"

沃克沃思转过身去。他进屋时那满面光彩，霎时收拢成又冷又硬的线条。这嗓音、这态度，是什么意思？他记得自己那三封信，一个字的回音也没收到。可他一直解释成：她正忙着搬家，焦头烂额，抽不出工夫写。

他走近茶桌，拉金顿勋爵抬起头来。他以他惯有的那种心不在焉的雍容招呼了来客——每逢记不清对方是谁，他便摆出这副架子。

"哦，他们要把你派到达——达去？那儿快要闹一场了。祝你同那些传教士相处愉快！"

"不，不去达——达。"沃克沃思微笑道，"没那么热闹。我的目的地是莫肯贝。"

"哦，莫肯贝！"拉金顿勋爵说，略有些讪讪的，"去年塞西尔·雷（Cecil Ray），R 勋爵的次子，就是死在那儿的——猎狮子死的？不对，是害热病死的。

By-the-way, a vile climate!”
“In the plains, yes,” said Warkworth, seating himself. “As to the uplands, I understand they are to be the Switzerland of Africa.”
Lord Lackington did not appear to listen.
“Are you a homoeopath?” he said, suddenly, rising to his full and immense stature and looking down with eagerness on Warkworth.
“No. Why?”
“Because it’s your only chance, for those parts. If Cecil Ray had had their medicines with him he’d be alive now. Look here; when do you start?” The speaker took out his notebook.
“In rather less than a month I start for Denga.”
“All right. I’ll send you a medicine-case﻿—from Epps. If you’re ill, take ’em.”
“You’re very good.”
“Not at all. It’s my hobby﻿—one of the last.” A broad, boyish smile flashed over the handsome old face. “Look at me; I’m seventy-five, and I can tire out my own grandsons at riding and shooting. That comes of avoiding all allopathic messes like the devil. But the allopaths are such mean fellows they filch all our ideas.”
The old man was off. Warkworth submitted to five minutes’ tirade, stealing a glance sometimes at the group of Julie, Meredith, and Delafield in the farther window﻿—at the happy ease and fun that seemed to prevail in it. He fiercely felt himself shut out and trampled on.
Suddenly, Lord Lackington pulled up, his instinct for declamation qualified by an equally instinctive dread of boring or being bored. “What did you think of Montresor’s statement?” he said, abruptly, referring to a batch of army reforms that Montresor the week before had endeavored to recommend to a sceptical House of Commons.
“All very well, as far as it goes,” said Warkworth, with a shrug.
“Precisely! We English want an army and a navy; we don’t like it when those fellows on the Continent swagger in our faces, and yet we won’t pay either for the ships or the men. However, now that they’ve done away with purchase﻿—Gad! I could fight them in the streets for the way in which they’ve done it!﻿—now that they’ve turned the army into an examination-shop, tempered with jobbery, whatever we do, we shall go to the deuce. So it don’t matter.”
“You were against the abolition?”
“I was, sir﻿—with Wellington and Raglan and everybody else of any account. And as for the violence, the disgraceful violence with which it was carried﻿—”
“Oh no, no,” said Warkworth, laughing.

"平原上是的。"沃克沃思坐下说，"至于高地，我了解下来，那是要做非洲的瑞士的。"

拉金顿勋爵像是没在听。

"你信顺势疗法么？"他忽然问，直起身来，立到他整个魁伟的身量，热切地俯视着沃克沃思。

"不信。怎么？"

"因为那是你在那些地方唯一的活路。塞西尔·雷当时若带着他们的药，如今还活着。听着，你几时动身？"说话人掏出了记事本。

"不到一个月，我先去登加（Denga）。"

"好。我送你一只药箱——埃普斯（Epps）家的。生了病，就吃药。"

"您太好了。"

"哪儿的话。这是我的癖好——剩下的癖好之一。"那张漂亮的老脸上闪过开朗的、少年般的笑。"看看我；七十五了，骑马打枪，还能把我自己的外孙们全都拖垮。这就是像躲魔鬼一样躲开一切对抗疗法汤药的好处。可对抗疗法那帮家伙小气得很，尽偷我们的方子。"

老人一发不可收拾。沃克沃思忍受了五分钟的独角演说，不时偷眼望望远处窗前那一伙——朱莉、梅雷迪思和德拉菲尔德——望望笼罩着那一伙的自在和欢闹。他狠狠觉得自己被关在门外、被踩在脚下。

忽然，拉金顿勋爵刹住了——他酷爱演讲的本能，被另一种同样出自本能的畏惧钳住：怕烦人，也怕人烦。"你觉得蒙特雷索的声明怎么样？"他突兀地问，指的是蒙特雷索上星期力图推销给满腹狐疑的下院的那一揽子陆军改革。

"就事论事，都挺好。"沃克沃思耸耸肩。

"正是！我们英国人要陆军要海军；大陆上那帮家伙在我们面前耀武扬威，我们不喜欢；可轮到给船给人掊钱，我们又都不肯。不过，如今他们既已废了捐纳制——天哪！就凭他们废的那副德行，我真可以上街跟他们干一仗！——如今他们既把陆军办成了考试铺子，掺上些营私舞弊，我们横竖做什么都要完蛋。所以也就无所谓了。"

"您当年反对废除？"

"反对，先生——同威灵顿、拉格伦，同所有有点分量的人一起。至于办这件事时的那股暴力，那股丢人的暴力——"

"哦不，不。"沃克沃思笑道，

===Original===

“It was the Lords who behaved abominably, and it’ll do a deal of good.”
Lord Lackington’s eyes flashed.
“I’ve had a long life,” he said, pugnaciously. “I began as a middy in the American war of 1812, that nobody remembers now. Then I left the sea for the army. I knocked about the world. I commanded a brigade in the Crimea﻿—”
“Who doesn’t remember that?” said Warkworth, smiling.
The old man acknowledged the homage by a slight inclination of his handsome head.
“And you may take my word for it that this new system will not give you men worth *a tenth part* of those fellows who bought and bribed their way in under the old. The philosophers may like it, or lump it, but so it is.”
Warkworth dissented strongly. He was a good deal of a politician, himself a “new man,” and on the side of “new men.” Lord Lackington warmed to the fight, and Warkworth, with bitterness in his heart﻿—because of that group opposite﻿—was nothing loath to meet him. But presently he found the talk taking a turn that astonished him. He had entered upon a drawing-room discussion of a subject which had, after all, been settled, if only by what the Tories were pleased to call the coup d’état of the Royal Warrant, and no longer excited the passions of a few years back. What he had really drawn upon himself was a hand-to-hand wrestle with a man who had no sooner provoked contradiction than he resented it with all his force, and with a determination to crush the contradictor.
Warkworth fought well, but with a growing amazement at the tone and manner of his opponent. The old man’s eyes darted war-flames under his finely arched brows. He regarded the younger with a more and more hostile, even malicious air; his arguments grew personal, offensive; his shafts were many and barbed, till at last Warkworth felt his face burning and his temper giving way.
“What *are* you talking about?” said Julie Le Breton, at last, rising and coming towards them.
Lord Lackington broke off suddenly and threw himself into his chair.
Warkworth rose from his.
“We had better have been handing nails,” he said, “but you wouldn’t give us any work.” Then, as Meredith and Delafield approached, he seized the opportunity of saying, in a low voice:
“Am I not to have a word?”
She turned with composure, though it seemed to him she was very pale.
“Have you just come back from the Isle of Wight?”
“This morning.”

===Chinese===

"混账的是上院，而废除捐纳制好处大得很。"

拉金顿勋爵眼睛里火星直冒。

"我活的年头长。"他好斗地说，"我起头是1812年对美战争里的见习水手，那场仗如今谁也不记得了。后来我弃海上岸。我闯荡过世界。克里米亚那会儿我指挥过一个旅——"

"那谁不记得呢？"沃克沃思微笑道。

老人把他俊美的头微微一颈，受了这份敬意。

"而且你尽管信我的话：这套新制度，给不了你配得上旧制度下那些花钱买路、钻营进来的人十分之一的人才。哲学家们爱要不要，反正事实如此。"

沃克沃思强烈反对。他颇有些政见，本人就是个"新人"，站在"新人"一边。拉金顿勋爵越吵越起劲，而沃克沃思——因为对面那一伙，心里正苦——也巴不得迎战。可不多时他发觉，这谈话拐上了叫他吃惊的路子。他原本不过走进一场客厅里的旧案讨论——那桩案子毕竟早有了结，靠的不过是托利党人乐得称之为"王室敕令政变"的那一手，早激不起前几年的火气了。可他真正招到身上的，是一场贴身角力：对手这个人，一受人反驳，便拿出全副气力记恨，铁了心要把反驳者碾碎。

沃克沃思打得漂亮，却越打越惊愕于对手的腔调和路数。老人的眼睛在秀气的弓眉底下放出战火。他看年轻人的眼神越来越敌意，简直带着恶意；他的论证渐渐转向人身、转向冒犯；他的箭一支支多起来、支支带倒刺，末了沃克沃思觉得自己脸上发烧，火气快压不住了。

"你们到底在谈什么呀？"朱莉·勒布雷东终于开口，起身朝他们走来。

拉金顿勋爵戛然收住，跌回椅子里。

沃克沃思从他的椅子上站起来。

"我们还不如去递钉子，"他说，"可你就是不派活给我们。"等梅雷迪思和德拉菲尔德走近，他抓住机会低声说：

"我就讨不到一句话么？"

她从容转过身来，虽然在他看来她脸色很白。

"你刚从怀特岛回来？"

"今天早晨。"

===Original===

He looked her in the eyes. “You got my letters?”
“Yes, but I have had no time for writing. I hope you found your mother well.”
“Very well, thank you. You have been hard at work?”
“Yes, but the Duchess and Mr. Delafield have made it all easy.”
And so on, a few more insignificant questions and answers.
“I must go,” said Delafield, coming up to them, “unless there is any more work for me to do. Goodbye, Major, I congratulate you. They have given you a fine piece of work.”
Warkworth made a little bow, half ironical. Confound the fellow’s grave and lordly ways! He did not want his congratulations.
He lingered a little, sorely, full of rage, yet not knowing how to go.
Lord Lackington’s eyes ceased to blaze, and the kitten ventured once more to climb upon his knee. Meredith, too, found a comfortable armchair, and presently tried to beguile the kitten from his neighbor. Julie sat erect between them, very silent, her thin, white hands on her lap, her head drooped a little, her eyes carefully restrained from meeting Warkworth’s. He meanwhile leaned against the mantelpiece, irresolute.
Meredith, it was clear, made himself quite happy and at home in the little drawing-room. The lame child came in and took a stool beside him. He stroked her head and talked nonsense to her in the intervals of holding forth to Julie on the changes necessary in some proofs of his which he had brought back. Lord Lackington, now quite himself again, went back to dreams, smiling over them, and quite unaware that the kitten had been slyly ravished from him. The little woman in black sat knitting in the background. It was all curiously intimate and domestic, only Warkworth had no part in it.
“Goodbye, Miss Le Breton,” he said, at last, hardly knowing his own voice. “I am dining out.”
She rose and gave him her hand. But it dropped from his like a thing dead and cold. He went out in a sudden suffocation of rage and pain; and as he walked in a blind haste to Cureton Street, he still saw her standing in the old-fashioned, scented room, so coldly graceful, with those proud, deep eyes.
---
When he had gone, Julie moved to the window and looked out into the gathering dusk. It seemed to her as if those in the room must hear the beating of her miserable heart.

"收到了，可我一直腾不出工夫写。但愿你母亲安好。"

"是，不过公爵夫人和德拉菲尔德先生把什么都弄顺了。"

如此这般，又几句无关紧要的问答。

沃克沃思微微一躬，半带讥诮。见鬼，这家伙这副道貌岸然、居高临下的做派！他不需要他的恭喜。

他磨蹭了一会儿，心里发苦，满腔怒火，却又不知怎么走。

拉金顿勋爵眼里的火熄了，小猫又大着胆子爬上他的膝头。梅雷迪思也寻了把舒服的扶手椅坐下，不多时便试着把邻座的小猫逗到自己这边来。朱莉端坐两人之间，一言不发，一双薄薄的白手搁在膝上，头微微垂着，眼睛小心翼翼地避开沃克沃思的目光。他呢，倚着壁炉台，进退不定。

看得出，梅雷迪思在这小客厅里过得自在极了。跛脚的孩子进来，在他旁边一张软凳上坐下。他抚着她的头，一边同她讲傻话，一边时不时对朱莉大发议论，讲他带回来的几份校样里几处必须改的地方。拉金顿勋爵此刻完全恢复了常态，重新沉入梦境，微笑着，全然不觉小猫已被人偷偷拐走。黑衣小妇人坐在后面织毛线。一切都透着奇异的亲昵与家居——只是沃克沃思插不进去。

"再会，勒布雷东小姐。"他终于开口，几乎认不出自己的嗓音，"我外面有个饭局。"

她起身把手伸给他。可它从他手里滑落，像一件又死又冷的东西。他走出去，一阵怒气和疼痛堵得他喘不过气；他昏头昏脑、急匆匆地朝丘尔顿街走，眼前还立着她——立在那间旧式的、香气氤氲的房间里，那样清冷地优雅，带着那双骄傲而深沉的眼睛。
