Nowhere do buttressed masses rise behind each other, to right and left of a blue waterway, in lines statelier or more noble than those kept by the mountains of the Lecco Lake, as they marshal themselves on either hand, along the approaches to Lombardy and Venetia; bearing aloft, as though on the purple pillars of some majestic gateway, the great curtain of dazzling cloud which, on a sunny day, hangs over the Brescian plain﻿—a glorious drop-scene, interposed between the dwellers on the Como Mountains, and those marble towns, Brescia, Verona, Padua, which thread the way to Venice.
And within this divine framework, between the glistening snows which still, in April, crown and glorify the heights, and those reflections of them which lie encalmed in the deep bosom of the lake, there’s not a foot of pasture, not a shelf of vineyard, not a slope of forest where the spring is not at work, dyeing the turf with gentians, starring it with narcissuses, or drawing across it the first golden network of the chestnut leaves; where the mere emerald of the grass is not in itself a thing to refresh the very springs of being; where the peach-blossom and the wild-cherry and the olive are not perpetually weaving patterns on the blue, which ravish the very heart out of your breast.

===Chinese===

"凡是去得了天堂的人，还有谁肯留在英国？"公爵夫人懒洋洋倚在船角，手指在科莫湖（Como）的水里划着说。

这是四月里一个温煦的下午，她同朱莉正漂过一片令人心醉、举世无双的景色。当春天降临科莫湖畔，她带来天地所能有的全部风韵、全部美色、全部精微温润的欢愉，倾泻在一片完美可爱的土地上。别的湖畔——马焦雷、卢加诺、加尔达——蓝山升起，葡萄园把翠绿耀目的层台铺向太阳。唯独科莫，能以无匹的结合，既拿出气势宏大、和谐无伦的整体构图，又兼有每一处珠宝般、燃烧般、精雅般的细部。再没有哪里的山峦，像环抱着科莫北岸的群峰那样，以如此有秩序的壮丽相互俯就。再没有哪里的山体，像莱科湖两岸的山那样——它们一路列队相送，直抵伦巴第和威尼托的入口——在一条蓝色水道的左右，层层叠叠拔地而起，线条更庄严、更高贵；它们高高擎起，仿佛擎在某座雄伟门廊的紫柱上，那幅耀目云霞的大幕——晴日里笼罩着布雷西亚平原——一幅壮丽的落幕景，隔在科莫山区的居民与那些大理石之城——布雷西亚、维罗纳、帕多瓦——之间；那一线城郭，串起了通往威尼斯的路。

而在这神圣的画框之内——四月里仍为峰巅加冕、为峰巅增辉的皑皑积雪，与沉映在湖的深怀里的一片雪影之间——没有一尺草场、一层葡萄园、一面山坡，不是春天正在其上劳作：用龙胆把草皮染蓝，用水仙把草皮缀星，或为它拉出栗树新叶第一张金色罗网；那里的草地纯是翡翠，其本身便是润泽生命之泉的东西；那里的桃花、野樱、橄榄，不断地在蓝底上织出纹样，把人的心从胸腔里都勾了出去。

===Original===

And already the roses are beginning to pour over the walls; the wistaria is climbing up the cypresses; a pomp of camellias and azaleas is in all the gardens; while in the grassy bays that run up into the hills the primrose banks still keep their sweet austerity, and the triumph of spring over the just banished winter is still sharp and new.
And in the heart and sense of Julie Le Breton, as she sat beside the Duchess, listening absently to the talk of the old boatman, who, with his oars resting idly in his hands, was chattering to the ladies, a renewing force akin to that of the spring was also at its healing and life-giving work. She had still the delicate, tremulous look of one recovering from a sore wrestle with physical ill; but in her aspect there were suggestions more intimate, more moving than this. Those who have lain down and risen up with pain; those who have been face to face with passion and folly and self-judgment; those who have been forced to seek with eagerness for some answer to those questions which the majority of us never ask, “Whither is my life leading me﻿—and what is it worth to me or to any other living soul?”﻿—these are the men and women who now and then touch or startle us with the eyes and the voice of Julie, if, at least, we have the capacity that responds. Sir Wilfrid Bury, for instance, prince of self-governed and reasonable men, was not to be touched by Julie. For him, in spite of her keen intelligence, she was the *type passionné*, from which he instinctively recoiled﻿—the Duke of Crowborough the same. Such men feel towards such women as Julie Le Breton hostility or satire; for what they ask, above all, of the women of their world is a kind of simplicity, a kind of lightness which makes life easier for men.
But for natures like Evelyn Crowborough﻿—or Meredith﻿—or Jacob Delafield﻿—the Julie-type has perennial attractions. For these are all *children of feeling*, allied in this, however different in intelligence or philosophy. They are attracted by the storm-tossed temperament in itself; by mere sensibility; by that which, in the technical language of Catholicism, suggests or possesses “the gift of tears.”

===Chinese===

玫瑰已经开始翻墙倾泻；紫藤正往柏树上攀；山茶与杜鹃的盛装开满各家花园；而在那些伸进山里的草湾中，报春花的堤岸还守着它们甜美的清素，春对刚被放逐的冬的凯旋，还新鲜、还分明。

而在朱莉·勒布雷东的心里和感官里，当她坐在公爵夫人身旁，心不在焉地听老船夫闲谈——他双桨闲闲地搭在手里，同两位女士唠着——一种与春天同族的更新之力，也在做它疗愈和赋生的功课。她仍带着那种大病苦斗初愈的人的纤弱震颤之色；可她的神态里还有更贴心、更动人的东西。那些曾与疼痛同卧同起的人；那些曾同激情、愚行和自讼面对面的人；那些被迫急急追问过"我的生命正把我引向何处——它于我、于任何一个活人，又值几何"的人——我们多数人是从不问这些的——这样的人，才有时会以朱莉那样的眼睛和嗓音，触动我们、惊动我们，倘若我们至少还有感应的能力的话。譬如威尔弗里德·伯里爵士，自制而理性的人中之王，就绝不为朱莉所动。在他眼里，任凭她聪明绝顶，她是那种激情型（type passionné），他本能地退避——克劳伯勒公爵也是一样。这样的男人对朱莉·勒布雷东这样的女人，只有敌意或讥诮；因为他们向他们那个世界的女人头一条要的，是一种简单、一种轻省，好叫男人的日子好过。

可对于伊芙纶·克劳伯勒——或梅雷迪思——或雅各布·德拉菲尔德——这类天性，朱莉这一型却有常青的魅力。因为他们都是感情的孩童——在这一点上同盟，尽管智力和哲学各不相同。他们受那风暴颠簸的气质本身所吸引；受纯粹的敏感所吸引；受那种——用天主教的技术语言说——暗示着或拥有着"泪之恩赐"的东西所吸引。

===Original===

At any rate, pity and love for her poor Julie﻿—however foolish, however faulty﻿—lay warm in Evelyn Crowborough’s breast; they had brought her to Como; they kept her now battling on the one hand with her husband’s angry letters and on the other with the melancholy of her most perplexing, most appealing friend.

> “I had often heard” [wrote the sore-tried Duke] “of the ravages wrought in family life by these absurd and unreasonable female friendships, but I never thought that it would be you, Evelyn, who would bring them home to me. I won’t repeat the arguments I have used a hundred times in vain. But once again I implore and demand that you should find some kind, responsible person to look after Miss Le Breton﻿—I don’t care what you pay﻿—and that you yourself should come home to me and the children and the thousand and one duties you are neglecting.
> “As for the spring month in Scotland, which I generally enjoy so much, that has been already entirely ruined. And now the season is apparently to be ruined also. On the Shropshire property there is an important election coming on, as I am sure you know; and the Premier said to me only yesterday that he hoped you were already up and doing. The Grand Duke of C﻿—﻿— will be in London within the next fortnight. I particularly want to show him some civility. But what can I do without you﻿—and how on earth am I to explain your absence?
> “Once more, Evelyn, I beg and I demand that you should come home.”

To which the Duchess had rushed off a reply without a post’s delay.

===Chinese===

无论如何，对她那可怜的朱莉——不管多傻、多少毛病——的怜与爱，暖暖地卧在伊芙纶·克劳伯勒胸前；是它们把她带到了科莫；也是它们，此刻撑着她一面抵挡丈夫气势汹汹的信，一面抵挡她那位最缠人、最动人心魄的朋友的忧郁。

> "我素来听说"［饱受折磨的公爵写道］"这种荒唐而毫无道理的女人友谊会给家庭生活造成的祸害，可我从未想到，把祸害带进我家的会是您，伊芙纶。我用过一百次而无一次奏效的那些道理，不再重复了。但我再一次恳求并要求您：找一位可靠的好人照看勒布雷东小姐——多少钱我不在乎——而您自己，回到我和孩子们身边来，回到您正措着不干的一千零一件职分上来。
>
> "至于苏格兰那一个月的春居——我一向那么喜欢——已经叫您毁得干干净净了。如今看来社交季也要毁了。什罗普郡（Shropshire）的产业上正有一场要紧的选举，我想您清楚；首相昨天还对我说，希望您早已振作行动起来。C—大公半个月内就到伦敦。我特别想对他尽几分礼数。可没有您我怎么办——您的缺席，我又怎么向人解释？
>
> "再说一遍，伊芙纶，我恳求您、要求您：回家来。"

公爵夫人当即回了信，一邮未误。

===Original===

> “Oh, Freddie, you are such a wooden-headed darling! As if I hadn’t explained till I’m black in the face. I’m glad, anyway, you didn’t say command; that would really have made difficulties.
> “As for the election, I’m sure if I was at home I should think it very good fun. Out here I am extremely doubtful whether we ought to do such things as you and Lord M﻿—﻿— suggest. A duke shouldn’t interfere in elections. Anyway, I’m sure it’s good for my character to consider it a little﻿—though I quite admit you may lose the election.
> “The Grand Duke is a horrid wretch, and if he wasn’t a grand duke you’d be the first to cut him. I had to spend a whole dinnertime last year in teaching him his proper place. It was very humiliating, and not at all amusing. You can have a men’s dinner for him. That’s all he’s fit for.
> “And as for the babies, Mrs. Robson sends me a telegram every morning. I can’t make out that they have had a finger-ache since I went away, and I am sure mothers are entirely superfluous. All the same, I think about them a great deal, especially at night. Last night I tried to think about their education﻿—if only I wasn’t such a sleepy creature! But, at any rate, I never in my life tried to think about it at home. So that’s so much to the good.
> “Indeed, I’ll come back to you soon, you poor, forsaken, old thing! But Julie has no one in the world, and I feel like a Newfoundland dog who has pulled someone out of the water. The water was deep; and the life’s only just coming back; and the dog’s not much good. But he sits there, for company, till the doctor comes, and that’s just what I’m doing.
> “I know you don’t approve of the notions I have in my head now. But that’s because you don’t understand. Why don’t you come out and join us? Then you’d like Julie as much as I do; everything would be quite simple; and I shouldn’t be in the least jealous.
> “Dr. Meredith is coming here, probably tonight, and Jacob should arrive tomorrow on his way to Venice, where poor Chudleigh and his boy are.”

===Chinese===

> "哦，弗雷迪，你真是个木头脑袋的宝贝儿！好像我没有解释到脸色发黑似的。反正，幸亏你没说'命令'；那可真要闹出别扭了。
>
> "选举的事，我要在家，准觉得好玩得很。可在这儿，我实在疑心，您同M—勋爵出的那些主意，究竟该不该做。公爵不该干预选举。反正，把这事掂量掂量，对我的品性总有好处——尽管我满心承认，你们也许会输掉选举。
>
> "那位大公是个可恶的坏东西；他若不是大公，头一个不再理他的就是您。去年我不得不费了一整顿饭的工夫教他摆正自己的位置。丢死人了，还一点都不好玩。您可以为他办一场男人晚宴。他只配那个。
>
> "孩子们呢，罗布森太太每天早晨给我来电报。我离开以来，看不出他们手指头疼过一下；我算看明白了，母亲完全是多余的。话虽这么说，我还是常常想他们，尤其是夜里。昨夜我想试着想想他们的教育——偏偏我又是个瞌睡虫！不过，反正我这辈子在家里可从没试着想过。这就算净赚了。
>
> "真的，我很快就会回去的，你这可怜的、被抛下的老东西！可是朱莉在世上一个亲人也没有，我觉得自己就像一条纽芬兰犬，刚把人从水里拖上来。水很深；那口气才刚回来；这条狗呢，也不顶什么用。可它守在那儿作伴，等医生来——我干的正是这个。
>
> "我知道你不赞成我脑袋里现在的这些念头。那是因为你不明白。你何不出来同我们会合？那你就会同我一样喜欢朱莉；一切都会清清楚楚；我也一点不会吃醋。
>
> "梅雷迪思医生今晚多半到；雅各布明天也该到了，他去威尼斯，可怜的查德利和他的孩子在那儿。"

===Original===

The *breva*, or fair-weather wind, from the north was blowing freshly yet softly down the lake. The afternoon sun was burning on Bellaggio, on the long terrace of the Melzi villa, on the white mist of fruit-blossom that lay lightly on the green slopes above San Giovanni.
Suddenly the Duchess and the boatman left the common topics of every day by which the Duchess was trying to improve her Italian﻿—such as the proposed enlargement of the Bellevue Hotel, the new villas that were springing up, the gardens of the Villa Carlotta, and so forth. Evelyn had carelessly asked the old man whether he had been in any of the fighting of ’59, and in an instant, under her eyes, he became another being. Out rolled a torrent of speech; the oars lay idly on the water; and through the man’s gnarled and wrinkled face there blazed a high and illumining passion. Novara and its beaten king, in ’49; the ten years of waiting, when a whole people bode its time, in a gay, grim silence; the grudging victory of Magenta; the fivefold struggle that wrenched the hills of San Martino from the Austrians; the humiliations and the rage of Villafranca﻿—of all these had this wasted graybeard made a part. And he talked of them with the Latin eloquence and facility, as no veteran of the north could have talked; he was in a moment the equal of these great affairs in which he had mingled; so that one felt in him the son of a race which had been rolled and polished﻿—a pebble, as it were, from rocks which had made the primeval framework of the world﻿—in the main course and stream of history.
Then from the campaign of ’59 he fell back on the Five Days of Milan in ’48﻿—the immortal days, when a populace drove out an army, and what began almost in jest ended in a delirium, a stupefaction of victory. His language was hot, broken, confused, like the street fighting it chronicled. Afterwards﻿—a further sharpening and blanching of the old face﻿—and he had carried them deep into the black years of Italy’s patience and Austria’s revenge. Throwing out a thin arm, he pointed towards town after town on the lake shores, now in the brilliance of sunset, now in the shadow of the northern slope﻿—Gravedona, Varenna, Argegno﻿—towns which had each of them given their sons to the Austrian bullet and the Austrian lash for the ransom of Italy.

===Chinese===

布雷瓦风——科莫湖的好天气北风——正清爽而柔和地沿湖吹下。午后的太阳照着贝拉焦（Bellaggio），照着梅尔齐别墅的长台，照着圣乔瓦尼上方绿坡上轻笼的一层果花的白雾。

忽然，公爵夫人撇开了那些日常话题——她本想借这些练意大利语：贝维尤旅馆的扩建计划啦、雨后春笋般新起的别墅啦、卡洛塔别墅的花园啦，等等。伊芙纶随口问老人，'59年那几仗他打过没有；转眼间，在她的注视下，他换了一个人。滔滔的言语滚了出来；双桨闲闲地平在水上；那张沟壑纵横的老脸上，烧起一团高昂的照人的激情。诺瓦拉和它被打败的国王，那是'49年；然后是十年的等待，整整一个民族以欢快而阴冷的沉默守着自己的时辰；马真塔那场吝啬的胜利；那场五倍的血战，从奥地利人手里硬拧下圣马蒂诺的山头；维拉弗兰卡的羞辱与狂怒——这一切，这条干瘦的白胡子老汉都亲与其事。他谈起来，带着拉丁民族的那种雄辩和流利，北方的老兵绝谈不出；一瞬间，他已同他亲历的那些大事平起平坐；于是在他身上，你感到一个种族的儿子——一块从造世的岩层上剥落的卵石，在历史的主河道里滚了又滚、磨了又磨。

随后，从'59年的战事，他退回'48年的米兰五日——那不朽的日子，一城百姓赶走了一支军队，几乎以玩笑开场的事，收场在一场狂喜、一场胜利的目瞪口呆里。他的语言火热、破碎、纷乱，恰似它所记述的巷战。再往后——那张老脸又添几分尖削、几分刷白——他把她们带进了意大利隐忍与奥地利复仇的黑年月。他伸出一条瘦臂，指着湖岸一座又一座城镇——此刻或浴在落日的辉煌里，或浸在北坡的阴影里——格拉韦多纳、瓦伦纳、阿尔杰尼奥——座座都把自家的儿子献给了奥地利的子弹和皮鞭，作意大利的赎价。

===Original===

He ran through the sacred names﻿—Stazzonelli, Riccini, Crescieri, Ronchetti, Ceresa, Previtali﻿—young men, almost all of them, shot for the possession of a gun or a knife, for helping their comrades in the Austrian army to desert, for “insulting conduct” towards an Austrian soldier or officer.
Of one of these executions, which he had himself witnessed at Varese﻿—the shooting of a young fellow of six-and-twenty, his own friend and kinsman﻿—he gave an account which blanched the Duchess’s cheeks and brought the big tears into her eyes. Then, when he saw the effect he had produced, the old man trembled.
“Ah, *eccellenza*,” he cried, “but it had to be! The Italians had to show they knew how to die; then God let them live. *Ecco, eccellenza!*”
And he drew from his breast-pocket, with shaking hands, an old envelope tied round with string. When he had untied it, a piece of paper emerged, brown with age and worn with much reading. It was a rudely printed broadsheet containing an account of the last words and sufferings of the martyrs of Mantua﻿—those conspirators of 1852﻿—from whose graves and dungeons sprang, tenfold renewed, the regenerating and liberating forces which, but a few years later, drove out the Austrian with the Bourbon, together.
“See here, *eccellenza*,” he said, as he tenderly spread out its tattered folds and gave it into the Duchess’s hand. “Have the goodness to look where is that black mark. There you will find the last words of Don Enrico Tazzoli, the half-brother of my father. He was a priest, *eccellenza*. Ah, it was not then as it is now! The priests were then for Italy. They hanged three of them at Mantua alone. As for Don Enrico, first they stripped him of his priesthood, and then they hanged him. And those were his last words, and the last words of Scarsellini also, who suffered with him. *Veda eccellenza!* As for me, I know them from a boy.”
And while the Duchess read, the old man repeated tags and fragments under his breath, as he once more resumed the oars and drove the boat gently towards Menaggio.

===Chinese===

他把那些神圣的名字一一数过——斯塔佐内利、里奇尼、克雷谢里、龙凯蒂、切雷萨、普雷维塔利（Stazzonelli, Riccini, Crescieri, Ronchetti, Ceresa, Previtali）——几乎全是年轻人，有的因为藏了一杆枪或一把刀被枪决，有的因为帮奥地利军里的战友开小差，有的因为对一个奥地利士兵或军官"行止无礼"。

其中一次行刑，他亲眼在瓦雷泽（Varese）看过——枪决一个二十六岁的小伙子，他自己的朋友兼亲戚——他讲的那一番，讲得公爵夫人双颊失色、热泪盈眶。随后，老人看见自己造成的效果，哆嗦起来。

"啊，阁下（eccellenza），"他叫道，"可那是没办法呀！意大利人得叫人看见，他们死得起；上帝才放他们活。您瞧，阁下！"

他从怀里用发抖的手掣出一只拴着细绳的旧信封。解开之后，露出一张纸，年代久远而黄褐，翻读太多而磨损。那是一张印得粗糙的传单，记着曼图亚烈士——1852年那批密谋者——的临终遗言和受难始末；从他们的坟莹和地牢里，十倍新生的解放力量喷薄而出，不过几年，便把奥地利人连同波旁王一齐赶了出去。

"看这儿，阁下。"他一面把那破烂的折页珍爱地摊开，递到公爵夫人手里，"劳驾看看那道黑印在哪儿。您会在那儿找到恩里科·塔佐利神父（Don Enrico Tazzoli）的遗言，他是我父亲同父异母的兄弟。他是位神父，阁下。啊，那时候可不像如今！那时候神父们向着意大利。光曼图亚一地，他们就绞了三位。至于恩里科神父，他们先剥了他的神职，再绞了他。那些就是他的遗言，也是同他一道受难的斯卡尔塞利尼（Scarsellini）的遗言。您看，阁下！至于我，我从做孩子起就会背。"

公爵夫人读着，老人一面重新操桨，把船轻轻划向梅纳焦（Menaggio），一面在嗓底一遍遍背诵那些残句。

===Original===

> “The multitude of victims has not robbed us of courage in the past, nor will it so rob us in the future﻿—till victory dawns. The cause of the people is like the cause of religion﻿—it triumphs only through its martyrs.﻿ ﻿… You﻿—who survive﻿—will conquer, and in your victory we, the dead, shall live.﻿ ﻿…
> “Take no thought for us; the blood of the forerunners is like the seed which the wise husbandman scatters on the fertile ground.﻿ ﻿… Teach our young men how to adore and how to suffer for a great idea. Work incessantly at that; so shall our country come to birth; and grieve not for us!﻿ ﻿… Yes, Italy shall be one! To that all things point. **Work!** There is no obstacle that cannot be overcome, no opposition that cannot be destroyed. The **how** and the **when** only remain to be solved. You, more fortunate than we, will find the clue to the riddle, when all things are accomplished, and the times are ripe.﻿ ﻿… Hope!﻿—my parents, and my brothers﻿—hope always!﻿—waste no time in weeping.”

===Chinese===

> "牺牲者众，过去没有夺去我们的勇气，将来也不会——直到胜利破晓。人民的事业一如宗教的事业——唯有借殉道者才得胜利。……你们——活下来的人——必将得胜；而在你们的胜利里，我们这些死者将再活。……
>
> "不必为我们挂心；先行者的血，正如智慧的农人撒在沃土里的种子。……教导我们的青年，怎样为一个伟大的理想去爱、去受苦。为此孜孜不倦；我们的国家便将诞生；不要为我们悲伤！……是的，意大利必归于一！万事都指向它。工作！没有克服不了的障碍，没有摧不毁的反抗。剩下的只是如何与何时。你们，比我们有福，将在万事齐备、时机成熟之日，找到谜底。……希望吧！——我的父母，我的兄弟们——永远怀着希望！——不要把光阴浪费在哭泣上。"

===Original===

The Duchess read aloud the Italian, and Julie stooped over her shoulder to follow the words.
“Marvellous!” said Julie, in a low voice, as she sank back into her place. “A youth of twenty-seven, with the rope round his neck, and he comforts himself with ‘Italy.’ What’s ‘Italy’ to him, or he to ‘Italy’? Not even an immediate paradise. Is there anybody capable of it now?”
Her face and attitude had lost their languor. As the Duchess returned his treasure to the old man she looked at Julie with joy. Not since her illness had there been any such sign of warmth and energy.
And, indeed, as they floated on, past the glow of Bellaggio, towards the broad gold and azure of the farther lake, the world-defying passion that breathed from these words of dead and murdered Italians played as a bracing and renewing power on Julie’s still feeble being. It was akin to the high snows on those far Alps that closed in the lake﻿—to the pure wind that blew from them﻿—to the “gleam, the shadow, and the peace supreme,” amid which their little boat pressed on towards the shore.
“What matter,” cried the intelligence, but as though through sobs﻿—“what matter the individual struggle and misery? These can be lived down. The heart can be silenced﻿—nerves steadied﻿—strength restored. Will and idea remain﻿—the eternal spectacle of the world, and the eternal thirst of man to see, to know, to feel, to realize himself, if not in one passion, then in another. If not in love, then in patriotism﻿—art﻿—thought.”
---
The Duchess and Julie landed presently beneath the villa of which they were the passing tenants. The Duchess mounted the double staircase where the banksia already hung in a golden curtain over the marble balustrade. Her face was thoughtful. She had to write her daily letter to the absent and reproachful Duke.
Julie parted from her with a caress, and paused awhile to watch the small figure till it mounted out of sight. Her friend had become very dear to her. A new humility, a new gratitude filled her heart. Evelyn should not sacrifice herself much longer. When she had insisted on carrying her patient abroad, Julie had neither mind nor will wherewith to resist. But now﻿—the Duke should soon come to his own again.
She herself turned inland for that short walk by which each day she tested her returning strength.

===Chinese===

公爵夫人朗声读着意大利文，朱莉俯在她肩头跟着看。

"了不起！"朱莉回到自己座位上，低声说，"一个二十七岁的青年，绞索已经套在脖子上，还在拿'意大利'宽慰自己。'意大利'于他算什么，他于'意大利'又算什么？连一个立刻兑现的天堂都不是。如今还有谁做得到？"

她的脸和姿态，那份委顿不见了。等公爵夫人把这件珍宝还给老人，她欢喜地望了朱莉一眼。自她病倒以来，还没有过这样温暖、有劲的征候。

确实，船一路漂过贝拉焦的霞光，向远处湖面那一大片金黄与湛蓝驶去，这些死难的意大利人字里行间呼出的那种睥睨世界的激情，在朱莉仍旧孱弱的身心上，起着振奋和更新的作用。它同围拢这湖的远阿尔卑斯高山雪同族——同从那里吹来的净风同族——同那"微光、暗影与至高的宁静"同族；他们的小船正是在其中，向岸边推进。

"有什么要紧，"那颗心智喊道，却像是隔着呜咽——"个人的挣扎与苦痛有什么要紧？这些都熬得过。心可以叫它住口——神经可以稳住——气力可以复原。意志和观念还在——世界永恒的奇观，人要看见、要知道、要感受、要实现自己的永恒的渴——不在这一种激情里，就在那一种里。不在爱情里，就在爱国、艺术、思想里。"

---

公爵夫人和朱莉不久在她们暂时租住的别墅下上了岸。公爵夫人登上那道双分石阶，斑克木的金色垂幕已经挂在白石栏上。她一脸心事。她得给那位身在远方、满腹怨责的公爵写每日一信。

朱莉爱抚着同她道了别，伫立片刻，望着那小小的身影一级级上去，直到望不见。这位朋友于她已经变得何等亲。一种新的谦卑、一种新的感激充满她的心。伊芙纶不能再这样牺牲下去了。她当初执意要带她的病人出国时，朱莉既无心也无力抗拒。可如今——公爵很快就会物归其主。

她自己转身向内陆，走那段每天用来检验体力回转的短短的路。

===Original===

She climbed the winding road to Criante, the lovely village above Cadenabbia; then, turning to the left, she mounted a path that led to the woods which overhang the famous gardens of the Villa Carlotta.
Such a path! To the left hand, and, as it seemed, steeply beneath her feet, all earth and heaven﻿—the wide lake, the purple mountains, the glories of a flaming sky. On the calm spaces of water lay a shimmer of crimson and gold, repeating the noble splendor of the clouds; the midgelike boats crept from shore to shore; and, midway between Bellaggio and Cadenabbia, the steamboat, a white speck, drew a silver furrow. To her right a green hillside﻿—each blade of grass, each flower, each tuft of heath, enskied, transfigured, by the broad light that poured across it from the hidden west. And on the very hilltop a few scattered olives, peaches, and wild cherries scrawled upon the blue, their bare, leaning stems, their pearly whites, their golden pinks and feathery grays all in a glory of sunset that made of them things enchanted, aerial, fantastical, like a dance of Botticelli angels on the height.
And presently a sheltered bank in a green hollow, where Julie sat down to rest. But nature, in this tranquil spot, had still new pageants, new sorceries wherewith to play upon the nerves of wonder. Across the hollow a great crag clothed in still leafless chestnut-trees reared itself against the lake. The innumerable lines of stem and branch, warm brown or steely gray, were drawn sharp on silver air, while at the very summit of the rock one superb tree with branching limbs, touched with intense black, sprang high above the rest, the proud plume or ensign of the wood. Through the trunks the blaze of distant snow and the purples of craggy mountains; in front the glistening spray of peach or cherry blossom, breaking the still wintry beauty of that majestic grove. And in all the air, dropping from the heaven, spread on the hills, or shimmering on the lake, a diffusion of purest rose and deepest blue, lake and cloud and mountain each melting into the other, as though heaven and earth conspired merely to give value and relief to the year’s new birth, to this near sparkle of young leaf and blossom which shone like points of fire on the deep breast of the distance.
On the green ledge which ran round the hollow were children tugging at a goat.

===Chinese===

她爬上通往克里安泰的盘山道——卡登纳比亚上方那座可爱的村子；随后左转，登上一条小径，通到俯瞰卡洛塔别墅名园的树林。

怎样一条小径啊！左手边，而且仿佛就陡陡地悬在脚下，是全部的天与地——浩渺的湖，紫色的山，一片燃烧的天空的壮丽。平静的水面上浮着一层绯与金的微光，应和着云霞的庄严的光彩；蚊蚋似的小船从这岸爬到那岸；贝拉焦与卡登纳比亚之间，轮船是一个白点，犁出一道银沟。她右手是一片翠绿的山坡——每一根草、每一朵花、每一丛石楠，都被从隐没的西天泼过来的大片亮光举上了天、变了形。恰在山顶，几株疏疏的橄榄、桃树和野樱，潦草地画在蓝天上——它们赤裸倾侧的干、珠玉的白、金粉的桃红、羽绒似的灰，全都浴在落日的荣光里，成了些中了魔法的东西，飘飘欲仙、光怪陆离，像波提切利（Botticelli）的一群天使在山巅起舞。

不多时，一道绿谷里背风的草坡，朱莉坐下来歇息。可是在这宁谧之处，大自然还有新的盛典、新的魔法，来拨动惊奇的神经。谷对面，一座巨岩披着尚未抽叶的栗树林，朝着湖兀立。数不清的枝干线条，暖褐的、铁灰的，在银亮的空气里勾得历历分明；而就在岩顶，一株枝桠横逸的大树，染着浓黑，从林梢拔地高擎，是整片树林骄傲的翎羽与旌旗。树干的缝隙间，远处雪光灼灼，危峰泛紫；近前，桃李樱的花沫晶莹四溅，破开那庄严树群的残冬之美。而满空的空气里——从天上落下来、摊在山坡上、闪烁在湖面上——是一派至纯的玫瑰与至深的湛蓝，湖、云、山彼此交融，仿佛天地合谋，只为给这一年的新生作衬托、作底色——给这近处新叶新花的灼灼星火，它们在远处深邃的胸怀里亮得像点点火种。

环着绿谷的草坎上，几个孩子在拽一头山羊。

===Original===

Opposite was a *contadino’s* house of gray stone. A waterwheel turned beside it, and a stream, brought down from the hills, ran chattering past, a white and dancing thread of water. Everything was very still and soft. The children and the river made their voices heard; and there were nightingales singing in the woods below. Otherwise all was quiet. With a tranquil and stealthy joy the spring was taking possession. Nay﻿—the Angelus! It swung over the lake and rolled from village to village.﻿ ﻿…
The tears were in Julie’s eyes. Such beauty as this was apt now to crush and break her. All her being was still sore, and this appeal of nature was sometimes more than she could bear.
Only a few short weeks since Warkworth had gone out of her life﻿—since Delafield at a stroke had saved her from ruin﻿—since Lord Lackington had passed away.
One letter had reached her from Warkworth, a wild and incoherent letter, written at night in a little room of a squalid hotel near the Gare de Sceaux. Her telegram had reached him, and for him, as for her, all was over.
But the letter was by no means a mere cry of baffled passion. There was in it a new note of moral anguish, as fresh and startling in her ear, coming from him, as the cry of passion itself. In the language of religion, it was the utterance of a man “convicted of sin.”

===Chinese===

对面是一座灰石的农家屋子。一架水车在旁边转着，一条从山里引下的溪水，一条白色舞动的水线，潺潺流过。一切都又静又软。孩子和河水是仅有的声响；下面的树林里有夜莺在唱。除此之外万籁俱寂。春天正以一种安静而偷偷摸摸的欢喜占领一切。可不——晚祷钟！它荡过湖面，从一座村庄滚向另一座村庄。……

泪涌上朱莉的眼睛。这样的美，如今往往压碎她、折断她。她整个的人还在作痛，而自然的这声呼唤，有时她受不住。

沃克沃思走出她的生活、德拉菲尔德一举把她从毁灭里救下、拉金顿勋爵辞世——前后不过短短几个星期。

沃克沃思来过一封信，一封狂乱语无伦次的信，夜里写在索镇车站附近一家邋遢旅馆的小屋里。她的电报送到了他手上；对他，一如对她，一切都完了。

可这封信绝不仅仅是一声被挫的激情的呼喊。信里有一个新的音——道德的绞痛——从他的笔下来，在她耳中，同激情的呼喊一样新鲜、一样惊人。用宗教的语言说，那是一个"被罪定罪"之人的自白。

===Original===

> “How long is it since that man gave me your telegram? I was pacing up and down the departure platform, working myself into an agony of nervousness and anxiety as the time went by, wondering what on earth had happened to you, when the *chef de gare* came up: ‘*Monsieur attend une dépêche?*’ There were some stupid formalities﻿—at last I got it. It seemed to me I had already guessed what it contained.
> “So it was *Delafield* who met you﻿—Delafield who turned you back?
> “I saw him outside the hotel yesterday, and we exchanged a few words. I have always disliked his long, pale face and his high and mighty ways﻿—at any rate, towards plain fellows, who don’t belong to the classes, like me. Yesterday I was more than usually anxious to get rid of him.
> “So he guessed?
> “It can’t have been chance. In some way he guessed. And you have been torn from me. My God! If I could only reach him﻿—if I could fling his contempt in his face! And yet﻿—
> “I have been walking up and down this room all night. The longing for you has been the sharpest suffering I suppose that I have ever known. For I am not one of the many people who enjoy pain. I have kept as free of it as I could. This time it caught and gripped me. Yet that isn’t all. There has been something else.
> “What strange, patched creatures we are! Do you know, Julie, that by the time the dawn came I was on my knees﻿—thanking God that we were parted﻿—that you were on your way home﻿—safe﻿—out of my reach? Was I mad, or what? I can’t explain it. I only know that one moment I hated Delafield as a mortal enemy﻿—whether he was conscious of what he had done or no﻿—and the next I found myself blessing him!
> “I understand now what people mean when they talk of conversion. It seems to me that in the hours I have just passed through things have come to light in me that I myself never suspected. I came of an Evangelical stock﻿—I was brought up in a religious household. I suppose that one can’t, after all, get away from the blood and the life that one inherits. My poor, old father﻿—I was a bad son, and I know I hastened his death﻿—was a sort of Puritan saint, with very stern ideas. I seem to have been talking with him this night, and shrinking under his condemnation.

===Chinese===

> "那个人把您的电报递给我，有多久了？我在出发月台上一趟趟来回走，时间一分分过去，我把自己逼进一阵神经质的焦灼，不知道您到底出了什么事；站长走过来：'先生在等电报？'是些莫名其妙的例行手续——末了我终于拿到了。可我仿佛早已猜到了里面的内容。
>
> "这么说，是德拉菲尔德去接的您——是德拉菲尔德把您扳了回去？
>
> "昨天我在旅馆外见着了他，我们交谈了几句。我一向不喜欢他那张长长的白脸和他那副高高在上的做派——至少是对我这样不入流的粗人。昨天，我比往常更急着打发他走。
>
> "那么，他猜着了？
>
> "绝不会是碰巧。他不知怎么猜着了。而您被从我身边夺走了。我的上帝！我若够得着他——我若能把他的轻蔑摔回他脸上！可是——
>
> "我整夜在这间屋子里来回走。对您的渴望，是我平生所知最锐利的苦。因为我不是那种以苦为乐的人。我一向能躲多远躲多远。这一回它逮住了我，攥紧了我。可又不只这个。还有别的东西。
>
> "我们这些人是些多么奇怪的、七拼八凑的造物！您可知道，朱莉，天亮的时候，我竟跪了下来——感谢上帝，我们分开了——感谢您正走在回家的路上——平安——够不着我了？我是疯了，还是怎么了？我解释不了。我只知道，这一刻我还把德拉菲尔德当不共戴天的仇人恨着——不管他自己知道不知道他做了什么——下一刻，我竟发现自己在为他祝福！
>
> "如今我懂了人们说'皈依'是什么意思。我仿佛觉得，刚刚过去的这几个钟头里，我自己从不曾疑心的东西，在我身上亮出来了。我出身于福音派的家系——我是在一个宗教气的人家长大的。我想，人到底挣不脱自己承袭的血和生命。我那可怜的老父亲——我是个逆子，我知道是我催死了他——是某种清教徒式的圣徒，见解极严。这一夜我仿佛一直在同他谈话，在他的谴责底下缩着。

===Original===

I could see his old face, as he put before me the thoughts I had dared to entertain, the risks I had been ready to take towards the woman I loved﻿—the woman to whom I owed a deep debt of eternal gratitude.
> “Julie, it is strange how this appointment affects me. Last night I saw several people at the Embassy﻿—good fellows﻿—who seemed anxious to do all they could for me. Such men never took so much notice of me before. It is plain to me that this task will make or mar me. I may fail. I may die. But if I succeed England will owe me something, and these men at the top of the tree﻿—
> “Good God! how can I go on writing this to you? It’s because I came back to the hotel and tossed about half the night brooding over the difference between what these men﻿—these honorable, distinguished fellows﻿—were prepared to think of me, and the blackguard I knew myself to be. What, take everything from a woman’s hand, and then turn and try and drag her in the mire﻿—propose to her what one would shoot a man for proposing to one’s sister! Thief and cur.
> “Julie﻿—kind, beloved Julie﻿—forget it all! For God’s sake, let’s cast it all behind us! As long as I live, your name, your memory will live in my heart. We shall not meet, probably, for many years. You’ll marry and be happy yet. Just now I know you’re suffering. I seem to see you in the train﻿—on the steamer﻿—your pale face that has lighted up life for me﻿—your dear, slender hands that folded so easily into one of mine. You are in pain, my darling. Your nature is wrenched from its natural supports. And you gave me all your fine, clear mind, and all your heart. I ought to be damned to the deepest hell!
> “Then, again, I say to myself, if only she were here! If only I had her *here*, with her arms round my neck, surely I might have found the courage and the mere manliness to extricate both herself and me from these entanglements. Aileen might have released and forgiven one.
> “No, no! It’s all over! I’ll go and do my task. You set it me. You shan’t be ashamed of me there.
> “Goodbye, Julie, my love﻿—goodbye﻿—forever!”

===Chinese===

> 我看得见他那张苍老的脸，他把我的胆敢想过的念头、我对那个我爱的女人——那个我欠着永世感念的女人——准备去冒的险，一一摆在我面前。
>
> "朱莉，这个任命对我的影响，说来奇怪。昨晚我在使馆见到几个人——都是好人——好像都急着为我出力。这样的人从前从没这样看重过我。我心里明白，这桩差事成也我在、败也我在。我可能失败。我可能死。可我若成功，英格兰就欠了我一份情，而这些身居绝顶的人就——
>
> "上帝啊！这我怎么还能写下去？那是因为我回到旅馆，翻来覆去半宿，想的都是这些人——这些正派的、出色的人——准备怎么看同我，同我自己心里明白的那个恶棍之间，差着多远。什么，从一个女人手里什么都拿，转过身却要把她往泥里拖——向她提议那种事，谁若向自己的姐妹提议，就该吃枪子！贼，懦夫。
>
> "朱莉——仁厚的、亲爱的朱莉——把这一切都忘了吧！看在上帝份上，让咱们把它统统抛到身后！只要我活着，您的名字、您的记忆就活在我心里。我们多半许多年见不着了。您会嫁人，还会幸福。此刻我知道您在受苦。我仿佛看得见您在火车里——在轮船上——您那张照亮过我生命的苍白的脸——您那双轻轻一叠就合进我一只手掌的、亲爱的、纤细的手。您在痛，我的宝贝。您的天性被从天然的支柱上硬拧了下来。而您给了我您全部清明的心智、全部的心。我该打下最深的地狱！
>
> "可随后我又对自己说，她若是就在这儿！我若把她——就带在这儿，她的胳膊搂着我的脖子，我本一定找得到那股勇气、那点男子气，把她和我两个都从这些乱麻里解出来。艾琳本可以放开我、饶了我。
>
> "不，不！全完了！我去办我的差事。是您交给我的。您不会在那边以我为耻。
>
> "再会，朱莉，我的爱——再会——永别了！"

===Original===

These were portions of that strange document composed through the intervals of a long night, which showed in Warkworth’s mind the survival of a moral code, inherited from generations of scrupulous and God-fearing ancestors, overlaid by selfish living, and now revived under the stress, the purification partly of deepening passion, partly of a high responsibility. The letter was incoherent, illogical; it showed now the meaner, now the nobler elements of character; but it was human; it came from the warm depths of life, and it had exerted in the end a composing and appeasing force upon the woman to whom it was addressed. He had loved her﻿—if only at the moment of parting﻿—he had loved her! At the last there had been feeling, sincerity, anguish, and to these all things may be forgiven.
And, indeed, what in her eyes there was to forgive, Julie had long forgiven. Was it his fault if, when they met first, he was already pledged﻿—for social and practical reasons which her mind perfectly recognized and understood﻿—to Aileen Moffatt? Was it his fault if the relations between herself and him had ripened into a friendship which in its turn could only maintain itself by passing into love? No! It was she, whose hidden, insistent passion﻿—nourished, indeed, upon a tragic ignorance﻿—had transformed what originally he had a perfect right to offer and to feel.
So she defended him; for in so doing she justified herself. And as to the Paris proposal, he had a right to treat her as a woman capable of deciding for herself how far love should carry her; he had a right to assume that her antecedents, her training, and her circumstances were not those of the ordinary sheltered girl, and that for her love might naturally wear a bolder and wilder aspect than for others. He blamed himself too severely, too passionately; but for this very blame her heart remembered him the more tenderly. For it meant that his mind was torn and in travail for her, that his thoughts clung to her in a passionate remorse; and again she felt herself loved, and forgave with all her heart.
All the same, he was gone out of her life, and through the strain and the unconscious progress to other planes and phases of being, wrought by sickness and convalescence, her own passion for him even was now a changed and blunted thing.
Was she ashamed of the wild impulse which had carried her to Paris?

===Chinese===

这份奇特的文书，便是透过漫漫长夜的一处处间隙写成的；它显出沃克沃思心里留存着一套道德法统——承自一代代敬畏上帝的清慎先人，被自私的活法覆埋多年，此刻在深化的激情与重大的责任这两种提纯之下，苏醒了。这封信语无伦次、不合逻辑；时而露出性情的卑劣面，时而露出高贵面；可它是人的；它出自生命温暖的深处；而它对收信的她，终于施出了平抚与安宁的力量。他爱过她——哪怕只在离别的那一刻——他爱过她！临了有感情，有真诚，有剧痛；有这些，一切都可以被饶恕。

而她眼中有什么可饶恕的，朱莉早已尽数饶恕了。他们初见时，他已因种种社交的、实际的缘故——她的心智完全认得清、也懂得的缘故——许给了艾琳·莫法特（Aileen Moffatt），这难道是他的错？她同他之间的关系熟成了友谊，而这友谊又只能靠化为爱情来维系，这难道是他的错？不！是她——她那隐秘而执拗的激情——滋养它的，实在是一场悲凉的懵懂——把他原本全有权给出的、给出的东西，变了质。

她这样为他辩护；因为这样辩，也就是为自己辩。至于巴黎那一议，他有权把她当作一个能自己拿主意、决定爱情把她带到多远的女人；他有权假定，她的来历、她的教养、她的境遇，都不是寻常深闺少女的那一套，对她来说，爱情自然可以比旁人披一副更大胆、更狂野的形貌。他把自己责备得太狠、太激切；而正因为这份自责，她的心把他记得更柔。因为那意味着他的心为她撕裂、为她产痛，他的思念以一种炽烈的悔疚缠着她；于是她又一次感到自己被爱着，也就全心全意地饶恕了。

尽管如此，他终究走出了她的生活；而经过病与康复带来的那番消耗，和那番不知不觉向存在的其他层面与阶段的推移，连她对他的激情，如今也成了一件变了质、钝了刃的东西。

她为那把她卷去巴黎的狂乱冲动感到羞耻么？

===Original===

It is difficult to say. She was often seized with the shuddering consciousness of an abyss escaped, with wonder that she was still in the normal, accepted world, that Evelyn might still be her companion, that Thérèse still adored her more fervently than any saint in the calendar. Perhaps, if the truth were known, she was more abased in her own eyes by the self-abandonment which had preceded the assignation with Warkworth. She had much intellectual arrogance, and before her acquaintance with Warkworth she had been accustomed to say and to feel that love was but one passion among many, and to despise those who gave it too great a place. And here she had flung herself into it, like any dull or foolish girl for whom a love affair represents the only stirring in the pool of life that she is ever likely to know.
Well, she must recapture herself and remake her life. As she sat there in the still Italian evening she thought of the old boatman, and those social and intellectual passions to which his burst of patriotism had recalled her thoughts. Society, literature, friends, and the ambitions to which these lead﻿—let her go back to them and build her days afresh. Dr. Meredith was coming. In his talk and companionship she would once more dip and temper the tools of mind and taste. No more vain self-arraignment, no more useless regrets. She looked back with bitterness upon a moment of weakness when, in the first stage of convalescence, in mortal weariness and loneliness, she had slipped one evening into the Farm Street church and unburdened her heart in confession. As she had told the Duchess, the Catholicism instilled into her youth by the Bruges nuns still laid upon her at times its ghostly and compelling hand. Now in her renewed strength she was inclined to look upon it as an element of weakness and disintegration in her nature. She resolved, in future, to free herself more entirely from a useless *Aberglaube*.
But Meredith was not the only visitor expected at the villa in the next few days. She was already schooling herself to face the arrival of Jacob Delafield.
It was curious how the mere thought of Delafield produced an agitation, a shock of feeling, which seemed to spread through all the activities of being. The faint, renascent glamour which had begun to attach to literature and social life disappeared.

===Chinese===

难说。她常被一阵战栗攣住，意识到自己逃过一道深渊，惊异自己居然还在正常的、被认可的世界里，惊异伊芙纶还能做她的同伴，惊异泰蕾兹（Thérèse）崇拜她，仍旧热得胜过历书上任何一位圣徒。或许，说实话，她更受不了的，是自己眼里那桩约会之前的那番自弃。她素有心智上的傲气；结识沃克沃思之前，她惯于说也惯于觉：爱情不过是众激情中的一种，并且看不起把它摆得太大的人。可如今她自己一头栽了进去，同一个把一段情事当作一生水塘里唯一波澜的愚钝蠢丫头，毫无二致。

唉，她必须重新捕捉自己、重新铸造自己的生活。她坐在意大利宁静的暮色里，想起那老船夫，想起他那阵爱国的激越唤回她心头的那些社会的、心智的激情。社交，文学，朋友，以及由它们引出的种种抱负——让她回到它们中间，重新搭建自己的日子。梅雷迪思医生要来了。在他的谈吐和陪伴里，她会重新淬炼心智与趣味的工具。不再做无谓的自我控诉，不再有无用的悔恨。她辛酸地回想病愈初期的某个软弱时刻——在致命的疲惫和孤单里，一个傍晚，她溜进了农场街的教堂，把心卸在告解里。正如她对公爵夫人说过的，布鲁日的修女们注进她少女时代的天主教信仰，如今仍时时向她落下它幽冥而无可抗拒的手。此刻，在恢复的气力里，她倒倾向于把它看作自己天性中一种软弱与溃散的因子。她决意今后更彻底地摆脱这种无用的迷信。

可未来几天别墅里要到的客人，不止梅雷迪思一个。她已经在训练自己，去面对雅各布·德拉菲尔德的到来。

奇怪，单单想到德拉菲尔德，就会搅起一阵激动、一阵情绪的震荡，仿佛传遍存在的全部活动。刚刚开始附着到文学和社交生活上的那点微弱的新光，消散了。

===Original===

She fell into a kind of brooding, the sombre restlessness of one who feels in the dark the recurrent presence of an attacking and pursuing power, and is in a tremulous uncertainty where or how to meet it.
The obscure tumult within her represented, in fact, a collision between the pagan and Christian conceptions of life. In self-dependence, in personal pride, in her desire to refer all things to the arbitrament of reason, Julie, whatever her practice, was theoretically a stoic and a pagan. But Delafield’s personality embodied another “must,” another “ought,” of a totally different kind. And it was a “must” which, in a great crisis of her life, she also had been forced to obey. There was the thought which stung and humiliated. And the fact was irreparable; nor did she see how she was ever to escape from the strange, silent, penetrating relation it had established between her and the man who loved her and had saved her, against her will.
During her convalescence at Crowborough House, Delafield had been often admitted. It would have been impossible to exclude him, unless she had confided the whole story of the Paris journey to the Duchess. And whatever Evelyn might tremblingly guess, from Julie’s own mouth she knew nothing. So Delafield had come and gone, bringing Lord Lackington’s last words, and the account of his funeral, or acting as intermediary in business matters between Julie and the Chantrey brothers. Julie could not remember that she had ever asked him for these services. They fell to him, as it were, by common consent, and she had been too weak to resist.
At first, whenever he entered the room, whenever he approached her, her sense of anger and resentment had been almost unbearable. But little by little his courtesy, tact, and coolness had restored a relation between them which, if not the old one, had still many of the outward characters of intimacy. Not a word, not the remotest allusion reminded her of what had happened. The man who had stood before her transfigured on the deck of the steamer, stammering out, “I thank God I had the courage to do it!”﻿—it was often hard for her to believe, as she stole a look at Delafield, chatting or writing in the Duchess’s drawing-room, that such a scene had ever taken place.
---
The evening stole on.

===Chinese===

她坠入一种沉思，一种阴郁的不宁——像一个在黑暗里觉出某种进袭与追逐之力反复现身的人，颤抖着拿不准在哪里迎它、怎样迎它。

她心里这场昏乱的骚动，其实是异教与基督教两种人生观的相撞。在自立上，在个人的骄傲上，在把万事诉诸理性裁断的愿望上，朱莉——不论实行为何——在理论上是斯多噶派、异教徒。而德拉菲尔德的人格，体现着另一种"必须"、另一种"应当"，全然不同的两种。而那是一个"必须"——在她一生的一场大危机里，她自己也被迫服从过的。刺痛和羞辱正落在这里。而这事实无可挽回；她也不看不出，自己怎样才能永远逃开它在两人——她和这个爱她、违着她的意救了她的人——之间建立起来的那种奇异的、无声的、穿透的关系。

她在克劳伯勒府养病期间，德拉菲尔德常被放进来看她。要把他挡在外面是不可能的，除非她把巴黎之行的全部故事吐给公爵夫人。而无论伊芙纶怯怯地猜到什么，从朱莉嘴里她一无所知。于是德拉菲尔德来了又去，捎来拉金顿勋爵的遗言，讲他下葬的情形，或在朱莉同钱特里兄弟之间充当事务的中人。朱莉不记得自己何曾求过他这些效劳。它们仿佛经众口默许，自然而然落到他身上；而她那时太弱，无力抗拒。

起初，每逢他进屋，每逢他走近，她的愤懑和怨恨几乎难忍。可渐渐地，他的殷勤、得体和冷静，在两人之间恢复了一种关系——虽不是旧的那一种，却还保有亲密的种种外表。没有一句话、最间接的一点点影射，提醒她发生过的事。那个在轮船甲板上站在她面前、浑身放着异彩、结结巴巴说"我感谢上帝给了我做的勇气"的人——当她偷眼望着在公爵夫人客厅里谈笑或写字的德拉菲尔德，她常常很难相信那一幕真的发生过。

---

暮色悄悄行进。

===Original===

How was it that whenever she allowed the thought of Delafield to obtain a real lodgment in the mind, even the memory of Warkworth was for the time effaced? Silently, irresistibly, a wild heat of opposition would develop within her. These men round whom, as it were, there breathes an air of the heights; in whom one feels the secret guard that religion keeps over thoughts and words and acts﻿—her passionate yet critical nature flung out against them. How are they better than others, after all? What right have they over the wills of others?
Nevertheless, as the rose of evening burned on the craggy mountain face beyond Bellaggio, retreating upward, step by step, till the last glorious summit had died into the cool and already starlit blues of night, Julie, held, as it were, by a reluctant and half-jealous fascination, sat dreaming on the hillside, not now of Warkworth, not of the ambitions of the mind, or society, but simply of the goings and comings, the aspects and sayings of a man in whose eyes she had once read the deepest and sternest things of the soul﻿—a condemnation and an anguish above and beyond himself.
---
Dr. Meredith arrived in due time, a jaded Londoner athirst for idleness and fresh air. The Duchess and Julie carried him hither and thither about the lake in the four-oar boat which had been hired for the Duchess’s pleasure. Here, enthroned between the two ladies, he passed luxurious hours, and his talk of politics, persons, and books brought just that stimulus to Julie’s intelligence and spirits for which the Duchess had been secretly longing.
A first faint color returned to Julie’s cheeks. She began to talk again; to resume certain correspondences; to show herself once more﻿—at any rate intermittently﻿—the affectionate, sympathetic, and beguiling friend.
As for Meredith, he knew little, but he suspected a good deal. There were certain features in her illness and convalescence which suggested to him a mental cause; and if there were such a cause, it must, of course, spring from her relations to Warkworth.
The name of that young officer was never mentioned. Once or twice Meredith was tempted to introduce it. It rankled in his mind that Julie had never been frank with him, freely as he had poured his affection at her feet. But a moment of languor or of pallor disarmed him.
“She is better,” he said to the Duchess one day, abruptly.

===Chinese===

怎么会这样：只要她容德拉菲尔德这念头在心里真正落了户，连沃克沃思的记忆都会一时消隐？无声地、不可抗地，一股狂热的对抗便在她心里烧起来。那些周身仿佛呼吸着高处空气的男人；那些让人觉出宗教对言语行为暗加守护的男人——她那激越而挑剔的天性，便朝他们甩出去。他们到底比旁人好在哪儿？他们凭什么支配旁人的意志？

尽管如此，当黄昏的玫瑰在贝拉焦对面嶙峋的山脸上燃烧，一步一步向高处退去，直到最后一线辉煌的峰顶熄进清凉的、已布星光的夜蓝——朱莉，仿佛被一种又勉强又带着醋意的魔力扣住，坐在山坡上出神；这回想的不是沃克沃思，不是心智或社交的抱负，而只是一个人的来来去去、一言一笑——那个男人的眼睛里，她曾读到过灵魂里最深、最严的东西——一种超出他自身之上的谴责与绞痛。

---

梅雷迪思医生按期到了，一个疲惫的伦敦人，渴着安逸和新鲜空气。公爵夫人和朱莉带着他，坐上为夫人租的四桨船，在湖上四处游。在这里，他端坐在两位女士中间，过了些奢华的时辰；他谈政治、谈人物、谈书，给朱莉的智性和兴致带来的那种刺激，正是公爵夫人私下渴盼的。

朱莉的脸颊回了第一抹淡淡的红。她重新开口说话了；重新续起几处通信；间或重新显出——那副多情、体贴、逗人欢喜的朋友的旧面目。

至于梅雷迪思，他知道得少，疑心的多。她这场病和康复的某些特点，向他暗示着一个心因；而真有此因，自然必出自她同沃克沃思的关系。

那位年轻军官的名字，谁也不提。梅雷迪思有一两回想引出它。朱莉从不曾对他坦诚相见——尽管他把自己的爱慕倾倒在她脚下——这根刺始终在他心里。可她一阵的委顿或一阵的苍白，总缴了他的械。

"她好些了。"一天他突兀地对公爵夫人说。

===Original===

“Her mind is full of activity. But why, at times, does she still look so miserable﻿—like a person without hope or future?”
The Duchess looked pensive. They were sitting in the corner of one of the villa’s terraced walks, amid a scented wilderness of flowers. Above them was a canopy of purple and yellow﻿—rose and wistaria; while through the arches of the pergola which ran along the walk gleamed all those various blues which make the spell of Como﻿—the blue and white of the clouds, the purple of the mountains, the azure of the lake.
“Well, she was in love with him. I suppose it takes a little time,” said the Duchess, sighing.
“Why was she in love with him?” said Meredith, impatiently. “As to the Moffatt engagement, naturally, she was kept in the dark?”
“At first,” said the Duchess, hesitating. “And when she knew, poor dear, it was too late!”
“Too late for what?”
“Well, when one falls in love one doesn’t all at once shake it off because the man deceives you.”
“One *should*,” said Meredith, with energy. “Men are not worth all that women spend upon them.”
“Oh, that’s true!” cried the Duchess﻿—“so dreadfully true! But what’s the good of preaching? We shall go on spending it to the end of time.”
“Well, at any rate, don’t choose the dummies and the frauds.”
“Ah, there you talk sense,” said the Duchess. “And if only we had the French system in England! If only one could say to Julie: ‘Now look here, *there’s* your husband! It’s all settled﻿—down to plate and linen﻿—and you’ve *got* to marry him!’ how happy we should all be.”
Dr. Meredith stared.
“You have the man in your eye,” he said.
The Duchess hesitated.
“Suppose you come a little walk with me in the wood,” she said, at last, gathering up her white skirts.
Meredith obeyed her. They were away for half an hour, and when they returned the journalist’s face, flushed and furrowed with thought, was not very easy to read.
Nor was his temper in good condition. It required a climb to the very top of Monte Crocione to send him back, more or less appeased, a consenting player in the Duchess’s game. For if there are men who are flirts and egotists﻿—who ought to be, yet never are, divined by the sensible woman at a glance﻿—so also there are men too well equipped for this wicked world, too good, too well born, too desirable.

===Chinese===

"她的头脑满是活力。可为什么，她还时时显得那么痛苦——像个没有指望、没有将来的人？"

公爵夫人神情怅惘。两人坐在别墅一台层小径的角落里，四围是馥郁的花的荒野。头顶是紫与黄的华盖——玫瑰与紫藤；而沿路藤棚的拱隙间，闪动着造就科莫之魅的种种蓝——云的蓝白，山的紫，湖的青碧。

"唉，她爱过他嘛。我想，总得一点时间。"公爵夫人叹道。

"她为什么爱他？"梅雷迪思不耐烦地说，"莫法特家那桩婚约，她自然是被瞒着的？"

"起初是。"公爵夫人迟疑着说，"等她知道的时候，可怜的宝贝，已经晚了！"

"晚了什么？"

"唉，人一旦爱上了，不会因为男人骗了你，说甩就甩掉的。"

"应该会。"梅雷迪思用力说，"男人不值女人们花在他们身上的一切。"

"哦，这话对！"公爵夫人叫道——"对得吓人！可说教有什么用？我们到世界末日还会照花不误。"

"那么至少，别挑那些绣花枕头和骗子。"

"啊，这话在理。"公爵夫人说，"英国若也行法国那一套就好了！若能对朱莉说：'瞧，这位就是你的丈夫！万事俱备——连盘碗床单都置齐了——你就得嫁他！'咱们大家该多幸福。"

梅雷迪思医生瞪着眼睛。

"您心里已经有人了。"他说。

公爵夫人迟疑着。

"陪我到林子里走一小段吧。"她末了说，撩起白裙子。

梅雷迪思依了她。两人去了半个钟头；回来时，这位新闻家那张被思虑烧红、刻满沟壑的脸，不大好读了。

他的心绪也不佳。非得登上克罗乔内山（Monte Crocione）的绝顶，才能叫他多少顺过这口气，回心转意，做公爵夫人牌局里一个点头的牌手。因为世上既有那种轻薄自利、明明该被明白事理的女人一眼看穿却永远看穿不了的男人——也就有那么一种男人，装备得太好，不适于这个坏世道：太好，出身太好，太可欲。

===Original===

It was in this somewhat flinty and carping mood that Meredith prepared himself for the advent of Jacob Delafield.
---
But when Delafield appeared, Meredith’s secret antagonisms were soon dissipated. There was certainly no challenging air of prosperity about the young man.
At first sight, indeed, he was his old cheerful self, always ready for a walk or a row, on easy terms at once with the Italian servants or boatmen. But soon other facts emerged﻿—stealthily, as it were, from the concealment in which a strong man was trying to keep them.
“That young man’s youth is over,” said Meredith, abruptly, to the Duchess one evening. He pointed to the figure of Delafield, who was pacing, alone with his pipe, up and down one of the lower terraces of the garden.
The Duchess showed a teased expression.
“It’s like something wearing through,” she said, slowly. “I suppose it was always there, but it didn’t show.”
“Name your ‘it.’ ”
“I can’t.” But she gave a little shudder, which made Meredith look at her with curiosity.
“You feel something ghostly﻿—unearthly?”
She nodded assent; crying out, however, immediately afterwards, as though in compunction, that he was one of the dearest and best of fellows.
“Of course he is,” said Meredith. “It is only the mystic in him coming out. He is one of the men who have the sixth sense.”
“Well, all I know is, he has the oddest power over people,” said Evelyn, with another shiver. “If Freddie had it, my life wouldn’t be worth living. Thank goodness, he hasn’t a vestige!”
“At bottom it’s the power of the priest,” said Meredith. “And you women are far too susceptible towards it. Nine times out of ten it plays the mischief.”
The Duchess was silent a moment. Then she bent towards her companion, finger on lip, her charming eyes glancing significantly towards the lower terrace. The figures on it were now two. Julie and Delafield paced together.
“But this is the tenth!” she said, in an eager whisper.
Meredith smiled at her, then flung her a dubious “*Chi sa?*” and changed the subject.
---
Delafield, who was a fine oar, had soon taken command of the lake expeditions; and by the help of two stalwart youths from Tremezzo, the four-oar was in use from morning till night.

===Chinese===

梅雷迪思便是揣着这副多少尖刻挑剔的心肠，准备迎接雅各布·德拉菲尔德的到来的。

---

可德拉菲尔德一到，梅雷迪思的种种暗中敌意很快烟消云散。这位年轻人身上，确实没有一点得意扬扬的挑战气。

乍一看，他还是从前那个快活人，随时肯走一趟路、划一回桨，同意大利仆役或船夫顷刻打得火热。可不久，别的事实露了头——悄悄地，仿佛是从一个强人的有意掩藏里溜出来的。

"这个年轻人的青春完结了。"一天傍晚，梅雷迪思突然对公爵夫人说。他指的是德拉菲尔德的身影——他正独自叼着烟斗，在花园下面的一层台地上来回走。

公爵夫人露出一副被搅扰的神情。

"像什么东西磨透了底。"她慢慢说，"我猜它一直在那儿，只是从前不显。"

"给您的'它'起个名。"

"我起不出。"可她轻轻一颤，叫梅雷迪思好奇地看她。

"您觉出什么鬼气的东西——不属于尘世的？"

她点头称是；随即又马上叫起来，像是过意不去：他可是顶可爱、顶好的小伙子之一。

"当然是。"梅雷迪思说，"不过是他身上的神秘气质露出来了。他是那种有第六感的男人。"

"反正我只知道，他对人有一种最古怪的威力。"伊芙纶说着又打了个寒噤，"弗雷迪若有这个，我的命就别想好过。谢天谢地，他连一丝一毫也没有！"

"归根到底，这是教士的威力。"梅雷迪思说，"你们女人对它太容易动心。十回里有九回，它闯的祸不小。"

公爵夫人沉默了一会儿。随后她朝同伴俯过来，手指抵着唇，那双迷人的眼睛意味深长地瞟向下层台地。台地上的人影现在是两个了。朱莉同德拉菲尔德并肩踱着。

"可这是第十回！"她热切地悄声说。

梅雷迪思朝她笑了笑，抛给她一句含混的"谁知道呢？（Chi sa?）"，便换了话题。

---

德拉菲尔德是把好桨，不久便接管了湖上远征的指挥；靠特雷梅佐（Tremezzo）来的两个健壮小伙子，那条四桨船从早用到晚。

===Original===

Through the broad lake which lies between Menaggio and Varenna it sped northward to Gravedona; or beneath the shadowy cliffs of the Villa Serbelloni it slipped over deep waters, haunted and dark, into the sunny spaces of Lecco; or it coasted along the steep sides of Monte Primo, so that the travellers in it might catch the blue stain of the gentians on the turf, where it sloped into the lucent wave below, or watch the fishermen on the rocks, spearing their prey in the green or golden shallows.
The weather was glorious﻿—a summer before its time. The wild cherries shook down their snow upon the grass; but the pears were now in bridal white, and a warmer glory of apple-blossom was just beginning to break upon the blue. The nights were calm and moonlit; the dawns were visions of mysterious and incredible beauty, wherein mountain and forest and lake were but the garments, diaphanous, impalpable, of some delicate, indwelling light and fire spirit, which breathed and pulsed through the solidity of rock, no less visibly than through the crystal leagues of air or the sunlit spaces of water.
Yet presently, as it were, a hush of waiting, of tension, fell upon their little party. Nature offered her best; but there was only an apparent acceptance of her bounties. Through the outward flow of talk and amusement, of wanderings on lake or hill, ugly hidden forces of pain and strife, regret, misery, resistance, made themselves rarely yet piercingly felt.
Julie drooped again. Her cheeks were paler even than when Meredith arrived. Delafield, too, began to be more silent, more absent. He was helpful and courteous as ever, but it began to be seen that his gayety was an effort, and now and then there were sharp or bitter notes in voice or manner, which jarred, and were not soon forgotten.
Presently, Meredith and the Duchess found themselves looking on, breathless and astonished, at the struggle of two personalities, the wrestle between two wills. They little knew that it was a renewed struggle﻿—second wrestle. But silently, by a kind of tacit agreement, they drew away from Delafield and Julie. They dimly understood that he pursued and she resisted; and that for him life was becoming gradually absorbed into the two facts of her presence and her resistance.
“*On ne s’appuie que sur ce qui résiste.*” For both of them these words were true.

===Chinese===

它有时穿过梅纳焦与瓦伦纳之间的宽阔湖面，向北疾驰去格拉韦多纳；有时贴着塞尔贝洛尼别墅幽暗的绝壁，滑过深黑而森然的水，驶进莱科一带阳光的地界；有时沿普里莫山（Monte Primo）陡峭的山腰巡行，好叫船上的人看得见草坡上龙胆的蓝斑——草坡一路斜进下方清亮的波里——或看礁石上的渔人，在碧绿或金黄的浅水里叉他们的猎物。

天气壮丽——一个提前的夏天。野樱桃把雪摇落在草上；梨树已经一身新娘的白；苹果花一层更暖的辉煌，正要破上蓝天。夜晚静而有月；黎明是神秘得不可思议的美之幻象——山、林、湖在其中不过是衣裳，轻纱似的、无形似的，裹着某种精微的、内住的光与火之灵；它透过岩石的实体呼吸、搏动，同透过晶明的空气或阳光下的水面一样清楚。

可不久，仿佛有一阵等待的、绷紧的静默，罩住了他们这一小队。自然献出了她最好的；可对她的厚赐，只有一种表面的领受。透过谈话与游乐的外流，透过湖上山上的一次次漫游，痛苦的、纷争的、悔恨的、凄惨的、抵拒的种种丑陋的暗力，稀稀落落而又扎人地让人觉出来了。

朱莉又蔫了下去。她的脸颊甚至比梅雷迪思刚到时更白。德拉菲尔德也更沉默、更出神了。他还是一样殷勤周到，可人们开始看出，他的快活是撑出来的；言语举止里，间或冒出尖刻或苦涩的音，扎人，并且久久不散。

不久，梅雷迪思和公爵夫人发现自己正屏息而惊讶地旁观着两个人格的角力、两个意志的摔跤。他们哪里知道，这是一场重开的角力——第二场摔跤。可是他们无声地、仿佛达成了默契似的，从德拉菲尔德和朱莉身边撤开了。他们模模糊糊地懂得：他追，她抗；而对他来说，生活正渐渐吸进两个事实里——她的在场，和她的抗拒。

"人只有靠抵抗自己的东西才立得住。（On ne s'appuie que sur ce qui résiste.）"对他们两个，这话都真。

===Original===

Fundamentally, and beyond all passing causes of grief and anger, each was fascinated by the full strength of nature in the other. Neither could ever forget the other. The hours grew electric, and every tiny incident became charged with spiritual meaning.
Often for hours together Julie would try to absorb herself in talk with Meredith. But the poor fellow got little joy from it. Presently, at a word or look of Delafield’s she would let herself be recaptured, as though with a proud reluctance; they wandered away together; and once more Meredith and the Duchess became the merest bystanders.
The Duchess shrugged her shoulders over it, and, though she laughed, sometimes the tears were in her eyes. She felt the hovering of passion, but it was no passion known to her own blithe nature.
And if only this strange state of things might end, one way or other, and set her free to throw her arms round her Duke’s neck, and beg his pardon for all these weeks of desertion! She said to herself, ruefully, that her babies would indeed have forgotten her.
---
Yet she stood stoutly to her post, and the weeks passed quickly by. It was the dramatic energy of the situation﻿—so much more dramatic in truth than either she or Meredith suspected﻿—that made it such a strain upon the onlookers.
One evening they had left the boat at Tremezzo, that they might walk back along that most winning of paths that skirts the lake between the last houses of Tremezzo and the inn at Cadenabbia. The sunset was nearly over, but the air was still suffused with its rose and pearl, and fragrant with the scent of flowering laurels. Each mountain face, each white village, either couched on the water’s edge or grouped about its slender campanile on some shoulder of the hills, each house and tree and figure seemed still penetrated with light, the glorified creatures of some just revealed and already fading world. The echoes of the evening bell were floating on the lake, and from a boat in front, full of peasant-folk, there rose a sound of singing, some litany of saint or virgin, which stole in harmonies, rudely true, across the water.
“They have been to the pilgrimage church above Lenno,” said Julie, pointing to the boat, and in order to listen to the singing, she found a seat on a low wall above the lake.

===Chinese===

说到底，越过一切一时的悲愤之因，两人各自都被对方天性的全部力量迷住了。谁也忘不掉谁。时光带上电，每一桩最小的事都充了灵性的意味。

朱莉常常一连几个钟头想把自己埋进同梅雷迪思的谈话里。可这可怜人从中得的乐很薄。不多时，德拉菲尔德的一个字、一瞥，她又由人捉了回去，仿佛带着高傲的不情愿；两人一同走开；梅雷迪思和公爵夫人便又一次沦为纯粹的旁观者。

公爵夫人为此耸耸肩；她虽然笑，眼里有时却含着泪。她觉出激情的盘旋；可那不是她自己那副快活天性认得的任何一种激情。

但愿这桩奇事早些有个了局，好歹都行，放她自由，好扑进她公爵的脖颈，为这几个星期的抛下讨他的饶！她苦笑着对自己说，她的娃娃们怕真要把她忘了。

---

可她仍牢牢守着岗位，几个星期飞快过去。是局面的那股戏剧性的劲——实在远比她或梅雷迪思疑心的更富戏剧性——叫旁观的人这样吃力。

一天傍晚，他们在特雷梅佐下了船，好沿那段最动人心的湖畔小径步行回去——小径从特雷梅佐最后几户人家延伸到卡登纳比亚的旅店。日落已近尾声，空气里还漫着玫瑰与珍珠的余光，开花月桂的香气袭人。每一面山坡，每一座白村子——或卧在水边，或环着山肩上纤秀的钟楼——每一座屋、每一棵树、每一个人影，仿佛都还被光浸透着，是某个方才显形、已在隐去的世界的荣化生灵。晚钟的回声浮在湖上；前面一条挤满乡民的船上，升起一片歌声，某支圣徒或圣母的连祷，以粗朴而真挚的和声，渡水而来。

"他们是从伦诺（Lenno）上面的朝圣教堂回来的。"朱莉指着那条船说；为了听那歌声，她在湖上方一堵矮墙上寻了个座位。

===Original===

There was no reply, and, looking round her, she saw with a start that only Delafield was beside her, that the Duchess and Meredith had already rounded the corner of the Villa Carlotta and were out of sight.
Delafield’s gaze was fixed upon her. He was very pale, and suddenly Julie’s breath seemed to fail her.
“I don’t think I can bear it any longer,” he said, as he came close to her.
“Bear what?”
“That you should look as you do now.”
Julie made no reply. Her eyes, very sad and bitter, searched the blue dimness of the lake in silence.
Delafield sat down on the wall beside her. Not a soul was in sight. At the Cadenabbia Hotel, the table d’hôte had gathered in the visitors; a few boats passed and repassed in the distance, but on land all was still.
Suddenly he took her hand with a firm grasp.
“Are you never going to forgive me?” he said, in a low voice.
“I suppose I ought to bless you.”
Her face seemed to him to express the tremulous misery of a heart deeply, perhaps irrevocably, wounded. Emotion rose in a tide, but he crushed it down.
He bent over her, speaking with deliberate tenderness.
“Julie, do you remember what you promised Lord Lackington when he was dying?”
“Oh!” cried Julie.
She sprang to her feet, speechless and suffocated. Her eyes expressed a mingled pride and terror.
He paused, confronting her with a pale resolution.
“You didn’t know that I had seen him?”
“Know!”
She turned away fiercely, choking with sobs she could hardly control, as the memory of that bygone moment returned upon her.
“I thought as much,” said Delafield, in a low voice. “You hoped never to hear of your promise again.”
She made no answer; but she sank again upon the seat beside the lake, and supporting herself on one delicate hand, which clung to the coping of the wall, she turned her pale and tear-stained face to the lake and the evening sky. There was in her gesture an unconscious yearning, a mute and anguished appeal, as though from the oppressions of human character to the broad strength of nature, that was not lost on Delafield. His mind became the centre of a swift and fierce debate. One voice said: “Why are you persecuting her? Respect her weakness and her grief.” And another replied: “It is because she is weak that she must yield﻿—must allow herself to be guided and adored.”
He came close to her again.

===Chinese===

没有人应声；她环顾四周，吃了一惊：身边只剩德拉菲尔德，公爵夫人和梅雷迪思早已绕过卡洛塔别墅的拐角，不见了。

德拉菲尔德的目光正定在她身上。他脸色很白，朱莉忽然觉得气都透不上来。

"我想我再也受不住了。"他走近她说。

"受不住什么？"

"受不住您这副模样。"

朱莉没有回答。她那双盛满悲哀和苦涩的眼睛，默默地在湖上那片蓝色的微暗里搜寻。

德拉菲尔德在她身旁的墙上坐下。四下不见一个人影。卡登纳比亚旅店的包桌晚餐把客人都收进去了；远处几条船来来往往，岸上却一片寂静。

他忽然一把攥住她的手，攥得很紧。

"您永远不饶恕我了？"他低声说。

"我想我该为您祝福。"

在他看来，她的脸表达着一颗受了重伤、也许无可救药的心那种颤巍巍的凄苦。情绪如潮而起，他把它压了下去。

他俯向她，说得温柔而字斟句酌。

"朱莉，您记得您答应过临终的拉金顿勋爵什么吗？"

"哦！"朱莉叫了一声。

她跳起来，说不出话，透不过气。她的眼里是骄傲与惊恐的混合。

他停住，以一副苍白的决断迎着她。

"您不知道我见过他？"

"知道！"

她猛地扭过身去，被几乎按捺不住的呜咽哽着——那逝去一刻的记忆重新漫上来。

"我料到了。"德拉菲尔德低声说，"您指望那承诺永远不再被提起。"

她不作答；只是重新跌坐在湖边的座位上，一只纤细的手撑着，攀住墙头的压顶石，把那张苍白泪湿的脸转向湖水和暮天。她的姿势里有一种不自觉的渴慕，一种无言而绞痛的呼吁——仿佛从人格的重压之下，诉向自然的博大伟力——德拉菲尔德全看在眼里。他的心成了短兵相接的一阵激烈辩论的战场。一个声音说："你为什么逼迫她？敬重她的软弱和悲伤吧。"另一个答："正因为软弱，她才必须降服——必须容自己被人引导、被人爱慕。"

他又向她走近。

===Original===

Any passerby might have supposed that they were both looking at the distant boat and listening to the pilgrimage chant.
“Do you think I don’t understand why you made that promise?” he said, very gently, and the mere self-control of his voice and manner carried a spell with it for the woman beside him. “It was wrung out of you by kindness for a dying man. You thought I should never know, or I should never claim it. Well, I am selfish. I take advantage. I do claim it. I saw Lord Lackington only a few hours before his death. ‘She mustn’t be alone,’ he said to me, several times. And then, almost at the last, ‘Ask her again. She’ll consider it﻿—she promised.’ ”
Julie turned impetuously.
“Neither of us is bound by that﻿—neither of us.”
Delafield smiled.
“Does that mean that I am asking you now because he bade me?”
A pause. Julie must needs raise her eyes to his. She flushed red and withdrew them.
“No,” he said, with a long breath, “you don’t mean that, and you don’t think it. As for you﻿—yes, you are bound! Julie, once more I bring you my plea, and you must consider it.”
“How can I be your wife?” she said, her breast heaving. “You know all that has happened. It would be monstrous.”
“Not at all,” was his quiet reply. “It would be natural and right. Julie, it is strange that I should be talking to you like this. You’re so much cleverer than I﻿—in some ways, so much stronger. And yet, in others﻿—you’ll let me say it, won’t you?﻿—I could help you. I could protect you. It’s all I care for in the world.”
“How can I be your wife?” she repeated, passionately, wringing her hands.
“Be what you will﻿—at home. My friend, comrade, housemate. I ask nothing more﻿—*nothing*.” His voice dropped, and there was a pause. Then he resumed. “But, in the eyes of the world, make me your servant and your husband!”
“I can’t condemn you to such a fate,” she cried. “You know where my heart is.”
Delafield did not waver.
“I know where your heart was,” he said, with firmness. “You will banish that man from your thoughts in time. He has no right to be there. I take all the risks﻿—all.”
“Well, at least for you, I am no hypocrite,” she said, with a quivering lip. “You know what I am.”
“Yes, I know, and I am at your feet.”
The tears dropped from Julie’s eyes. She turned away and hid her face against one of the piers of the wall.
Delafield attempted no caress.

===Chinese===

随便哪个过路人都会以为，他们两个只是在望远处那条船、听那朝圣的歌。

"您以为我不明白您许那个诺的缘故？"他非常温和地说，单是这嗓音和举止的自制，就对身旁的女人施着一道符。"那是您对一个垂死的人的仁心绞出来的。您以为我永远不会知道，或者永远不会索取。好——我自私。我占这个便宜。我索取。拉金顿勋爵去世前几个小时我还见过他。'她不能一个人过。'他对我说了好几遍。到末了，几乎最后：'再向她求一次。她会考虑的——她答应过。'"

朱莉冲动地转过身。

"我们谁也不受那个约束——谁也不。"

德拉菲尔德笑了。

"这是说，我此刻求您，是因为他嘱托了我？"

一阵沉默。朱莉不由得抬眼望他。她红了脸，又把眼睛移开。

"不。"他长吁一口气说，"您不是这个意思，也不这样想。至于您——不，您受着约束！朱莉，我再一次向您呈上我的恳求，您必须考虑它。"

"我怎么能做您的妻子？"她说，胸口起伏着，"发生过的一切您都知道。那就荒唐了。"

"一点儿也不。"他平静地答，"那是自然的、正当的。朱莉，说来奇怪，我竟会同您这样说话。您比我聪明得多——有些方面，也强得多。可在另一些方面——您容我说，好不好？——我能帮您。我能护您。这是我在世上唯一在乎的事。"

"我怎么能做您的妻子？"她绞着双手，激动地重复。

"在家随您怎样——做我的朋友、同志、同屋的人。我再无所求——无所求。"他的声音低下去，一阵静默。随后他接着说："可是在世人的眼里，让我做您的仆人和丈夫吧！"

"我不能把您判给这样的命运。"她叫道，"您知道我的心在哪里。"

德拉菲尔德毫不动摇。

"我知道您的心曾在哪里。"他坚定地说，"时候一到，您会把那个人从思绪里赶出去。他没有权利待在那儿。一切风险我担——全部。"

"那么至少对您，我不做伪君子。"她嘴唇发颤地说，"您知道我是什么人。"

"是，我知道，而我拜倒在您脚下。"

泪从朱莉眼里落下来。她转过脸，把脸藏进墙的一根墩柱上。

===Original===

He quietly set himself to draw the life that he had to offer her, the comradeship that he proposed to her. Not a word of what the world called his “prospects” entered in. She knew very well that he could not bring himself to speak of them. Rather, a sort of ascetic and mystical note made itself heard in all he said of the future, a note that before now had fascinated and controlled a woman whose ambition was always strangely tempered with high, poetical imagination.
Yet, ambitious she was, and her mind inevitably supplied what his voice left unsaid.
“He will have to fill his place whether he wishes it or no,” she said to herself. “And if, in truth, he desires my help﻿—”
Then she shrank from her own wavering. Look where she would into her life, it seemed to her that all was monstrous and out of joint.
“You don’t realize what you ask,” she said, at last, in despair. “I am not what you call a good woman﻿—you know it too well. I don’t measure things by your standards. I am capable of such a journey as you found me on. I can’t find in my own mind that I repent it at all. I can tell a lie﻿—you can’t. I can have the meanest and most sordid thoughts﻿—you can’t. Lady Henry thought me an intriguer﻿—I am one. It is in my blood. And I don’t know whether, in the end, I could understand your language and your life. And if I don’t, I shall make you miserable.”
She looked up, her slender frame straightening under what was, in truth, a noble defiance.
Delafield bent over her and took both her hands forcibly in his own.
“If all that were true, I would rather risk it a thousand times over than go out of your life again﻿—a stranger. Julie, you have done mad things for love﻿—you should know what love is. Look in my face﻿—there﻿—your eyes in mine! Give way! The dead ask it of you﻿—and it is God’s will.”
And as, drawn by the last, low-spoken words, Julie looked up into his face, she felt herself enveloped by a mystical and passionate tenderness that paralyzed her resistance. A force, superhuman, laid its grasp upon her will. With a burst of tears, half in despair, half in revolt, she submitted.

===Chinese===

德拉菲尔德没有试图爱抚。他安安静静，动手向她描画他能奉献给她的那种生活、他向她提议的这种同志之谊。世人所谓他的"前程"，一个字也没有出现。她清楚得很，他自己开不了那个口。倒是另一种音——苦修的和神秘的——在他谈到将来的每句话里响着；正是这种音，从前就迷惑过、降服过一个女人——她的野心，从来就奇异地掺着高远的、诗意的想象。

然而她终究野心勃勃；他嗓音没说出的，她的心智不由自主替他补上了。

"他愿不愿意，都得去坐他的位子。"她自语道，"倘若他当真要我的帮手——"

随即她又为自己的动摇畏缩了。无论朝生活的哪一处看，她都觉得一切荒唐、一切脱了榫。

"您不明白您求的是什么。"末了她绝望地说，"我不是您所谓的好女人——这您太清楚了。我不用您那套尺子量事。我干得出您撞见我的那种事。在我自己心里，我找不出我对它有半点悔。我会撒谎——您不会。我会起最卑鄙、最龌龊的念头——您不会。亨利夫人当我是耍手段的——我就是。这在我血里。而且我不知道，到头来我懂不懂您的语言、您的生活。我若不懂，我会叫您痛苦。"

她抬起头，纤瘦的身架在一副——实在说来——高贵的倔强之下挺直了。

德拉菲尔德俯下身，用力把她的双手攥进自己手里。

"倘若这些都是真的，我也宁可把这风险冒上一千回，也不肯再作为一个陌生人走出您的生活。朱莉，您为爱情做过疯狂的事——您该知道爱情是什么。看我的脸——看——您的眼睛对着我的！降服吧！死者向您求这个——这也是上帝的旨意。"

当朱莉被这最后几句低语牵引着、仰起脸望进他的脸时，她觉得自己被一种神秘而炽烈的温柔裹住了，它瘫痪了她的抵抗。一股超人的力量攫住了她的意志。泪如泉涌——半是绝望，半是抗辩——她降服了。

## XXII / XXII / 第二十二章
===Original===

In the first week of May, Julie Le Breton married Jacob Delafield in the English Church at Florence. The Duchess was there. So was the Duke﻿—a sulky and ill-resigned spectator of something which he believed to be the peculiar and mischievous achievement of his wife.
At the church door Julie and Delafield left for Camaldoli.
“Well, if you imagine that I intend to congratulate you or anybody else upon that performance you are very much mistaken,” said the Duke, as he and his wife drove back to the “Grand Bretagne” together.
“I don’t deny it’s﻿—risky,” said the Duchess, her hands on her lap, her eyes dreamily following the streets.
“Risky!” repeated the Duke, shrugging his shoulders. “Well, I don’t want to speak harshly of your friends, Evelyn, but Miss Le Breton﻿—”
“Mrs. Delafield,” said the Duchess.
“Mrs. Delafield, then”﻿—the name was evidently a difficult mouthful﻿—“seems to me a most undisciplined and unmanageable woman. Why does she look like a tragedy queen at her marriage? Jacob is twice too good for her, and she’ll lead him a life. And how you can reconcile it to your conscience to have misled me so completely as you have in this matter, I really can’t imagine.”
“Misled you?” said Evelyn.
Her innocence was really a little hard to bear, and not even the beauty of her blue eyes, now happily restored to him, could appease the mentor at her side.
“You led me plainly to believe,” he repeated, with emphasis, “that if I helped her through the crisis of leaving Lady Henry she would relinquish her designs on Delafield.”
“Did I?” said the Duchess. And putting her hands over her face she laughed rather hysterically. “But that wasn’t why you lent her the house, Freddie.”
“You coaxed me into it, of course,” said the Duke.
“No, it was Julie herself got the better of you,” said Evelyn, triumphantly. “You felt her spell, just as we all do, and wanted to do something for her.”
“Nothing of the sort,” said the Duke, determined to admit no recollection to his disadvantage. “It was your doing entirely.”
The Duchess thought it discreet to let him at least have the triumph of her silence, smiling, and a little sarcastic though it were.
“And of all the undeserved good fortune!” he resumed, feeling in his irritable disapproval that the moral order of the universe had been somehow trifled with.

===Chinese===

五月的第一个星期，朱莉·勒布雷东在佛罗伦萨的英国教堂嫁给了雅各布·德拉菲尔德。公爵夫人在场。公爵也在——这场婚事，他认定是妻子别出心裁又为害不浅的杰作，他悻悻然、死不服气地在旁观礼。
教堂门口，朱莉同德拉菲尔德动身去卡马尔多利（Camaldoli）。
“好，你要是以为我会为这场表演向你或向任何人道贺，那你就大错特错了，”公爵同妻子一起驱车回“大不列颠旅馆”的路上说。
“我不否认这——有风险，”公爵夫人双手叠在膝上，眼睛恍惚地追着街景说。
“风险！”公爵耸耸肩，重复道。“唉，我不想说你朋友的坏话，伊芙纶，可勒布雷东小姐——”
“是德拉菲尔德太太，”公爵夫人说。
“那好，德拉菲尔德太太，”——这名字显然难以下咽——“在我看来是个毫无教养、毫无办法管束的女人。她干吗在自己婚礼上摆出一副悲剧王后的架势？雅各布比她强出一倍，她有他受的。至于你在这件事上把我骗得这样彻底，你怎么能对得起自己的良心，我实在无法想象。”
“骗你？”伊芙纶说。
她这份无辜实在有点让人消受不起；连她那双蓝眼睛的美——如今总算物归原主——也安抚不了身旁这位导师。
“你让我明确地相信，”他不依不饶地重复，“只要我帮她渡过离开亨利夫人那个关口，她就会放弃对德拉菲尔德的图谋。”
“是吗？”公爵夫人说着，双手掩面，笑得近乎歇斯底里。“可弗雷迪，你借房子给她，可不是为了这个。”
“还不是你哄的，”公爵说。
“不，是朱莉本人把你拿下的，”伊芙纶得意地说。“你也着了她的道，同我们大家一样，想为她做点什么。”
“没那回事，”公爵说，凡是于己不利的记忆一概拒不承认。“全是你一手办的。”
公爵夫人觉得，让他至少赢得她沉默的胜利，算是明智——尽管这沉默含着微笑、含着一点讥诮。
“再说这好运来得全无道理！”他又开了腔，惴惴的反对里，只觉宇宙的道德秩序不知被谁戏弄了一场。

===Original===

“In the first place, she is the daughter of people who flagrantly misconducted themselves﻿—*that* apparently does her no harm. Then she enters the service of Lady Henry in a confidential position, and uses it to work havoc in Lady Henry’s social relations. That, I am glad to say, *has* done her a little harm, although not nearly as much as she deserves. And finally she has a most discreditable flirtation with a man already engaged﻿—to her own cousin, please observe!﻿—and pulls wires for him all over the place in the most objectionable and unwomanly manner.”
“As if everybody didn’t do that!” cried the Duchess. “You know, Freddie, that your own mother always used to boast that she had made six bishops and saved the Establishment.”
The Duke took no notice.
“And yet there she is! Lord Lackington has left her a fortune﻿—a competence, anyway. She marries Jacob Delafield﻿—rather a fool, I consider, but all the same one of the best fellows in the world. And at any time, to judge from what one hears of the health both of Chudleigh and his boy, she may find herself Duchess of Chudleigh.”
The Duke threw himself back in the carriage with the air of one who waits for Providence to reply.
“Oh, well, you see, you can’t make the world into a moral tale to please you,” said the Duchess, absently.
Then, after a pause, she asked, “Are you still going to let them have the house, Freddie?”
“I imagine that if Jacob Delafield applies to me to let it to *him*, that I shall not refuse him,” said the Duke, stiffly.
The Duchess smiled behind her fan. Yet her tender heart was not in reality very happy about her Julie. She knew well enough that it was a strange marriage of which they had just been witnesses﻿—a marriage containing the seeds of many untoward things only too likely to develop unless fate were kinder than rash mortals have any right to expect.
“I wish to goodness Delafield weren’t so religious,” murmured the Duchess, fervently, pursuing her own thoughts.
“Evelyn!”
“Well, you see, Julie isn’t, at all,” she added, hastily.
“You need not have troubled yourself to tell me that,” was the Duke’s indignant reply.
---
After a fortnight at Camaldoli and Vallombrosa the Delafields turned towards Switzerland. Julie, who was a lover of Rousseau and Obermann, had been also busy with the letters of Byron. She wished to see with her own eyes St. Gingolphe and Chillon, Bevay and Glion.

===Chinese===

“第一，她是公然失德之人的女儿——这居然无损于她分毫。然后她进入亨利夫人家，居于亲信之位，却借它在亨利夫人的社交关系里大加摧折。这一条，我很高兴地说，倒是伤了她一点，虽然远不及她应得的。最后，同一个订了婚的男人——同她自己的表妹订着婚，请注意！——放肆调情，还四处为他牵线奔走，那副做派最叫人反感，全无妇道。”
“仿佛人人不这么干似的！”公爵夫人叫道。“你知道的，弗雷迪，你自己的母亲就总夸口说她造就了六位主教，拯救了国教。”
公爵不理这个茬。
“可她偏偏就在那儿！拉金顿勋爵给她留下一笔财产——至少是一份体面的进项。她嫁了雅各布·德拉菲尔德——依我看是个傻瓜，可依旧是世上最好的人之一。而且，听听人家怎么说查德利同他儿子的健康，她随时都可能当上查德利公爵夫人。”
公爵往椅背上一仰，那副神情像是等着上苍给个答复。
“唉，你瞧，你没法把这世界编成一篇道德故事来讨自己欢喜，”公爵夫人心不在焉地说。
顿了顿，她又问：“那座房子，你还让他们住吗，弗雷迪？”
“我想，若是雅各布·德拉菲尔德本人向我开口租它，我是不会拒绝的，”公爵硬邦邦地说。
公爵夫人藏在扇子后面笑了。可她那颗软心肠，为自己的朱莉其实并不真快活。她知道得很清楚，他们方才见证的是一桩奇特的婚姻——一桩埋着许多不祥种子的婚姻，除非命运比鲁莽的凡人有权指望的更仁慈，那些种子太可能发芽了。
“老天在上，但愿德拉菲尔德别这么虔诚，”公爵夫人喃喃道，颇为恳切，只顾想自己的心事。
“伊芙纶！”
“你瞧，朱莉可一点儿也不，”她急忙补了一句。
“这个就不劳你告诉我了，”公爵愤然答道。
---
在卡马尔多利和瓦隆布罗萨盘桓两星期后，德拉菲尔德夫妇转向瑞士。朱莉素来钟爱卢梭（Rousseau）与《奥伯曼》（Obermann），近来又忙着读拜伦的书信。她要亲眼去看看圣然戈尔夫与西庸城堡，看看贝韦与格利永。

===Original===

So one day at the end of May they found themselves at Montreux. But Montreux was already hot and crowded, and Julie’s eyes turned in longing to the heights. They found an old inn at Charnex, whereof the garden commanded the whole head of the lake, and there they settled themselves for a fortnight, till business, in fact, should recall Delafield to England. The Duke of Chudleigh had shown all possible kindness and cordiality with regard to the marriage, and the letter in which he welcomed his cousin’s new wife had both touched Julie’s feelings and satisfied her pride. “You are marrying one of the best of men,” wrote this melancholy father of a dying son. “My boy and I owe him more than can be written. I can only tell you that for those he loves he grudges nothing﻿—no labor, no sacrifice of himself. There are no half-measures in his affections. He has spent himself too long on sick and sorry creatures like ourselves. It is time he had a little happiness on his own account. You will give it him, and Mervyn and I will be most grateful to you. If joy and health can never be ours, I am not yet so vile as to grudge them to others. God bless you! Jacob will tell you that my house is not a gay one; but if you and he will sometimes visit it, you will do something to lighten its gloom.”
Julie wondered, as she wrote her very graceful reply, how much the Duke might know about herself. Jacob had told his cousin, as she knew, the story of her parentage and of Lord Lackington’s recognition of his granddaughter. But as soon as the marriage was announced it was not likely that Lady Henry had been able to hold her tongue.
A good many interesting tales of his cousin’s bride had, indeed, reached the melancholy Duke. Lady Henry had done all that she conceived it her duty to do, filling many pages of notepaper with what the Duke regarded as most unnecessary information.
At any rate, he had brushed it all aside with the impatience of one for whom nothing on earth had now any savor or value beyond one or two indispensable affections. “What’s good enough for Jacob is good for me,” he wrote to Lady Henry, “and if I may offer you some advice, it is that you should not quarrel with Jacob about a matter so vital as his marriage.

===Chinese===

于是在五月底的一天，他们到了蒙特勒。可蒙特勒已经炎热拥挤，朱莉的眼睛渴望地转向高处。他们在沙尔内寻着一家老客栈，园子正俯瞰整个湖头，便在那里安顿下来，住上两周——事实上，直到事务把德拉菲尔德召回英国。查德利公爵对这桩婚事表现出一切可能的亲善与热诚，他欢迎表弟新妇的那封信，既触动了朱莉的感情，又满足了她的骄傲。“你嫁的是世上最好的人之一，”这位痛失爱子的忧郁父亲写道。“我的孩子和我欠他的情，笔墨写不尽。我只能告诉你：对他所爱的人，他不吝惜任何东西——不吝辛劳，不惜自身。他的感情从不掺半分假。他为我们这样的病苦之人耗费自己太久了。该轮到他为自己得一点幸福了。你会给他的，默文同我会深深感激你。倘若欢乐与健康永远轮不到我们，我也还没有卑劣到眼红别人的份上。上帝保佑你！雅各布会告诉你，我的家不是一个快乐的家；但你和他若肯有时来看看，便能为它的阴郁减一分颜色。”
朱莉写着措辞极优雅的回信时，心里琢熿：公爵对自己的底细究竟知道多少。她知道，雅各布已把她的身世、把拉金顿勋爵认下外孙女的经过告诉了表兄。可婚讯一传开，亨利夫人是不可能守口如瓶的。
关于表弟新娘的种种有趣传闻，确实也传到了忧郁的公爵耳中。亨利夫人自认为责无旁贷，写满了好几页信纸，全是公爵眼里全然多余的信息。
无论如何，他把这一切拂到一边，带着那种不耐——对这样的人来说，尘世间除了一两样不可或缺的亲情，再没有任何东西有滋味、有价值。“配得上雅各布的，就配得上我，”他写信给亨利夫人，“若容我进一言：不要在雅各布婚姻这样重大的事情上同他闹翻。你讲的这段是非曲直，我实在无从置评；但与其同雅各布决裂，他领来见我的任何人我都愿意接纳。何况据我所知，这位女士十分聪慧出众，且父母两系血统皆良。亲爱的弗洛拉，你一生莫非从未有过患难，才能这样轻飘飘地兴战启衅？若有，我倒羡慕你；可我既无这份精力，也无这份兴致来效法你。”

===Original===

Into the rights and wrongs of the story you tell me, I really cannot enter; but rather than break with Jacob I would welcome *anybody* he chose to present to me. And in this case I understand the lady is very clever, distinguished, and of good blood on both sides. Have you had no trouble in your life, my dear Flora, that you can make quarrels with a light heart? If so, I envy you; but I have neither the energy nor the good spirits wherewith to imitate you.”
Julie, of course, knew nothing of this correspondence, though from the Duke’s letters to Jacob she divined that something of the kind had taken place. But it was made quite plain to her that she was to be spared all the friction and all the difficulty which may often attend the entrance of a person like herself within the circle of a rich and important family like the Delafields. With Lady Henry, indeed, the fight had still to be fought. But Jacob’s mother, influenced on one side by her son and on the other by the head of the family, accepted her daughter-in-law with the facile kindliness and good temper that were natural to her; while his sister, the fair-haired and admirable Susan, owed her brother too much and loved him too well to be other than friendly to his wife.
No; on the worldly side all was smooth. The marriage had been carried through with ease and quietness. The Duke, in spite of Jacob’s remonstrances, had largely increased his cousin’s salary, and Julie was already enjoying the income left her by Lord Lackington. She had only to reappear in London as Jacob’s wife to resume far more than her old social ascendency. The winning cards had all passed into her hands, and if now there was to be a struggle with Lady Henry, Lady Henry would be worsted.
All this was or should have been agreeable to the sensitive nerves of a woman who knew the worth of social advantages. It had no effect, however, on the mortal depression which was constantly Julie’s portion during the early weeks of her marriage.
As for Delafield, he had entered upon this determining experiment of his life﻿—a marriage, which was merely a legalized comradeship, with the woman he adored﻿—in the mind of one resolved to pay the price of what he had done. This graceful and stately woman, with her high intelligence and her social gifts, was now his own property. She was to be the companion of his days and the mistress of his house.

===Chinese===

这番通信朱莉自然一无所知，但从公爵给雅各布的信里，她猜到发生过这样的事。不过有一点她看得清清楚楚：像她这样一个人，进入德拉菲尔德这样有钱有势的家族圈子时常见的种种摩擦与为难，都将同她无干。同亨利夫人，那一仗确实还得打。可雅各布的母亲，一头受儿子影响、一头受族长影响，以她天生的随和与好脾气接纳了儿媳；他的妹妹，那位金头发、人极好的苏珊，欠哥哥太多、爱哥哥太深，对哥哥的妻子只能友好。
是的，世俗这一面一切顺遂。婚礼办得轻松而安静。公爵不顾雅各布的推辞，大幅加了表弟的薪水；拉金顿勋爵留给朱莉的进项，她已在享用。她只须以雅各布之妻的身份在伦敦重新露面，便可恢复远胜从前的社交威望。好牌尽数到了她手里；倘若这回还要同亨利夫人较量，输家必是亨利夫人。
这一切，对一个深知社交种种好处的女人的敏感神经来说，本该、也理应舒心适意。然而婚后最初几周始终缠着朱莉的那份致命的消沉，却纹丝不为所动。
至于德拉菲尔德，他步入这一决定他一生的实验——同他挚爱的女人结一场不过是合法名分的伴侣之谊——心里抱定一个宗旨：甘愿为自己所做的事付出代价。这个优雅庄重的女人，连同她的高智与社交才具，如今是他的了。她将是他岁月的伴侣、他宅子的女主人。

===Original===

But although he knew well that he had a certain strong hold upon her, she did not love him, and none of the fusion of true marriage had taken place or could take place. So be it. He set himself to build up a relation between them which should justify the violence offered to natural and spiritual law. His own delicacy of feeling and perception combined with the strength of his passion to make every action of their common day a symbol and sacrament. That her heart regretted Warkworth, that bitterness and longing, an unspent and baffled love, must be constantly overshadowing her﻿—these things he not only knew, he was forever reminding himself of them, driving them, as it were, into consciousness, as the ascetic drives the spikes into his flesh. His task was to comfort her, to make her forget, to bring her back to common peace and cheerfulness of mind.
To this end he began with appealing as much as possible to her intelligence. He warmly encouraged her work for Meredith. From the first days of their marriage he became her listener, scholar, and critic. Himself interested mainly in social, economical, or religious discussion, he humbly put himself to school in matters of belles-lettres. His object was to enrich Julie’s daily life with new ambitions and new pleasures, which might replace the broodings of her illness and convalescence, and then, to make her feel that she had at hand, in the companion of that life, one who felt a natural interest in all her efforts, a natural pride in all her successes.
Alack! the calculation was too simple﻿—and too visible. It took too little account of the complexities of Julie’s nature, of the ravages and the shock of passion. Julie herself might be ready enough to return to the things of the mind, but they were no sooner offered to her, as it were, in exchange for the perilous delights of love, than she grew dumbly restive. She felt herself, also, too much observed, too much thought over, made too often, if the truth were known, the subject of religious or mystical emotion.
More and more, also, was she conscious of strangeness and eccentricity in the man she had married. It often seemed to that keen and practical sense which in her mingled so oddly with the capacity for passion that, as they grew older, and her mind recovered tone and balance, she would probably love the world disastrously more and he disastrously less.

===Chinese===

可他虽深知自己在她身上握有一分有力的把柄，她并不爱他；真正婚姻里的那种交融，没有发生，也不会发生。那便如此吧。他着手在两人之间筑起一种关系，足以偿清他对自然律与心灵律的强暴。他自己感觉与体察的细腻，同他激情的力量合在一处，使他们共同生活中的每一个动作都成了象征与圣事。她的心还念着沃克沃思，怨苦与渴念、那份未耗尽、受挫的爱情必时时罩在她心头——这些他不但知道，还时刻提醒自己，仿佛把它们往意识里钉，一如苦行僧把铁钉钉进皮肉。他的任务是安慰她，使她遗忘，把她带回寻常的平和与开朗。
为达此目的，他先尽量诉诸她的智性。他热忱鼓励她为梅雷迪思做的工。从婚后头几天起，他便做她的听众、学生与批评者。他自己主要的兴趣在社会、经济或宗教的讨论，如今却谦卑地把自己送进纯文学的学堂。他的目标，是让朱莉的日常生活盛满新的抱负与新的乐趣，好替换她疾病与将息期间的种种冥想；再让她感到，在这位人生伴侣手边，有一个人对她的一切努力怀着天然的关切，对她的一切成功怀着天然的自豪。
唉！这盘算太简单——也太外露。它太低估了朱莉天性的繁复，低估了激情的劫掠与震撼。朱莉本人或许巴不得回到心智的事物上去；可这些事物一旦仿佛被摆出来，同爱情那些危险的欢愉相交换，她便哑然生出倔强。她还感到，自己被观察得太多、被思虑得太多——说句实话，还太经常地成了宗教或神秘情感的题目。
而且她越来越意识到，自己嫁的这个人身上有一种奇异古怪。她身上那种敏锐而实际的识力——同激情的能力奇特地混杂在一起——常使她觉得：随着年岁增长，随着她心神恢复常态与平衡，她多半会不可救药地越来越爱这个世界，而他则不可救药地越来越不爱。

===Original===

And if so, the gulf between them, instead of closing, could but widen.
